CONFIDENTIAL. 


Correspondence respecting the Rights and Privileges of 
the Latin and Greek Churches in Turkey. 


(EASTERN QUESTION) 


Part VII. 


No. 1. 
Lord Cowley to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received June 25.) 


(No. 785. 
My Lord, Paris, June 24, 1855. 

ON Friday last Count Walewski addressed two despatches to M. de 
Bourqueney, in answer to the last communications made by M. de Hubner. In 
the first Count Walewski objects to the instructions sent by Count Buol to the 
Austrian Minister at St. Petersburgh, alluded to in my despatch No. 756 of the 
19th instant, as contrary, in part of their contents, to the engagements taken by 
Austria towards the belligerent Powers; in the second he animadverts in strong 
language upon the instructions addressed by Count Buol to the Internuncio, to 
which 1 allude equally in my above-mentioned despatch. 

These despatches of Count Walewski were accompanied by a confidential 
letter to M, de Bonrqueney, in which his Excellency desires the French Envoy to 
call the serious attention of Count Buol to the present state of the relations of 
Austria with the Western Powers. He remarks, that facts are stronger than 
assurances, and that while Count Buol is assuring Great Britain and France ‘of 
his desire to maintain the Treaty of the 2nd of December, the acts of the 
Austrian Government evince a very different spirit, He remonstrates against 
the publication of Count Buol’s despatch of the 20th ultimo, in the “ Inde- 
pendance Belge,” which he observed could rot have occurred without the 
connivance of the Cabinet of Vienna, but which is calculated to show to Europe 
the differences of opinion which exist between Austria and the belligerent 
Powers. He animadverts again upon the instructions to the Austrian Ministers 
at St, Petersburgh and Constantinople, ta which I have alluded above; and he 
adds, that facts such as these are almost in themselves evidence of the non- 
existence of the Treaty. He cannot therefore conceal from Count Buol that the 
time is come when, if he is desirous of maintaining the Treaty, he must take 
some further means of showing it; and he hints that Count Buol might 
summon a meeting of the three Powers to consider how the Vth Article could be 
carried out. 

If Count Buol refuses to take some step of the kind, it can only be upon 
the plea that it is through the belligerent Powers that peace has not been made 
—an insinuation which the French Government will always repel. 

Count Walewski then says, that great and sincere as is the desire of France 
to maintain the most friendly relations with Austria, he cannot conceal from 


himeelf that those relations are seriously menaced, and in case of a rupture 
(274) 


Confidential.) 


"1 7 Wie TAAL ré a 
eg eS De ae AN BE Le ee eee 


Austria must be prepared to see the allies make peace hereafter without her 
participation. 

I should observe, that I have not seen Count Walewski’s despatches, and 
am merely recounting what his Excellency stated to me to be their purport. 


I have, &c. 
(Signed) COWLEY. 


No.2. 
Lord Cowley to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received June 25.) 


(No. 786. Confidential.) 


My Lord, Paris, June 24, 1855. 
COUNT WALEWSKI received this morning despatches from M. de 
Bourqueney, which state that although the language of Count Buol continued 
to be satisfactory, the acts of the Austrian Cabniet were at variance with that 
language. The reduction of the Austrian army was imminent. A dislocation 
of the troops was determined upon. The Emperor Francis Joseph had received 
at Cracow a complimentary visit from a Russian General, sent thither by the 
Czar. M. de Bourqueney had called Count Buol’s attention to these fuets, and 
he had been told that a reduction of the army was necessary to prevent the 
bankruptey of the State, and the dislocation of the troops had been called for to 
put a stop to disease and to prevent a dearth of provisions ; and as to the visit of 
the Russian General, Count Buol observed that that officer had only remained 
four-and-twenty hours in Cracow, and that all he could say further was, that if 
the Emperor Alexander had come to Warsaw, he should not have advised the 
sending an Austrian officer to compliment him. 
I have, &e. 
(Signed) COWLEY, 


No. 3. 


The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Bloomfield. 
(No. 203.) 


My Lord, Foreign Office, June 26, 1855. 

TINCLOSE a copy of a despatch* from Baron Manteuffel to the Count de 
Bement who, after reading it to me, had the goodness to leave a copy of it in 
my hands. 

i I told Count Bernstorff that Her Majesty’s Government had long been 
desirous to re-establish the relations of cordiality and confidence that had 
subsisted between England and Prussia, and that they would, therefore, receive 
with satisfaction this communication from the Cabinet of Berlin; and although 
at this moment there did not appear any prospect of the proposition being 
practically useful, yet that in the course of events Prussia might resume the 
position she had a right to occupy, aud might then render important services to 
the cause of peace and general reconciliation. ‘Such an event, I said, would be 
accelerated by Prussia nowtaking a more active part with the Western Powers, and 
in her own interest, und in that of Germany, exhibiting a determination to resist 
the aggressive policy of Russia. am, &c, 

(Signed) CLARENDON,

My Lord, Paris, June 25, 1854, 

COUNT HATZFELDT has made to Count Walewski a communication 
of the same nature as that which appears to have been made to your Lordship 
by Count Bernstoff, with reference to the maintenance of friendly relations 
between Prussia and the belligerent Powers; and Count Walewski informs me 
that he answered M. de Hatzfeldt verbally in much the same terms as those 
employed by your Lordship in your despatch to Lord Bloomfield No. 195 of 
the 19th instant. | 

There was, however, Count Walewski added, a passage in M. de Man- 
teuffel’s despatch, to which he had alluded with Count Hatzteldt more 
particularly—that in which the possible mediation of Prussia is hinted at. ‘This, 
Count Walewski said, he had declined in the most positive terms. 

I have, &e. :

No. 7. 


The Earl of Clarendon to Mr. Magenis. 
(No, 21. Seeret.) 


Sir, Foreign Office, June 28, 1855, 

WITH reference to the attempts which have of late years been made by 
Russia to obtain a footing in Norway, which attempts Russia, so far from 
abandoning, is evidently intent upon pushing forward at the first convenient 
opportunity, as is sufficiently proved by the coercive measures she has put in 
force against Norway, in consequence of the refusal by the latter to accede to 
the repeated demands of Russia for the concession of more extended fishing 
rights on the coast of Norway; with reverence also to the fortifications com- 
menced with so much secresy, and upon a seale so vast at Bomarsund, which 
leave no doubt that the ambitious designs of Russia are as menacing to the 
safety and independence of Europe in the north as they have been proved to be 
in the south, and that in a few years her power, if unchecked, would become 
most dangerous, 

Her Majesty’s Government taking these facts into consideration, and 
being earnestly desirous to prove the friendly feelings by which Her Majesty is 
animated towards her ally, the King of Sweden and Norway, and to render to 
him any assistance in resisting the demands which, at the conclusion of peace, 
will no doubt again be addressed to him by Russia, will be prepared to enter 
into an agreement by which the King of Sweden and Norway shall declare his 
intention to make no concession to Kussia, whether of fishery rights or 
pasturage rights, or of territory; and the Queen of Great Britain shall engage, 
should any attempt be made by Russia to obtain any such concessions by force, 
to come to the assistance of the King of Sweden and Norway with such an 
amount of navel foree as may be necessary for the purpose of repelling any 
ageressive act on the part of Hossia. 

~ Her Majesty's Government have been in communication with that of 
France on this subject; and they have been inoomed by Lord Cowley that 
corresponding instructions will be sent to the French Minister at Stockholm. 

The course which the two Governments have decided to pursue is, that 
their respective Ministers shall sound the ground in the first instance, and ascer- 
tain whether such a proposal as that which I have sketched out would be 
agreeable to the King of Sweden; and should it prove to be so, the Ministers 
will make it formally to the Swedish Government, and embody it in a Treaty. 

If the King of Sweden should wish both the proposal and the Treaty to be 
kept secret, there would be no objection to comply with His Majesty's wish, 
though the manilestly aggressive intentions of Russia would fully justify His 
Swedish Majesty in resorting openly to this measure for his protection. 

Indeed it is fortunate for the safety of Sweden that this opportunity should 
have offered itself for concluding such an important Treaty with the maritime 
Powers. If England and France had been at peace with Russia, however much 
their suspicions might have been awakened, and however threatening to Sweden 
might have been the course taken by Russia, they might have felt some hesita- 
tion in entering with Sweden upon a negotiation for a Treaty directed against 
Russia: whereas being now at war with Hussia, they are fairly entitled to take 
any measures to provide against future schemes of RaUTANC IA Hen} on her part. 

am, &c. 
(Signed) CLARENDON,

No. 11. 
Lord Cowley to the Earl of Clarendon.—( Received July 1.) 


(No, 834. Confidential.) 
My Lord, Paris, June 30, 1855. 

M. DE BOURQUENEY gives an account, in a despatch received this 
morning, of his conversation with Count Buol, in acquitting himself of the 
instructions which I informed your Lordship, in my despatch No. 785 of the 
94th instant, had been sent him, to call the attention of the Austnan Minister 
for Foreign Affairs to the perilous state of the Treaty of the 2nd of December, 
in the presence of the communications made by the Cabinet of Vienna to 
St. Petersburgh and the Porte, and of the publication in the Brussels papers of 
Count Buol’s despatch of the 20th ultimo to the Austrian Representatives in 
Paris and London. 

M. de Bourqueney fancies that the language he was desired to hold had 
made a certain impression upon Count Buol, who said that he would renew the 
conversation with him on the following day, though he could conclude nothing 
before the return of the Emperor Francis Joseph to his capital on the 10th 
proximo. | Bae | . 

Count Buol excused himself for the publication of the despatch, by saying 
that he had authorised it in consequence of hearing that Her Majesty’s Govern- 
ment had determined on laying it before Parliament. I remarked, that in these 
days of telegraphic communication, there was no excuse for taking serious steps 
on surmises or hearsay. 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) COWLEY.

No. 12. 
Mr. Paget to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 1.) 
(No. 32.) 
My Lord, The Hague, June 29, 1855. 
DURING the visit of the King of the Netherlands to Luxemburg, His 


Majesty was presented by the King of Prussia with the regiment, the 10th 
Hussars, formerly commanded by the late King William II; and Colonel 


Oltberg, an aide-de-camp of General Wedel, and recently named Governor of 


the Fortress of Luxemburg, has, within the last few days arrived at the Hague, 
charged by His Prussian Majesty with the presentation to His Netherlands 
Majesty of the commission to command the said regiment. 

General Hert is to leave the Hague to-night for Berlin, to convey His 
Majesty’s thanks to the King of Prussia. ; | 

It’ is a matter of some surprise to the French Minister’ that Colonel 
Oltberg, who was attached to the suite of General Wedel in his mission to Paris, 
where he was treated with every civility, should not have paid him the courtesy 
of calling at the French Legation sinee his arrival at the Hague, and should 
have made this omission the more remarkable by his having visited the Russian 
Minister, M. Lomonosoff. aah . 

It appears that Colonel Oltberg, assuming a tone of pity and eommisera- 
tion, has been holding forth, sinee he has been here, on the disasters which await 
the allied armies in the Crimea, predicting amongst other things their decimation 
by disease, &c., during the summer campaign. eet 

I should net have thought these circumstances worth mentioning to your 
Lordship were they not another instance of the animus which, in little things as 
well as in great, is invariably to be found amongst all the agents of the Prussian 
Government towards the allies, Unfortunately such opinions meet with but too 
much sympathy in this capital, Kither from personal civility received at the 
hands of the late Emperor, the distribution of orders and decorations, the 
influence—perhaps dread—of the Queen Mother, there is seareely an individual 
attached to this Court, or moving in the higher classes of society, who may not 
be called a Russian partizan. 


7 


A feeling of self-preservation will ever prevent any Government of this 
country from exposing their national interests by acts which might be taken as 
a departure from their neutral position, but it is at the same time beyond a doubt 
that any success which might attend the Russian arms, and any disaster which the 
ailies might have to deplore, would be hailed with feelings of satisfaction by, L 
may almost say, the whole of the class I have alluded to. 

Reeollections of events connected with the separation from Belgium is 
perhaps also in some instances a reason for this ill-will, : 

1 do not know that | ought to apply the above observations to His Majesty 
personally. It is difficult to ascertain what are his real sentiments, and I am 
frequently told that they are in fayour of the cause of the Western Powers; 
but I cannot believe them to be very strong when 1 listen to the language of his 
courtiers. 

Amongst the commercial classes also the same unfriendly feelings, although 
deriving their origin from different sources, exist to a certain extent towards 
England and France. 

Many of these have considerable sums vested in the Russian funds, others 
by the blockades established of the Russian ports see their commercial specula- 
tions put an end to. 

They lose sight of, or have never understood, the real merits of the question 
at issue—-who was the originator of the war, and by whose obstinate persistence 
in refusing to accede to just and honourable terms, its continuation is to be 
attributed. 

They are incapable or unwilling to appreciate the efforts made by the 
allies in the first instance to prevent the war, and then to conclude a peace. 
They would almost appear to be aware of one fact only—that they are unable 
any longer to direct their commercial enterprises to Russian ports, and that 
the obstacle in their way is the presence of the fleets of England and 
France. . 

What, however, they desire most of all—and this is navural enough—is 
peace; and could this be obtained, whether by a reverse to the allies, or by the 
annihilation of all the Russian armies and strongholds, provided their Russian 
stock did not suffer, either event would probably be a matter of great 
indifference to them. 

If any sympathy exists in this country towards the Western Powers, it is 
to be found in what is called the demoeratie or liberal party, of which 
M. Thorbecke is the head. Your Lordship will perhaps recollect a fact 
mentioned in Sir Ralph Abercromby's despatch No. 188 of the 24th of 
Noveinber last, that M. de Zuylen Van Nyveldt, who was Minister for 
Foreign Affairs in M. Thorbecke’s last_administration, addressed some «ues- 
tions to M. Van Hall in the Second Chamber of the States-General, as to 
the nature of the neutrality which this Government intended to observe, and 
expressed it as his opinion that while involving the country in no active part on 
either one side or the other, they would openly declare their sympathy with the 
Western Powers, as defending the cause of justice and civilisation against 
intolerance and barbarous aggression. 

‘This language from a late Minister for Foreign Affairs, and who would 
»robably be so again in the event of his party coming into power, ts sufliciently 
strong; but it must be observed that the speaker was perfectly aware at the 
time of the remote responsibility which attached to his words, by the improba- 
bility of his being placed in a position to act up to them; and [think it yet 
remains to be proved whether, if M. Thorbecke and his friends were — to 
return to power, which at present nothing appears to render likely, they would 
take a line of policy in any way different from that followed by the present 
Government, 

The Grésten van Prinsterer or Ultra-Protestant party, of which only a few 
members are in the Chamber, corresponds very much with the Kreuz party in 
Prussia, and I need, therefore, say nothing with regard to their opinions on the 
present struggle, or in favour of which side their good wishes are enlisted. 

As far os his Excellency M. Van Hall is personally concerned, [ can 
only repeat what I have before mentioned to your Lordship, that 1 have always 
found him perfectly fair, straightforward, and impartial on all the occasions on 


which I have had to speak to him officially. His own private feelings | take to 
be, in a great measure, thuse of his party, although perhaps not so strongly 


2) 


pronounced ; but so long as his Excellency in the official transactions betwee 
the two countries continues to act up to the same judicious line of conduct which 
has hitherto guided his dealings with us, I think that Her Majesty s Govern- 
ment would have only cause for regret at a change of Ministry im this country. 

In commercial policy M. Van Hall is liberal and far-seeing, and during his 
administration considerable reductions have been effected in the tariff. He 1s a 
great admirer of England and of English stitutions. He thoroughly under- 
stands the spirit and temper of his own country, and is, of all men in it, the one 
best calculated to lead it on gradually in the paths of progress and amelioration, 
and at the same time to resist all violent attempts at subversive and unnecessary 
and uncalled-for innovations, which a less enlightened and more democratic 
mind might unintentionally encourage or designedly foster. 

I trust your Lordship will excuse the length at which I have ventured to 
trespass on your time, but I thought that I might have no better opportunity 
of stating what appear to be the views and feelings of this country with regard 
to the events which are now passing, and towards the parties engaged in 
them. 


T have, &e. 
(Signed) A, PAGET.

My Lord, Berlin, June 28, 1855, 
BARON Manteuffel has left Berlin, and will not return until after the usual 
period of the departure of the messenger for Calais, ; 
His Excellency received last night from Count Esterhazy a confidential 
communication of the views of the Austrian Government respecting the commu- 
nication to be made to the German Diet on the subject of the closing of the 
Vienna Conferences, and it does not appear probable that the Prussian Govern- 
ment will be in a hurry to reply to it, : 
Intelligence has arrived here that Baron Prokesch is to resume his duties at 
Frankfort, and that Count Rechberg is to prepare to accompany the Emperor 
rancis Joseph to Italy. : 
The return of Baron Prokesch to the post of President of the Diet has 
vive, much dissatisfaction here. 
: I have, &e. 
(Signed) BLOOMFIELD.

No. 14. 
Lord Bloomfield to the Earl of Clarendon —(Received July 2.) 


(No. 270.) 
My Lord, Berlin, June 30, 1855, 

{ AM unable as yet to send to your Lordship a correct analysis of the 
communication which was made to this Government by the Austrian Minister 
on the 27th instant, as reported in my despatch No. 261 of the following day. 

It commences, | am told, with an historical account of what passed at the 
Vienna Conferences, and is written in a mild and moderate tone. It proposes 
that the resolution of the German Diet of the 9th of December, which recog- 
nizes the stipulations of the Treaty between Austria and Prussia of the 
20th April, 1854, and the defensive engagements therein agreed to by which 
Austria is guaranteed from any attack on her terntory that may be a consequence 
of the complication in the East, and that the further resolution adopted by the 
Diet on the 8th of February last, stipulating the “ kriegsbereitschafl,” shall be 
duly maintained. It states that Austria still will continue to watch over the 
interest of Germany in the East; it calls for the protection of her military 


) 


position in the Principalities, and the continuance of the state of preparation for 
war in which the German armies have placed themselves. 

The four bases of the late negotiations are alluded to, but I do not under- 
stand that more is urged on the consideration of the Prussian Government with 
reference to them than that the spirit of these points shall be looked upon as an 
equitable ground for future negotiations. 

Baron Manteuffel is not expected from the country for a couple of days, 
and the answer cannot consequently be sent to Vienna before the end of next 
week. 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) BLOOMEIELD. 


No. 15. 
Lord Bloomfield to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 2.) 
(No, 271.) 


My Lord, Berlin, June 30, 1855. 

I HAVE the honour to forward to your Lordship herewith a copy of a 
despatch addressed by Baron Manteuffel to the Prussian Representative at 
Vienna of the 9th instant, inclosing, for communication to Count Buol, copy of 
the Prussian note to the Cabinet of St. Petersburgh in reply to the communica- 
tion made at Frankfort through M. de Glinka, and inviting the Austrian Cabinet 
to address a reply on this subject to St. Petersburgh in the same spirit as that 
adopted by Prussia. 

Your Lordship will perceive in Baron Manteuffel’s despatch to Baron Werther 
that the Prussian Government does not respond to the invitation of neutrality 
submitted in M. de Glinka’s communication, but that they call to the remem- 
brance of the Russian Government the engagement entered into with Austria 
and the German Diet in the Convention of April 20, which they declare to be 
binding, and which it is given to be understood, although vaguely and not 
decidedly expressed, are incompatible with a declaration of neutrality. 

Baron Manteuttel, in his communication to the Austrian Cabinet, seeks 
rather to induce that Government to follow in the wake of Prussia, than to evince 
any disposition to yield to the Austrian policy, 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) BLOOMFIELD. 


P.S.—Sinee writing the above I understand that the despatch to Baron 
Werther of the 6th instant has been ill received at St. Petersburgh, and that 
Count Nesselrode has manifested dissatisfaction at its contents.

rences dans leur ensemble n’aboutiraient pas 4 une transaction formelle. Les 
Protocoles auxquels la dépéche du 4) Avril se référe ayant parvenus depuis a

l’Allemagne reste étrangére a Ja lutte et continue & garder sa neutralité, nous ne

TInclosure 2 in Iho. V5. 
Baron Manteujfel to Count Arnim. 


(Translation.) Berlin, June 9, 1855. 
COUNT ESTERHAZY lias had the goodness to communicate to me the 
inclosed copy of a despatch from Count Buol of the 3lst ultimo. Under the 
same date I have begged your Excellency to return my best thanks to the 
Minister of Foreign Affairs for the highly interesting confidential commiunica- 
tion of the 24th, made in the interim. The Austrian proposals haying in the 
meantime been declined by the Western Cabinets, and a final sitting of the 
Conference having taken place, we may be allowed to look forward to further 


a 


detailed communications by the Austrian Cabinet on the course of these 
proceedings. 

The remarks which the inclosed despatch contains, in reply to mine of the 
23rd ultimo, and which refer specially to the Russian despatch of the d0th April, 
proceed from a point of view that T cannot, however, entirely adopt. Tf, first 
of all, it is therein meant to be implied that Russia desired, unknown to 
Austria, to influence the views of the other members of the Confederation, 1 beg 
to observe that M. Von Glinka made his communication to the Austrian Repre- 
sentatives at the Diet, as well as to his other colleagues. There is also just as 
little proof of Russia’s intention to present to the Confederation a basis for 
deliberations In opposition to the views of Austria. Russia has, in fact, made 
no communication at all to the Confederation; but if such had been the case, it 
is scarcely probable, from the purely enunciative character of the despateh, that 


—— . 


i] 


a deliberation could have been founded upon it. On the other hand, I feel 
especially hound to remark, that if at that time the Russian communication in 
question appeared to give us no cause for a manifestation in the German Diet, 
and we in this respect willingly complied with the wishes of the Austrian 
Cabinet, we nevertheless could not thereby pledge ourselves to give no kind of 
effect whatever to the communication. 

The contents of the Russian despatch have, on the contrary, formed the 
subject of our serious consideration, especially since the Vienna Protocols to 
which it refers have come to our knowledge; and His Majesty has ordered a 
despatch upon the subject to be forwarded to his Minister at St. Petersburgh, 
which, by the express command of His Majesty, I beg your Excellency 
confidentially to communicate to Count Buol by reading to him the inclosed 
copy of the same. | 

His Majesty also thinks he has some claim to be considered the ally of 
Austria, though Count Buol’s despatch applies this appellation to the two 
non-German Cabinets of London and Paris alone, in contradistinction to Prussia. 
His Majesty will at least always be led by this thought, and will therefore 
willingly endeavour, especially on the subject of this position with reference to 
the other European Powers, to come to an understanding with Austria. 

It appears to us that the manner in which the Russian despatch of the 
30th April is characterised in the annexed, and in which cognisance 1s taken of 
it, are in no respect opposed to the true interests of Austria, and we should find 
the most weleome confirmation of this view, ‘f the Austrian Cabinet would also 
think fit on its part to send a reply to St. Petersburgh on the whole similar to 
ours, but in which of course its own peculiar position would be taken into 
account. 

The obligations that bind Prussia, Austria, and the rest of Germany together, 
are therein most distinctly represented to the Russian Cabinet as continuing to 
exist, and the latter is invited to show itself as disposed as possible to listen to 
the anticipated overtures of Austria. But on the other hand the value to 
Germany of the concessions mace by Russia with reference to the two points 15 
also recognized; it is signified that therefore a stronger reason exists for Germany 
not to leave the position she has hitherto maintained, 

Your Excellency will have the goodness to couple the above observations 
with the verbal communication of the inclosed despatch, and to report to us 
the remarks that they may give occasion to Count Buol to express. 

Receive, &c. 
(Signed) MANTEUFFEL.

No, 16. 
Mr. Elliot to the Earl of Clarendon —(Received July 2.) 
(No. 2.) . 
My Lord, Vienna, June 26, 1855. 

1 YESTERDAY took an opportunity of communicating to Count Buol the 
substance of your Lordship’s despatch to the Earl of Westmorland No, 232, 
Confidential, of the 19th instant, relative to the attitude it will be necessary for 
Austria to hold if she concurs with the Governments of England and Franee in 
desiring that the alliance established by the Treaty of December 2 should be 
maintained. 

Count Buol showed much satisfaction at the desire expressed by your 
Lordship for the continuance of the alliance, and said that your Lordship might 
rest assured that the attitude of Austria towards Russia would undergo no 
change, and that in his language to the Diet he should endeavour to make this 
evident. He intends to make communications to the Diet on this subject, which 
he trusts will satisfy your Lordship that he is no less anxious than Her Majesty’s 
Government to avoid any appearance of a weakening of the alliance. 

With respect, however, to not reducing the army, that was a different 
wuestion, Austria had undertaken to defend the Principalities against attack 
wom the side of Russia; and had declared to Russia that she would continue to 
hold them. Count Buol said we may be perfectly satisticd that she will keep 


12 


her engagements, but he will not admit that we have any right to discuss the 
number of men which Austria might think necessary for the purpose. 

[ said I apprehended that the hope expressed by your Lordship, that the 
Austrian army should not be reduced, nor the troops moved from their present 
cantonments to positions indicating a change of policy, was not meant to be 
confined to the troops occupying the Principalities, but was equally to be applied 
to the Austrian forces in other parts of the Russian frontier; that any const- 
derable reduction of the number of troops in Gallicia, or any change in their 
position, such as above described, would not only be universally believed to be 
caused by a change of policy on the part of Austria, and so give increased courage 
to the partisans of Russia in Germany, but would also cause material injury to 
the military operations of the allies, by relieving Russia from apprehension of 
the course which Austria hereafter might adopt. 

To this Count Buol replied, that it was impossible for Austria to continue 
to keep up for an indefinite period all the preparations for immediate war, and 
that her finances would not allow it. That, as for relieving Russia from appre- 
hension, he did not think much stress should be laid on that consideration; for, 
whether the army is reduced or not, Russia is perfectly aware that it is not the 
intention of Austria to go to war at present, and that she does not change her 
policy from day to day, I regretted the unsatisfactory tone of Count Buol’s 
language with reference to the army, and said that I was instructed not to 
conceal from him that if he declined to adopt the course indicated, the responsi- 
bility of the estrangement which might follow would rest upon Austria alone. 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) HENRY ELLIOT.

Mr. Eihot to the Earl of Clarendon.—( Received July 2. 
(No. 3.) 


My Lord, Fienna, June 26, 1855, 

ACCORDING to your Lordship’s instructions, I yesterday communicated 
to Count Buol your despatch to the Earl of Westmorland No. 227 of the 
13th instant, upon the subject of his despatch to Count Colloredo, expressing 
his regret at the closing of the Conference, and his surprise at the objections 
made by the Cabinets of London and Paris to the proposition he had brought 
forward at the Conference of the 4th instant. 

Count Buol said he would not now discuss the various points touched upon 
in your Lordship’s despatch (of which I left a copy with his Excellency), as no 
henefit could be derived from his doing so; but that he could not help remarking 
that your Lordship appeared to wish to dispute his right to propose to the 
Conference a project which had not received the assent of the allies. 

I replied that I did not discover in your Lordship's despatch any disposition 
to contest his liberty of action; but that as his proposition had been formally 
rejected by the Governments of England and France previously to its being 
brought forward, it was not easy to perecive how he should feel surprised at its 
not being entertained on being ultimately proposes {0 the Conference. 

have, &c. 


(Signed) HENRY ELLIOT,

oe sanuedene to your Lordship’s instructions, I yesterday communicated

* Count Buol fully admitted the correctness with which your Lordship statec 

the substance of his despatch to Count Colloredo, and regretted the opinions

In the meantime he would content himself by stating that he was very

solution he had proposed of the third point. 1 cae 

The difference between the two Governments was one in which each might

inefficient for the object in view, he thought that on our side we might believe

Mr. Elliot to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 2.) 


(No. 6.) 
My Lord, Vienna, June 27, 1855. 

BARON DE BOURQUENEY having received and communicated to the 
Austrian Government a despatch similar to your Lordship's despatch No. 223 
of the 12th instant to the Earl of Westmorland, I waited this morning upon 
Count Buol, and read to him your despatch, stating that now that the Conferences 
had closed, Her Majesty’s Government wish to place upon record that they 
consider the belligerent Powers to have resumed their freedom of action in 
regard to any future negotiation. 

Count Buol said he perfectly agreed with the views expressed by your 
Lordship in this despatch, and that all parties are entirely free as far as any 
engagements taken at the Conferences were considered, but that he 1s not of that 
opinion with respect to the understanding come to previous to the Conference, 
and he hopes that your Lordship will also concur with him in thinking that the 
basis on which peace must ultimately be made is that which was then agreed 
to. 

T have, &c. | 
(Signed) HENRY ELLIOT.

SS —————————— 


Inclosure in No. 21. 


General Order. 
(Translation.) 


1 SEIZE with pleasure the earliest possible opportunity afforded me of 
giving back to their homes and their families, at the same time that I express 
my fullest satisfaction with them, the reserves that have been called out for 
placing upon the full war-complement the drd and 4th Army corps. 

Notwithstanding that for the most part these reserves had already entered 
into the relations of civilians, were married men and fathers of families, yet one 
and all they answered with the greatest possible alacrity the call made upon 
them, they fulfilled their duties with punetuality, and by the exemplary manner 


in which they bore themselves and performed the services required of them, 


they, to the utmost degree, answered the purpose for which they were embodied, 


They will now bring back to the performance of their duties as civilians the same 
spirit of fidelity and honour which they have been used to display as soldiers, 


(Signed) FRANZ JOSEPH. 
Lemberg, June 24, 1855. 


No, 22. 


Sir John Milbanke to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 3.) 


(No, 45.) 
My Lord, Munich, June 26, 1855, 


| HAVE just returned from an interview with Baron Von der Pfordten, 


at which I communicated to his Excellency the contents of your Lordship’s 


-—— _ 2 a 


iouheaeaia 


_ - 
a aps 
~ @ Maw 


— a 


16 


despatch, marked * Circular,” of the 19th instant, relative to the closing of the 
Conferences at Vienna. 

Baron Von der Pfordten did full justice to the ability with which the 
views of Her Majesty's Government on this most important question were 
expressed; but he did not seem at all inclined to concur in the reasoning 
applied to the recent project of pacification submitted to the English and 
French Governments by the Cabinet of Vienna. He could not at all com- 
prehend, he said, why those propositions which originated with the French 
Government should have been rejected by the Western Powers, at a moment 
when there was at least some chance that good might come of them, and when 
it was notorious that they had found favour in the sight both of M. Drouyn 
de Lhuys and Lord John Russell, who had gone so far as to undertake to sup- 
port them with their respective Governments. To this I replied, that without 
being very exactly informed on the subject, | could not avoid suspecting that 
the favourable opinion of them, as the bases of an arrangement attributed to the 
English Plenipotentiaries, must have been purposely very much exaggerated at 
Vienna; that he must not forget, that although it might be true that proposals 
for an understanding between Russia and Turkey had been put forward by 
M. Drouyn de Lhuys, the mode of proceeding did not mect with the concur- 
rence of the Russian Plenipotentiaries, and thus rendered any further discussion 
of them mere waste of time; that, under such. cireumstances, it could scarcely 
be matter for wonder that the allies chould refuse to entertain them when repro- 
duced ina vet less acceptable form. Baron Von der Pfordten here interrupted 
me, to remark that M. Drouyn de Lhuys must still have attached some value 
to them, or else he would not have sacrificed his sosition to the maintenance of 
them, as was commonly understood to have been the case. 

His Excellency then proceeded to comment upon the four Articles, and 
especially upon that one which led to the rupture of the Conferences, and 
which he characterised as going beyond the bounds of moderation, and, with the 
interpretation given to it by the Western Powers, as forming an insurmountable 
obstacle to the conclusion of peace; for never, he said, whatever reverses she 
may suffer, will you be able to extort from Russia a promise to limit her 
naval forces in the Black Sea, or elsewhere, to a given number of vessels : the 
sretension on the part of other Powers to prescribe the law to her on this point, 
isan attack upon her dignity, and an attempt to weaken her, to which she cannot 
submit. Why, continued he, should Russia be the only maritime Power 
called upon to reduce the foree of her fleet? Why are not others, and particu- 
larly France and England, whose naval preponderance is at least as dangerous to 
their neighbours as that of Russia, compelled to contract their naval armaments ; 
and what sort of a principle are you secking to establish, and how would it find 
its application as between France and Baden, for instance, if in cases where a 
powerful and a weak State adjoin you proceed to endeavour to pare down the 
one to the level of the other? What do you imagine would be the answer of 
Franee, if she were summoned by eny other Power to raze the fortress of 
Strasbourg, or reduce the effective force of her cavalry? ‘The truth is, that you 
aim at the destruction of the Russian fleet, because it is inconvenient to you, 
and you may possibly ultimately succeed in burning or sinking it in the harbour 
of Sebastopol, but you will be but small gainers, for in the course of a few years 
you may rely upon its being replaced upon a more formidable footing, You 
have taken upon yourselves the thankless and hopeless task of revivilying an 
Empire whieh is crumbling under the rottenness of its own internal institutions, 
and not from any outward pressure; and the very measures to which you have 
had recourse to prop itupa little longer, are those which are most powerfully 
contributing to precipitate its final dissolution, Abandon at once the delusion 
of an independent ‘Yurkish Empire, and open the Euxine to the ships of all 
nations. Erect the European provinces into an independent Christian State, 
under the seeptre of a Christian Sovereign, and you may arrive at a final settle- 
ment of the Eastern dispute. These are the only conditions on which such i 
desirable result is possible ; for so long as you persevere in your present course 
of trymg to sustain a helpless Potentate, even peace on almost any terms Is 
altogether unattainable, Blinded by passion, and yielding to the demoeratic 
pressure for war, you have thought proper to reject the overtures which the 
‘Austrian Cabinet considered to offer a fair basis of arrangement, even after they 
had been accepted by your ally the Emperor of the French; but whether you 


* =) z 


16 


have done wisely in submitting to the democratic party, the evil effects of whose 
rule is likely hereafter to tell severely upon your institutions, is a question 
which time alone can solve. In the meanwhile be assured that Germany will 
never allow herself to be drawn into a war which has for its sole object the 
annihilation of the Russian flect, the existence of which in no way affects her 
interests. 

1 have thus given, with as much conciseness and as close an adherence to 
his words as was in my power, the substance of Baron Von der Pfordten’s 
remarks, suggested by the perusal of your Lordship’s despateh ; and although 
they evinee the prevalence of many erroneous impressions, upon which I was 
quite aware that all argument was uscless, I did not allow them altogether to 
pass in silence, J told him, in the first place, that I could not admit: the 
correctness of the.parallel he had drawn with respect to Russia and other Euro- 
pean Powers, inasinuch as the strong and weak, although their frontiers were, in 
most cases, purely conventional, subsisted together in perfect amity under existing 
Treaties—no question of the more powerful ever assuming an ageressive attitude 
towards their weaker neighbours, whilst even cases of representation or remon- 
stranee were extremely rare amongst them. With Russia, I said, the case is alto- 
eether different ; she maintains in the Black Sea a maritime feree which, as recent 
events have proved, is totally incapable of contributing to the defence of her 
coasts when menaced by well-appointed flects; and in ordinary times, when the 
Straits are closed, it is of still less necessity in its defensive capacity, because no 
danger can possibly be felt of an attack on the part of Turkey. In faet, if proof 
were wanting of the ageressive character of the Russian fleet in the Black Sea, I 
thought it was furnished in the most ample manner by the progress of the siege 
of Sebastopol, which had brought to hight the immense accumulation, on an 
extreme point of the Empire, of stores of war of every description. 

I tried once more to convince Baron Von der Pfordten that it was, at least, 
as much the interest of those States which bordered on the Danube, as it was 
that of the Powers now contending for the free navigation of that river, that 
Russia should not, by her ships of war, be able to oppose obstacles to the passage 
of vessels engaged in commerce; but his Excellency seemed to be of opinion 
that there was no ground for apprehension on that head, and answered my 
arguments by observing, that they would equally apply to English or French 
ships of war in other German rivers. I noticed also the singular piece of advice 
he had volunteered, relative to the opening of the Black Sea, to Powers who had 
undertaken a great and onerous contest ayowedly for the purpose of maintaining 
the independence of the Ottoman Empire ; but I had little hope of showing him 
the inconsistency involved in his plan of settlement, for he universally derides 
the notion that even a shadow of independence is left to the Sultan, 

| inquired by what process he contemplated amalvamating, and moulding 
into an orderly state, the different half-savage races who inhabit the Provinces 
of European Turkey; and from what Royal family he proposed—or, in short, it 
would be possible—to select a Sovereign of capacity and firmness sufficient to 
rule over, and preserve tranquilly among. such heterogeneous elements ; and, 
finally, Lasked him whether he really seriously thought that, if his plan were 
carried into execution, the intrigues of Russia would cease in those countries, 
and the Christian population at once enter upon the enjoyment of peace and 
happiness, 

| did not quite understand whether Baron Von der Pfordten alluded to the 
reigning King of Greece, or to some other Prince, but his reply to my question 
was, that there would be no great difliculty in finding a Ruler competent to 
undertake the task, 

] pass over, as possessing no very striking interest, other points touched 
upon in the course of our conversation ; and shall terminate the present report 
of it by stating that the impression left by it on my mind was, that although 
Baron Von der Pfordten may feel some disappointment, and even alarm, ut the 
abrupt conclusion of the Vienna Conferences, he is, upon the whole, better 
pleased that the rejection of the Austrian propositions should have come from 
the Western Powers than from Russia, for this reason,—that the Austrian Cabinet 
is, for the moment at any rate, relieved from the necessity of forwarding anything 
in the shape of an ultimatum to St. Petersburgh, and, consequently, of adopting 
such active measures as could hardly have failed, after the adhesion of this 
Government unreservedly given, as 1 am informed, to those propositions, to 


17 


render a further eseape from a participation in the struggle a matter of extreme 
ifficulty. | ' 

Sena of the much-talked-of ultimatum, I have reason to believe that the 
Cabinet of Vienna addressed to the Austrian Minister at St. Petersburgh, about 
a fortnight ago, a despatch explanatory of its views in the present altered ata 
of affairs, the main object ot which appeared to me to be to impress upon te 
Russian Government the fact, that the Emperor means strictly to fulfil the 
engagements taken with his allies ; that he still looks upon the four point’ ee 
constituting the only basis upon which a solid peace can be concluded; t et HE 
‘ntends to continue to occupy the Principalities until a definitive peace eh 
have been signed; whilst at the end of the despatch a hope is expressed, 
almost in terms of menace, that Russia will not add to her present saber: 
ments, by interfering in any way with that occupation. A great pomen of ae 
despatch is devoted to the Austrian propositions ; and without actually asking 
the opinion of the Russian Cabinet upon them, a door 1s left open for any 
appreciation of them that the latter may feel disposed to make known. 

Your Lordship is of course acquainted with the steps recently taken at 
Berlin by the Austrian Cabinet, for the purpose of securing the concurrence ol 
the Prussian Government in the long-promised communications to the Diet. 1 
cannot, however, ascertain, and 1 do not believe, that any agreement on the

Draft of Despatch from Count Walewsk to Baron de Bourqueney. (Communicate

M. le Baron, Paris, le Juin, 1855. 


IL importe au plus haut degré aux Puissances signataires du Traité du 
8 Dicembre de se rendre compte de la situation qui leur est faite par la rupture 
des négociations ouvertes avec li Russie. Il y aun moyen sir d’y parventr,

Lord Cowley to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 4.) 


(No. 859.) 
My Lord, Paris, July 3, 1855. 

COUNT WALEWSKI wishes me to inform your Lordship that, when 
there was a question of withdrawing the French Military Commissioner from 
Vienna, in consenting that General Letang should remain for the present, his 
Excellency wrote to M. de Bourqueney to desire to be apprised, whenever he 
(M. de Bourqueney) should think the time was come that General Letang should 
be instructed to return home, M. de Bourqueney had now written to say that 
he considered that the Commissioners should be withdrawn, and Count Walewski 
proposed, if it met with the concurrence of Her Majesty’s Government, to direct 
General Letang to take leave and quit Vienna as soon as the Emperor of Austria 
had returned to his capital. 

I] have, &c. 
(Signed) COWLEY.

My Lord, Paris, July 3, 1855. 


COUNT WALEWSK1 informed me this morning, that M. Baudin had 
written to him that your Lordship was hot indisposed to adopt the project of 
despatch alluded to in my despatch No. $25 of the 29th ultimo, as one that the 
French Government was desirous of addressing to Vienna, but that Her Majesty's 
Government haying some time back come to the conclusion that it would be 
better to let Austria alone for the moment, you could return no answer until the 


Cabinet had been consulted. 


Yesterday Count Walewski spoke to me in the strongest manner of his 
hope that Her Majesty's Government would not make objections to adopting 
the despatch, so necessary did he consider it to have some such document on 


record. 


To-day his Excellency was at equal pains to make me understand that if 
Her Majesty's Government had the faintest seruples as to the propriety of the 


eourse he had recommended, he should not press it, 


I attribute this change of language to the despatches received from Vienna 
since Count Walewski spoke to me yesterday, to which 1 have adverted in a 


subsequent despatch.

No, 26. 
Lord Cowley to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Recewed July 4.) 


(No. 866. Confidential.) 
My Lord, Paris, July 3, 1355. 

COUNT WALEWSKT has received the despatches which I had the honour 
to inform your Lordship, in my despatch No, $34 of the 30th ultimo, he was 
expecting from Vienna by messenger. fis Excelleney was much pressed for 
time when | saw him this morning, but he gave me the following rapid analysis 
of them. 

MM. ‘Thouvenel and de Bourqueney had had a three hours’ conversation 
with Count Buol. The tone of the Austrian Minister left nothing to be desired 
‘1 so faras it evineed an inclination to remain upon amicable terms with the 
belligerent Powers. 

Count Buel, while adhering to his own views respecting the last proposition 
made by Austria, admitted that he was not surprised that those Powers should 
wish for some more positive result to the ‘Treaty of December 2. He said, 
however, that it was impossible for Austria to go to war; that her army was in 
a deplorable condition; and that it was in order to avoid showing its weakness 
and inefficiency that the French and British Military Commissioners had not 
heen invited to accompany the Emperor on his tour of military inspection. But 
‘Austria had no intention of deserting the alliance which she had contracted with 
the Western Powers, or of declaring herself neutral—her position in the Princi- 
palities would prevent that: and in case of a reverse (to the allies), which, 
however, Count Buol would not contemplate, she would be found ready to act 
as an‘ arriére-garde,” and she would still continue to insist with Russia on the 
maintenance of the four points. 

Count Buol offered many excuses for the despateh lately addressed to the 
Porte in respect to not carrying the war to the Danube, He said that it was 
meant that it should be kept secret ; nevertheless, in order to have no conceal- 
ments from the French and English Governments, he had sent copies of it to 
Paris and London. ‘The refusal of the Porte, however, to enter into the engage- 
ment proposed to her he considered as certain, since France and England 
objected to it; and as Austria could not take ymbrage at this refusal, the 
Russian Government would not profit much, even if the whole proceeding 
became known to them. 

Count Buol said, further, that he should be very glad to find some means 
of showing his desire to abide by the Treaty of the gnd of December; and he 
hinted that perhaps the Emperor Francis Joseph would not object to binding 
himself to protect European ‘T urkey from Russian invasion, and even to declare 
war upon Russia incase, on necount of some unforeseen successes, OF for any other 
reason, she should declare hereafter that the four points were not binding upon 
her, Your Lordship, however, will understand that this was not a proposition 
on the part of Count Buol, and that he distinetly stated that he could say nothing 
more until the return of His Imperial Majesty to Vienna. 

Count Walewski seemed inclined to think that some advantage might be 
found in binding Austria to protect European Turkey. 

Both M. de Thouvenel and M, de Bourqueney state that the last French 
despatches have made a serious impression on Count Buol. He feels that, if 
the estrangement of France is to become more marked, it will be taken advan- 
tage of in Italy, and he has communicated his fears in this respect to those two 
gentlemen. They are consequently of opinion that matters should not be 
pushed further at the present moment, as the fears of Austria may be so much 
excited as to induce her, for safety to herself, to turn suddenly round and join 
the Russian standard. 

T have, &e. 
(Signed) COWLEY.

My Lord, Foreign Office, July 4, 1899. 

M. BAUDIN has read to me the draft of a despatch which Count 
Walewski proposes to address to Baron de Bourqueney, and has requested that, 
“f it meets with the concurrence of Her Majesty’s Government, similar imstruc- 
tions may be sent to the Charge d’ Affaires of Her Majesty at Vienna. 

This despatch, of which a copy 1s imelosed® for your Excellency’s own infor- 
mation, is written with great ability, and records with perfect correctness the facts 
and proceedings connected with the recent negotiations at Vienna. 

The only objection entertained by Her Majesty's Government to addressing 
a similar despatch to Mr. Elliot is, that the various points alluded to in Count 
Walewski’s despatch have already been brought by Her Majesty’s Government 
in more than one form under the consideration of Count Buol, and that answers 
to those communications have been received. 

By despatches to Lord Westmorland, Nos. 223, 224, 226, 227, 247, and 
248, of the 12th, 18th, and 95th ultimo, have been transmitted to your Excel- 
lency, and you have probably shown them to Count Walewski. If not, you 
will do so, together with my despateh No. 232 of the 19th of June to his Lord- 
ship, and my No. 2 of the 26th of June to Mr. Elliot, and also Mr. Elliot’s 
Aer Nos. 2, 3, 4, and 6 of the 26th and 27th ultimo, of which copies are 
inclosed. 

T believe that similar communications have been made to M. de Bourqueney, 
and your Excellency will therefore suggest to Count Walewski whether, as the 
Cabinet of Vienna is now fully informed of the views of the two Governments 
upon the obligation and the proceedings of Austria, we could hope for any 
practical advantage from sueh an identic despatch as Count Walewski proposes 
to send; and whether it might not be the cause of irritation, and serve as a 
pretext to establish a greater voolness than now already exists between Austria 
and the Western Powers.

No, 30, 
Sir A, Malet to the Earl of Clarendon,— (Received July 8.) 


(No. 82.) 
My Lord, Frankfort, July 5, 1855. 

REPORTS are rife that a Treaty is about to be negotiated between Austria 
und Prussia, with a view to fix the relations of the two States, in respect to 
Eastern affairs, 

1 should not have noticed these reports to your Lordship without more 
definite information than I as yet possess on the subject, had not circumstances 
indicated the great probability of endeavours being made in that direction. 

Until some definite understanding is arrived at between the Cabinets of 
Berlin and Vienna, it is improbable that either of then: will make a move in 
the Diet, which unless a previous agreement had been arrived at, would only 
serve to mark and to widen the dissidence of opinion which still exists between 
them, 

Austria, it is said, means to claim payment of a part of the expense 
incurred in occupying the Danubian Pyincipalities with her troops, from the 
Confederation, and also to invite adherence on the part of the Diet to the 
four points, which it is alleged she still proposes to maintain as established buses 
for any joint negotiation with Russia. 

There would not appear to be much prospect of the Prussian Government 
consenting to bind itself to either of these conditions, unless the suggestion were 
made to her from St, Petersburgh that her so doing would be the surest way 
of detaching Austria from her still apparently existing ties to the Western 
Powers. 

T have, &c. 
(Signed) A. MALET.

Mr. Magenis to the Ear! of Clarendon.—(Received July 9.) 
(No. 46.) 


My Lord, Stockholm, July 2, 1855. 

I HAD the honour to receive your Lordship’s circular of June 19th, 
relative to the unsuccessful termination of the Vienna Conferences, by post on 
the 28th ultimo, and, in conformity with your instructions | read this despatch 
on the 30th ultimo to Baron Stierneld, the Minister for Foreign Affairs, 


ao 


His Excellency was much gratified by the perusal of this important docu- 
ment, and appeared to coincide in its reasonings, though he made no comment- 
aries on it, only observing it was richer in illustration than English State Papers 
usually are. 

Baron Stierneld informed me that General Nordin in one of his late despatches, 
of, I think, the 19th ultimo, mentioned that on the preceding evening he had 
a conversation with Count Nesselrode; the Russian Chancellor had observed to 
him, referring to the late proposals of the Austrian Cabinet, though he did not 
further specify those proposals, that if they were such as it was reported, he 
believed that the Emperor of Russia, consistent with the principles which he had 
announced, would reject them. 

It is, however, to be expected that knowing those proposals had been 
already rejected by the British and French Governments, the ussian Govern: 
ment should declare that they would also have rejected them. A contrary 
course would bring with it no corresponding advantage, but would be simply an 
admission that they had been obliged to accept conditions which they had before 
declared to be inadmissible. 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) ARTHUR C. MAGENIS. 


No. 32. 
Lord Bloomfield to the Karl of Clarendon.—(Received July 9.) 


‘No. 277, Confidential.) 


My Lord, Berlin, July 7, 1855, 

WITH reference to your Lordship’s despatch No, 217, inclosing au extract 
of one from Her Majesty's Chargé d’Affaires at the Hague, No. 32 of June 29, 
relative to the presentation of the command of a Prussian regiment to the King 
of the Netherlands, and to the tone assumed at the Hague by Colonel Oldberg, 
the Prussian officer charged to deliver the commission to His Netherlands 
Majesty, | have thought it advisable to mention incidentally to Baron Manteuffel 
the language and proceedings of Colonel Oldberg during his stay in Holland. 

Baron Manteuffel said that he deeply regretted the favour and confidence 
shown by the King to Colonel Oldberg; that the character of that officer stood 
very low in the army; and that the Prince of Prussia was greatly distressed at 
his late advancement; but notwithstanding all this, the King had taken him 
under his special protection. 

That Colonel Oldberg should be indisposed to show civility to the French 
Legation is however not to be wondered at, for the French Government some 
time ago made complaints against him, and requested his removal from Paris, 
believing that he was employed in the interests of Russia during the late mission 
of General Wedell; and upon that complaint reaching the King of Prussia, 
His Majesty immediately withdrew Colonel Oldberg from Paris, and appointed 
him Commandant at Luxembourg, and [am assured that he will soon eh raised 
to the rank of a general officer, over the heads of many others, really deserving 
of promotion. 

* I have, &c. 
(Signed) BLOOMPITELD.

No, 33. 


Lord Bloomfield to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 9.) 
(No, 280.) 
My Lord, Berlin, July 7, (855. 
I HAVE received from a confidential source the following information 
respecting the present strength and position of the Russian armies, which has 


been derived from the official reports transmitted to this Government, and which, 
1 am informed, emanate directly from the Ministry of War at St. Petersburgh. 


24 


The effective Russian foree ‘1 the Crimea is computed to be 160,000 men 
of al! arms, inclusive of 40,000 sick and wounded. 

In Bessarabia is stationed a corps of 80,000 men, under the command of 
General Liiders. 

The western army, under Field-Marshal Paskiewitch, is stated to be 
160,000, of which a corps of 40,000, composed of the corps of Grenadiers and 
the Reserves of the Srd Corps, were (on the 15th of June) on their march to 
the Crimea. ; 

It was not expected that the Grenadiers could arrive at Sebastopol before 
the 15th of July at the earliest. The Reserves of the 3rd Corps are said to have 
commenced their march at an early date. 

When this intelligence was forwarded from St. Petersburgh, the Emperor 
Alexander entertained the ‘dea of detaching a further corps of 60,000 men from 
the western army, and of stationing it at Kieff, midway between Poland and the 

trimen, to serve a8 a reserve depot to the army in the Crimea, as well as for 
any dora purposes which eventualities might require in the north or in 
Poland. 

‘The eastern army, stationed along the southern shores of the Baltic to 
St, Petersburgh, consists of 40,000 men, under the command of General Sievers. 

In addition to this force, a further corps of 20,000 men is stationed in 
Esthonia, under the orders of General Grabbe. 

The army in Finland and its dependencies amounts to 80,000 men, under 
the command of General Berg. 

In and around St. Petersburgh there is a corps of 60,000 men, commanded 
by General Riidiger, under the immediate orders of the Emperor. 

This gives a total of 600,000 men, to which may be added 120,000 
scattered through the distant Eastern Russian provinces, but which are totally 
unavailable for the operations of the present war. 

The foregoing statement of the Russian forces is independent of the army 
of the Cancusus. 

These returns were made up at St. Petersburgh on the 14th of June, at 
which time the military reports of the fairs of the 7th and Sth ultimo, before 
Sebastopol, had not yet arrived, and consequently the losses sustained by the 
Russians on those occasions are not taken into account. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) BLOOMFIELD,

Lord Bloomfield to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 9.) 


(No, 284.) 
My Lord, Berlin, July 7, 1856. 
WITH reference to my des atch No. 270 of the 30th ultimo, on the 
subject of a communication from the Austrian Government, inyiting this Court 
to approve of all the late proceedings of the Cabinet of Vienna on the Eastern 
uestion, and support propositions which they intended to submit to the German 
Diet, | have the honour to inform your Lordship that the Prussian answer was 


the Austrian communication, and that they had now sent a civil reply to decline 
the propnsals generally, as it was the King's intention that the position occupied 
by Prussia at this moment should be maintained. 


replied, that the answer was drawn up in vague language, but that Prussia was 
resolved not to adopt as her own the unsuccessful policy of Austria, and would 
not tie herself down to any particular course, and would remain free and 
unfettered, 

In reply to an inquiry which T made as to the question of the “ Kriegs- 
bereitschatt,” Baron Manteuttel said that Prussia and Austria were agreed on 
this point, and considered it advisable that the German armies should be main- 
tained in their present state of readiness, and that he thought that opposition 
was to be expected only from Bavaria and Wurtemberg, where there was a desire 
that a prolongation of the period for preparation should be stipulated, and that 
one month instead of one fortnight should be allowed to bring the troops 
together. 

Your Lordship will judge from the foregoing, and from what I have already 
written on this matter, that Prussia and Austria are not likely to come to an 
understanding at the Diet; and to judge from Baron Manteuffel’s tone, he seems 
hopeful that the policy of Prussia ig that which will prove most tasteful to the 
German Governments. His Excellency mentioned that he understood it was 
the intention of the Austrian Government to lay the Treaty of the 2nd of 
December before the Diet.

I HAD the honour to transmit to your Lordship, on the 28th ultimo, the 
Imperial order for the dismissal of the reserves of the 3rd and 4th armies, which 
however, to the present moment, has not been followed by the more important 
orders for the reduction of the Austrian army, which will unquestionably be very 

ly issued, 
show he number of men hitherto dismissed probably does not much, if at all, 
exceed 50,000, who could, if required, join their regiments in ten days; while 
the total reduction known to be in contemplation is expected to amount to 
eer hive sisted to Count Buol that the measure would be viewed with deep 
regret by Her Majesty's Government, and was peculiarly unfortunate at a 
moment when your Lordship had been defending Austria against the charges of 
‘asincerity which had been brought against her in the House of Lords. 

His Excellency did not appear to be blind to the bad effects likely to be 
produced on public opinion by this measure, but spoke of the necessity of it on 
financial grounds. He added, also, that it might be well | should prepare Her 
Majesty’s Government for the other reductions, which he admitted to be in 
Se aT hoped these reductions would, at least, not comprise Artillery and 
Cavalry, which on an emergency cannot be quickly replaced ; but his Excellency’s 
answer was far from satisfactory, and his allusion to the great expense of those 
branches of the service lead me to apprehend that they will not escape the 
operation of the proposed measure. iene

No. 39. 
Lord Stratford de Redeliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 9.) 


(No, 467.) 
My Lord, Therapia, June 28, 1855. 

WHEN | visited the Grand Vizier on his return from Vienna, his Highness 
informed me that Count Buol had apprised him of the intention of his Court to 
make known to the Russian Government, that if an army belonging to that 
Power were to recross the Danube into the Turkish Provinces, the operation 
would be considered by Austria as a casus belli. This communication confirms 
what the Austrian Envoy had previously stated to Fuad Pasha, accompanied 
with the expression of a hope that the allied Powers would not undertake, and 
that the Porte would, if necessary, dissuade them from undertaking, any expedi- 
tion into the Russian territory beyond the Danube, which might have the effect 
of provoking any hostile aggression on that side in return, 

- Fuad Pasha’s reply, as he assured me at the time, was calculated to 
impress upon Baron Koller the Porte's unwillingness to enter into any engage- 
ment or understanding upon a subject as to which both the Sultan and his 
allies were free to act according to their common interests in the prosecution of 
the war. 

I have, &e 


(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE. 


No. 40. 


Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 5.) 
(No 468.) 


My Jord. Therapia, fune 28, 1855. 

WITH my despatch No. 410 of the 7th instant, [ forwarded to your 
Lordship a paper which I had sent in to Fuad Pasha, It contained a series. of 
remarks and inquiries intended to elicit some further information as to the real 
state of the Porte’s financial system. 

My expectation that the answers would be sent to me in two or three days 
has not been realised. More than two weeks elapsed before I received them, 
and it is only now that I have an opportunity of forwarding them to your 
Lordship. ‘They were transmitted to me in Turkish, and an English translation 
is forwarded herewith, 

I have not time to comment upon them, although I cannot say that they 
inspire me with much confidence. 

The revenue of the Porte before the war is set down, I observe, at 
10,000,0001, sterling. I firmly believe that it never exceeded 7,000,0001., or, 
at the outside, 8,000,000/. The deficit is stated at 300,000 I am under a 
strong impression that it was far more considerable. 

I have, &e, 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE. 


Inclosure in No, 40. 


Memorandum. 
(Translation, ) 


THE meeting of equality between the income and the expenditure of the 
Porte in time of peace, is the balance of the accounts. In fact, the expenses 
were met by the income; and if there appeared a little (deficit), it was not to 
any degree that could impair or embarrass the administration of affairs, because 
the difference between the revenue amounting to nearly 2,000,000 purses, and 
the expenditure covered by it was perhaps 50,000 or 60,000 purses. The 
greatest portion of this was a sacrifice made by the Porte for keeping down the 


28 


exchange for a time, in the sole interest of commerce. Agriculture and com- 
merce are daily on the increase, and likewise the revenue of the Porte derived 
therefrom. By careful attention, moreover, to the expenditure, no doubts were 
entertiined of making good this defieit; but it was never intended to do so, by 
increasing the taxation. If the Porte had levied this deficit upon the country, it 
would have been cleared off without any one feeling the burden of it. The 
balance-sheet of the ordinary expenditure and income of the State 1s now to be 
drawn up in a new and regular form, and the relations between the two are to 
be again established. Although the extraordinary expenses of the war form a 
separate branch, yet owing to the increase in the price of articles caused by the 
war, and to other reasons, the ordinary expenses @tt likewise on the increase. 
On the other hand, it must be gratefully admitted that while the effects of a 
state of war on all countries is well known, the general increase in the Ottoman 
States has not been impaired ; and that God Almighty has rewarded with plenty 
and abundance the sacrifices made by the nation at large. 

Qndly. The military forces of the Porte, owing to the war, have been 
doubled, and, perhaps, during a time, tripled. I mean that the land and sea- 
forces amounted in reality to 300,000, and, during a time, to 400,000 men, 
and upwards. If the irregular troops being included in this amount, the 
imperial troops from the provinces of Tunis and Egypt, who are temporarily 
srovided for here, must be included also, On the occasion of the Hellenic 
incursion, a body of not less than nearly 40,000 troops, regular and irregular, 
were assembled at Joanina, Tricala, and Salonica. ‘This in confirmation of the 
above statement. Such is the general estimate of the amount, The balance of 
the extra expenses of the war is drawn up separately, and the number of troops 
entertained at all times is clearly set forth, 

3rdly. The meaning of the ordinary expenses having become mixed up 
with the extraordinary expenses is, not that the accounts are confused, but that 
the ordinary income allotted to the ordinary expenditure is expended in the 
extraordinary outlay. ‘The accounts of the extraordinary expenditure are being 
examined into; and although they will be cleared up, yet so long as there are 
extraordinary means for meeting the extraordinary expenses, the war expenses 
cannot be delayed; but as matters stand now, the Treasury pay the most 
essential part of the extraordinary expenses out of the ordinary revenue. 

4thly. ‘The Sublime Porte wishes that the Commission for the war expenses 
be organized, so as to be really and truly useful, and to inspire confidence. 
Necessary measures are 10 contemplation with this view. 

Sthly. In organizing the balance-register of the extraordinary expenses, the 
debt of the State and the revenue will be placed separately, , 

Gthly. In organizing the bulance-register as above stated, the war expenses 
hitherto incurred will be set forth, and the extraordinary means provided to meet 
them, and an approximate estimate will be made of the yearly expenses to be 
‘icurred hereafter, in the event of the war continuing, With respect to the 
collection of these, the Sublime Porte has not levied anything on the country for 
war expenses, in order that they may not be a burden on His Majesty's subjects 
inany way, Although the nation, animated by a certain degree of zeal, has 
made payment in anticipation to 8 small account, these have not come to any 
large sum. ‘They will, however, be set forth in the balance-sheet. 

‘thly. The species, and the amount of the income appointed to meet the 
payment of the capital and the interest of the loan, are set forth in the note 
which has been given in. 

Sthly. ‘The question of a sale of land is a very important one, Land in 
‘Turkey belongs in general to the Sultan’s subjects, and there exists very little 
out of this category. Supposing there did not, there would be no purchasers in 
the country for it. With reference, algo, to selling land to persons seeking to 
purchase it from abroad, it 1s impossible to change the present state of the 
question, so long as the exceptions established by Treaty in favour of foreigners 
are not modified, For this reason a positive opinion cannot be pronounced on 
this subject, based as it is on established principle. 

With reference to the produce of the soil, and to that of timber pointed out, 
the Porte has had those subjects in her mind; and the means of carrying out her 
views form one of the improvements which are in contemplation. Nevertheless, 
it is, at the present crisis, one of those things necessary to meet the obvious 
necessities of the Porte. 


Pa) 


This applies exactly to the subject of the cannon. It would not, moreover, 
be suitable, at the present period, to upset anything belonging to the system of 
defence, In the same manner, if the present field-guns were exchanged against 
iron guns, the surplus of the money that would be expended for the new guns, 
and the special sort of ammunition required for it, would go very little way in 
the remedy now sought for.
