Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 9.) 
(No 469.) 


My Lord, Therapia, June 28, 1855. 

GENERAL VIVIAN informs me that he has now under his command m 
camp seven regiments of Infantry, three batteries of Artillery, and a regiment of 
Cavalry, with an additional troop, ‘Two other regiments are to join his camp the 
day after to-morrow, and the regiment of Cavalry detained so long at Volos for 
means of transport, is partly arrived, 

He has had some cases of desertion, and some of cholera, The former have 
ceased, the latter are disappearing. He speaks with much satisfaction of the 
conduct and goodwill exhibited by the aaldiens under his command. 

General Beatson writes me word from the Dardanelles that the service of 
enrolment for his corps of Irregular Cavalry is everywhere carried on with 
success, The difficulties which have occasionally obstructed the progress of his 
officers, have yielded to their firmness and judicious management, ‘An affray 
which recently took place between two troops of his Cavalry, terminated, not 
indeed without bloodshed, but without compromising, in any respect, the rela- 
tions established between the soldiers and the British officers. 

There are particulars, not destitute of interest, which | am obliged to 
reserve for a future opportunity. 

1 have, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE, 


No, 42. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl af Clarendon.—(Received July 11.) 
(No. 476.) 


My Lord, Constantinople, June 30, 1855. 
THE meeting which | had previously announced to your Lordship, took 
place this morning, at the Grand Vizier's house on the Bosphorus. In addition 
to his Highness, the Seraskier and Fuad Pasha were present. Iwas aceom- 
panied by Brigadier-General Mansfield. 
_ We found that the Porte had received advices from Vassif Pasha, brought 
by an officer who had left Kars about eleven days before. Despatches from 
General Williams also came to hand at the moment we were entering into 
conference. Their latest date was the 19th instant. | | 

_. Tt appeared from both sources that the Russians, advancing from Guinri 
with an amount of force varying from 20,000 to 30,000, had presented them- 
selves before Kars; that a partial engagement of Cavalry had taken place, 
followed two days later by an attack, which had been repulsed, on the part of 
the Sens and that the town was threatened with a siege. 

_ I collected from the Turkish officer that when he left the scene of action 
rain Was fallin in torrents, the waters of the river were out, and the Russians 
had no choice but to excamp. It appears from the English statements that the 
defences of the fortress were deemed to be of considerable strength, that the 
place was provisioned for a month, and that the Turkish army may be estimated 
at 16,000 men, ; 

t was clear to all present that whether the Russians besieged or tu 
Kars, the Turkish army required an effort to be made for its aie withall 
practicable dispatch, and that of three possible modes of acting for that purpose, 

; ty 


30) 


the only one likely to prove effective was an expedition by Kutais into Georgia. 
To send reinforceinents by ‘Trebizond would be at best a palliative. ‘To establish 
an entrenched camp at tedoute-Kaleh would, at this unhealthy season, be 
equivalent to consigning the troops to destruction. 

The real question was, whether a foree numerically sufficient, and in all 
respects effective, could be collected ‘1 time at Kutais, to make an incursion 
into Georgia, and threaten the communications of the Russian army, placing it 
indeed between two hostile forces, should the Turkish army still be in a 
condition to take the field. 

It was for the Turkish Ministers to solve this problem, and they proposed 
that the expeditionary force should be composed of 12,000 men from Batoom 
and the neighbouring stations ; of the troops made over to General Vivian, and 
estimated at 10,000 of all arms; of General Beatson’s Irregular Cavalry ; of 
10,000 men to be detached from the army in Bulgaria, a5 the complement of 
the Turkish Contingent; of 5,000 more derived from the same source; of an 
Egyptian regiment of Horse, now here; and of another regiment. expected from 
Tunis. ‘Io these the Seraskier proposed to add 2,000 Albanians, by way of 
riflemen, ‘These several forces, completed according to the figures, would present 
a total of 44,400 men, not perhaps to be reckoned with prudence at more than 
36,000 effectives. 

Admitting the urgency of the ease, and the consequent necessity of meurring 
a certain degree of hazard, | ealled attention to the importance of not exposing 
the Turkish Contingent or General Beatson’s Horse prematurely to a trial beyond 
their strength. It was accordingly understood, that supposing the expedition 
to be resolved upon, neither of these corps would be required to embark for 
Redoute-Kalch until the preparations were completed in other respeets, and it 1s 
to be hoped that the interval thus cained for their benefit, would suflice to 
secure their efficiency. I took, moreover, the liberty of remarking that the 
proposed expedition, besides being prepared with secrecy and sanctioned by 
superior authority, must: finally depend for its adoption on our available means 
of providing it with all the requisite appliances. This indispensable field of 
inquiry might be investigated with advantage by General Vivian, the Seraskier 
Pasha, and Brigadier-General Mansfield, who, indeed, have undertuken to meet 
to-morrow for that purpose. 

The Turkish Ministers having expressed their readiness to entrust the 
direction of the expedition, should it eventually take place, to a British 
commander, and to accept General Vivian in that capacity, subject, of course, 
to the approval of Her Majesty's Government, 1 lost no time after our separation 
in communicating personally with that officer, and putting him in possession of 
all that had passed on the subject of our discussion. 

It only remains for me at present to refer your Lordship to the aecom- 
panying memorandim, for whieh Lam indebted to General Mansfield, It 
contains information resulting from inquiries addressed to the Seraskier and his 
colleagues, on seyeral matters from which it is most desirable, with a view to 
out ultimate success, that all uncertainty should, as far as possible, be removed. 

1 have, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

QUESTIONS having been put to the Seraskier, the following information 
was obtained : 


L. The country in the neighbourhood of Redoute-Kaleh is declared to be low 
and marshy. 

4. A suflicient supply of grain for the maintenance of an army without the 
importation of corn cannot be found, ‘lhe country from the coast to Kutais 
cannot supply the necessary magazines. 


31 


3. Ships engaged in the supply of the army would seek Batoom for safe 
anchorage. Redoute-Kaleh is impracticable, except for boats of small draught. 

4, The exact distance from Redoute-Kaleh to Kutais is eighteen hours, or 
about fifty English miles. 

5. ‘There is no coast-road practicable for guns from Batoom. 

&. The River Rhion is navigable by boats as far as Kutais. The necessary 
boats can be sent from Trebizond, . 

7. ‘The streams which tun into the Rhion below Kutais are not of any 
significance, and are not serious obstacles to the advance of al Bay: 

8, The population of Kutais is about 10,000; rather under than over that 
number. 

9. The climate of Kutais is more healthy than that of the coast. Itis, 
therefore, more prudent to throw the troops forward after their disembarkation as 
quickly as possible. : 

10. Labourers can be secured in the country to assist the engineers im 
forming an entrenched camp at Redoute-Kaleh. | 

11. ‘The population in the region referred to is indifferently disposed towards 
the Turkish Government since the fatal affairs of last year. lt would be difficult 
to attract recruits to the ranks, independently of raising the country on a errand 
scale by acting on the Chiets. | 

12, The force at Batoom and in its neighbourhood is 15,000 strong, of 
whom 12,000 effective men can be spared for the expedition. 

13. Of these, 700 or 800 men are Artillerymen. There are 30 or 32 field- 
guns quite ready, besides some guns of position. 

14. It is also said that means of land-transport for the 12,000 men at 
Batoom are actually available. 

15. It is declared that the Russians have constructed a chaussée from ‘Tiflis 
to Redoute-Kaleli, the commerce on that line having been always much favoured 
by them. 

"16. ‘Lhe Porte would depend upon the British and French Admirals for 
assistance in transporting the troops to Redoute-Kaleh ; but many steamers and 
transports may be spared from the transport fleet engaged in supplying Omer 
Pasha, besides that the Porte 1s prepared to buy other transports for this 
purpose, 

17. The Porte proposes to give the command of the army, which, when 
concentrated, will teed 45,000 men of all arms, to Lieutenant-General Vivian, 
now in command of the Turkish Contingent. The latter would form a Division 
of the force. 

18. The details of the foree cannot, however, be analysed and decided on 
till a decision is made that the design shall be put into execution. 

19. ‘There are some officers attached to the Seraskier who possess consi- 
derable local knowledge of the country alluded to, and would be placed at the 
disposal of Licutenant-General Vivian.

My Lord, Therapia, July 1, 1855. 

AGREEABLY to what I stated in my preceding number, Lieutenant- 
General Vivian and Brigadier-General Mansfield waited this morning on the 
Seraskier, at his Excellency’s olfice in town. Fuad Pasha attended, at the 
request of the Seraskier, M. Pisar. was also present, 

Inclosed herewith, for your Lordship’s information, 18 a memorandum 
containing General Mansfield’s notes of what took place on the oceasion, ‘The 
statement promised by General Vivian can hardly reach me before to-morrow, 
and perhaps not im time to be forwarded by the messenger. 

Your Lordship will do me the honour to bear in mind that the proposed 
diversion at Redoute-Kaleh originated with the Porte; that, recognising the 
urgency of the case, I have endeavoured to obviate such manifest objections as 
the premature employment of the Turkish Contingent, and the exposure of 
troops toa climate which at this season has been characterised as pestilential 
on the coast of Cireassias and that I lean entirely on military opinions as to the 
means of eventually carrying out the proposed expedition. 

I proposed at the same time to reserve the approval of Her Majesty's 
Government, and to afford the Commanders-in-chief an opportunity of declaring 
their opinions. 

I have to add, in conclusion, that an entrenched position at Redoute-Kaleh 
will probably be found indispensable, as a point of retreat in ease of any signal 
yeverse; and, with a view to future questions, it may be desirable to have it 
clearly understood that the Turkish Contingent is to be regarded in the light of 
«a British force, composing the principal part of the expeditionary army. | 

I Mave. ke. : 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE. 


Inclosure in No. 43. 


Memorandum of an Interview belween the Seraskier, Fuad Pasha, Lieutenant- 
General Vivian, and Brigadter-General Mansfield, on the S0th of June, 
1855. 


THE Seraskier, before the arrival of Fuad Pasha, spoke of the affairs of 
the Turkish Contingent under the command of Lieutenant-General Vivian. 
That force has now actually in camp 8,500 men, two battalions having joined 
this very day. The remaining 1,500 men, necessary to complete the first half of 
the Contingent, are in Constantinople, ready, and will be sent to the camp 
forchwith, 

The second half of the Contingent to be assembled at Varna. Ifthe plan 
discussed at the house of Aali Pasha is decided on, the orders for them to 
assemble at Varna to be immediately sent. If there is no such object, the 
Seraskier considers a further reference must be in the first instance made to 
Omee Pasha, who would take offence ifa large portion of the troops in Bulgaria 
were drafted away without his consent. But in ease of the project being decided 
on to form an army to operate towards Georgia, of course that such a reference 
might be put aside, considering the urgency of the matter, Supposing the order 
to be sent without delay to Bulgaria, 10,000 men could be assembled and ready 
to be transferred to the officers deputed by General Vivian at Varna in filteen days. 

On being asked what steps could be taken to form a dep6t, in. case of the 
Contingent being sent on service, he replied that it was not the Turkish custom 
to form depats ; that the Porte raised about 18,000 or 20,000 recruits a-year, 
who were immediately sent to the different battalions. ; 

Qn the matter of arms, he declared that he was ready to supply the 


a3 


Contingent with 15,000 percussion muskets immediately, although he trusted 
that the Minié guns sent for to England by General Vivian would soon make 
their appearance. The percussion muskets are now in the arsenal, and there is 
plans of ammunition, 

Fuad Pasha having arrived, General Vivian desired that his Excellency 
would have the goodness to explain exactly the views of the Turkish Govern- 
ment on the subject of an expedition to the coast in whieh it was proposed to 
employ him. Fuad Pasha then at great length went over all the matters which 
had been brought forward before his Exeellency Lord Stratford de Redcliffe the 
day before. 

He explained the situation of the army of Kars, and that it was proposed 
to relieve that army by a powerful diversion. That this diversion could be best 
made, indeed only made, from Redoute-Kaleli, or some place in its vicinity, for 
the landing of troops and the establishment of a basis of operations. That 
troops so landing should be thrown in advance as quickly as possible on Kutais, 
and operate towards Tiflis, where it 1s considered the real objects of a war against 
Russia by the allies may be best obtained. That by such a grand operation the 
best chance for the army of Kars is secured ; whereas by any other mode means 
would be frittered away, without result, either immediate or ultimate, to the good 
of the cause. That if the project is not adopted for want of means, they must 
have recourse to those measures in which they have no confidence. 

General Vivian replied that he should like to know what means were 
disposable for so great a plan. 

Fuad Pasha answered, that in the first place there was of the contingent 
now ready : 


10,000—Actually at Buyukdéré. 
10,000—That part of the contingent to be immediately assembled in Bul- 
rail. 
5,000—To be drawn in addition from the garrisons in Bulgaria, and 
attached to the British contingent at Varna in camp. 
3,000—Beatson's Horse, 
9,000—Albanian Light ‘Troops. 
12,000—Garrison at Batoom and in the neighbourhood. 
800—One regiment of Egyptian Regular Cavalry. 
G00—A body of Cavalry expected immediately from Tams. 


43,400 


It was proposed to give the command of their army to General Vivian 
himself, 

On being pressed as to the means of transport inland, Foad Pasha declared 
that the plans once fairly decided on, every effort would be made to furnish all 
the means of whatever description, 

Genoral Vivian said that he begged that he might not be misunderstood, 
That he put himself out of the question ; but that it was one thing to throw an 
army ona coast, and another to feed and move it. That it was necessary for 
him to have exact details on this matter, in which success depends altogether. 
Further, that he quite admitted the justice of the reasoning addressed to him, 
which seemed to vouch for the necessity of employing his contingent; that 
there was a state necessity against which he, as a military officer, had nothing to 
say. But that while he admitted this, he must inform the Porte that the 
employment of the contingent was premature, and, as 15 self-evident, cannot but 
interfere with the arrangements and organization he has so mueh at heart, 

Important, however, as these considerations are, that it is his business to 
carry out the orders as far as lies in his power of the Government under which 
he is acting, and that his only desire is to do that as effectually as may he, 
according to the injunetions he may receive from Her Majesty's Ambassador. 
But that putting all personal considerations on one side, they cannot be too 
careful in arranging the necessary matters of detail, sea and land transport, 
provisioning and victualling, ammunition, payment, &G, 

To this Fuad Effendi agreed, and proposed that General Vivian should 
sketch out on paper what he considered indispensable to enable this project to 
be cariied out, ‘That the officers of the Porte would then be able to judge 
whether they had the necessary means; but that there ought to he no delay. 

K 


34 


General Vivian promised to send such a document the next day, and 
expressed his gratitude for the high honour which has been conferred on him. 
(Signed) H. B. MANSFIELD, 
Brigadier- General.

No. 44. 
The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


(No. 561.) 
My Lord, Foreign Office, July 13, 185. 

THE plan proposed by the Porte for the relief of (he Turkish army at Kars, 
as cketched out in your Excellency s despatches Nos. 476 and 477 of the 30th 
of June and Ist instant, has been attentively considered by Her Majesty’s 
Government, and I have to state to your Excellency that it appears to them 
objectionable for the following reasons: 

It would be in the greatest degree imprudent to throw on an unwholesome 
coast, without means of lund transport; without any certainty of provisions ; 
without an assured communication with the rear; without an accurate know- 
ledge of the country to be traversed, or the strength of the enemy to be 
encountered; and with a probability of a hostile population; 40,000 men, 
hurriedly collected from various quarters, imperfectly disciplined, doubtfully 
armed and equipped, and as yet unorganised, and to expose them at once to all 
the hazards and hiffculties of a campaign against a Russian army. They would 
fall ill between Redoute-Kaleh and Kurais, and be defeated between Kutais and 
Tiflis, Moreover, the fragments to be united for the purpose of composing this 
army are so scattered ubout, that the crisis, if it is to take place, would be over 
long before it could reach the scene of action. 

Her Majesty's Government are of opinion that the wiser course would be 
to send reinforcements to the rear of the Turkish army, instead of sending an 
expedition to the rear of the Russian army. ‘The reinforcements might go to 
Trebizond and be directed from thence upon Erzeroom. The distance from 
Trebizond to Erzeroom is less than that from Redoute-Kaleh to ‘Tiflis, and the 
march is through a friendly instead of through a hostile country; and at 
Frzeroom the army would meet supporting frionds instead of opposing enemies, 
and supplies instead of famine. 

If the army at Kars cannot maintain that position against the Russians, it 
should fall back upon Erzeroom, and the whole ‘Turkish force should be concen- 
trated there. If the Russians are to be defeated, it will be easier to defeat them 
by the whole force collected, than by divided portions of that foree; and a 
defeat would be the more decisive the further it took place within the Turkish 
frontier. 

Trebizond is a port where supplies of all kinds might be landed; and Her 
Majesty’s Government believe it is a healthy place, and that Erzeroom is 80 
likewise. 

Such an arrangement as that which I have described would give time for 
collecting and organising the various detached corps of which the proposed army 
of 40,000 men is to be composed; and Ter Majesty's Government entirely 
coneur in Lieutenant-General Vivian's opinion that an army thrown on a eoust 
without means of transport and supplies ts doomed to destruction.

Lord Cowley to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 9.) 


(No, 899.) 
My Lord, Paris, July 8, 1890, 


COUNT WALEWSKI read me this afternoon a despatch, which he is 
about to send this evening to M, de Persigny, on the subject of the communt- 
cations recently made to him by M. de Hiibner; and he then stated to me that, 
all things considered, the French Government had come to the same conclusion 
as that of Her Majesty, that it would be useless to press Austria more closely at 
the present moment, and that, in view of the future, it would be as well to let 
her maintain the ground of the Treaty of the 9nd of December as long as she 
could. He had, therefore, resolved mot to send to Vienna the despatch which 
had been communicated to your Lordship, in project, by M. Baudin, He is, 
however, fearful, from some words which fell from M. de Hitbner, that Count 
Buol has some intention of replying to the Emperor's speech, particularly to 
that part which alludes to the engagements of Austria; and he has written to 
M. de Bourqueney to eay that, if Austria really holds to the continued existence 
of the ‘Treaty of the 2nd December, he recommends Count Buol to abstain from 
all further discussion of what are and what are not the obligations of Austria, for 
that a discussion of the kind might end in France declaring the ‘Treaty to be 
virtually at an end. 

The despatches communicated by M. de Hiibner were five in number, ancl, 
although containing much matter for contradiction, will, on the principle of 
avoiding all further irritating discussion, not be replied to by the French Govern- 
ment, As the same despatches will be shown to your Lordship by Count 
Colloredo, I need not allude to their contents; but one point calls for observa- 
tion, because Count Walewski dwelt upon it. It appears that Count Buol 
remarked on what he conceived to be a shade of difference between Count 
Walewski’s last despatches and those of your Lordship, in a matter of some 
importance. The Austrian Minister understands that. Her Majesty's Govern- 
ment, in any future negotiation, consider themselves bound to the four points; 
and M. de Hiibner is instructed to call Count Walewski’s attention to the fact, 
and to ask him whether the French Government consider themselves to be under 
the same obligation. 

Count Walewski refused to give M. de Hiibner any answer until he had 
consulted your Lordship. He wishes to make precisely the same answer as 
your Lordship makes to Count Colloredo, and that that answer should be to the 
effect that the belligerent Powers do not consider themselves any longer bound 
to the four points, though it is probable that they may not refuse, when 
occasion offers, to take them as the point of departure for other negotiations. 

1 have, &e. 


(Signed) COWLEY.

No. 46. 

Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 9.) 
(No, 470.) 
My Lord, Therapia, June 28, 1855. 


THE intelligence, however scanty, which comes in from the north-eastern 
frontier of Asiatic Turkey, is not calculated to diminish the Forte's anxiety. 
The latest advices from Trebizond, which came round to me through Lord 
Raglan, describe the Russians from Gumri as being within a few hours’ march 
of Kars. Their force is stated at 86,000,—a number which considerably 
execeds the previous estimate, but which may perhaps comprise the detachment 
which appears to have been directed upon Ardahap. 

_ Be that as it may, the Turkish forces, including all between Kars and 
Erzeroom, with the circumjacent stations, cannot be sately curried beyond an 
amount of 20,000, if so much, and the Seraskier Pasha has prepared me for a 


36 


retrograde movement on their part, it being his decided opinion that the 
positions at Kars are not tenable against the enemy. 

His Excellency and his colleagues are Saeitally desirous. that no time 
should be lost in counteracting the Russian attack, an it is some consolation to 
me to find that even at this cleventh hour the necessity of listening to my 
advice and sending off reinforcements without further delay is recognized. 

Bat where are reinforcements to be found in sufficient quantity ? How are 
they to be provided with necessary supplies? What plan of operation offers 
the best chance of employing their services with effect ? 

Nothing can with prudence or consistency be detached from the army 
acting under Omer Pasha in the Crimea, <At Batoom, Soukoum Kalé, and 
other neighbouring stations on the coast, it would be extremely difficult to 
muster more than 11,000 men, though a higher figure was quoted at first. In 
Bulgaria, I question the existence of more than 50,000 men, including garrisons. 
The other parts of the Empire afford no additional reserves, with the exception 
of Bosnia, where it is still possible that a few thousand men might be detached ; 
[ speak of Regulars. Bashi-Bozouks may be procured, but your Lordship knows 
what little dependence is to be placed on such undisciplined hordes. There 
remains the halfformed corps of General Vivian, and the Irregular Cavalry 
collected by General Beatson and his officers. 

The ‘Turkish Ministers, who had talked of sending 10,000 men from 
Batoom to Erzeroom, now, in their embarrassment, and swayed, 1 think, by 
General Guyon’s advice, incline to another plan. ‘They propose to form an 
entrenched camp at Redoute-Kalch, and to concentrate there the corps of 
General Vivian, completed by a draft of 10,000 men from the Bulgarian army, 
that of General Beatson and the detachment from Batoom, reduced to 7,000 
men. ‘The total of these combined forces would be about 30,000 of all arms, 
Stationary, they might operate asa diversion in favour of the army at Kars or 
Erzeroom. Advancing ie Kutais on Georgia, they might either attack the 
Russians in the rear or force them to retreat. 

The main objections to this plan are the extreme insalubrity of the Circas- 
sian ecasts and valleys from now till October, the incomplete organization of 
Beatson’s Horse and the Turkish Contingent, the dificulty of procuring supplies 
or transport, and the danger of exposing a mixed Turkish foree to collision with 
a well-appointed and numerous enemy in the open field. 

On the other hand, there is ample evidence of a necessity which must. be 
met at no small risk, and with more or less of saerifice. The Russians appear 
to have opened their campaign with a great nuinerical superiority than had been 
expected; and the consequences of leaving Erzeroom without any additional 
support, and of abandoning the unarmed population to its own resources, might 
be very difficult to repair, Reinforeements advancing from Trebizond would 
require to be strong enough for their own defence, as, before they could reach 
Erzeroom, the enemy might have taken or invested that place, and obtained the 
command of the high road leading to it from Constantinople. ‘Ten or fifteen 
thousand men taken from the means of collecting an army ut Redoute-Kaleh 
would be a serious drawback from the plan, which promises the greatest amount 
of advantage ; and it is not probable that either the Porte or Omer Pasha would 
consent to detach a sufficient foree from Bulgaria to meet singly the require- 
ments of the ease, Austria, it is true, has declared her intention of considering 
the passing of the Danube by Russia a casus belli, and she also stands pledged to 
the exelusion of that Power from the Danubian Principalities ; but the resolu- 
tion, which in such an emergency would enable the Porte to take its line upon 
those assurances, and to overlook the awkwardness of leaving an important position 
inadequately defended, is more fit to be admired than likely to be embraced. 

Qn a review of all these circumstances and considerations, your Lordship 
will easily comprehend my embarassment in being consulted by the Porte on 
mutters involving so much responsibility, without time for reference to the 
Commanders-in- chief, 

I have naturally communicated with Lieutenant-General Vivian, and I’ have 
also availed myself of the military talents and professional experience of 
Brigadier-General Mansfield. But with all the advantage derived from their 
views and counsels, | am still in presence of two very difficult and delicate ques- 
tions; first, whether to approve the destination which the Porte proposes thus 


prematurely to give to the Turkish Contingent and Beatson’s Horse ; secondly, 


a7 


whether to recommend the formation of an entrenched camp at Redoute-Kaleh 
by means of a force detached from Bulgaria. 

The Turkish Ministers have invited me to meet them in conference on the 
subject, and the cireumstances are too urgent for me to decline the invitation. 

My next report will inform your Lordship of the result; I am, meanwhile, 
anxious to apprize Her Majesty's Government of the situation without loss of 
time. 

I have, & 


? Cc. : 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

I THINK it right to state, in consequence of certain observations made 
during the late debates in the House of Commons, on the subject of the Vienna 
negotiations, that M. Drouyn de Lhuys’ resignation was not occasioned by the 
refusal of the Emperor to agree to the last Austrian propositions, but was the 
consequence of his having misunderstood the intentions of His Majesty in 
regard to certain instructions sent to Count Walewski in London, M. Drouyn 
de Lhuys considered that his own position was compromised by his misappre- 
hension of the Emperor's orders, and he determined in consequence to retire. 

I feel certain, that if this eminent statesman was still in a situation to do 
50, he would put this statement officially on record, for, although | had not an 
opportunity of seeing him myself after he left office, 1 know that he was at 
great pains to assure the Emperor, lis suceessor in the Foreign Office, and others 
eth whom he conversed on the subject, that his determination to resign was not 
caused by the rejection of Count Buol’s propositions.

Sir, Foreign Office, July 13, 1850. 

COUNT COLLOREDO has read to me a despatch from Count Buol 
stating, with reference to my despatch No. 232 of the 19th ultimo which you 
had yead to him, and in which I expressed the hope of Her Majesty's Govern. 
ment that the attitude of Austria towards Russia would undergo no change, and 
that no reductions would take place in the Austrian army, that he had nothing 
to add to the declaration which the Austrian Government has already made 
upon this subject. 

"Faithful to the spirit of her alliance Austria will continue ready to seize 
every opportunity for the reestablishment of peace upon the basis of the four 
points, She will continue at the same time, by the occupation of the Princi- 
palities, to furnish her contingent for the defence of the territorial integrity of 
the Ottoman Empire upon the side of the Danube. 

As regards her military measures, these, Count Buol observes, are questions 
which are within the domain of her own liberty of action, just as the direction of 
their military operations belongs to the belligerent Powers, without being subject 
to any coutral upon the part of Austria, and expresses the expectation of the 
Emperor that in such matters the confidence shown to his allies will be recipro- 
cated by them, 

The Emperor, whilst diminishing the burdens imposed upon his people by 
the extension of his armaments, will maintain his forces upon such a footing as 


will enable him to give effective assistance in obtaining the object of the alliance, 
On the other hand, the Emperor hopes that his allies will use their liberty of 
L : 


vo 


action with moderation, and will not give the operations of the war a character 
which might compromise the original object of the alliance. 

The Austrian Government, whilst it does not contest that, strictly speaking, 
the belligerent Powers are not, since the rupture of the negotiations, bound by 
the partial understanding established in the Conferences, hopes that the four 
bases are still considered by Her Majesty's Government as the essential elements 
uf peace, 

Count Buol incloses copy of a circular, of which I transmit a translation, 
explaining the nature of a statement which the Austrian Government is about to 
submit to the Diet.

Inclosure in No, 45. 
Circular addressed to Austrian Representatives at Foreign Courts. 


(Translation.) Vienna, June 28, 1855. 

THE manner in which the Governments of Germany have received the 
communication of the Protocols of the Vienna Conferences, has been a source of 
the sincerest pleasure to the Imperial Court. AlLits confederates have testified 
their acknowledgment of its endeavours to contribute to the restoration of peace 
on a just basis suitable to the interests of Germany, and it announces with the 
most lively satisfaction the value whieh it sets upon this testimony. 

But it is now the wish of the Imperial Court also to be assured of the con: 
sent of its German allies in regard to the attitude which it has: assumed since 
the close of the Conferences. It appears to us to be not only a duty of federal 
and amicable confidence to explain clearly this attitude to the members of the 
Confederation, but we believe also to act in @ manner responsive to the claim 
equally appertaining to all the German Governments, if we put the Con federation 
in possession of the means of adopting a resolution whieh 1s proper under exist- 
ing circumstances, in regard to the future attitude of the whole of Germany. 

Baron von Prokeseh, who is about to proceed to his post, will therefore be 
empowered to explain fully to the Federal Assembly the present position of the 
Austrian Court, at the same time that he lays before it the ‘Treaty of Alliance 
of the 2nd December last, between Austria, France, and Great Britain, the 
memorandum of the 28th December, and the Protocols of the Conferenees. “The 
confidential communications which we have had with Prossia upon this measure 
give us hope for her full assent to the declarations which we are about to make 
in the Federal Assembly. 

We believe that in what follows, we are stating briefly the tenour of the 
statement which the Imperial Legate is empowered to make, in arder to put the 
Federal Governments beforehand in possession of the extent of what is proposed, 
and thereby enable them to furnish their Representatives in the Federal Assembly 
with instructions as to the statement of their views. 

The present well known state of affairs clearly shows that a union of views 
upon the precucal application of the third guarantee point has not taken place 
between Austria and the two Western Powers, There were no grounds for 
Austria’s joining in the war, because the ¢ uestion was of a mode of carrying it out 
which Austria did not consider to be indispensable. But the belligerent Powers 
did no more than avail themselves of their undoubted right in declaring for a 
continuance of the war, their conditions being more rigid. The case of the 
netive cooperation of the allies in the joint carrying out of the conditions of 
peace, has consequently not occurred: but the acknowledged objects of the 
alliance remain unchanged, and the mutually contracted obligation retains for 
both parties its strength and validity. It consequently follows that Austria will 
not only make it her especial task to riaintain the admission already obtained 
from Fussia in regard to the first and second point, and partially the third also, 
but she will also continue to be bound to cooperate for a clear and effectual 
fulfilment of the four points. On the other side (he attitude remains unaltered, 
which she has assumed for defending the integrity of the Ottoman Empire 
and maintaining the interests of Austria and Germany in the Danubian 


39 


Principalities, The Imperial Court has taken care to put this its idea of the 
situation out of all doubt by the most unambiguous declarations made to the 
European Powers, and the ‘structions given to the Imperial Legate will fulfil the 
same object towards the German Confederation. 

Baron von Prokesch will state also that inthe uncertainty of the continuance 
of this situation, a double necessity exists for Austria’s considering the deter- 
mination of her military state to trace back to a sinaller measure the efforts 
which circumstances have hitherto rendered necessary, but yet with this to renin 
completely in preparation both for securing a reserve to our troops in the Princt- 
palities, and in case of need to be able to complete the state of the army, in the 
shortest time possible, to its former strength. 

Being persuaded that throughout the whole of the affair we have perfectly 
maintained the position that the German Confederation assumed by its reso- 
lutions of the 24th June and 9th December of Inst year, we believe that we 
may add to the above statement the expression of our confidence that the full 
and candid approval of the Confederation will not be refused to our’ conduct. 
We shall close our statement by declaring that in regard to the resolution to be 
taken by the Federal Assembly, the present situation in our opinion implies ne 
cause for the contraction of new obligations by the Federal body, or for the 
enlargement of those existing; that the interest of peace and the attainment of 
a firm union of Germany appear to require that the Confederation, maintaining 
firmly the position it has hitherto held, and looking to the former Federal 
resolution mentioned above, should resolve to continue provisionally the state 
of preparedness appointed on the 8th of February of last year. 

The Imperial delegate is charged to submit the foregoing statement so soon 
as the Tener of the Federal Assembly find themselves in possession of 
instructions for their voting, We may assuredly assume that all parties desire 
to see, as soon as practicable, a unanimous attitude secured for Germany ; and 
we therefore hope that all the Governments will hasten to furnish instructions 
with all speed. 

You will give the Government a copy of this paper. 

ive, we.

The Earl of Clarendon to Mr. Ellot. 
(No, 15.) 
Sir, Foreign Office, July 13, 1855, 
COUNT COLLOREDO has read to mea despatch from Count Buol in 
which his Excellency adverts to the remark at the conclusion of my cireular 
despatch of June 19 last, that when the period shall arrive when nezollations for 
peace may with any prospect of suecess be renewed, Her Majesty's Government 
will steadily keep in view the principles which have cuided them throughout the 
t negotiations, and inquires whether the Austrian Government 1s authorised 
to infer from this declaration that Her Majesty's Government, whilst it does not 
recognise any obligation in the partial understanding come to on some of the 
points during the negotiations, does not the less continue to consider these 
oints collectively as the common ground which the Powers who signed the 
Treaty of December 2 are under a mutual engagement to take us the point of 
departure for their endeavours to restore peace. 
| told Count Colloredo that whenever nevotiations for peace were resumed, 
there could be no doubt that the questions of putting an end to Russian prepon- 
derance in the Black Sea; of bettering the condition of the Christian subjects of 
the Porte; and of securing the privileges of the Principalities and the free 
navigation of the Danube, must come prominently wider discussion ; and I said 
+ had been my duty to announce to Her Majesty’s Ministers at foreign Courts 
the determination of Her Majesty’s Government steadily to keep in view the 
principles which had euided them throughout the past negotiations, but beyond 
this Her Majesty’s Government were not disposed to contract any other obligation, 
nor to bind themselves by the terms of the four bases of negotiation which had 
lately been discussed at Vienna. 
Count Colloredo earnestly, and in the same friendly spivit which he hus 


ee a oe ee 


40 


always manifested in favour of an alliance between England and Austria, pressed 
fora compliance with the desire expressed by Count Buol, as it was important 
that there should still exist a common ground of agreement between the Powers 
who signed the Treaty of December 2. 
I replied, that although the Treaty of December 2 was now, by the course 
ursued by Austria, reduced to a dead letter, yet it was not the intention of Her 
Majesty's Government to press Austria any farther with respect to it, because 
they were determined to avoid, as far as possible, appeals and diseussions of a 
nature to impair the friendly relations which we desired to maintain with 
Austria, but when we were asked to renew engagements that had become void, 
I must take the liberty of reminding Count Colloredo of the position in which 
the two countries now stood towards each other. 

Austria, 1 said, having more to fear from the aggressive policy of Russia 
than either England or France, and her interests being completely identified 
with theirs in the war, the utmost deference to her wishes was throughout 
exhibited by the Western Powers, who never failed to bear in mind the 
geographical position and the German difficulties of Austria. 

The Protocols were agreed to at the time fixed by Austria; the Treaty of 
December 2 was signed at her invitation; the Conferences which, by England 
and Franee, were thought to be premature, were entered upon at the desire of 
Austria, in the firm belief on the part of the Western Powers that if peace were 
not obtained by negotiation, the active participation of Austria in the war would 
be the immediate consequence. 

‘The negotiations failed, and the Treaty of December 2 was barren of 
results: but no sooner were the Conferences closed, than Austria lost nota 
moment in officially announcing her neutrality to Russia, and in pressing the Porte 
not to permit the theatre of war to be transferred to the line of the Danube. 

She reduced her army by 150,000 men, and the Russian Guards, as might 
he expected, were sent from Poland to the Crimea, Austria had thus done great 
harm to the Western Powers, and she now still further sought to tre their hands 
for the future, by a renewal of the engagements which have already failed to 
produce the desired result, on account of the interpretation put upon them 
by Austria. 

Austria, moreover, makes to us this proposal, after having voluntarily pledged 
herself to Russia to seek no other terms of peace than those contained in the 
four bases (her own construction being, as a matter of course, put upon the third 
basis), and to use her influence to prevent any further conditions being demanded 
by others. 

\ In conclusion, I said that if the Cabinet of Vienna was animated by the 

game desire as Her Majesty’s Government to maintain friendly relations between 
the two countries, Count Buol will rest satisfied with the assurances given in the 
cireular to Her Majesty’s Ministers, and he will not attempt to impart an 
apparent vitality to the Treaty of December 2, by proposals which cannot be 
accepted ; while Austria allows that Treaty to be practically a dead letter, by 
declining to give to it that effect, the expectation of which, in the contingency 
which has actually happened, was one of the chief motives which induced the 
British Government to conclude it. 

You will accordingly inform Count Buol that although, as his Excellency 
infers from my circular despatch of the 19th ultimo, Her Majesty’s Government 
does not recognize any obligations in the en understanding come to on some 
of the points during the negotiations at Vienna, yet Her Majesty’s Government 
does adhere to the principles which form the foungaHinn of bee negotiations.

No, 51. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 16.) 


(No. 483.) 
My Lord, Therapia, July 5, 1855. 

THE Porte is chiefly engaged in continuing to prepare the elements of an 
expedition destined to relieve the army of Kars. Brigadier-General Mansfield, 
accompanied by M. Pisani, waited on the Seraskier Pasha yesterday. 1 had 
previously received from Licutenant-General Vivian the statement and estimate 
which he undertook to send in. They were preceded by a prefatory letter, on 
which I thought it necessary to offer a few words of remark, Copies, six in 
number, of these several papers, are inclosed herewith for your Lordship’s 
information. 

The large amount of transport and supplies required by General Vivian, 
doubtless on sufficient grounds, for landing anh moving forward a force of more 
than 40,000 men, and the delays inseparable from so extensive an operation, 
suggested another and more expeditious mode of proceeding, which General 
Mansfield threw upon paper for my consideration and that of the Turkish 
Ministers. It is inclosed herewith. I lost no time in submitting it, together 
with General Vivian's papers, to the Seraskier Pasha; and for the guidance of 
General Mansfield in communicating them to his Excellency, I drew up the 
accompanying minute of the points which it was important to keep distinctly 
in view. 

General Mansfield has since reported to me the substance of what passed 
in conversation between the Seraskier and himself. It leaves me nothing to 
add for the present, and joined with the papers previously enumerated, will 
serve, I imagine, to put your Lordship in full possession of all that has taken 
place on the important subject in question, as well as of the views entertained by 
the Porte, and the means required, and in part commanded, for carrying them 
into effect. 

T have, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

These documents give a proximate idea of the general requirements of the 
army for both sea and land transport, and your Excellency will be enabled to 
judge whether, with the aid it is intended to seek, the Turkish Government will 
have at its disposal the means indispensably necessary for carrying oub a 
movement of such invportance,

provided it can be efficiently arranged; but it appears to me to be useless to go 
further into the subject until it be known etuel shipping in sufficient number 


can be permanently made over to me for the expedition. 
There ean, however, be no objection to preparing the contingent as far as

charge of the division which it is intended to hand over to me at that place, as

Inclosure 2 in No. 51. 
Memorandum. 


Buyukderd, July 2, 1855. 
Confidential Memorandum on the means of Conveyance and requirements of an 


pee! of 25,000 men, to be moved from the Bosphorus to the Coast of 
sia, 


WHEREVER it may be decided to convey a force of this magnitude, the 
amount of sea-transport will be the same, with this modification, that, should it 
he decided to make a descent in the neighbourhood of the enemy, the landing 
would be necessarily simultaneous; but if at a secure place, the troops might be 
conveyed by detuchments. 

he amount of tonnage in either case would be the same, but fewer vessels 
would be required in the latter. 

Assuming that the army would be required to act on the offensive away 
from the sea-coast immediately on disembarking, it should be accompanied by 
the necessary Cavalry, Artillery, and transport animals. 

For a corps of 25,000 men, there may be estimated as follows 


Florses. 
For guns = - - - - 1,200 
Cavalry - - - - - 4,000 
Reserve musket ammunition - - - 2,000 
"Tents - - : - - 1,000 
Ambulance - - = - - 500 
Engine and pontoon team = - - 500 
Officers - - - - : BOO 
Transport for provision - . - - 5,000 
Total - - . - 15,000 


The number of transport animals are calculated for the carriage of provi- 
sions and barley for three days to be constantly present with the army, for one 
relay returning to the depdts, and one moving from the depots, 

It is evident the number is under-estimated, for as the army increased their 
distance from the base of operations several relays of horses would be required. 

The ammunition reserve is calculated for 200 rounds a man for 20,000 
men, 

A rough estimate of the transport required may be stated as for: 

Infantry.—20 vessels capable of containing 1,000 each. 

Cavalry.—140 vessels capable of the transport required for the conveyance 
of one month's provisions and forage, which may be estimated ut 3,000 tons of 
shipping. 

Taking the accidents, delays, difficulties in loading and unloading, and other 
demands which would subsequently be made on this shipping, into consideration, 
it may safely be said that this amount ghould be doubled. 

This latter tonnage must be entirely at the disposal of the Lieutenant- 


43 


General, or no reliance could be placed on the supply of the troops being 
maintained. | 

Preparations should be made immediately to ensure a supply of flat- 
bottomed boats, without which neither Cavalry nor Artillery can be embarked or 
disembarked ; thirty should be at once provided. 

‘A safe and commodious harbour accessible and capable of giving shelter im 
all weather is absolutely essential to the safety of the expedition. 

The nature of the harbour at Redout-Kalé, if one exists, should be accu- 
rately ascertained. , 

The mouth of the River Rhion, by which it is stated there is water com- 
munication with Kutais, is some miles south of Redout-Kalé; is it known. if 
there is an anchorage or shelter there? Is there a bar? Or supposing there is 
shelter for shipping at Redout-Kalé, can boats enter the Rhion at all seasons ? 

No reliance can be placed on obtaining any resources at Redout-Kalé, and 
should provisions or transport animals be obtainable on that coast oy would be 
sent by sca; the same amount of sea transport would, therefore, be required 
whether they were collected here or not. 

If there are to be collected here, three months is about the earliest date at 
which a sufficient number of animals could be obtained to enable the army to 
take the ficld. It would hardly be possible to obtain 1,000 a-week, which would 
take nine weeks under the most favourable circumstances. Should the army be 
collected at Kutais at an earlier period, it could not, therefore, move from thence 
before the end of September. : 

Considerable time would be required to form magazines of camp equip- 
ments, boots, blankets, great coats, &e.: none at present exist with this force, 
nor has the ambulance, engineer park, or pontoon trains arrived, 

Under favourable circumstances, with all the details previously arranged, it 
would take about ten days to embark this force. 

(Signed) E. R. WETHERALL.

Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to General Vivian. 


Sir, Therapia, July 2, 1659. 
1 HAVE the .honour to acknowledge the receipt of your letter dated 
this day. 

‘As the Turkish Ministers have it in view to employ your corps in the opera- 
tion to which your letter refers, I thought it desirable that you should confer 
with them; and after receiving their confirmation of what I had told you the 
evening before, that you should state in full the nature and amount of the means 
which you conceive to be necessary for carrying eventually into effect their 
intended project. 

The information for whieh, in concert with them, I look to your experience 
and military judgment, was not 80 much relative to the expediency of their plan, 
er to the command of means required for its exeention, as to the means ee 
selves, taken with reference to the force which could be employed, the country 
‘n which it would have to act, and the object to which it would be directed, 

[ understand from your letter, that with an army of 45,000 men you would 
hanily think of landing at Redout-Kulé, without having their full complement of 
stores, both Ordnance and Commissariat, with land transport ready for its 
advance. You add, that sea transport toa very large extent, security for con- 
tinued independence of uction, and an abundant command of money, would be 
necessary throughout your operations ; and you reserve whatever relates to 
arming, clothing, and rationing such portion of the army as would not be 
ineluded in the Contingent. 

The question at issue 1s so urgent, that I have thought it my duty to bring 
the leading points under your immediate consideration, 1 hope you will find 
‘ourself at liberty to meet my wishes as fully as the pressing interests of the 
situation would seem to require, 

I have, &e, 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

military capacity not so much an opinion on the expediency of the proposed 
plan, and the command of means required for its execution, as a report on the

which it is to act, and the object to which it 18 to be directed. 


45 


At the time my letter, of yesterday’s date, No. 39, was written, the limit 
within which the report expected from me was to be confined had not been 
prescribed, and I thought it advisable, pending the preparation of documents in 
detail on transport and Commissariat, to bring to your Lordship’s notice some 
points which appeared to me of vital importance in reference to the contemplated 
expedition. 

Some time prior to the receipt of your Lordship’s letter now under 
acknowledgment, t this morning forwarded reports which convey a general idea of 
the amount of transport required to move an army of 25,000 men to the locality 
proposed for their operations, and the Commissariat stores required for their 
supply on landing. | 

To enter more minutely into the subject, much information in detail must 
be obtained, and with this view I request your Excellency will be so good as to 
vender such assistance as you may be enabled to give by putting me in ecommu- 
nication with persons who have a local knowledge of the country, and from 
whom I may learn some particulars as to the strength and position of the 


enemy. |

Inclosure 8 in No. 51, 
Minute. 


GENERAL VIVIAN’S estimates to be communicated to the Seraskier 
Pasha. 

His Excellency to be asked how far the Porte can undertake to answer for 
furnishing the various objects of transport, supply, &c., and in what time. 

The new plan to be submitted to him in consideration of the time required 
for preparation, and the urgency of giving relief to Kars. 

His opinion of its merits to be ascertained. 

It is to be distinctly understood that the idea of beginning by an advance of 
troops on Kutais, and the construction of an entrenched camp at or near Redoute- 
Kaleh, is not to be taken as 4 eubstitution for the original plan, but rather as a 
commencement, leading eventually to the execution of the whole. 

Can the Seraskier provide a battering train to accompany the first advance 
af troops ! 

Can he provide the necessary hoats for the conveyance of heavy guns to 


utais ? 
If the half of the contingent now encamped proceeds to Redoute-Kaleh, will 
measures be taken at once for repairing and organizing the other half of it 
completely, and preparing the other troops intended to form part of the ulterior 
expedition ? 
Let it be clearly understood that the assent of Her Majesty's Government 
is a necessary condition of the enter rise, and that reference be also made at a 
proper moment to the Cormmanders-in-ehief of the allied forces in the Crimea. 
Notice of the plan in contempla ion is already on its way to London. 
Therapia, July 4, 1899.

Inclosure 9 in No. Sl. 


Report of an Interview between the Seraskier Mehmet Rushte and Brigadier- 
General Mansfield, July 4, 1859. 


IN the first place the Seraskier alluded to some details regarding the 
contingent under the command of Licutenant-General Vivian. He said 6,000 
stand of arms (percussion) had actually been sent to that officer, as well as a 
large quantity of new clothing, That it wae the habit of the Government to 
supply the troops every four months with shoes, and that on service the men 
in general preferred walking in a sort of sandal. A band of music which had 
been asked for by General Vivian was also ready, but that there had been a little 
difficulty about this in consequence of the battalions forming the contingent 
having been drafted from different regiments, thus leaving only one band for 
each regiment, which consists of several battalions. 7 

The Seraskier, who seemed much pleased at the energy displayed by his 
Government, the object of the relief of the army of Kars being one to which he 
is evidently most keenly alive, gaid at once, “1 have written the order for Ismail 
Pasha, who commanded in Bulgaria, to hold in immediate readiness to march, 
15,000 soldiers, viz, 10,000 to complete the contingent, and 5,000 as a supple- 
inentury force, to be attached to it in the camp at Varna, for the expedition.” 


47 


On my touching on the question of arms he said, after supplying General 
Vivian's contingent at Buyukderé, or about 3,000 Infantry, there still remained 
7,000 percussion muskets for those to be collected at Varna. He then sent for 
the letter he had written to Ismail Pasha, and in my presence added a postscript, 
directing that officer to send 4,000 Minié rifles with the troops held in readiness 
for service. 

I then submitted the estimates of General Vivian for the transport and 
supply of an army of 95,000 men, and they are now in the hands of the Seraskier 
for the consideration of the Council of Ministers this day. 

The Seraskier said distinctly, ‘* 1 ean undertake for the supply of an army 
of 40,000. 1 have one month’s supply here ready, and ina few days another 
will be ready, The only difficulty 1s in procuring steam-transport. We are 
taking heavy guns out of our ships and making the latter transports for stores, 
&e. But the Council will consider all this in the discussion on General Vivian’s 
estimates, and exact details, shall be afforded in writing.” 

The plun drawn up by me for the consideration of his Excellency Viscount 
Stratford de Redcliffe was then read, viz., to dispatch the troops by detachments 
to save transport, of which there ‘sa deficiency, the first detachment consisting 
of about 10,000 men. The men in the neighbourhood of Batoom, to the 
amount of 12,000, to be concentrated ot Redoute-Kalech. On the first detach- 
ment being landed, a forward movement of 22,000 men to be made immediately 
on Kutais; engineers being left at Redoute-Kaleh to trace and establish an 
intrenched camp, the labourers of the country being called in to assist their 
work. ‘The first detachment being landed, the ships to return and take up the 
remainder of the foree as it could be got ready, and to land it by battalions at 
the camp. 

L explained that the object of this was at once to render available for the 
relief to the army of Kars, the resources and troops actually now ready, and not 
to interfere with the ullimate and larger objects of the expedition. That it was 
the opinion of Her Majesty’s Ambassador that the plan was feasible, but that 
‘+ would be necessary to use the utmost diligence, both in throwing forward the 
force and in providing the supports, transports, and supplies. 

To all this the Seraskier agreed most joyfully. He said it was exactly lis 
own idea, which he had two days hefore expressed at the Council of Ministers. 
Tt was the only chanee for the army of Kars. ‘There was a canal from Poti to 
Redoute-Kaleh, the entranee of which had been stopped up by the Russians when 
Redoute-Kaleh was evacuated by them. Poti, being out of range from the fire 
of shipping, is still held by them. He believed there are two battalions there 
now. ‘The concentration of the men at Redoute-Kaleh would probably cause 
its evacuation ; at all events, it must be taken. 

1 agreed to this. 

The Seraskier quite understands that the first advance on Kutais, as has 
heen detailed, is not a substitute for the larger plan. 

There are fifty guns of different calibre for siege purposes at Batoom, of 
5, 7, and 9 akes respectively 56 okes being taken as the equivalent of 12 lbs. 

Twenty heavy guns will be immediately put in a state of readiness at the 
arsenal in Pera, for the service of a sicge. 

Plenty of boats ean be procured at Batoom and Trebizond for conveyance 
of stores and guns on the River Ition, when Poti has been taken. It is only a 
question of paying the boatmen. ‘There will be no difficulty in that respect. 

T then informed his Exeelleney that Her Majesty's Ambassador had 
communicated to the British Government on the subject, and that it would be 
necessary ta do the same ‘due time with the allied commanders, Without the 
consent of the former the enterprise could not proceed, 

The Seraskier replied, “Of course not, the contingent could not be 
employed by the Porte without the consent of Her Majesty’s Government, and 
it is. a8 absolutely necessary for the Turkish Government to communicate with 
Omer Pasha, as it is for the English to advise with the Generals in the Crimea. 
But, in the mean time, there need be no delay, and all the preparations are tn 
progress.” Five ships (steamers) have been already procured. ‘The Tunisian 
Horse have arrived. ‘There is the grentest diligence in collecting provisions. 
Orders have been issued for the collection of 6.000 baggage animals, 

All the reports received by the Turkish Government are to the eficet that 


45 


the Russian forces are in very slender numbers in that country, where the first 
operations would take place. ; 

I concluded by recommending secresy ; that I should wish to be furnished, 
for the information of Her Majesty's Ambassador, withas many details regarding 
the country and resources as possible, and particularly with regard to roads and 
the defences of Kutais. 

This the Seraskier promised, but doubted whether a Georgian officer and 
deserter from the Russian service, now in his employ, who knows the whole 
country, was sufficiently trustworthy ; but he would see about it. 

I then complimented him on the appearance of the camp, of the contin- 
gent, which I had seen that day, and the good understanding between the 
officers and men, and took my leave. 

(Signed) J. W. MANSFIELD, 
Brigadier-General,

The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 
(No, 572.) 
My Lord, Foreign Office, July 16, 1855. 
I HAVE received this morning your Excellency’s despatch No. 483 of the 
Sth instant and its inclosures, in continuation of your despatches No. 476 and 
477, on the subject of the proposed expedition to Redoute-Kaleh, and from 
thence to Kutais. , 

I have already stated to your Excellency, in my despatch No. 561 of the 
13th instant, and in my telegraphic despatch of the following day, the objections 
entertained by Her Majesty’s Government to the proposed operations; and those 
objections are not removed, but on the contrary rather strengthened, by the 
papers inclosed in your despatch No. 483, because it appears that the troops to 
he sent forward in the first instance would have to be detained for some time in 
the unhealthy country between Redoute-Kaleh and Kutais. | 

Her Majesty’s Government would still recommend that whatever force is 
sent for the relief of the army at Kars should proceed to Trebizond ; but it 
might perhaps be advantageous to send a force to Anapa, to move on from 


thence and take possession of the passes of Viadi Kaucos in cooperation with 


the Cireassians, and thus cut off all communication between Russta and 
Georgia. | 

If, indeed, Omer Pasha, who we understand 1s about to proceed to Con- 
stantinople, should determine to take any part of his own army, with Tunisians 
and Albanians, to Redoute-Kaleh, Her Majesty’s Government would have nothing 
to say to that proceeding ; but as regards the Contingent under General Vivian 
and General Beatson’s Horse, Her Majesty’s Government abide by their opinion 
that they should be directed through Trebizond or Erzeroom. 


am, &c. 
(Signed) CLARENDON.

Inclosure in No, 53. 
Précis of an Article inserted in the * National Zeitung” of July 13, 1855. 


(Translation.) 

PRIVATE reports from Taganrog state that three churches in that town 
have suffered considerably, in one of which a shell had damaged the altar. 

Wherever (says the report) flags were hoisted to designate the buildings and 
hospitals—and this applies more especially to the old citadel—the roofs and 
walls remained uninjured, but all others were literally riddled like a sieve. The 
report of General Todtleben, who has hitherto conducted the defence of Sebas- 
topol, having been wounded, has created a great anxiety at St. Petersburgh. 
The bombardment of Gros Tschora and Krasnajarjorka by the En lish, had also 
created great sensation. Nevertheless the enemy only directed their forces 
against the property of the State. Neither of these places have suffered damage. 

The report then reproduces, from Russian journals, some details with Se 
to the commercial importance of the towns of Berdiansk and Rostoff. The 
former, it states, was little else thirty years ago than a fishing village, but 
produced to the country the last year before the war a revenue of 4,000,000 of 
‘ilver roubles, ‘The latter enjoyed considerable commerce from the fairs held 
there in spring and autumn, In the autumn were brought thither Russian 
manufactures —woollens, cotton, silk, and leather—to the value of 1,200,000 
silver roubles, as also wine from the Crimea. The spring fair had greatly 
increased, by the development of the navigation of the Don. Foreign merchan- 
dize at the fair was in the proportion of sis The export trade of Rostolf had 
become the greatest lever to the development of agricultural cultivation in 
Southern Russia. Odessa firms often brought produce here for ready money 
fourteen months in advance, which ts brought here in enormous masses, Sure of 
asale, Rostoff had become the central point for the produce of the district of 
Rostoff; of the military colonies of the Don and of the Black Sea; of Sebastopol, 
and of the Governments of the districts of the Wolga, In Rostoff, where 
formerly there was not a single foreign commercial house, eight very large ones 
had been established. ‘The difference between the commercial revenue of the 
years 1836 and 1853 is most clearly seen from the account of the Ministerial 
papers, Which gives an ‘nerease to the export trade for the last year of 20,722,000 
silver roubles to, foreign countries, and of 3,428,000 silver roubles to Russia, 
For the last month the export trade has been almost nothing.

Inelosure in No. 50. 


Extract from the “* Vienna Gazette” of July 7, 1859. 


(Translation-) 

A SPBECH from the Throne, delivered either at the opening or at the 
close of the session of a Legislative Assembly, belongs, from, its very nature, to 
the internal polities of a country. Whatever a Sovereign may deem it 
right to communicate to his people, or to their Representatives, bears the 
character of a conversation in a famil circle, even when relations with 
a foreign State are touched upon. he point of view, therefore, fom 
which a document of this kind must be criticized is quite different from that: 


51 


from which an international State paper must be submitted to public examina- 


tion. 
This consideration not only points out to the foreign critic the proper 


boundary for lus criticism, but it enables him to weigh, without prejudice, a 
mode of representation ealeulated from certain definite points of view. 

No one, as experience has shown, more thoroughly understands the 
character of the French nation and its peculiarities than the illustrious Prince 
who has saved France from therefore, 


the abyss of a revolution. All the more, 
are addresses of the Emperor Napoleon 


to be looked upon as calculated 
specially for his people. 

As, however, in the late speech from the French Throne, mention is several 
times made of Austria, 1b may not be out of place, with all due deference to the 
‘lustrious organ from which it emanates, and which gives to those expressions i 

cculiar weight, to comment upon sone passages in the same, 50 far at least as 
they touch upon historical facts. 

We can safely leave to the judgment of the public, who are in possession of 
the facts, the question as to who it was that saved Turkey and foreed the 
Russian army to evacuate the Principalities 5 whether this result was alone due 
to the victories of the Anglo-French arms, oF whether, in some degree at least, 
« strateical grounds,” this side the Austrian frontier, contributed to it. 

As regards the Austrian proposition of which the French: speech treats, its 
object was that the Russian fleet in the Black Sea should not again reach the 
numbers which it counted previous to the commencement of the war. 

The difference in principle between reaching (atteindre”’), and passing 
ée dépasser ”), is 80 clear—its Importance so great—that on the very ground of 
this difference the first Plenipotentiaries of the Western Powers declared them- 
eclves ready to support, at their respective Courts, the point in question. 

The difference here pointed out is however practically set right by the 
article printed in the French “ Moniteur,” simultancously with the publication of 
the Emperor's speech, and which reproduces the Austrian proposition In its 
correct form. | 

The omission in the apecch of the important fact of the counterbalance to 
the possible augmentation of the Russian fleet. in the Black Sea before the 
attainment of the specified maximum provided for by the Austrian proposal, is 
likewise supplied by the article in the '* Moniteur.” 

‘As we stated before, these remarks are only ‘ntended to demonstrate the 
proposition with which we started—that addresses af Sovercigns to the Represen- 
tatives of the nations over which they rule cannot he eriticized from the same 
point of view as international State papers. 

If we turn from this to the question a3 to whether Austia 1s holding back 
from the engagements to which she liad pledged herself, ‘twill be sufficient for 
our purpose to quote the two ‘Articles in. the ‘Treaty of Alliance of the Und of 
December which bear upen the subject : 

‘éAyticle V.—In the event of the general peace not being restored upon the 
basis fixed by Article I, His Majesty the Emperor of Austria, His Majesty the 
Emperor of the Trench, and Her Majesty the Queen of Great Britain, &e., 
will, without delay, deliberate upon the most effectual means for the attainment 
af the object of their alliance, 

“Art, IUL—In the event af the breaking out of hostilities between Austria 
and Russia, their Majesties the Emperor of Austria, &e., &e., respectively promise 
to each other their affensive and defensive alliance, &e., &e.” 


The Protocol of the agth of December had declared that the preponderance 
of Russia in the Black Sea was to cease. ‘Che Austrian proposition fulfilled 
this condition, according to the testimony of the Ambassadors of England aril 
France, ‘The busts of peace stipulated for in the ‘Treaty was thereby obtained. 
The propositions of the Imperial Cabinet can never be described as insulticient 
if placed in natural and necessary connexion with the draft of ‘Treaty simul- 
taneously projected, which declared an increase 10 the Russian fleet in the Black 
Sea up to the point at whieh it was before the breaking out of the war ts an net 
of aggression against Europe. [tis true that a form humiliating to Russia was 
avoided, butall that the Protocol of December really required was obtained. 

it must be granted that the Western Powers had kept te themselves the 


) ae 


1 oe 


jay DIENnd 


LRONOT ‘3Diddio: avo 


a2 


right to demand further conditions, but there was no obligation for Austria, 
cither morally or by Treaty, to back these further demands. 3 

Each of the Contracting Parties has exercised its right. No claims 
founded on right have been violated, none have remained unfulfilled, and the 
cotnmon objects of the alliance remain the same.

My Lord, Stockholm, July 6, 1855. 

GENERAL NORDIN, in a despatch dated the 23rd ultimo, which was 
forwarded by way of Germany, and which reached this Government subse- 
quently to one of the 26th sent by the Finland post, mentions that Count 
Nesselrode had again alluded to the rejection by Great Britain and France of the 
last Vienna proposals for carrying into effect the third point of the basis of 
negotiation, and stated that the allied Governments had thereby rendered a 
most important service to Russia, and relieved her from a most embarrassing 
position, for His Imperial Majesty would never have consented to accept the 
terms proposed. | mention this renewed statement of the Russian Chancellor, 
thong beyond confirming what I had the honour to report to ou Lordship in 
my despatch No. 46, it contains nothing to weaken the doubts I ventured to 
express us to its sincerity. 

The news of the repulse on the 18th ultimo of the attack of the alles on 
the Malakoff and Redan Towers at Sebastopol, was known at ot. Petersburgh 
when General Nordin wrote on the 23rd, and had produced great exultation, 
the more so as very lively apprehensions lad been for some time felt at 
St, Petersburgh as to the fate of Sebastopol. The Emperor had ordered a 
T» Deum to be celebrated on the occasion, and the mourning for the late 
Emperor to be laid aside for twenty-four hours.

Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon,—(Received July 18.)

My Lord, Therapia, July 6, 1855. 

LIRUTENANT-GENERAL VIVIAN has now in camp under his com- 
mand about 10,000 men, including Infantry, Cavalry, and Artillery, Of the 
latter arm there ure four batteries of six guns each, The remaining half of the 
contingent is ready to be detached from the Bulgarian army, and also to be 
placed under General Vivian's command, The General is satisfied with the 
soldiers in his camp. ‘There is reason to presume that those in Bulgaria are not 
‘nferior. ‘Thus the whole contingent is composed of troops of the Line, already 
well drilled and exercised, in part even of those who have engaged the enemy. 
According to the Convention, 5,000 of the men might have been Reditts. 

General Beatson isat the head of about 1,000 horse; 3,000 more have 
been collected by his officers. Their reports and those of the Consuls justify a 
belief that not only the additional thousand required to complete his corps, but 
that many thousands more, might be obtained if such were the pleasure of 
Her Majesty's Government. 

There is every appearance of the whole force being ready for service in_ the 
field before the end of next month, provided the means of transport can be obtained, 
and Her Majesty's Government be not backward in providing whatever is still to 
come from them. General Beatson complains of the want of British officers for 
his troops, and the deficiency of ther transport has been and still is a great 
cause of delay, Medical men are also wanted, and General Beatson owes to 
General Vivian the loan of several,

administration, in the provinces. More, however, may be done; and I hope by 
repeated endeavours and unwearied perseverance to extort gradually a more 
substantial measure of justice from the Turkish Government. In cases where 
British rights and British interests aru not directly concerned, we can hardly 
expect an immediate and complete success. It 15 much if we obtain an occa- 
gional instalment, and keep the Porte alive to the necessity of advancing, how- 
ever slowly, towards a fairer dispensation of justice and a steadier application of 
the principles which it professes. 

T shall have occasion to returm Peet to the subject of this despatch.

No. 60. 
Lord Stratford de Redeliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 18.) 


(No. 499.) 
My Lord, Therapia, July 9, 1855. 

I HAVE been of late several times under the necessity of recommending 
the dismissal of Turkish functionaries, in consequence of flagrant acts of miscon- 
duct on their part; and 1 observe, with concern, that even when the Porte 
is compelled to recognize the justice of my demand, there is an evident inclina- 
tion to spare the offender as far as public decency will allow. 1 have therefore 
thought it my duty to insist, in one signal instance, that a formal and exemplary 
character should be given to the act of dismissal, and to urge strongly that the 
same principle shall be observed in dealing with other cases. 

The officer whom I particularly marked out for public disgrace, is one who 
bears the title of “ Monavin,” or Assistant to the Council of Police. 1 received 
the most pointed complaints against him from Rear-Admiral Grey and from 
Her Majesty's Consul- eneral. 1 therefore required that his distnissal should 
be gazetted, with express reference to his failure in duty towards the British 
authorities. Fuad Pasha complied reluctantly, after some resistance, and not 
hefore I had appealed to the Grand Vizier. 

Hussein Hey, whose dismissal was announced to your Lordship on good 
authority some time ago, 18 still in office, [am assured that the Porte’s engage- 
ment to dismiss him will be ultimately fulfilled; but the object of the delay is to 
give a colouring to his disgrace by making it time with some kind of change in 
the office he holds, He is supported, in spite of his notorious unworthiness, by 
gome secret influence in the Seraglio, and my efforts to make his retirement more 
exemplary have hitherto failed of success. 


55 


Osman Pasha is more completely sheltered for the present by an assertion 
conveyed to me officially from the Porte, that the paper on which I had 
grounded my charges against him, was not drawn up by him, but forwarded 
from a private source, Lord Napier’s approaching visit to Salonica will, I hope, 
enable me to ascertain the truth of this excuse. Meanwhile I think it better to 
suspend my proceedings against the Mushir. 

Akif Pasha, however culpable in his conduct, as described by Mr. Hughes, 
concluded by doing justice to the Christian girl, and promising the law's 
venzeance against her persecutors, Apprehensive that his dismissal under such 
circumstances might be interpreted as a proof of his having been disapproved for 
sanctioning the girl’s emancipation, I have confined myself to requiring that he 
shall be strongly reprimanded by his Government, and compelled to redeem his 
pledge of bringing the offenders to justice. 

T have, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE,

Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 18.) 


(No. 500. Confidential.) 


My Lord, Therapia, July 9, 1855. 

; INCLOSED herewith, in the form of a translated copy, is the draft of an 
instruction prepared for transmission to the Turkish Ambassador at Vienna, 
after it shall have received the Sultan's sanction. It relates to what has passed 
between Fuad Pasha and Baron Koller with reference to the present position of 
Anstria towards Russia in the Principalities on the Danube, and to the wish 
expressed by Baron Koller that the Porte and her allies should place themselves 
in u corresponding position as to military operations in that quarter. 

Conceiving it to be desirable that the Porte should record its opinion in a 
sense favourable to the rights of the belligerents, 1 offered the suggestion of an 
ostensible despatch to Aarif Effendi on the part of the Porte, and I hope your 
Lordship will approve of the step, and the manner in which Fuad Pasha is 
prepared to carry it into effect. 

I have, &e.
