Inclosure in No, 61. 
Draft of a Despatch to be addressed (o the Ottoman Minister at Vienna. 


(Translation.) Constantinople, July 6, 1855. 

BARON KOLLER has read to me confidentially a despatch received by 
him from Count Buol inclosing a despatch addressed to the Court of St. Peters- 
burgh containing the opinion of the Austrian Cabinet respecting the breaking 
off of the Conferences of Vienna, The purport of it is as follows :—“ After 
expressing regret at the rupture of the Conterences without being able to obtam 
the object desired, itstates that Austria has notified in a definitive manner to 
the Cabinet of Russia that, while protecting the two Danubian Prineipalities 
held by her, if the Russian troops should be again in a position for invading 
those two Ottoman Provinces, the Austrian troops will oppose them, and 
likewise that, if they should again cross the Danube, Austria will consider ita 
casus belli, Moreover, that, although the rights and the engagements of the 
Sublime Porte are known and secure if the Ottoman troops and those of the 
allies were to cross the Danube, it would give rise to certain objections which 
would impair that status which Austria has to defend, and Austria expresses the 
desire which she entertains that that measure should be abstained from. 

With reference, firstly, to the regret evinced by Austria at the breaking off 
of the Conferences, when the Sublime Porte and her allies were invited to 
discuss a pacification consistent with the dignity and the interests of all parties 
they complied, and afforded proofs of the moderation of their views; and a

Power, likewise is more in 4 condition than Austria to appreciate the equity and 
the uprightness of their proceedings at the Conferences. It is well known from 
«hence the obstacles to the desiret result proceeded, and the Sublime Porte, in 
perfect accordance with her allies, participates in the regret evinced also by the 
Austrian Cabinet on this score. 

The definitive intention notified by the Imperial Government of Austria in 
the event of Russia invading the Principalities or crossing the Danube, is 4 mark 
of her friendly disposition towards the Sublime Porte, and a natural result of the 
alliance between the Powers. The Porte has nothing to say on this subject but 
to express her satisfaction, 

With reference to the observations and the desire expressed by Austria that 
the Ottoman troops and those of the allies should abstain from crossing the 
Danube, and carry the war to the other side, Austria was among the first to 
confirm and acknowledge the right of the Sublime Porte in this respect, and her 
engagements with her allies. It is the natural consequence of those rights and 
engagements that thePorteshould be under the necessity of preserving full freedom 
of action together with her allies. Our allies are, moreover, united with us on 
this subject, and we do not entertain the smallest doubt that the Imperial 
Government of Austria will in her wisdom and equity appreciate the objections 
of the Sublime Porte to complying with this desire. 

‘These observations were made to Baron Koller on receiving Count Buol's 
communication ; but for the removal of all doubt that may arise on this subject, 
+t ig His Majesty the Sultan’s orders that this communication should be conveyed 
through your Excellency to the Cabinet of Austria. You will, therefore, see 
Count Buol, and convey to him in full, confidentially, the present statement.

Mr. Buchanan to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 18.) 
(No. 155.) 


My Lord, Copenhagen, July 14, 1855. 

M. DE SCHEEL informs me, that according to the reports which he 
has received from Baron Plessen, great fears were entertained at St. Petersburgh 
for the safety of Sebastopol subsequently to the capture of the Mamelon and 
that there was a general disposition to blame the measures adopted by Prince 
Gortchakoff for the defence of the fortress, ‘his feeling had, however, been 
greatly, if not entirely removed, by the repulse of the a lies on the 18th, and 
hopes were again entertained of a successiul resistance. Sel) 

As to the effect. which might be produced on the feelings of the Russian 
Government and people by the eventual fall of the fortress, his Excellency said 
Baron Plessen did not expect, as far as an opinion could be formed from the 
language of the public at St. Petersburgh, that it would render Russia more 
disposed to accept the terms of the allies; and that he deseribed the opinion a8 
almost universal, that Russia could never have accepted the “ humiliating” 
terms contained in the last Austrian proposal for the solution of the third point 
of guarantee discussed at Vienna: but M. de Scheel observed at the same time 
that it ought to be remembered that these opinions were expressed at St. Peters- 
burgh after the rejection of the proposal by England and France.

Mr. Buchanan to the Earl of Clarendon,—(Received July 18.) 


(No, 156. Confidential.) 


My Lord, Copenhagen, July 14, 1855. 

HAVING been informed that a Danish traveller had arrived a few days 
ago from Riga, I sought an interview with one of his friends, and learnt that he 
had described the population there as being heartily tired of the war, and as 
suffering greatly from its consequences, though there was no apparent want of 
men or money at the disposal of the Government. 

I requested my informant to see his friend again, with a view to obtain 
some further particulars ; and [ have the honour to inclose herewith, for your 
Lordship's information, the copy of a memorandum embodying the result of 
his_ inquiries. [ ought to add that the Danish traveller’s sympathies are 
entirely Russian. 


te have, &c. 
(Signed) ANDREW BUCHANAN. 


el 


Inclosure in No. 65. 


Memorandum, 


MR. —— has nothing further to communicate to Mr. Buchanaw 
than what he verbally had the honour of doing on Wednesday, respecting the 
state of things in Riga, where it appears the depression of the population is 
very great, and peace eagerly wished for; but it must be borne in mind that 
native Russian feelings hardly can be expected in Riga from its original German 
nationality. 

It appears the conseription presses most eruelly on the population every- 
where: for on an estate of 400 souls, after deducting 200 for females, and 
100 for old men and children too youre to be enlisted, the amount of 8 or 10 


ee een i | 


ite 


per 1,000 souls are levied in proportion on the remaining 100, and yet youths of 
16 and 17 years are taken as conscripts. 

In the neighbourhood of Riga, at the beginning of July, there were about 
50,000 men, but by the end of this month it was expected the number would 
amount to 160,000, principally militia. The demoralization in the army is 
excessive, and thefts on a large scale are the order of the day; and as a Major- 
General's pay is only 1,500 silver roubles annually, corruption and bribery 
pervade all grades of the army, now as much as ever. 

Prince Suwarrow, Governor of the District, was fetched in a hurry from a 
dinner party last Sunday week, on a report being brought from the fortress that 
the English were in the act of landing, which however proved incorrect, and 
arose from two frigates having neared the land. 

The harassing kind of war which the allies are following, causes great 
injury to the interests of Russia. 

In private circles politics are freely discussed; and it is not expected 
Sebastopol will hold out long. ‘The service of the Cossacks, who are very quick 
in their movements, is very severe, owing to the number of outposts. | 

The Government has plenty of money at its disposal.

No. 66. 
Mr. Magenis to the Earl of Clarendon.— (Received July 18.) 


(No. 42. Secret and Confidential.) 
My Lord, Stockholm, July 11, 1855. 

UPON the receipt of your Lordship’s despatch No. 21, Secret, of the 
98th June, by the messenger Haviland, on the evening of the 4th instant, | 
loat no time in communicating with my French colleague, M. Lobstein, as to 
the course we were to pursue. I learnt from him that he had not received 
‘astructions from his Government of a similar nature to those your Lordship 
was pleased to forward to me in the above despatch, and though M. Lobstein 
thought it possible that he might receive them by the Litheck steamer 
on the Sth, they have not yet reached him. It is, however, more than probable 
that they will be forwarded by Count de la Rochelambert, attached to the 
French Mission at this Court, at present on leave of absence, and they may 
reach this by the Stettin boat on the 12th, or by that from Liibeek on the 
15th instant. 

As [ had already asked for an audience of the King to present to His 
Majesty Mr. Grey on his return here, my French colleague agreed with me that 
T had better avail myself of that opportunity to sound His Majesty as to his 
willingness to come to an arrangement of the nature sketched out by your 
Lordship in your despatch No.2). His Majesty's occupation at the camp, and 
a military excursion which His Majesty made with five squadrons of Cavalry and 
a portion of the Horse Artillery to Gripshalm, from whence he only returned on 
the 9th instant, caused some delay in my audience, which took place yesterday 
abeTnon when Mr. Grey and myself were invited to dine with their Majesties 
at Haga. 

Thad previously made known to the King through Prince Osear the general 
nature of the proposals which I was desirous of making to him, and though a 
dinner was not the most favourable opportunity for overtures of so important 
and delicate a nature, I felt pretty sure His Majesty would enable me to, make 
them. 1 was not disappointed, for after dinner His Majesty took me into an 
‘nner room and invited me to sit down by him on the sofa. 

I commenced our conversation by stating, that I believed His Majesty had 
been made acquainted by Prince Oscar with the nature of the proposals I was 
about to make; to which he replied that Prince Oscar had spoken to him on 
the subject, but he appeared to wish me to believe it had been in a very vague 
manner, and as if he was less aware of the real nature of those proposals than I 
think His Majesty really was, 

L then said that I had brought with me your Lordship’s instructions, and 
that I was prepared to adopt the course which seemed the least liable to error, 
and at the same time to be the most frank, namely, to show them to His 


: ; > ee eS ee 
; + ‘ . *: * Sait LE 
Sy 4% Th j ah ie yl : 


ra 


59 


Majesty, though I expressed some doubt as to the possibility of doing so where 
we were, 

His Majesty at once said there was no difficulty whatever, and having 
expressed his desire to see them, I placed in his hands your Lordship’s despatch 
No. 21. He read it over with much attention, and dwelt some time on the last 
page, more, as it appeared to me, to collect his idea on the whole, than on the 
importance of that part of the despatch. He then returned it to me, merely 
observing at first that it was a most interesting and important communication. 
We subsequently had a conversation which lasted a considerable time, the 
substance of which I will endeavour to report to your Lordship. 

His Majesty commenced by observing that he had always been desirous of 
intimate relations and alliance with Great Britain: the sympathies, the constitu- 
tional tendencies, and the interests of the two countries were identical, and thus 
offered the surest basis for such an alliance, and he appealed to my experience of 
the feelings of his people as a proof of his assertion. He dwelt upon the danger 
to Great Britain if Russia should succeed in obtaining a port in Finmark, and 
added that the Waranger Fiord would admirably suit her purpose, where it never 
froze, and where there existed every capability to make it the “* Sebastopol of the 
north,” which it would speedil become in the hands of Russia, at no great 
distance from the coast of Scotland. His Majesty then added such a fact was & 
proof that our interests were identical. 

In replying to His Majesty, 1 at first dwelt on the proof of the friendly 
feelings which animated Her Majesty's Government towards Sweden in making 
proposals so advantageous to her as those I had just submitted to His Majesty, 
and offering so great security against the future ambitious projects of Russia—a 
security which was inereased by the cordial eoncurrence of the French Govern- 
mentin those views. | added, that were we at peace with Russia, or if the present 
war was concluded before the termination of a Treaty securing Sweden from 
future aggressions of Russia in Tinmark, great difficulty might be felt by both 
the British and French Governments in concluding such an arrangement, 
but His Majesty might be assured that if such an arrangement was once 
concluded, we never should shrink from its obligations. T further remarked, 
that though the ambitious projects of Russia might for the moment be laid aside, 
they were by no means abandoned, and would surely be brought forward at a 
future and more auspicious moment, ‘The “fishery night " was, I said, merely 
a pretext for inserting the sharp point of the wedge, which would be driven home 
hereafter if such endeavourssucceeded. | fairly admitted our interest and wish to 
prevent Russia from obtaining a port in Finmark. ‘This interest is so self evident, 
and is so strongly felt by His Swedish Majesty, that any denial of it would have 
appeared futile in his eyes, and possibly might have tended to weaken the 
‘unlimited confidence ” which His Majesty has been pleased, an more occasions 
than the present, to express in me. 

The King mentioned as a proof the Russian Government desired to let the 
question slumber for the present, in order to renew it at a more favourable 
moment, that he had pressed, by frequent instructions to His Minister at 
St. Petersburgh, for a final settlement of the question, and that General Norden 
was always met by the answer that, at a moment when Russia was engaged in 
such a war, it was impossible to attend to so trivial a subject ; and His Majesty 
said that a Norwegian Plenipotentiary, who came some time ago to Stockholm 
by his orders to treat the question, had waited here in vain four months for the 
arrival of the Russian negotiator. 

His Majesty then admitted to me, that he regarded the conclusion of an 
arrangement such as I had submitted to him as the most important event which 
had occurred for the policy of the north of Europe since the commencement of 
the present war. THe said that if the ambitious designs of Russia were checked 
in the south, she would endeavour by increased activity to obtain a compensation 
for that check in the north, and he authorised me to state to your Lordship his 
general acceptance of the principle of an arrangement of the nature i had 
proposed. He was, however, desirous of seeing the engagements we required, 
and the nature of the guarantees which we offered, detailedin the Secret Articles 
of a Treaty, to which he would give his best attention. 

| told His Majesty that 1 had not touched upon this subject with his 
Minister for Foreign Affairs, before I learnt his own views on the subject, and 
he authorised me to speak to Baron Stierneld, which | shall not fail to do, 


60 


though it ts ponnible I may not have an opportunity before the departure of the 
messenger, who has already been detained here longer than 1 could have wished. 
As my French colleague is still without instructions, I think it, however, the less 
important. Aa 

_ With regard to keeping both the proposal and the Treaty secret, His 

Majesty appeared to think it was our wish to adopt such a course; but upon my 
assuring him that such was not the case, and that although we would accede to 
such secresy, if His Majesty desired, your Lordship's instructions stated “ the 
manifestly aggressive intentions of Russia would fully justify His Swedish 
Majesty in resorting openly to this measure for his protection.” 

The King then said with great frankness, and with a courage which I think 
does him credit, that it was quite useless to endeavour to conceal such matters— 
they must be known a little sooner or later; the conclusion of such a Treaty 
was perfectly justifiable on his part, it was purely defensive; if no aeressive 
projeets were cherished or put forward by Russia, it became “ non avenw;” in 
the contrary case his precaution was at once justified ; and finally, that he 
looked on concealment as unworthy of the three Powers parties to such a 
Treaty, His Majesty however desired that until the Treaty was finally concluded, 
the negotiations should be carried on in seeresy ; a course which I ventured to 
assure him would meet with no objection on the part of your Lordship. 

His Majesty finally adverted to the intimacy of his relations with Denmark, 
and the deference which His Danish Majesty had invariably shown for his 
opinions on all political questions since 1848. and inquired whether I thought 
it would be possible that Denmark might become a party to the proposed 
Treaty? He at the same time admitted that some difficulty might arise from 
Denmark’s connection with the German Confederation, and appeared to me to 
put forward this idea more as a compliment to that country than as an essential 
condition, T answered, that the accession of Denmark to the Treaty could offer 
no greater security to Sweden than what we already proposed without her; that 
Great Britain had shown every disposition to protect Danish interests and 
integrity when menaced in the Schleswig-Holstein question ; and that although 
I was unable to pronounce any opinion on the subject, I would not fail to. bring 
it under your Lordship's notice. I may observe in doing so, that His Majesty 
did not appear to attach any great weight to his suggestion, or in any respect 
to make it a sine qud non, ‘I hope that 1 am not mistaken in this opinion, for 
I should regard Denmark becoming a party to the Treaty as a most fruitful 
source of difficulties and delays. . 

_ I must observe that the King made no comment on the partial guarantee 
which was proposed in your Lordship’s despatch, nor expressed any expectation 
that the guarantee should be general, and extend to the whole of his possessions. 
I think it, however, probable that such an expectation may be put forward by 
his Ministers when we enter more fully on the question, and I think it right to 
prepare your Lordship for it, 

I have, [ believe, reported the substance of His Majesty's conversation, and 
Lam sure that I have stated correctly all the sense of the opinions which | have 
put into His Majesty's mouth. 

___Mr. Sampson, the British Vieo-Consul at this place, returns to England on 
the 17th on private business, and I shall have by him a safe opportunity to 
report any conversation which I may have with Baron Stiemeld on the subject of 
this despatch. 
I have, &e. . 
(Signed) ARTHUR C, MAGENIS.

Mr. Magenis to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Recewved July 23.) 


(No. 53, Seeret and Confidential.) 

My Lord, Stockholm, July 14, 1855. 

___ MY French colleague received by the Stettin steam-boat on the 12th instant, 
instructions dated the Ist similar to those which your Lordship was pleased to 
transmit to mie in your secret despatch No. 21 of the 28th ultimo. 


Gil 


M. Lobstein called on me the following morning to compare our instruc- 
tions, and to concert on the course we were to pursh® Se 

The instructions forwarded to him by Count Walewski are identical with, 
and appear to have been translated from, those of your Lordship, though the 
‘ntroduction adverting to the former history of Finmark is more detailed. | 

We agreed, as I had already sounded the ground with the King, and 
obtained His Majesty's consent to the principle of the proposed arrangement, to 
bring it more formally before the Minister for Foreign Affairs; and M. Lobstein 
went from my house to the Foreign Office, where he saw Baron Stierneld, 
After having informed his Excellency that T had agreed with him not to bring 
the proposed arrangement to his notice until he, M. Lobstein, had received his 
instructions, but that I had mentioned the subject to the King when I lately 
had the honour to dine with their Majesties at Haga, M. Lobstein read to Baron 
Stierneld his instructions. 

He informs me that Baron Stierneld received this communication at first 
with some signs of coldness, and ap weared to wish to attach less importance to 
the danger and menace of the Russian fortifications of Bomarsund than Count 
Walewski attributed to them. His Excellency finally became more cordial, said 
he saw no objection to the proposed arrangement, and promised to confer with 
the King on the subject and to give M. Lobstein an answer at the weekly 
Conference on Monday next. I called at a fater hour on the 13th at the 
Foreign Office, but was unable to. see Baron Stierneld, who was engaged at a 
Cabinet Council. I requested Count Ebrensvard to inform his Excellency that 
I had called, and begged him to fix an hour to see me on the following day. 

His Excellency called on me early this day, when | read to him, slowly and 
distinctly, your Lordship’s instructions, offering him at the same time, if he 
desired, to read them himself. He declined my offer, saying he perfectly under- 
stood them, and that they were precisely similar to those of M. Lobstein. He 
made no objection to accept, as His Majesty had already done, the principle of 
the proposed arrangement ; but he added, that when your Lordship spoke of the 
“ qeoressions of Russia,” he must say that no ‘aggressions had as yet been 
made.” I replied at once that he was under-a mistake. Your Lordship’s expres- 
sion was, “the manifestly aggressive ‘atentions of Russia,” and that that expres- 
sion referred equally to Bomarsund and to Finmark. In that case, replied his 

Excellency, mk if yourefer to Bomarsund, I at once admit the correctness of the 
expression. 1 mention this more particularly to show that he did not with me 
make light of the danger or menace of Bomarsund. Our conversation was 
short, as Baron Stierneld was on his way to. a Council of the Ministers, but he 
was very cordial, promised to confer with the King on the subject, and to give 
me an answer at the next Conference. 
I have, &c. 
(Signed) ARTHUR C, MAGENIS,

No. 68. 
Mr. Magenis to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Reeeived July 23.) 


(No. 54, Secret and Confidential.) 
My Lord, Stockholm, July 16, 1855, 


IT was intimated to my French colleague that the King would probably 
wish to converse with him on the subject of his late communication to Baron 
Stierneld, which I adverted to in my preceding despatch, and M. Lobstein was 
accordingly invited to dinner at Haga yesterday, No other member of the 
diplomatic body was present, and no other stranger; and the Queen having 
withdrawn early after dinner with her suite, M. cobstein was left alone with 
the King. 

My French colleague adopted the same course which I had previously 
done, and put into the King's hands, for his perusal, Count Walewski’s instruc- 
tions, M. Lobstein has obligingly read to me his report of the King’s observa- 
tions, the substance of which is as follows: 

After having carefully read the despatch, His Majesty commenced by 

R 


62 


expressing his gratitude to the Emperor of the French for the proof of his 
friendly feelings evinced in the proposed arrangement. He then said, M. Lobstem 
had probably been acquainted by me that he had accepted the principle of the 
arrangement, and he did not mean to retract that acceptance. He had, however, 
since my communication, reflected deeply and fully (murement refléchi) on the 
subject, and he was convinced that what our respective Governments aimed at 
could only be obtained by a defensive ‘Treaty extending to all the Swedish 
and Norwegian possessions, and he accordingly desired that the proposed defen- 
sive Treaty should extend to both of those kingdoms. His Majesty said that if 
Russian projects of agerandizement in the south were checked, that Power on 
the canelusion of peace would surely endeavour to obtain an equivalent by 
increasing her influence in the north. Our object then, he presumed, was to 
establish a barrier against such projects by strengthening and supporting 
Sweden. Were Russia to obtain possession of Gothland, she would at once com- 
mand the Baltic; were she to obtain Landskrona—a port of which the captains 
of the English men-of-war who had sought refuge there this spring knew the 
value—she would command the Sound. His Majesty did not deny the great 
importance of our guaranteeing Finmark to Norway, but he said 1t was a subject 
little understood in Sweden, and he appealed to M. Lobstein whether he had 
heard it mentioned here as a subject of apprehension. 

He was Sovereign of both countries, but he felt that the Crown of Sweden 
was by far the more important; his duties consequently to this country were 
the stronger, and he felt he might require something more important in the 
judgment of his Swedish subjects, who had supported him so liberally and 
cordially in all he had asked of the Diet for his military preparations than the 
evarantee of Finmark, to justify the entire change of that policy which had been 
followed in this country since 1815; namely, instead of leaning for support on 
their powerful neighbour, now to seek for that support from England and France, 
for such His Majesty said would be the result of the proposed arrangement. 
He was certainly justified in adopting the proposed arrangement as a precau- 
tionary measure of defence; but it would show a distrust of Russia, and could 
not fail to produce feelings of coldness on her part, and though he would obtain 
increased protection in Finmark, he might in consequence be so much the more 
exposed in Sweden, 

His Norwestan subjects had met his proposals to increase the military 
defences of the country with a far less liberal. spirit, and a feeling of jealousy 
might be created in this country by the defensive Treaty not being extended 
to it. 

M, Lobstein, I believe, made some remark on His Majesty's suggestion to 
me, that Denmark should be a party to the proposed Treaty, and 1 conclude 
those remarks were unfavourable, for His Majesty observed that he felt the 
difficulty, and did not insist upon that accession. 

Such, as well as I can recollect, were the important points of His Majesty's 
observations to my French colleague. 

I called on Baron Stierneid this day at the Foreign Office. His Excellency 
said he supposed I had learnt from M. Lobstein the nature of his communi- 
eation with the King yesterday; to which I answered that I had. He then 
went over pretty much the same arguments. and | do not think brought forward 
anything new, excepting that he, as a Swede, felt constitutional difficulties as 
to signing a defensive ‘Treaty in which Norway alone was concerned. Sweden, 
in ease it was necessary to repel by force of arms any aggression of Russia m 
Finmark, would expose her own soil to those aggressions, if the latter were not 
included in the arrangement. I replied, that though a Swede, his Excellency 
was Poreign Minister for both countries, and I thought ought to look to Nor- 
wegian interests as well as Swedish; what we proposed was undoubtedly 
favourable to Norway; what His Majesty desired would be advantageous to 
both countries, no doubt; but as the kingdoms were united, Sweden must 
protect Norwegian interests if violently attacked, and would thus be exposed to 
the danger he apprehended, whether the guarantee of Finmark existed or not. 

| promised to report to your Lordship His Majesty's desire to extend the 
defensive Treaty to both kingdoms, and to lose no time in apprising his Excel. 
leney of the decision of Her Majesty's Government, so soon as T should be 
acquainted with it, 
| learn from M. Lobstein that Baron Stierneld made use of the same argu- 


63 


ments to him, and that he likewise promised to forward to his Government His 
Majesty’s proposal, which he does by Mr. Samson, the bearer of this despatch. 

In my secret and confidential despatch No. 52, 1 remarked that I did not 
think it improbable that when we entered more fully into negotiations, an 
endeavour would be made to extend the proposed arrangement to the whole 
possessions of the United Kingdoms, and your Lordship will have been some- 
what prepared for the present proposals. Those proposals will, I feel sure, 
receive the attention which they deserve on the part of Her Majesty’s Govern- 
ment, and, if they should appear not to increase unduly the responsibility which 
the Governments of Great Britain and France have declared their willingness to 
incur, may be accepted. 

The King of Sweden has behaved, it appears to me, with great frankness 
and honour in declaring that he did not retract his acceptation of the principle 
of the partial guarantee ; and his motives for desiring that it should be general 
are natural and evident, neither can I think that the reasons which he gives are 
without weight. ‘The possession of Gothland by Russia would certainly give 
her the command of the Baltic, and Landskrona of the Sound, and I conclude 
that we should oppose by force of arms either of these aggressions. I may 
remark that in Gothland the port of Stild is very rarely frozen, so that in addition 
to the commanding position of that island in the Baltic, one of the great objects of 
Russian ambition would be obtained by its possession. The danger to England 
would be less imminent than if Russia obtained a port in Finmark; but tt 
appears to me that there still might be considerable danger, and every successful 
aggression of Russia would increase her power, and weaken the power of 
resistance of Sweden. By a general guarantee we should incur a much greater 
responsibility in appearance; but T cannot but think that it would be alone in 
appearance, and that the fact of a guarantee on the part of two Powers like 
England and France would, when known, put a stop to any projects of aggran- 
dizement which Russia may cherish against Sweden, while such a guarantee 
could hardly fail te bind this country more closely to the interests and cause of 
the Western Powers. 

T beg to apologize to your Lordship for trespassing on your time with these 
observations, which can hardly fail to suggest themselves to your own considera- 
tion. 

IT have, &c. 
(Signed) ARTHUR C, MAGENIS.

My Lord, Therapia, July 12, 1850, 
IMMEDIATELY after my representations to the Sultan on behalf of 
Omer Pasha, who sent in his resignation, His Majesty determined to xratily his 
Commander-in chief by a complimentary message with a sword of honour, 
conveyed to him by one of the Fenpiial chamberlains, and accompanied with an 
injunction not to give up the command. From some motive unexplained to me 
His Majesty subsequently changed his mind, and took the resolution of writing 
what was styled an autograph letter to each of the three Commanders-in-chief. 
On hearing this intention | remarked to Fuad Pasha that the measure, lor one 
which had originated in a wish to gratify Omer Pasha, did not appear, in my 
judgment, to be well-conceived, and, after the unfortunate issue of the allied 
attack on the Redan and the Malakoff Tower on the 18th ultimo, T remarked to 
his Excellency that a more auspicious moment might hereafter be chosen. The 
Ministers, however, did not think proper to interfere, and Fuad Pasha may 
possibly have been called upon to respect his own suggestion. The consequence 
was, that Ettem Pasha, aide-de-camp and chamberlain to the Sultan, started for 
the Crimea without any communication with the Embassy, and arrived in time 
to deliver the Sultan’s letter for Lord Raglan, not to his Lordship himeelf, but 
to his successor, the Commander-in-chief of Her Majesty's army there,

CONCELVING that your Lordship must feel anxious to obtain as much

I have, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE, 


——————————————————<“« 


Inclosure in No. 70. 
Extract of a Private Letter, dated Erzeroom, June 26, 185. 


OUR letters from Kars are dated the Yard. The Russians were then in 
their camp, almost swamped by the rain. They sent out their horsemen, and 
they have full liberty to do so as they please, for the Turks have no mounted 
men to oppose them. ‘They came to Chiplaklee,* one station beyond Yenikin,” 
and there they destroyed some grain, and retired to their camp. They sent to 
Gumri for cight heavy guns to play on Kars; they stuck in the mud near 
Arpa-chai, but horses amd men were sent to get them out, and bring them to 
their camp, It is about an your from Kars, and the tents can be seen and 
counted; but they are spread over a wide space, probably to make the army 
appear larger: they have ascertained that their force is 24,000, among which a 
large proportion of cavalry, a great part irregular. ‘The Turkish troops are in 
high spirits, and the townspeople full of courage and alacrity ; about 1,000 have 
been supplied with arms at their own request, and come to their post on the 
least alarm, without being sent for. The Russians will never take Kars but by 
a regular siege, and those within have no fears while they have ammunition ; 
nevertheless, it were indispensable to send up succours.

Tord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 23.) 


(No. 509.) 
My Lord, Therapie, July 12, 1855. 

TAM grieved to state that a considerable desertion has taken place from 
among the Albamans at the Dardanelles, under General Beatson’s command. 
Lhave the honour to forward herewith the official correspondence relating to 
that untoward incident. I have no knowledge of the causes beyond what is 
stated by General Beatson and Mr, Calvert. It is impossible not to deplore the 
excesses Which have been committed, and difficult not to ap rehend a continu- 
ance, and even an aggravation, of the outrages, bad as they already are. 

T have communicated with the Porte, and also with Rear-Admiral Grey, 
upon the subject; and adopting Mr. Calvert's suggestions, I am engaged in 
urging the Porte to send down succours as quickly as possible to the Dardaneiles, 
together with orders for the seizure of the mutineers, addressed to all the 
provincial authorities within whose districts they are likely to be found. 

At the game time, it would scareely be wise to rush to a decided conclusion 
against the experiment entrusted to General Beatson. ‘The conduct of the men 
was exemplary, from the time of their arrival at the Dardanelles, The same 
wood opinion of their conduct is reported from other quarters. The reports of 
Colonel Walpole and of Consul-General Moore are alike favourable to them ; 
and your Lordship will not fail to remark in the correspondence of the former, 
how easily those whom he had enrolled allowed themselves to be marched over 
the mountains of Syria, disarmed and embarked. 

I have placed Mr. Skene, who enjoys General Beatson’s confidence, in 
yerzonal communication with that officer at the Dardanelles; and I trust that 
no length of time will glupse befure we shall be able to ascertain the true 
character of the late outbreak, and to judge how fur it may be possible to apply 
an effective remedy to the existing evils. 

: I have, &e. 

(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE,

Inclosure 2in No 71. 
General Beatson to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Dardanelles, July 7, 1855, 

1 REGRET to have to report that an unpleasant affair took place yesterday 
with a portion of the Albanians of the Ist and 2nd Regiments, which has 
ended in the desertion of 79 of the 2nd Regiment and 68 of the Ist. 

The night before last, when | was in the camp of the 1st Regiment, a 
number of the men came round me, and complained of the conduct of Adem, 
the Colassie of the Ist Regiment. 1 told them two men from each troop 
should go to the Commanding Officer of the Ist Regiment, who would write down 
their statements and submit them for my orders. The men were all perfectly 
satisfied with this, and returned quickly to their tents. 

Yesterday morning Colonel Bruce, commanding lst Regiment, took down 
the depositions, copy of which is herewith sent, 

This paper was sent to me by Colonel Bruce early in the day, but did not 
reach me till late in the afternoon, the messenger having been stopped on the 
road by some of the friends of Adem, who appear to have been under the 
impression that he would be punished when the report reached me. Acting on 
this impression, Adem appears to have got his friends together for the purpose 
of deserting, and in a state of great excitement they proceeded to a police 
guard-house in the town, and forcibly released a prisoner, an Albanian. 

When I heard that the men had gone off, I sent for the Ist Regiment to 
pursue them, and at the same time got Mr. Calvert to go to the Military Pasha 
to know what assistance he would give in case of their being any backwardness 
on the part of the other Albanians to assist in apprehending the deserters. 

The Pasha at first hesitated to give any; but on the representation of 
Mr. Calvert, he consented to-give two guns and a detachment of artillery. 

It was by this time dark, so that any chance of finding the deserters before 
morning was out of the question, besides which Ll regret to say there seemed to 
be considerable disinelination on the part of the other Albanians to undertake tle 
duty; a great number of them declaring that, although they were ready to fight 
against the Russians, they did not like fighting against their friends. 

With such feelings on the part of the Albanians, added to its being dark 
and our not knowing where our deserters were, there was nothing to be done 
but to wait till morning. At daybreak [| was with the men, and found the same 
disinclination to act against the deserters, who have disappeared no one knows 
where, expecting that they are gone to the south, 


OF 


I have repeatedly pointed out to the Minister of War the danger to which 
he exposed the success of the organization of the body of wild men, such as the 
Albanians and others, by leaving me without a sufficient number of others, and 
the present affair is one of the many evils which have arisen from the neglect of 
this force in respect to officers. The best remedy now is to send the whole of 
the men at once to the Crimea, where we can give them something to occupy 
them. I can organize just as well at Balaklava or Kamara as at the Dardanelles. 

Another cause of such scenes as that of yesterday is the Albanians being 
left alone at such a place as this. If transports had been sent to bring the 
Arab troops from Syria, | should have had no ditheulty in getting them to put 
down any excesses of the Albanians, and thus one would have kept the other in 
check. 

Both tribes make excellent soldiers, but they require to be kept in order, 
which can only be done by having a mixture of other tribes with them. 

I have, &c. | 
(Signed) F, BEATSON. 


Inclosure 3 in No, 71. 
Consul Calvert to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Dardanelles, July 7, 1855. 

| REGRET extremely to have to inform your Lordship that yesterday 
about fifty men of Beatson’s Horse came from their camp to this town, and 
rescued a prisoner confined for gross misconduct. Thy then proceeded in a 
body to the bridge on the Rhodius, and were presently joined by small parties 
from their regiment, increasing their numbers to nearly 150 mounted troopers, 
mostly from Monastir, They then placed themselves under the command of a 
man who was formerly Mr. Consul Longworth’'s cavass, and marched off towards 
the village of Renkioi, plundering everybody they met on the road, 

General Beatson ordered a regiment to turn out immediately, for the 
purpose of stopping and disarming the mutinous men; but only three troops 
obeyed his orders, and came down to the town. 

By this time, however, it was too late to do anything against them. Mean- 
while, [ considered it prudent to request the Pasha, with General Beatson’s 
concurrence, to call the garrison under arms, and to take up positions outside 
the town, with ficld pieces loaded with grape. Other troops were also brought 
over during the night from Kilid Bahar, and a detachment sent down by the 
steam-tuz “ Redpole,” to protect the British Military Hospital at, and the 
village of Renkioi, where they arrived just in time to prevent the mutinous 
troops from committing any disorders, though they were unable to prevent them 
from proceeding on towards the town of [jiné. 

Her Majesty's steam-vessel ‘‘ Harpy,” and the French war steamer 


- Tisiphone,” in the meantime, at my request, anchored as close as possible to 


the Consulate and General Beatson’s residence, and in a position likewise to 
protect the town. 

The Turkish garrison and the three troops of Irrezulars who obeyed 
General Beatson, remained under arms outside the town until the morning, when 
they returned to their quarters. 

These demonstrations will, it is to be hoped, produce a proper effect on 
the troops remaining in camp, and prevent a recurrence of any such serious 
outrages, and which keep the inhabitants of the town ina state of perpetual 
fear and agitation. 

T hav 


c, &e, 
(Signed) F. W. CALVERT.

Inclosure 5 in No. 71. 


Consul Calvert to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 

My Lord, Dardanelles, July 9, 1855. 

AFTER addressing your Lordship yesterday, the Military Governor came 

to the determination of sending 200 of his troops to Koum Kali, for the 

urpose of clearing the Plains of Troy of the marauding deserters from 

Raion Horse; bul positive intelligence haying been received that they had 

decamped from that quarter, and were forty miles distant on the road to Smyrna, 
the measure was, of course, abandoned. 


The two men reported as killed in my despatch of yesterday, were only 
wounded and made prisoners. 

The “Niagara” steam-transport has just towed in the “Chalmers” 
transport, bringing between them 244 men, chiefly Syrians, for Beatson’s 
Horse. 

I have, 


ie. 
(Signed) F. W. CALVERT. 


Inclosure 6 in No. 71. 


Consul Calvert to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 
My Lord, Dardanelles, July 10, 1855. 

IN continuation of my despatch of yesterday’s date, I have now to 
inform your Excellency that during the might an express steam-boat was 
dispatched to me by Dr, Parkes, Superintendent of the Renkioi Hospital, to 
acquaint me that about 100 of the Bashi-Bozouk deserters had returned to the 
village of Dumbreck, about three miles from Renkioi, with the avowed intention 
of liberating two of their comrades who had been captured, or of burning down 
the latter village. ‘The Pasha has therefore, at my request, sent by the steamer 
a detachment of troops to protect the inhabitants. The prisoners have been 
removed to an English ship anchored near the hospital, and on board of which 
the ladies of some of the principal medical gentlemen have taken refuge. 

I further regret to report that the remainder of the Albanian troops who 
ure here evince a spirit of extreme insubordinatton, as General Beatson himself 
will have doubtless explained to your Excellency, 

Meanwhile, as the Arab Irregulars who arrived yesterday have no arms, and 
a8 it is impossible to say what degree of dependence is to be placed upon them, 
as also the local Artillerymen are mostly raw recruits, who, if unsupported, 
could scarcely act with effect in case of need, I bez to urge upon your Lordship 
the absolute necessity of sending here with the least possible delay an adequate 
foree of Regular Cavalry, and which might be embarked at once on board of the 
transports “ Niagara,” “ Chalmers,” or “ Medway,” proceeding to-day to 
Constantinople. 

In case this suggestion be adopted, as I trust it will, it might be desirable 
that orders be given for these vessels to call on their way down at Lampsacus, 
where I would have a person waiting to inform the commander of the force 
whether he should disembark his troops there, or at any other point, according 

ns circumstances may require. 

I am informed that the Moudir of Eginé hos succeeded in capturing eight of 
the deserters, whom he is sending here. In order to provide against any 
attempt at releasing them, T have induced the Pasha to have them confined in 
the fortress of Kilid Bahar, on the Europe side of the Straits. 


L have, &e. 
(Signed) I, W. CALVERT. 


P.S.—The Pasha has this moment informed me, that the fact of the Moudir 
of Eviné having captured cight of the deserters has not been confirmed.

No. 72. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 23.) 


(No. 510.) 
My Lord, Therapia, July 12, 1855. 

THE extreme importance of obtaining correct data before the expedition 
proposed for the relief of the Turkish army ot Kars be finally submitted to Her 
Majesty's Government, produces an unavoidable delay in the progress and pre- 

aration of the plan, The Turkish Government has decided on sanding 
confidential officers to examine the localities at Trebizond, Batoom, and Redoute- 
Kaleh, with a view to forming a more correct idea of their resources and 
difficulties. 

I hope that General Vivian and Sir Edmund Lyons will pursue the same 
course, and that an officer from each service will be sent in a suitable vessel to 
obtain the requisite information on the coast. 1 have already applied to General 
Vivian and Rear-Admiral Grey for that purpose; and I shall avail myself of the 
earliest opportunity to make a similar application to Admiral Sir Edmund 
Lyons. 
y L abstain from troubling your Lordship with particulars, until T can submit 
the complete result of the present inquiries to Her Majesty's Goyernment. The 
ceneral nature and object of the plan entertained by the Porte, and promoted as 
a matter of eventual execution by Her Majesty's Embassy, has been explained 
to your Lordship in my preceding despatches. 

T have, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

Inclosure in No. 73. 


Consul-General Colquhoun to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Bucharest, June 30, 1855. 


ALL PASHA arrived at Roustchouk on the afternoon of the 19th, and 
was received with every possible honour, | paid my respects to His Highness 
on the morning of the 20th at 8 o'clock, He asked me about the state of 
public feeling in the province; and I did not hesitate to repeat to him the many 
causes of complaint which the ‘nhabitants of Wallachia have against the 
Hospodar and his administration, Ee appeared well au courant of the state of 
affairs, and said that if the Porte had not hitherto acted as she had intended 
towards the Prince, it had been owing to the peculiar circumstances of the 
moment before which she had thought it right to bow, 

Prince Stirbey came over the same morning, and had an interview with 
the Vizier. Nothing has transpired of what passed ; but the Prince loft imme- 
diately for Bucharest. Understanding that a deputation of the Boyars intended 
to cross the Roustchouk, with a view of submitting their sufferings to the 
Vizier, the Hospodar issued strict orders that no one should pass the Danube 
without a passport. Information of this having been given to Ali Pasha, he 
sent over a boat to Giurgeyo, with orders to the Boyars ta come to Roustchouk, 


71 
and accaecnely EBs Alexander Ghika, ex- Hospodar, arrived, accompanied by 
Messrs. Balliano, — Jalatchano, A. Ghika, and Count Rosetti: they were sub- 
sequently admitted to an audience, and submitted to the Vizier their doleances. 
He received them very graciously, but with a certain reserve. The Vizier lett 
Roustchouk at 10 A.m. for Shumla and Varna. 

While 1 was at Roustchouk, Colonel Neale arrived with Major Havelock, 
to remove some obstacles which the old Pasha Said had thrown in the way of 
collecting men for the Turkish sontingent 


have, &c. 
(Signed) ROBERT G. COLQUHOUN.

My Lord, Berlin, July 21, 1855, 

| HAVE the honour to transmit to your Lordship herewith, in translation, 
copy of the reply of the Prussian Government, dated the Sth instant, to the 
proposal of the Austrian Cabinet, dated 28th ultimo, with respect to a com- 
munication to be addressed to the Diet on the Oriental question. 

I am informed that a further correspondence has since taken place between 
the Austrian and Prussian Cabinets, in which the former proposed some modi- 
Rentions of its previous propositions, in order to meet the views of the Prussian 

abinet. 

These modifications, however, are said to relate more to form than to 
substance ; and inasmuch as they pourtrayed no material change in the views of 
the Austrian Cabinet, they have not obtained the approval of the Prussian 
Government. 

There appears no doubt but that the Prussian Government will firmly 
adhere to the views and intentions expressed in their answer, herewith inclosed, 
of the Sth instant. 

In conversation yeaterday with the Under-Secretary of State for Foreign 
Affairs on the subject of these communications, | could only extract from hin 
that there wasa prospect of improved relations between Austria and Prussia, 
and that Austria deserves full credit from Germany for the pacific intentions 
displayed by her in the Vienna Conference. THe further stated, that he believed 
that the modified Austrian communication to the Diet was made on the 19th. 

‘Much secresy 18 now observed here as to what is now passing between 
Berlin and Vienna; but I shall use my best endeavours to procure for your 
sarcenp copies of the communications that have passed within the last few 

ye:

Inclosure in No. 74. 
Baron Manteuuffel to Count Arnim. 


(Translation.) Berlin, July 5, 1855. 
WE have now been fuvoured by Count Esterhazy with the confidential 
communication of the draft of the declaration concerning the Eastern question 
which Baron Prokesch in, the name of his Government, is to be instructed to lay 
before the German Dict. 
_ ‘The Cabinet of Vienna has also had the goodness to acquaint us with the 
cireular in which Count Buol, on the 28th ultimo, informs the German Govern- 
ments of the steps intended to be taken by the Imperial Austrian Court. 
It is scarcely necessary for me to assure your ixcellency that these docu- 
ments have imparted the liveliest interest to His Majesty’s Government, and 
that, in considering them as members of the Diet, we are animated by the most 


72 


friendly feelings towards the Austrian Imperial Cabinet, as well as by the sincere 
desire to give, as fur as possible, a character of unanimity to the declarations 
which the Diet may be called upon to make. 

This being the case, it is with particular satisfaction that I especially notice 
the express declaration made in the circular, that there can be no question of the 
Diect’s entering into new engagements, or of extending those already existing, 
The numerous documents that have lately proceeded from His Majesty’s Govern- 
ment will furnish the most indubitable evidence that this view algo agrees with 
our own, and therefore the only question that could arise would be, whether any 
new declaration whatever on the part of the Diet is necessary in order solely to 
maintain its former resalutions? We should be inclined to answer in the nega- 
tive if we had only to consider our own situation ; a5 Prussia, in her character of 
an European Power, is already, in a military sense, under any circumstances, in 
that state of preparation which corresponds with the resolution of the Diet of the 
Sth February. 

If, however (although the hope for the restoration of peace hetween the 
belligerent Powers has not yet been fulfilled), we may be allowed to suppose, 
from the late turn of affairs, according to the communications made to us, that 
the active cooperation of Austria in the war is removed toa remote period, and that 
thereby also, as regards Germany, apprehensions on this account are considerably 
diminished, then we cannot consider otherwise than justifiable the wishes of our 
German allies to sce themselves relieved ina suitable manner, as, for example, 
by a prolongation of the © Kriegsbereitschaft ” from the engagements imposed 
upon them by the resolution of the Sth of February. | 

The consideration of this matter in the Committees will furnish the best 
opportunity of coming to the necessary understanding upon this point, and it 
will be the more requisite to lay the Austrian declaration before the Committee, 
as the purport of the same has not been communicated to the other German 
Courts, andl a thorough and general examination of such a comprehensive 
exposition appears (and assuredly the Austrian Cabinet is of the same opinion) 
indispensably necessary, . 

The position of Her Majesty's Government with regard to the December 
‘Treaty, and the negotiations conneeted with it, is known to all. Prussia did 
not join in this Treaty, notwithstanding the invitations addressed to it. An 
express approval both of the eonelusion of this Treaty, as well as of the 
deliberations in the Conferences connected with it, would therefore only place us 
in direct contradiction with our past policy. 

Weare in advance convinced that this cannot be the intention of the 
Cabinet of Vienna. 

If we willingly acknowledge that the latter, after it had concluded the 
December Treaty without consulting thereupon with us and with Germany, and 
after it had, upon the basis of this alliance, discussed also with non-German 
Powers questions at Vienna that concerned the interests of Germany; if we 
admit more especially her recent attitude in order to avert the danger of war 
from Austria, and thereby also from Germany, has done its utmost to protect 
the interests of Germany ; nevertheless an unreserved approval of all the circum- 
stances of the alliance (which, notwithstanding the momentary divergence upon 
a question of exceution, is expressly stated to continue in force as to its object, 
and therefore with its eventual obligations can be made available again in the 
most pressing forms in the future course of the development of the war), would 
Imply a jot and common oblization of mutual support which Prussia considers, 
now us hitherto, incompatible with her political convictions, and with her 
solicitude for her own and Germany's real interests, and therefore must withhold 
its approval just as frankly and freely naw as she did immediately after the 
conclusion of the December Treaty, 

Such an approval could (in order to avoid misunderstandings, which would 
be doubly unpleasant now, when it is mecessary above all things to set the 
position of the Diet in a clear light) only ensue if and in as fur as Austria, on 
the other hand, also expressly approved the policy which Prussia nod Germany, 
from their point of view, consistently pursued; but even such a supplementary 
mutual approval might, by a recapitulation of the faets, perhaps cause the diver- 
gences of the past to appear in a manner not to be desired, and we would therefore 
submit to the Imperial Cubinet whether it would not be preferable on both sides to 
renounce the same in the interest of the union sought to be attained, We are 


7d 


the more confirmed in this consideration by the declaration contained in the 
reel draft of her desire to maintain irrevocably the basis of peace as 
ae snowledged by Austria. Inasmuch as the carrying out of the afore-mentioned 
se points as an inseparable whole is hereby intended to be conveyed, it would 
ar exceed the limits of the resolution of the Diet of 9th of October last. No

bess the two points. Since Russia has declared that she will also hence- 
ri consider the same as binding upon her so long as Germany and Austria

My Lord, Munich, July 18, 1890. (No, 978. Confidential.) 

SINCE my despatch No. 47 of yesterdays date was written, I have learnt My Lord, Paris, July 23, 1855. 
that the Bavarian Government has availed itself of the pretext afforded by the | COUNT WALEWSKL has received farther adviees from Stockholm of 
Cabinets, that they no longer consider the 16th instant, ; 
M, de Lobstein reports, that having received his instructions anulogons To 


declarations of the British and French 
themselves irrevocubly bound by the four 


points of guarantee, to decline 


76 


those on which Mr. Magenis had acted in the matter of the proposed Treaty 
respecting Finmark, he had at once made overtures, in the sense of them, to the 
Swedish Minister for Foreign Affairs, and subsequently to the King; that the 
principle of such a Treaty had been accepted by both His Majesty and his 
Minister, but that both had declared that the Treaty must have greater extension 
than had been given it by the allies, and must include, in the defensive alliance 
which it proposed, not only Finmark, but the whole of the dominions of His 
Swedish Majesty. The King had more particularly dwelt with M. de Lobstein 
on the necessity of His Majesty’s showing that he had no predilections for one 
part of his subjects over the other; though in his heart he confessed that the 
Swedes were more congenial to him than the Norwegians; that they had come 
forward lately with far greater alacrity to meet the burdens imposed upon them 
hy present circumstances, and it was consequently more necessary to show that, 
Gothland and Lanskrona were as safe as Finmark, 

The King appears to have dwelt on the great changes which such a Treaty 
must make in his future policy, admitting that until now he had been accus- 
tomed to lean on Russia, while for the future he must look to the Western 
Powers for protection, He did not conceal from himself that he must be pre- 

red for the future hostility of Russia, as he was about to form a great 
Scandinavian barrier against her. 

Count Walewski is inclined to consider the extension which it is proposed 
by Sweden to give to the original project to be an improvement,

No. 80. 
Lord Cowley to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 24.) 


(No. 979.) 


My Lord. Paris, July 23, 1855. 

M. DE PERSIGNY will no doubt have put your Lordship in possession 
of the tenour of M. de Bourqueney’s late despatches from Vienna, and of the 
course Which the French Government proposes to pursue in consequence, 

A few days ago Count Walewski stated to me that Count Buol was much 
disappointed by the tone of the French Government in regard to the Treaty of 
the 2nd December, for it seemed, that while willing to admit its continued 
validity, they resolved to consider its stipulations as no longer obligatory. In 
reporting this language of Coupt Buol, M. de Bourqueney added that he could 
not conceal from himself that the alliance between Austria and the Western 
Powers had assumed a very critical appearance, and that unless something was 
done to reassure Count Buol that the four points were still considered binding 
by France and England, that Minister might become so alarmed at finding 
Austria deserted by them, and consequently in a state of isolation, that great 
danger would arise of seeing the Emperor Francis Joseph endeayour to come to 
a separate understanding with Russia on the basis of the four points, 

M. de Bourqueney recommended, therefore, that satisfactory assurances 
should be given to Count Buol, beg confident that very little in this sense 
would suffice, and that that little need only be verbally expressed, 

On receiving this despatch Count Walewski took the Emperor's orders, and 
it was determined to invite Her Majesty's Government to calm the appre- 
hensions of Count Buol, by joining France in a declaration that, in any future 
negotiations for peace, the two Governments would regard the four points 
as the minimum of what they would ask, 

Yesterday Count Walewski informed me that he had, on the previous day, 
transmitted to M. de Persigny a draft of the instructions which he intended 
sending on the following day (that is, yesterday) to M. de Bourqueney, with an 
intimation of his hope that similar instructions might be addressed by your 
Lordship to Mr, Elliot, Your Lordship will have been enabled to form your 
own judgment upon it, and | need not refer to it further, except to say that J 
observed to Count Walewski that if he was anxious that the two Governments 
should hold the same language at Vienna, he ought in courtesy to defer writing 


i 


to M. de Bourqueney until Her Majesty's Ministers had had time to consider 
the terms of the instructions which he proposed sending. Count Walewski 
replied that he would not forward the despatch to M. de Bourqueney until he 
had heard from London. 

He went on to state that since he had seen me he had received further 
advices from Vienna; that Count Buol was about to send /a messenger to Parts 
with instructions to M. de Hiibner to inquire whether, in future negotiations for 
peace, the Western Powers would take the four points as © leur point de 
départ,” and whether they would enter into a reciprocal engagement with 
Austria not to {reat eepersty with Russia. Count Buol had again assured 
M. de Bourqueney that he did not seek for any further written engagement. 
He admitted that the moment was not propitious for the siemature of official 
documents, he would be satisfied with a verbal assurance ; but he gave it clearly 
to be understood that no argument would be left to him to oppose the progress 
of the Russian party with the Emperor Francis Joseph, if something was not 
done to make His Majesty comprehend that the maritime] owers still considered 
him as bound by certain engagements to them. Already Prince Gortchakoff 
had, during an audience of His Imperial Majesty, endeavoured to obtain @ 
promise that Austria would not attack Russia, if Russia engaged neither to 
invade the Principalities nor to cross the Danube, The Emperor had pleaded 
his engagements with the Western Powers as a bar to any such promise, but if 
it became apparent that the Western Powers conecived themselves to be free 
from all obligations tawards Austria, how could the Emperor continue to plead 
engagements which were thus repudiated ¢ | 

*T asked Count Walewski whether, as he expected to receive so shortly a 
communication from the Austrian Government, it would not be as well to defer 
writing to M, de Bourqueney on this subject; but his Excellency observed that 
it might be more embarrassing to answer @ categorical demand, such as that 
which it appeared to be Count Buol’s intention to address. to the French 
Government through M. de Hiibner, than to give the somewhat vague assurances 
with which M. de Bourqueney had declared that Count Buol would be satisfied, 
and that for his part he should be glad to be enabled to state to M. de Hiibner, 
when the latter presented himself, that instructions were already on the road to 

. vweney. ‘ 

x ee is add, that the same dread of seving Austria draw nearer to 
Russia, and the same hope of preventing it by equivocal assurances, appear to 
exist in Count Walewski’s mind, as in that of his predecriers For instance, his 
Excellency stated to me yesterday, that by employing the word “ envisager, 
when speaking of the four points as the minimum to be asked, he incurred no 
obligation, and that hereafter France might insist on the four points, or abandon 
them, as she pleased. 

In conversing on this matter my language has been guided by the 
tenour of your Lordship's despatch No, 15 of the 13th instant to Mr. Elhot. 
LT may add, ‘in conclusion, that at bottom I do not conceive that there is much 
difference of opinion between your Lordship and Count Walewski as to what 
will happen hereafter in regard to the four guarantees. He is quite aware that 
they must come prominently forward in any future negotiation; but there is 
this difference of opinion in regard to entertaining them, that he had certainly 
understood that when the negotiations were broken off at Vienna, the two 
Governments had resumed entire liberty of action, either to Hols Oy or reject 
them, as the future interests of the two countries might dictate; a with this 


impression on his mind, he rather took objection to the latter part of the second 
paragraph of your Lordship’s despatch to Mr. Elliot, as admitting, In Be 
opinion, on the part of Her Majesty's Government, engagements from which the 
earch Government considered themselves absolved. At the same time I must 
state, that nothing has fallen from him which gives me the right to suppose that 
he thinks the two Governments ought to be satisfied with less than that which 
they have already asked.

No. 81. 
Lord Cowley to the Earl of Clarendon. —(Received July 26.) 


(No. 985.) 
My Lord, Paris, July 25, 1855. 

COUNT WALEWSEL read to me yesterday the further correspondence 
which had passed between M.de Persigny and himself, respeeting the declaration 
which he wished Her Majesty’s Government to joi him in making to Austria, 
He said that your Lordship desired that some definition should be given of the 
principles of the four guarantees, which Count Walewski proposed stating to 
Count Buol would be the minimum of the demands made by the belligerent 
Powers in any future negotiations for peace. 

Count Walewski was inclined to think that any such definition was useless, 
and perhaps calculated to engender further discussion between Austria and the 
maritime Powers. At all events, he said, he could not accept the definition as 
submitted by your Lordship to M. de Persigny, because he conceived that two 
essential parts of the four guarantees had been omitted, namely, “ the con- 
necting the Ottoman Empire with the European equilibrium” in the third, an 
(‘the abandonment by Russia of covering with an official protectorate the 
Christian subjects of the Sultan of the Oriental ritual” in the fourth, — 

T asked Count Walewski whether, im case your Lordship continued to 
think some definition necessary, he would be satisfied if you took the words of 
the four guarantees, as inserted in the Protocol of the 22th December. His 
Excellency replied, that he should prefer a simple assurance to Count Buol that 
France and England would consider the principle of the four guarantees as the 
ininiinum on which peace could be made, but that he would not object to the 
repetition of the text of the Protocol of the 28th December, in their develop- 
ment, if your Lordship pressed it. 

1 have, &c. 
(Signed) COWLEY.

(No. 48, Confidential.) | 
My Lord, Vienna, July 26, 1855. 

HAVING learnt that, in the interval between the audience at which 
Prince Gortchakoff delivered his credentials to the Emperor, and his departure 
for Stuttgardt, he had again heen received by His Majesty, | have endeavoured 
to ascertain the cause of this somewhat unusual occurrence, and am now 
enabled to communicate it confidentially to your Lordship. ; 

Immediately after his first audience, it became known that Prince Gortcha- 
koff had very generally expressed his satisfaction at the language of Lis Imperial 
Majesty, and had hinted that Count Buol could not be taken as a faithful 
exponent of the sentiments of His Imperial Master. a 

On this report reaching Count Buol, he considered it essential that it 
should receive a formal contradiction, and that the Russian Minister should be 
informed by the Emperor himself that, if in the previous interview anything 
which fell from him had been understood in w sense at variance with the 
language of his Minister, his meaning had been entirely misconstrued. 

At Count Buol's suggestion, therefore, the second audience Look place ; 
and while all possible misconception on the part of Prince Gortchakolf was 
removed, this renewed attempt to establish the existence of a discrepancy 
between the sentiments of His Majesty and those of his Minister tor Foreign 
Affairs, ended in the disappointment of the purty who consider Count Buol as 
the ehief obstacle to a line of policy entirely fuvourable to Russia. 

On the day of Prince Gortchakoff’s first audience, the Emperor also 
received Baron Manderstram, the new Swedish Minister, who informs me conti- 
dentially that the language of His Imperial Majesty on that occasion was 


79 


anything but favourable to Russia; and that while he made no concealment of 
his regret that the proposed ultimatum of Austria should not have met the 
views of the allied Governments, no leaning whatever towards the other side 
could be detected,

incompatible with the other engagements he had taken,

My Lord, . Vienna, July 26, 1855. 

COUNT BUOL a few days ago sent off a messenger to London and Paris 
with despatches to Count Colloredo and Baron Hubner, relative to his proposal 
that the English and French Governments should declare their continued adhe- 
sion to the four points as the basis of future negotiations. | 

As Count Buol’s despatch to Count Colloredo is to be communicated to 
your Lordship, it is only necessary for me to state that I understand from his 
Excellency that he has expressed himself willing to be satistied with the general 
declaration of Her Majesty’s Government that the questions involved in the four 
points must come under consideration, and that in any future negotiations they 
will bear in mind the principles which animated them in the last. 

There is only one point, Count Buol said, on which he required any 
further explanation, and respecting which it was necessary there should be no 
misunderstanding, and that he had therefore instrueted Count Colloredo to 
ascertain whether, in the event of Austria continuing pledged not to enter into 
any arrangement with Russia without consultation with the Governments of 
England and France, those Governments admitted thet they would be equally 
bound not to enter into negotiations without any communication with Austria,

Inclosure 1 in No. 86. 
Lord Stratford de Redelijfe to Lieutenant-General Beatson. 


Sir, Therapia, July 13, 1855. 

1 HAVE received with much concern the account of the desertiuns which 

have recently taken place from your corps, and ] have informed Mr. Calvert, 
who will show you my despatch, of the measures adopted in concert with the 
Turkish Government for your assistance and support. 
_ I trust that, by a firm and judicious course of measures, you will succeed 
in applying an effectual remedy to the spirit of insubordination and violence 
which has declared itself with so little apparent provocation among a part of 
the Albanian troops under your command. 

There is room for apprehending that sufficient attention has not been given 
to the characters of the men enrolled, and the late intolerable outbreak may 
afford a just opportunity for clearing the Cavalry of its worst ingredients, without 
resorting to the hazardous experiment of unmerited concessions.

Sir, Therayia, July 18, 1855. 

_ HAVE received your several despatches dated the 8th, 10th, and 11th 
instant, respecting the desertions from Beatson’s Horse and its deplorable 
consequences, 

At the same time that I lament so untoward an occurrence, and wait.for a 
further explanation of the causes, 1 have applied with success for everything 
calculated in my opinion, as well as in yours, to meet the emergency, and I have 
much satisfaction in upproving your conduct under such trying circumstances, 

Vizirial orders have been sent to the authorities in the several districts 
where the deserters are likely to be found. All are to be arrested, and those 
who resist to be shot. 

Several companies of Infantry will also be sent down immediately, with 
two or three troops of Cavalry, who are to follow, 

I applied for a score of artillerymen, and an officer of firmness and 
prudence to put himself in communication with the Pashas of the Dardanelles 
and General Beatson, with the view of concerting the most effective measures, 

Admiral Grey has directed a vessel of war to stay at present in the 
Dardanelles for the protection of Her Majesty's subjects, and at my request has 
appointed two vessels for the conveyance, if necessary, of the Turkish 
detachineny ‘a their destination, 

Those detachments will, however, in all probability, F the 
Dardanelles in Turkish vessels, . petri es Heise ate 

I observe with lively satisfaction the improvement which you announce in 
your despatch of the L1th instant; but as 1 conceive that it will be necessary to 
deal resolutely with the deserters and to take this opportunity of purging the

——— 


Inclosure in No. 87. 
Memorandum. 


Memorandum of a visit to the Seraskier Pasha made by Brigadier-General 
Mansfield at the desire of Her Majesty’s Ambassador. 


IN obedience to my instructions, I yesterday visited the Seraskier Pasha, 
accompanied by Mr. Doria. ‘The information elicited is as follows :— c 
The latest advices from Kars and Erzeroom received by the Turkish authorities 
being of the same date as the despatches of Brigadier-General Willams and the 
other officers and Consuls, and generally of a more favourable tendency, 500 
men had thrown themselves on the side of Batoom. ‘Till now the Seraskier had 
heen alarmed for the garrison on the score of force, but he had received lists 
showing that there are three months’ provisions in the place, viz, Kars. 
The enemy's horsemen swept all the country round. Erzeroom may be said to 
be threatened, a Russian Division having advanced one march in its direction, — 
The people of Erzeroom were much alarmed, and had appealed to Hafiz 
Pasha at Trebizond to come to their assistance. ‘That officer had started with 
20 guns and 500 artillerymen. The country in the neighbourhood of Trebizond 
had been raised. Ten thousand armed men by this were on the road to Erzeroom. 
Five hundred gunners collected in the Dardanelles were also to be sent 
forward without delay when landed. They are off Trebizond, now having 
sailed two days since in a steamer, Tussoum Pasha is actively engaged in 
raising men at Sivas, not for Erzeroom, but to guard the country Mm Tear of that 
lace, should the enemy advance upon it, ‘These irregular troops would also do 
good service if paid regularly. ‘The Seraskier Pasha informed his colleagues in 
Council the day before yesterday that if money were not provided for the war 
he would resign. 
The Seraskier Pasha does not believe there is ground for alarm regarding 
Batoom, It would be a great mistake for the Russians to detach largely from 
their proper line of operations. Mourauielf’ is far too clever, in his opinion, to 
make such a blunder. . 
There is plenty of ammunition in Erzeroom. ‘The country round 18 most 
fertile; grain is being stored; the population capable of bearing arms and 
contributing to the defence is 8,000, ‘The works are very extensive and require 
a large army for their proper defence; they are without regular troops, except a 
few artillerymen. ’ 
Kverything is forward, and being made ready for the contemplated expedi- 
tion to Redoute-Kalé; answers have heen received from Ismail Pasha, who 


83 


reports that the 15,000 men to be drafted from Bulgaria are ready to march with the 
baggage-animals, &c., &e., a5 directed, when the order is received. As stated in 
the previous conversations, 10,000 of these are for the contingent of General 
Vivian. What is now wanted is the order to give effect to the expedition. 

Three hundred picked Infantry soldiers, under a very experienced officer, 
have been sent to the Dardanelles. ; 

The artillerymen there are sufficient. It is proposed to send some Cavalry 
soldiers to meet the emergency. There are none available, except a regiment 
arriving from Volos in detachments for the Contingent. 

(Signed) W. R. MANSFIELD.

No. 88. 


The Earl of Clarendon to Mr. Elliot. 
(No. 30.) : 
Sir, Foreign Office, July 31, 1855. 

COUNT COLLOREDO has read to me a despatch from Count Buol, 
stating that in compliance with Article I of the Treaty of December 2, he was 
bound to make known to Her Majesty's Government a communication which 
had been received from Russia. 

His Excellency then informs Count Colloredo that Prince Gortchakoff had 
availed himself of an audience to deliver his credentials, to inform the Emperor 
of Austria that Russia was not disposed to extend her operations on the Danube, 
and that she would engage herself not to do s0 if Austria would give similar 
assurances on the part of the allies. 

The Emperor of Austria replied, that the Treaty he had made with 
England and France prevented him from entering into any engagement of the 
kin pope by the Russian Minister. 

count Colloredo likewise communicated to me the substance of a despatch 
to Count Buol from Count Esterhazy, who, after complaining of the reserve of 
Count Nesselrode, says, that he had discussed with Baron Brunnow the relations 
between Austria and Russia, and had distinctly told him that the Treaty of 
December 2 would be maintained by Austria. 

Baron Brunnow appears to have criticized Count Buol’s despatch, which 
he said had been received with dissatisfaction by the Russian Government.

(Signed) CLARENDON.

No. 89. 


The Earl of Clarendon to Mr. Eliot. 


(No. dl.) 
Sir, Foreign Office, July 31, 1895. 

COUNT COLLOREDO has read to me the despateh of Count Nessel- 
rode to Prince Gortchakoff, in reply to the despatch of Count Buol to Count 
Esterhazy, written shortly after the rupture of negotiations at Vienna. 

Count Nesselrode professes to be well satisfied with the explanations 
volunteered by Austria, and declares that there is no fear of disagreement 
between the two Powers respecting the Principalities, and that thei perfect 
understanding will keep the centre of Kurope sale, in spite of the bitter ani 
uncompromising hostility of the Western Powers. Count Nesselrode regrets 
the rupture of the negotiations ; he agrees with Count Buol in throwing upon 
the Western Powers the responsibility of their having been brought to a close, 
and considers that any fresh pacific offers on the part of Russia would now fail, 
in the same way that those mage by Austria have failed. 

Russia does not ask for arv engagement from Austria with respect to the 
Russian propositions which are the four bases as explained by Prince Gortchakoft, 
but she considers that the two first are gained to the cause of peace, and will 
so remain us long as Austria continues neutral. On the third point Russia will 


> 
Pe =5 


B4 


keep herself free until the Western Powers exhibit a real desire for peace. As 
regards the fourth point Russia will reckon upon the assistance of Austria for 
the settlement of a question which presents so many difficulties that England 
and France, rather than discuss it, preferred to break off the negotiations on the 
third point, but Russia must postpone, as Austria does, all further discussion, 
while England and France persist in their hostility to Russia. 

Count Buol intimates that he does not accept Count Nesselrode’s interpre- 
tation of his despatch to Count Esterhazy. 

Tam, &c. 
(Signed) CLARENDON.
