No, 91. 
Lord Cowiey to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received July 31.) 


(No. 1,007.) 
My Lord, Paris, July 30, 1855, 
AS Count Colloredo will have made to your Lordship the same commu- 
nication which M. de Hiibner made on Friday last to Count Walewski, in order 
ta obtain some more formal assurance on the part of the belligerent Powers that 
they still adhere to the Treaty of December 2, and that they will not enter 
into any separate negotiations with Russia, it is only necessary for me to record 
the reply which Count Walewski informed me he made to the Austrian Envoy. 
Tn answer to the first question, he told M. de Hiibner that a despatch had 
already been sent off to M, de Bourqueney, which he trusted would satisfy the 
Austrian Government. In answer to the second, he said that he could state 
nothing officially, without previous communication with Her Mujesty's Govern- 
ment, but that if seemed to him that if the Treaty of December 2 was not to be 
deemed a dead letter, it would follow that no negotiations could take place with 
Russia without a previous concert between the three Powers who had signed it. 
I observed to Count Walewski that it might be well to take this oppor- 
tunity of coming to un understanding with the Austrian Government as to the 
value of the word “concerter.”’ It seemed to me that although Austria took 
eredit to herself for having strictly adhered to her engagements on this point, 
it was very doubtful whether her last communications to the Russian Govern- 
ment were not a direct violation of them. 


85 


Count Walewski replied that such was also his opinion. 
Mis Excellency said further that he should be glad to know what your 
Lordship proposed to say to Count Buol. 
I have, &c. 


(Signed) COWLEY.

No. 92. 
Lord Cowley to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received August 1.) 


(No, 1,024. Confidential.) 
My Lord, Paris, July 31, 1855. 

IN the course of my late correspondence with your Lordship, I have had 
occasion to mention Count Walewski’s conviction that the King of Denmark 
was well disposed towards the Western Powers, and that His Majesty would 
willingly follow any example of hostility to Russia set by the King of Sweden. 

Count Walewski has now given me, in confidence, his reason for entertaining 
this conviction. It appears that a short time ago the King of Denmark, having 
learnt that the Emperor was making a collection of portraits, took the oppor- 
tunity of sending by a confidential agent to His Majesty the picture of himself, 
This gentleman addressed himself to Count Walewski, and told him that he was 
commissioned! by the King to express His Majesty's sympathy for the cause of 
the Western Powers, and his own readiness to join the King of Sweden in any 
measures that might be determined on to support that cause. The agent added 
that his mission was a secret to the Danish Government: that the King of 
Denmark hoped that his Ministers would not oppose his policy, but that if they 
did, he was prepared to change them. ‘The King, he added, was much vexed 
by the intrigues of the Duke of Glucksberg, who was entirely in the interests 
of Russia. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) COWLEY.

No. 94. 


Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received August 1.) 
(No. 525.) 
My Lord, Therapia, July 19, 1855, 

THE night before last 1 was greatly surprised to learn that Omer Pasha 
had arrived suddenly from the Crimea, [ learnt from Colonel Simmons, who 
accompanied him, that his Highness had intended to eall upon me before he 
passed down the Bosphorus; but, as I happened to be absent, he changed his 
plan, and went straight to the Seraskier, which even otherwise would have been 
the most advisable course to pursue. ; 

I heard at the same time from Lieutenant-General Simpson, who was good 
enough to send me an account of what had passed im Conference and corre- 
spondence between Omer Pasha and the other Commanders-in-chief before the 
departure of the former. 3 wens | | 

The correspondence to which I allude is inclosed herewith, in copy, for 
your Lordship’s information. Rae kee =e 

It appears that Omer Pasha, apprised of the danger to which the army and 
fortress of Kara were exposed, and also of the plans for reheving them which 
were under consideration here, had proposed to withdraw from the Crimea the 
25.000 ‘Turkish traops before Sebastopol, and to put himself at their head, in 
order to operate a diversion from the coast of Circassia, and not finding among 
his colleagues, the other Commanders-in-chief, a disposition to support him, 
announced his intention of proceeding at once to Constantinople, with the view 
of submitting his opinions to the Government there. me 

This impulsive resolution is by no means in keeping with the decided 
opposition offered by Omer Pasha to the late Seraskier's requisition for detaching 
5,000 of his men from the Crimea. His Highness may account for the change 
of view by referring to the pressure at Kars, and to the suspension of active 
operations near Sebastopol. But the Generals, his colleagues, deprecate the 
latter ground of justification, and means might upparently be employed for the 
rescue of Kars without deranging the caleulations of the allied armies in the 
Crimea, a a a : 

Your Lordship is already acquainted with the plan which has been for some 
time under consideration here on the part of the Sultan’s Government, and in 
concert with Her Majesty’s Embassy. An appeal has, moreover, been made by 


means of the electric telegraph to Her Majesty’s Government, who were 


entreated to lose no time in making known its pleasure as to the proposed 
diversion, supposing it to obtain the suffmages of the naval aud military autho- 
rities in point of means for carrying it into effect. he answer mity be expected 
from day to day, and a final decision may then be taken with the advice ancl 
concurrence of the Commanders-in-chief. It would have heen idle to make any 
earlier communication to them of an official description. — Phe state of the army 
at Kars was made known to the Uritish Commander-in-chief by General Williams 
CE eae from M. Pisani, who called by my desire yesterday upon the Grand 
Vizier, the Seraskier and Fuad Pasha, that the Generalissimo s arrival without 
the orders of his Government had created some feelings of dissatisfaction, and 
that he had explained his conduct by referring to. the perilous nature of the 
emergency, ne the inutility, as he thought, of his presence near Sebastopol 
under present circumstances, Ho has seen the Sultan, but nothing has. yet 
transpired as to the reception which he experienced from His Majesty, , 

On the whole, I am under a strong impression: that Omer Pasha lias 
committed an act of imprudenee in coming here alike without leave and without 
the approval of his colleagues; that his idea ot detaching a considerable portion 
of his army from the Crimea will be no more accepted here than it was 
sanctioned in the Crimea; and, although some advantage may be derived from 
personal communication between him and his Government, that he will best 
consult the interests of his Sovereign and of the alliance, by returning, without 
unnecessary delay, to the commiand of his forces in the Crimea, 

l have, &e. peer eee 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIPPFE.

Inclosure 1] in No, 94, 
General Simpson to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Before Sebastopol, July 16, 1855, 

I HAVE the honour to lay before your Excellency copies of a corre- 
spondence that has taken piace between Omer Pasha, General Pelissier, and 
myself, relative to.a proposal made by the former to withdraw a certain portion 
of his troops from the Crimea, and have them transported into Asia, 

At a conference which was assembled at the French head-quarters on 
Saturday the 14th, at the request of Omer Pasha, and at which he, General 
Pelissier, General La Marmora, Vice-Admiral Sir FE. Lyons, Vice-Admiral Bruat, 
Rear-:Admiral Stewart, and myself, were present, his Highness set forth his 
reasons and views on the subject in question; he also produced a very large 
sheet of paper, which conteined, as he affirmed, the instructions of his Govern- 
ment; but on beme asked for a translation of which, he said that it would take 
a fortnight to make one. 

The arguments used by Omer Pasha were those set forth in the corre- 
spondence, and failed to produce any effect on the minds of the other members 
of the conference, who all, without exception, entertain the strongest objection 
to the withdrawal of any troops from the Crimea at this moment. 

Omer Pasha, having failed in leading us to adopt his views, then 
announced his intention of proceeding to Constantinople to consult with his 
Government; and he starts this day at noon in Her Majesty's ship ‘* Valorous,” 
by which same vessel Licutenant-Colonel Suleau, attached to my Staff, in 
succession to the late Licutenant-Colonel Vico, proceeds with this letter, 
ostensibly for the purpose of restoring his health, 

General Pelissier has also charged him with a missien to the French 
Minister on the same subject. 

L earnestly, therefore, beg your Excellency to use your powerful influence 
with the Porte to cause our opinion to prevail over that of his Highness; for 
ereat public interests are at stake, and serious consequences might result from 
his success. 

I have, &e. 


(Signed) § JAMES SIMPSON.

No. 96, 
Consul-General Colquhoun to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Recetved August 1.) 


(No. 41. Confidential.) 
My Lord, Bucharest, July 11, 1855. 


I HAVE received, of late, frequent visits from several Boyars, as well of 
Wallachia as of Moldavia. 

The subject uppermost in their minds was naturally the Protocol of the 
Vienna Conferences which bore upon the Principalities. . 

These men are of different parties as regards their wish for the ultimate 
system of government: some desiring immediately a foreign Prince, still 
tributary to the Porte ; others, a native to be chosen from the two provinces, 
united under one administration; but all agree on one point—a most positive 
dread of Austria succeeding Russia, as the one natural conterminous influence 
in the country. and in the urgent necessity for the removal of power from the 

nt hands. 

I followed with much attention the various arguments advanced, and 
requested the parties to put their ideas on paper; they accordingly gave me, a 
few days ago, a memorandum, of which I have made copious extracts; there 
appears to me much in it which will repay the perusal. | 

In conversing over the subject, I found the minds of these men very 
uneasy on one question: Did the interruption of the Conferences absolve the 
patties from all engagements to which they had become bound by the earlier 
meetings? For example, was the decision come to regarding the provinces, to 
hold good in any subsequent negotiations? If so, I believe it is intended to 
protest in the strongest manner against such proceeding. ‘The Wallachians 
declare they have not been heard through the medium of men they can rely on; 
while the Russian party was represented not only by all that was most distin- 
guished in their diplomacy, but by every person who has had any transaction 
with the provinces. MM, 'Titoff and Khaltchinsky, and Kotzebue, formerly 
Russian Consuls-General, MM. Fonton, Mavros, and one or two such persons 
as the last-mentioned, who, though not ostensibly put forward, were the most 
active and best informed on all matters of detail of the late system of Govern- 
ment under Russian domination. 

While myself, disavowing much of what is said in the inclosed memo- 
randum, I venture to recommend it confidentially to your Lordship’s attention. 

Ihave not thought it my duty to allude to the second po the naviga-. 
tion of the Danube; but | may simply call to your Lordship’s notice that 
paragraph (6th of Article 11) wherein Russia claims full liberty to establish 
military or other works on certain islands northward of the point where 
St. George’s Channel quits the main arm of the river—a right she never yet 
had, and from which she was debarred by the Adrianople Treaty. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) ROBERT G. COLQUHOUN. 


Inclosure in No. 96. 
Memorandum. 


(Extract.) 

THE political position of the Principalities of the Danube previous to the 
declaration of the present war, was defined by the Treaty of Adrianople, and by 
that of Balta Liman. " ; a , 

By these two political acts the Danubian Provinces were to form an integral 
part of the Ottoman Empire, while enjoying under the Imperial mgis an internal 
and independent administration. | 

The Sublime Porte had guaranteed to them a form of government, voted 
by the inhabitants and corroborated by the Organic Statute; Russia reserved to 
herself a right of control, and in fact she became the protectress of these 
countrics. 


a Pe Ge) - d 
aie el ta Wh a 
: Sats 


wie 


restue o¢ 
ie - 


91 


Such were the rights which the Sublime Porte had granted at the termina 
tion of the wars in which she had been a loser, with, at the same time, a sincere 
desire to benefit the inhabitants of these countries. 

If Russia claimed for herself any of the sovereign rights of the Porte it was 
not in virtue of any Treaty right, but by force, by cunning, and by the intrigues 
which she perpetually fomented in the Provinces. It was only in consequence 
of the events of 1848, that the Sublime Porte granted to the Court of Russia a 
night of joint intervention, and that for only a limited term of seven years, at 
the expiration of which time the Porte was to resume the plenitude of her 
sovereign rights acquired by conquest by the Sultans, and to obtain which they 
had undertaken so many wars, and even the present war, with the material aid 
of the Western Powers and the moral help of Austria. 

What is the position which the Conferences at Vienna would assign to the 
Sublime Porte and to the Principalities at the close of the war, which certainly 
has hitherto, at least for Turkey, proved victorious, since she has successfully 
repulsed her enemies, and in concert with her allies has driven them beyond her 
frontiers ? 

The above is the question which requires to be examined with calmness and 
impartiality, although the Conferences are at the moment broken off. 

Baron Prokesch, representing the Power which had engaged to protect the 
Principalities against the recurrence of any new invasion on the part of Russia, 
submitted to the Conference a memorandum drawn up by Austria, composed of 
seven Articles. This memorandum corroborates in itself the Treaty of Adria- 
nople, that of Balta Liman, and adds other stipulations on the part of the 
Sublime Porte far more onerous, and which positively deprive her of the 
sovereign rights which she exercised over these Provinces before the war. 

Prince Gortchakoff and M. Titoff submitted identical memorandums, but 
elsimning to introduce such reforms into the country as the country should 
wish. 

From these three memorandums the Plenipotentiaries drew up the final 
Protocol, bearing the title of * Development of the First Point,” but his Excel- 
lency Aarif Effendi reserved to the new Turkish Plenipotentiary daily expected, 
full liberty of action respecting the third and following Articles : 


“Article 1. The Danubian Principalities of Wallachia, Moldavia, and Servia, 
shall continue subject to the Sublime Porte in virtue of ancient capitulations 
and Imperial hats, which have fixed and determined the rights and immunities 
which they enjoy.” 


This Article is in strict conformity with the interests of the Sublime Porte 
and of the Principalities, as well as with the sovereign rights of the Sultan and 
the rights of the Principalities granted them by the Sublime Porte, 

* No exclusive protection shall in future be exercised over these Provinces.” 

Russia was not a protecting Power in virtue of any Treaty. Her Plenipo- 
tentiary distinctly asserted so much; but she had arrogated to herself that right 
through abuses and the vicious administration of the Hospodars, who thus 
afforded ground for her to plead, with an appearance of sincerity, the cause of 
the unfortunate inhabitants, and to invoke the intervention of the Sublime 
Porte. 


“ Art. 2. The Sublime Porte, in the plenitude of its suzerain power, shall 
preserve to the said Principalities their independent and national administration, 
and consequently entire freedom of worship, legislation, commerce, and naviga- 
tion.” 


This Article has ever been respected by the Sublime Porte: as proof of 
this, we may bring, that these countries which have been under Turkey for about 
three centuries, still speak their own language, preserve their own religion, their 
national institutions, and if some abuses bad crept into and marred their com- 
mercial liberty, such is to be mainly attributed to the vicious system which 
prevailed throughout the Empire, previously to the introduction of the reform, 
The maintenance of these privileges is due to the magnanimity of the Sultans, to 
that respect which the Mussulman nation has ever shown towards all institutions 
they found existing in the nations they had conquered. Thus she has violated 
not one single privilege of the people she lus vanquished: all are in the enjoy- 
ment of the same privileges they held at the period of the Lower Empire. 


92 


Not so Russia. No sooner had she torn Bessarabia from Moldavia than 
she changed its institutions, introduced the Russian language as the official one, 
banished the Moldavian language from the church ritual, and day by day the 
last traces of Moldavian nationality are disappearing. ‘The same state of things 
obtains in Bueovina under the Austrian domination, 

It is therefore hardly logical to receive the guarantee from these two 
Powers, of privileges which both have equally violated as soon as they obtained 
possession of a portion of territory which formerly belonged to these Prinei- 
palities, Historical facts, on the other hand, speak in favour of the Porte, who 
respected these privileges, not only in these countries, but in those farther 
removed, which could not inspire any jealousy to limitroph Powers, and whose 
inhabitants live in a greater or less degree of independence of the SuZzcrain 
Power, 

“The territory of the said Principalities cannot he subjected to any 
diminution.” 

Surely, from the side of Turkey, no serious fear of any such diminution can 
arise ; her interest would be to increase the territory. 


“Art. 3. The Sublime Porte, considering, in its wisdom, that the 
political position of the three Prineipalities i question is very nearly connected 
with the general interests of Europe, will make arrangements in the most 
friendly spirit with the Contracting Powers, as well for the maintenance of the 
legislation in force in those Provinces, as for the modification to be therein 
made, ‘To this eflvet, the Porte will consult the wishes of the country, and will 
record in a solemn hatti-sherif the whole of the regulations relative to the rights 
and immunities of the said Principulities, Before promulgating it, this Act shall 
he communicated to the Powers, who, on their side, after examining it, will 
assume its guarantee.” 


From this point the Contracting Powers commence @ series of violations of 
the suzerain rights of the Sultan and those of the Principalities. This same 
Protocol, drawn up by Baron Prokesch, in the second Article, obliges the Porte 
euarantce to the country an independent and national administration. 

In this Article, on the contrary, it is stipulated that the legislation, modi- 
lied according to the wishes of the country and of the Sublime Porte, shall, in 
the first instance, bo examined by the Contracting Powers, shall receive their 
suzwestion for modilication, and after that the legal sanction. 

It is no longer then an independent administration, but requires to be 
submitted to the sanction of the Contracting Powers. Up to the present moment 
these Provinces could ofieially, with the consent of the Porte, adopt such form 
of government as they considered fit, Now, on the contrary, all modifications 
must, ina measure, depend on Russia and Austria, The Porte will have to 
heseoch these two Powers in any case,—anarchy must inevitably follow such a 
course, Remark that these modifications have been already beforehand decided 
onat Vienna, as the price at whieh the Hospodars procured their reinstallation. 
One of these Provinces drew up a memorandum on this view, entitled ‘ Défaut 
Wavenir et de stabilité:” it was sent to Constantinople to endeavour to procure 
the sanction of the Suzerain Power. 

France and England are too far off to be constantly intermeddling in the 
in the internal alairs of the Provinces. : 

Previously to this present war, ‘Turkey never submitted to such conditions, 
and if occasionally Russia claimed such a liberty she always chose some inoment 
when Europe was engaged in political troubles ; witness the Treaty of Balta 
Liman. 

Without, however, entering on a examination of the rights and privileges 
eranted by the Sublime Porte to the Provinces from time immemorial by the illus- 
trious Sovereigns who certainly possessed the right acquired by conquest, andwhich 
assured to these countries an independent administration without the interference 
of any Power whatever, should not in courtesy these inhabitants have been 
consulted as to whether they were desirous of being under European guarantee ¢ 
Why should the Porte, so to say, be compelled to make a separate ‘Treaty with 
cach of the Contracting Powers? Is she in the position of a vanquished Power, 
thut Europe should impose on her conditions more onerous than those of the 
Treaty of Adrianople? Is this, perhaps, the price she is to pay for her present 
‘alliance? Or, Piece it is the price she is to pay for the Austrian alliance 


3 


which undertook to expel the Russians, but which having a previous under- 
standing that she was to replace the latter, stipulated for the above conditions? 

The Russians under the walls of Adrianople dared not propose such 
conditions to the Sultan, while Austria having hitherto, throughout the present 
war, played the part of a very doubtful Power, requires, in the face of all 
Europe, guarantees from a Power which has ever respected the privileges of 
every and any people, which she (Austria) has ever violated by sowing intrigue 
and disunion wherever she went; and why should we guarantec the privileges of 
these Principalities and their territory when the whole Ottoman Empire ts under 
the guarantee of all the Powers? Are these Provinces and their territory 
unidentical with the rest of the Empire? No! but another Cracow is. con- 
templated to be partitioned so soon as any political complications shall arise 
between France and England, 

_ If the Ottoman Empire is to form part of the European Powers for the 
maintenance of the European equilibrium, why should Europe impose on her a 
task which can only tend to degrade her in the eyes of her peoples and in the 
eyes of the other Powers; for surely to be called on to guarantee the privileges 
of her own people to Europe, is an acknowledgment that she is capable of 
violating those privileges, or has, at least, had the intention soto do, It strikes 
at the suzerain rights of a people whom we now class amid the European family, 
and that blow falls on the Sovereign. 

If a like principle is to be recognised as part of the European public law, 
why should not, and with more reason, Austria be required to guarantee the 
rights of Hungary and Italy ? and why should not Russia be required to guarantee 
the Constitution granted by the ‘Treaty of Vienna to the Kingdom of Poland? 
But the averred object of Austria is to maintain the Treaty of Adrianople, the 
first Article of that of Balta Liman, to give it even a greater development, and 
to insert. into the public European law, what was in fact only a private affair 
between State and State, the result of unfortunate wars, and which must sooner 
or later have disappeared from the political code of Europe. 


_ “Art. 4. The national armed force organised with the object of main- 
taining the internal security and insuring that of the frontiers, ean be developed 
when necessary according to the resources of the country. The eonditions of a 
defensive system shall be taken into consideration, in the common interests of the 
Sublime Porte, of the Principalities, and of Kurope.” 


The tax-paying population capable of furnishing soldiers amounts, according 
to the recent census, to 600,000 families, Suppose a levy of 10 per cent. to be 
made (an enormous rate not only in peace time, but even in war), an army may 
be raised in the two Principalities of 60,000 men. Such an army is not sufficient 
to defend the country either against Austria or against Russia, in the event of 
either of these Powers invading these provinces, as the frontier of Austria 
extends from Orsova to Mamorintza, and that of Russia from this last point to 
Galatz. For such a line of defence, 200,000 to 300,000 men would hardly 
sulfice; moreover, a force of 10,000 men would be required for the maintenance 
of internal order and the wants of the administration, 

If the Principalities had not as neighbours two Empires such as those of 
Austria and Russia, they might certainly be in a position to defend themselves 
against all and every invasion; but against these two Powers the Principalities 
alone could never cope, not only on account of the insignificant numerical force 
of their population, but from the deficiency of financial means. 

The proposal of the Baron Prokesch forees on the provinces the necessity of 
expending all the revenues of the State in keeping up an army, which in the end 
would prove perfectly useless. | 
___ And what guarantee does the Conference of Vienna propose to give to the 
Sublime Porte, that the army of the provinces shall not unite itself in any special 
case to that of one of the two neighbouring Powers which might for the time 
be at enmity with Turkey? Has not the Porte an example of this in the present 
struggle? Was not the Moldo-Wallachia Militia an auxiliary of Russia ¢ 

What cuarantee can the Conference of Vienna give, that this force shall not 

rise mn revolt on any opportunity against the Suzerain Court—were there not 

exainples of this in 1821 and i848 7 A Wallachian army may be of the utmost 

utility both to Turkey and to the Principalities, 80 long as it be under the 

immediate orders of the Porte, not under those of the Hospodars, whio Tay 
2 6 


94 


betray, as they have already betrayed, their country and the interests of the 
Porte ; examples of this treason are not wanting from the days of Cantimir and 
Brancovano down to those of Stirbey and Ghika. A well-organised system of 
defence is requisite both for the Empire and for the provinces, for their interests 
are one and identical—any blow struck on the one has its echo on the other; 
but then the fortresses should not be garrisoned by Moldo-Wallachians alone, 
but by mixed troops, otherwise the propositions contained in the fourth Article 
would become a perpetual menace to the possessions of the Porte beyond the 
Danube, and a centre of intrigue and conspiracies against Turkey, against which 
she would have no means of defending herself. 


“Art. 5. In the event of doubts arising as to the interpretation of the 
constitutive Hat, the guaranteeing Powers will examine, in concert with the 
Sublime Porte, into the grounds and the object of the representation, and they 
will neglect no means to bring about an arrangement. 

“Art. 6 In the event of the internal tranquillity of the said Prinei- 
palities being compromised, no armed intervention shall take place in their 
territories without being or becoming the subject of agreement between the 
High Contracting Powers.” 


These two Articles are merely corollaries to the preceding ones ; it was with 
the view of giving more strength to them that these latter have been inserted in 
the Austro-Russian memorandum, and more especially with the view of conti- 
nually raising subjects of discussion hetween subjects and their Sovereign. 
Hence the consequence of any political interventions could only be to detach 
these Provinces either to render them independent of the Porte, or to effect 
their partition between the two Powers, History offers many examples of this 
policy in the days of Rome and in our own day : witness Poland, partitioned in 
consequence of the friendly intervention of Austria, Prussia, ant! Russia. 

This sixth Article is so diametrically contrary to the interests of the 
Sublime Porte that but little discussion is required to prove it, When have such 
revolutionary principles ever been advanced by Powers arrogating to themselves 
the term “ Conservative ?” A Province revolts against its lawful Sovereign, 
and that Sovereign must implore his powerful neighbour to permit his 
suppressing such revolt, with the further conditions that the whole of the Powers 
shall cooperate in laying the revolt, each as he interprets such a movement. Is 
this the manner in which the integrity of the Ottoman Empire is to be 
guaranteed, at a period when conspiracies, revolts, visions of nationality, are the 
order of the day, and these too often fostered by the very Powers professing to 
eheck such, when their effects appear to bear upon their own States 

If such principles are to be introduced into the public European law, why 
not apply them to the Austrian Empire, whieh threatens dissolution on the first 
revolutionary symptom breaking out, or to Russia, composed of sixty different 
and distinct nationalities? Such principles are not applied to these two Powers, 
because, inevitably, they would cause their dissolution. But, forsooth, they 
are proposed to be applied to ‘Turkey, in the preconceived determination to 
snatch from her these two rich Provinces. But the Provinces will not consent to 
this: their inhabitants prefer rather to live under the domination of Turkey, than 
under an influence Austro-Russian, ‘They will not, under existing cireum- 
stanees, accept an independent régime, with the example of Greece before their 
eyes,—i prey to continual anarchy and foreign intervention. 


“ Art 7. The Courts engage not to afford protection in the Principalities 
to foreigners whose proceedings might be prejudicial either to the tranquillity of 
those States, or to the interests of neighbouring States. Disapproving such 
proceedings, they engage reciprocally to take into serious consideration the 
representations which may be made on this subject by the Powers, or even the 
locul authorities. On its'side the Sublime Porte will enjoin on the Principalities 
not to tolerate in their territory foreigners such as above described, nor to allow 
the local inhabitants to meddle with matters dangerous to the tranquillity of 
their own country and of foreign States.” 


The avowed object of this Article, masked under circumlocutory phraseology 
on the part of Russia and of Austria, 1s to arrogate to themselves a right of 
ostracism for any political crime they shall invent, and to consecrate hy ‘Treaty 
that which hitherto was non-official respecting political refugees, as in 1849.

But we must not forget that the Austro-Russian demands of that day were 
rejected by the Porte, with the approval of the Western Powers, and in the 
interest of humanity. At that period Austria, almost torn to picees by revolu- 
tions, dared not raise her voice, and certainly, had not the British fleet appeared 
at the Dardanelles, Russia would then have found an excuse for laying hold of a 
material guarantee, 

This Article is entirely useless in a Treaty respecting the provinces, for the 
Porte can regulate all that concerns her internal administration, but she is not to 
apply an external policy other than what is applicable to the rest of the Empire, 
unless she consents to abandon these countries to the exclusive influence of 
Austria and Russia, 

As regards the memorandum of the French Cabinet presented by the Baron 
Bourqueney, and backed by Lord John Russell and the Earl of Westmorland, if 
the Sublime Porte were to take the initiative in such proposal, with some few 
modifications of importance to the interests of Turkey, it would indeed be the 
best adapted to the times, and would offer the surest Guarantee for the integrity 
of the Ottoman Empire. 

The union of the two Principalities, with one internal and independent 
administration without guarantee from any one single Power, without the 
memorandum from MM, Prokesch, Gortchakoff, Titoff; without a foreign 
Prince, for that already argues a certain independence ; but with a Viceroy, 
named by the Sultan, & volonté, responsible for his acts before the Imperial 
Divan; an army bound to combat for the general interests of the Empire; a 
Turkish army in the interior, or on the frontier, as the case may necessitate, and 
the fusion of that Royalty ‘by right appertaining solely to the Sultan) with the 
other parts of the Empire ; surely such a state of things would be far preferable 
to that proposed to the Porte on the other hand. 

Although in reality such a system inereases the value of the autonomy 
of the Principalities, and gives a greater strength to the Empire, its real source 


. 4 


of interest, under present circumstances, consists in maintaining the privileges 
of the Provinees, such as they existed previously to the conclusion of the 
Treaties with Russia. It is also in the true interest of the Principalities, which 
are not yet ripe for the enjoyment of a larger portion of liberty and for an 
extension of political action. 

The object of the present war is to preserve the integrity of the Ottoman 
Empire. continually menaced by Russia, ‘To arrive at this end, it was proposed 
to ane up the road to Russia on the Continent, by the Principalities ; on the 
sen, by the equalization of the fleets of both Empires. The maritime question 
is being decided at this moment, the Continental question has been laid down 
by the Austro-Russia Cabinet in such a manner as shall in no way impede the 
victorious march of Russia; on the contrary, so.as to facilitate and furnish her 
with opportunities for seizing material guarantees ; for the Treaties of Kainardji, 
Bucharest, and Jassy, the Convention of Akermann and Balta Liman, and the 
Treaty of Adrianople, only receive a wider development and ¢reater solemnity 
under the guarantees of the Western Powers. 

These Treaties hitherto were not recognized by the European publie law ; 
they had been extorted from a Powerwhich all Europe was supposed to protect ; 
naturally, therefore, they would have given rise to the present war, under the 
name of ‘Eastern question.” This war was no new one on the diplomatic 
arena—a favourable opportunity was only wanting to take up arms, Thus, at 
the conclusion of the war (had it now been concluded), the Sublime Porte 
instead of gaining weight and influence, not only in Europe, but even in herown 
States, would have found herself deprived of certain rights, of which a victory 
could not have despoiled her; she bas been made mistress of provinees which 
she has no right to repress in case of revolt; she is even refused a right to have 
a proper influence in the country, and is placed in such a state of political 
embarrassment as cannot fail sooner or later of provoking a European war, 

For what then has Europe been now conlescing ? Is it ta keep up a state 
of things worse than existed previously to the war? Is Turkey not perteetly 
justified in saying to Europe, You instigated and urged ime on to war; vou 
prevented me from subscribing to the demands of Prince Menchikoff, which 
would have proved fatal on a future occasion more or less distant, and you 
deliver up my northern provinces to an Austro-Russian policy 2” 

Russia made no difficulty in accepting the first point and guarantee, since

it placed her in a situation far better than that she occupied before the war. 
Her Treaty of Adrianople, under another name, receives a solemn sanction ; her 
Convention of Balta Liman, concluded merely for a limited term, with the 
approval of the Western Powers receives a new and perpetual confirmation. 
She therefore asks nothing farther on the Continent; but she shares that 
influence and those rights with Austria, with France, and with England. The 
policy of Austria before the Revolution of 1848 was most conservative as 
regarded the Ottoman Empire ; from that period Russia has forced her to adopt 
her policy, and it is about to become as menacing as the latter, both interests 
becoming identical ; but her governmental system, the antipathy with which her 
present occupation is looked upon, her present line of policy, her position as a 
first-rate Power, with no means of action, deprive her of all influence. In the 
Conferences she has rendered an eminent service to Russia, as she has also done 
by the occupation of the Provinces; she has defended Russia on her Bessarabian 
frontier; she furnishes her with the means of utilising her army in the Crimea: 
she restores to Russia her former influence in the Provinces, and guarantees to 
her a ‘Treaty far more favourable than the one she held before the war. 

Russia hastens to accept the first point, and she considers it as already 
assured to her, although the Conferences are not yet passed into'a Treaty. (See 
the circular note of M. de Nesselrode, lately addressed to M. Glinka for commu- 
nication to all the German Courts.) She will be able to say to Vrance and 
England on the conclusion of peace, “ You have already ceded this Article to 
me, and I have all Germany to support me, in whose interest | accepted it.” 

As for France and England they are, as said already, too far distant to 
pretend to exercise a real influence on the Principalities. They may possess 
their sympathies, but to support their policy, when having to deal with a Power 
like Russia, who will always drag Austria m her wake, such being the present 
condition of the existence of this latter, the Western Powers require an army 
always ready to support that policy. But what is surprising 1s, that the Cabinets 
of the western and maritime Powers should take into serious consideration the 
proposals of Austria. When, in 1828, Lord Aberdeen, then head of the Foreign 
Office, addressed to Russia a confidential note on the subject of the ‘Treaty of 
Adrianople, in which he exposed the fatal consequences which this Treaty must 
produce, and the position in which it placed Turkey, he then foretold the present 
war, and said @ propos of the Principalities, that Russia WAS SO modelling these 
countries as to prepare for subsequently detaching them from the Ottoman 
Empire. Has the policy of England changed since then? Does the Austrian 
alliance, or the exclusion of the sole guarantee of Russia by the Austro-Russian 
zuarantee, inspire her with greater confidence in her intentions ? The English 
Cabinet should bear in inind that the Austrian monarchy, since the footsteps of 
the Russian soldiery has traversed it, is no longer what it was. It has become a 
Government at the mercy of the Russian policy; if even it would offer any 
resistance it is unable todo so. The young Emperor and his Minister, Count 
Buol, may lean towards the Anglo-Irench policy, but ther soldiery, their nation, 
ure devoted to Russia, Europe persists in remaining under the delusion that 
Austria will aetively support the Western policy; neither her army of 500,000 
men, 80 highly vaunted, is able to cope with Russia, nor 15 it the intention of the 
Austrian Cabinet to take an active part in the war; she is simply occupied im 
supporting and serving the cause of Russia.

No. 7. 
Consul-General Colquhoun to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received August 1.) 


(No. 43.) 
My Lord, Bucharest, July 11, 1855, 

| HAVE been much disgusted at the spirit of satisfaction which has 
shown itself among a certain set (not entirely, as some would have it believed, 
of Grecks, but of Boyars and emyloyés high placed), on the non-success of our 
attempts on the Malukofi and the Redan forts on the 18th ultimo, Last week 
some bene and merchants eame to complain of this; the former that such 


exhibition took place in certain houses, the latter that in the public coffee. 


oF 


houses, indecent expressions of joy were uttered, and the hopes that after 

afew more such reverses, magnified to 18,000 men lost, the invaders would be 

driven out of Russia. I determined to speak to the Prince on the matter; and 

scoORInG ys last Sunday, I went out to Cotrocheni, and communicated what had 
ec, 

I told him, as British Agent, I was the last man here either to put a curb 
on the press, or a stop to free and proper liberty of conversation, but that two 
years ago the police had promulgated such stringent regulations against persons 
speaking of politics, which rules had been put in force by immediate imprison- 
ment and exile, without form of process, of persons of whatever nation, that I 
could not tolerate the present unbridled license which his own subjects were 
indulging in. I declined mentioning names, but told him | certainly would do 
so if the offence were repeated. 

I have just learnt, from the Under-Secretary of State, that orders had 
come from the Prince, that measures should be taken to endeavour to repress, 
at least in places of public resort, such exhibition of feclings as those I have 
alluded to. 

In the course of conversation, the Prince said it was difficult to check con- 
versation. I observed to him, | had at this moment a suit of damages against 
his Government, raised by a Sardinian merchant at Ibraila under British pro- 
tection, for his having been expelled from that town, where he had resided for 
years, on forty-eight hours’ notice, for simply admiring the valour of the Turks 
at Oltenitza. 

The rupture of relations between the local Government and the Prussian 
Consulate-General, was owing to the sending out of the country, without form of 
process, a Prussian subject, for speaking on the battle of Calatat. Two of his own 
young Boyars were at this moment arrested in Bender for similar causes, and many 
other cases I could cite; that I simply required a proper check should be put in 
time on what might degenerate into a serious abuse, and become inconvenient 
to the Government itself, if allowed to pass unnoticed. ‘There are others here 
whose conversations are not very measured; as these are foreigners, I do not. 
hesitate to treat such with the contempt that such an unfriendly line of conduct 
deserves. 

I have, &c. ¥ 
(Signed) ROBERT G. COLQUHOUN,

No. 103. 
The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Cowley. 


No. 830. | 
Dany Foreign Uffice, August 3, 1855. 

: HER Majesty's Government have learnt with regret, by your Excellency’s 
telegraphic despatch of yeaterday, that Count Walewski does not concur with 


them in the opinion that Omer Pasha and a portion of the army under his com- 
mand might be 


sent to Asia to effect a diversion for the relief of Kars and the 


99 


adjoining country, now menaced by the Russian forces under General Mouravieff, 
while their place in the Crimea might be filled up by the Turko-British Contin- 
gent under LecutenaneGeneral Vivian. 

Her Majesty’s Government, however, consider the relicf of the Turkish 
troops in Asia of such vital importance that they cannot abstain from laying 
before the Government of the fenipecor the various arguments by which they 
consider the plan recommended by vhem may be su ported. 

It is plain that without assistance the whole Turkish force in Asia must be 
destroyed or captured. The foree at Kars is surrounded, and even if able to 
defend its position against assault, which may be doubted, it must surrender 
when its provisions are exhausted, and that will happen ina few weeks. The 
immediate result would be, that 13,000 Turkish troops would become prisoners 
of war, and a strong position be occupied by the Russians. 

But, moreover, Kars taken, Erzeroom must share the like fate, and the 
whole of the neighbouring country would be in the hands of the Russians, 
while te season would be too far advanced for military operations to drive them 
out of it. 

In this event, if the allies take Sebastopol, the Russians will have an 
equivalent to set against it, and against any demands which the allies may 
found on the possession of that place, If, on the other hand, the allies do not 
tnke Sebastopol before the winter, the Russians, by occupying Asia Minor, will 
have a considerable advantage over the allies ; and as the Russians have nothing 
to do on the Danube, and are free from apprehension anywhere to the north of 
the Danube, they can send into Georgia, and thence into Asia Minor, a force of 
considerable magnitude. ‘This would be striking a serious blow at the Turkish 
Empire, and one, the effects of which it would be difficult to remedy. i, on the 
other hand, Omer Pasha were to go to Redoute-Kalé, or Erzeroom, with a 
sufficient force, the Russians would be driven back, or forced to retire; the 
Cireassians would be encouraged, Georgia might be cleared, and the allies would 
stand on vantage-ground in any negotiations which may be entered into during 
the ensuing winter. 

Omer Pasha’s knowledge of Asiatic Turkey would give him advantages in 
carrying on war there, which no other Commander ean possess, while in the 
Crimea his presence is comparatively of no value. 

Her Majesty's Government, indeed, feel doubtful whether, if the ‘Turkish 
Government should desire to avail itself of Omer Pasha’s: special qualifications 
for service in Asia, any just objections could be made to the Porte’s utilizing in 
that quarter the services of one who has proved himself to be so able a Com- 
mander, and who has succeeded in organizing an efficient army, at a moment 
when some of the most important provinces of the Turkish Empire are invaded by 
the enemy, and when a considerable ‘Vurkish force is in danger of being made 
prisoners of war. Her Majesty's Government would doubt whether it was wise, 
even if they felt sure they had the right to do so, to object to the adoption by 
the Porte of such a course; especially as the transfer of the contingent under 
Lieutenant-General Vivian either to Balaklava or to Eupatoria, whichever might 
might be deemed best, would fill up, or nearly so, the void occasioned by the 
removal of Omer Pasha, and a portion of his forre to Asia Minor; and the 
reinforcement sent to Lieutenant-General Vivian and to the British and French 
armies would ad still further to the aggregate force of the allied armies in the 
Crimea. 

Her Majesty’s Government wish that your Excellency should submit these 
observations without delay to the French Government, in the hope that they 
may be induced to take a different view of the question from that which they 
have hitherto entertained, and may enable your Inxcellency to convey to 
Viscount Stratford de Redelitte by the messenger of to-morrow, while a similar 
communication ig made to M. de Thonvenel by the French Government, 
authority to recommend the Porte to adopt the course of detaching Omer 
Pasha and an adequate portion of his army to Asia Minor; so that by this 
means the only resource which now remains for ave. ‘ng from the Turkish 
army in Asia the great disaster which there is too muca reason to apprehend 
is impending over it, may at all events not be left untuied. 

lam, &c. 
(Signed) CLARENDON,

No, 107, 
The Earl of Clarendon to Mr, Elliot. 


(ho .36) 
Sir, Foreign Office, August 7, 1855. 

COUNT COLLOREDO has read to me a despateh from Count Buol, 
stating that the Austrian Government were satisfied with the assurances which 
they had received of the desire of Her Majesty's Government to maintain the 
alliance with Austria, and that similar assurances had been received from the 
French Government. 

Count Buol infers from these explanations that England and France agree 
with Austria that the four guarantees cannot, in any ease, be excluded from the 
future conditions of peace, but always with the reservation of their right to put 
forward such conditions over and above the four guarantees as the general 
interest of Europe may appear to require, and with the understanding that they 
are released from all obligation to resume the negotiations purely upon the basis 
of the four principles, 

The Austrian Government, Count Buol states, see nothing in this which ts 
inconsistent with the engagements contracted between Austria and the Western 
Powers, since it is understood that in any case the four principles must form 
part of the guarantees to be given to Europe. 

Austria has no objection to the reserves made by England and France, 
regarding those reserves as the natural consequence of the position held by the 
belligerent Powers and of the sacrifices which the war imposes upon them. 

The Austrian Government does not, therefore, consider it necessary to enter 
into any fresh engagements; but they wish the respective positions of the three 
Powers to be more precisely defined than they have been yet. 

With reference to this point, Count Buol puts the following questions : 

Does Great Britain, looking to the position of Austria since the breaking 
off of the negotiations, and to the interpretation which Austria puts upon the 
Vth Article of the Treaty of December 2, 1854, still recognize the stipulations of 
that ‘Treaty as obligatory upon the Contracting Parties ? 

Are the three Powers, in consequence, bound together in the pursuit of a 
common object, and is their mutual engagement not to enter into any arrange- 
ment with Russia, without having deliberated thereupon in common, still in 
vigour ! 

Austria, on her part, Count Buol adds, has scrupulously observed, and will 
continue to observe, this last engagement, so long as she can count upon a 
perfect reciprocity upon the part of her allies, and upon the original object of 
the war, as laid down in the Acts signed in common, not being departed from ; 
but it would not, in Count Buol’s opinion, be consistent either with the dignity 
or convenience of any ef the Powers to leave so importunt a stipulation in doubt, 
or to muke its value depend upon the events of the war, |

Count Buol concludes by expressing his hope that the explanations of Her 
Majesty's Government will be satisfactory upon a point which cannot be without 
‘afluence on the line of conduet which Austria will for the future pursue. 

| told Count Colloredo that Her Majesty's Government, in conjunction 
with that of the Emperor of the French, would adhere to the principles which 
had guided them throughout as necessary for the future peace and security of 
Europe; that those principles, namely, the cessation of Russian Protectorate in 
the Principalities, the free navigation of the Danube, the formal acknowledgment 
of the Turkish Empire as an integral part of the balance of power in Europe, 
the cessation of Russian preponderance in the Black Sea, and the condition of 
the Christian subjects of the Porte, would come prominently under discussion 
as the foundation of any future negotiations, and that England and France 
reserved to themselves the right of proposing such further terms as they might 
think expedient and the events of war would justify, [ said also that we were 
prepared to consider the Treaty of December 2 as still in foree, and that, conse- 
quently, the three Powers were bound to make known to each other any proposal 
which might be addressed to either of them by Russia, and not to enter into any 
arrangement with Russia without having deliberated thereupon in common, 

T added that, in the opinion of Her Majesty’s Government, this engagement 
implied that no communication chould be made to Russia by either of the three 
Powers without the previous knowledge of the other two Powers. 

In conclusion I said that, if I did not advert to that part of Count Buol's 
despatch which had_reference to the interpretation which Austria put upon the 
Vth Article of the Treaty of December 2, Count Colloredo would not consider 
that the correctness of that interpretation was thereby admitted by Her Majesty's 
Government; but that [should not allude to the subject, because it was the 
wish of Her Majeaty’s Governmeut to avoid all eause of irritation, and to look 
to the future rather than to recur to past events, as they were unwilling not to 
believe that united action must, sooner or Inter, be the result of interests so 
completely identified as those of the three Powers who signed the Treaty of 
December 2. 

You will read this despatch to Count Buol.

Sir A. Malet to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received ) 
(No. 86.) 
My Lord, Frankfort, July 20, 1855. 


THE Austrian communication to the Diet was made in the sitting held 
yesterday, and with such trifling variation from the published document, that I 
subjoin it, pro memorid, as it appeared in the * Journal de Frankfort. ; 

The concluding sentence expresses the Emperor’s confidence that his 
confederates will recognise the efforts made by Austria to secure the advantages 
of peace, and to promote German interests. 

M. de Bismark observed, as | am informed, that, in so grave a conjuncture, 
his Government was desirous of showing all consideration and comity towards 
Austria: that, while resolved to uphold all existing engagements, the Cabinet of 
Berlin was unwilling to enter into any new ones, but that Is instructions 
permitted him to proceed to an immediate vote, expressing the thanks of the 
Assembly for the communication, and the readiness of his Government to main- 
tain the state of preparation for war. The Bavarian and Saxon Representatives 
were desirous of haying the matter referred to the standing Commission, and 
that the usual term of fourteen days should be allowed for the preparation and 
reception of their report. we 

After a brief discussion, altogether, as 1am informed, of a friendly character, 
it was decided to refer the communication to the Commission for Oriental 
Affairs, with the instruction to present their report in next Thursday's sitting. 

Your Lordship will observe that there is no indication of any understanding 
being arrived at between Austria and Prussia, us to the four points, or the other 
differences notoriously existing between them. ; Bele hes 

All mention of engaging the Diet to adopt the four points is studiously 
avoided, while Austria’s adhesion to the Treaty of the Jod of December, 1854, 
is upheld, sae 

With reference to the state of preparation for war, I have learnt that 
several of the minor States have not hesitated to reduce their military establish- 
nents, without waiting for the sanction of the Dict; and notwithstanding the 
impending resolution of that Assembly to maintain this attitude, | apprehend 
there is but little ehance of an inspection being made, or of the order being 
enforeed., ; ; Ne 

No other business of any general interest was transacted in Thursday's 
sitting. 
yes [ have, &e. 

(Signed) A, MALET.

LES Conférences de Vienne dans lesquelles ont été discutées les conditions 
du rétablissement de la paix générale sont closes sans avoir atteint leur grand et 
salutaire but. En face de cette issue profondement regrettable d’une tentative 
de paix qui paraissait promettre de plus heureux résultats, la Cour Impériale se 
sont appelée A exposer a cette haute assemblée ses vues sur état actuel des 
choses, et & donner ses confédérés Allemands l'occasion de déclarer qu’elle est 
Vattitude ultérieure de la Confédération qu’ils jugent eonforme aux résolutions 
préeédentes et aux importants intéréts que la Diete doit sauvegarder, 

Sa Majesté I'Empereur, fort de la conscience @avoir pris pour guide de 
tous ses efforts le bien géndral de 'Europe et celui de la Monarchie Autrichienne 
en méme temps que le bien de toute la Confédération Germamique, verra avec 
une grande satisfaction les Gouvernements Allemands partager les sentiments 
que, plein de franchise et de confiance, il fait exprimer 4 ses confédeérés. 

Une double tiche était imposée au Cabinet Impériale depuis que lévacua- 
tion des Principautés Danubiennes par la Russie avait écarté la cause immediate 
de la guerre, et qu’on pouvait espérer une solution pacifique. L'Autriche devait 
d'un cté tenir 1 voir réelée et soutenue par ses alliés la position militaire que, 
comme Puissance fortement intéressée aux événements, mats non belligérante, 
elle avait prise sur le Bas-Danube au nom du droit public de Europe et pour 
sauvegurder ses intéréts et ceux de |'Allemagne; de l'autre, il lui importait de 
faciliter la reprise des négociations entre les Puissances bellig¢rantes. 

A cet effet, elle a cru devoir contribuer autant que possible a la fixation et 
4 l'exéeution des bases d’une paix juste et offrant des garanties contre le retour 
des complications actuelles. Par \’Article Additionnel du 26 Novembre et la 
Résolution Fédérale du 9 Décembre, 1854, la Prusse et la Confédération ont 
donné, sous ces deux rapports, une application précise au principe de Valliance 
du 29 Avril (24 Juillet}, et. offert ainsi Ala Cour Impériale un soutien dont elle 
apprécie toute la valeur. 

Les documents que l'Envoyé Impérial est chargé de communiquer i la 
haute Assemblée, prouvent avee quelle persévérante activité le Cabinet Autri- 
chien a poursuivi cette tendanee dans ses négociutions avec les Puissances 
Européennes. Il a lhonneur de lui soumettre le texte d'un ‘Traite d'Alliance 
conclu le 2 Décembre entre I'Autriche, la France, et la Grande Bretagne; un 
memorandum remis le 28 Décembre par les Répresentants de ces Puissances i 
'Envoyé de Russie prés la Cour Imperiale; enfin les Protocoles 1 a 14 des 
Conférences de Paix tenues A Vienne du 15 Mars au 4 Juin de cette annee.

pacifique et en renongant i tout avantage particulier, placé la solution de In 
question Orientale sur le terrain des interéts Européens géncraux, et les notes du 
8 Aodt avaient désigné les bases d'une paix que la Cour Impériale jugea favorable 
pour l’Allemagne comme pour I'Europe. Pour y arriver, !'Autriche a conelu 
l’Alliance du 2 Décembre, dont les conditions étaient données par Ventente des 
Contractants sur les besoins de l'Europe, et est entendue avec les signataires de 
oe ee sur le sens des quatre points préliminaires contenus dans les notes du 
8 Aout,

est résultée et des obligations qui lui incombent dans les cireonstances présentés, 
Elle croit devair déclarer avec franchise comment elle comprend sa position et 
désire qu'elle soit comprise par tout le monde. 

Il ya eu divergence entre l’Autriche et les Cours de Paris et de Londres 
sur une question d’application ; mais les principes établis en commun n'ont rien 
perdu de leur force, et aux yeux du Cabinet Impérial, rien n'est changé dans les 
rapports des Puissances alliées entr’elles. 

L’Empereur n'a pas cru qu'il était de l'intérét de son pays de prendre les 
armes pour l'interprétation que la France et l'Angleterre donnent au troisiéme 
point, Mais tandis que les Puissances belligérantes usent de leur droit, le 
sentiment de Sa Majesté sur le but A atteindre en commun reste le méme. 
Décidée d persévérer dans la voie actuelle, l'Autriche maintiendra d'une manieére 
invariable les bases reconnues de la paix. Elle sera toujours préte & ouvrir de 
nouvelles négociations sur ces bases, et elle les soutiendra dans la mesure 
des obligations qu'elle a contractées de toute son influence et de toutes ses 
forces, 

Une paix qui n’assurerait pas l’exécution des quatre points de garantie, tels 


109 


quils ont été posés dans la Conférence, n’offrirait pas a l’Autriche des garanties 
suffisantes pour la tranquillité future de l'Europe. L’Autriche considére done 
comme un fait acquis pour la paix future l'entente sur les premiers points. 

Elle a indiqué une voie pour le réglement du troisiéme point, et elle croit 
nécessaire que cette question recoive une solution claire et precise, reposant sur 
le principe de la sécurité de Vintérét Européen, mais dont Vacceptation ne soit 
blessante pour la dignité d’aucune des Puissances. 

L’Autriche a prouvé par son intervention quelle importance elle attachait 
A Vintégrité de ’Empire Ottoman; elle persistera ¢galement a l'avenir a placer 
la Turquie sous une garantie générale et efficace. 

Non seulement elle respectera elle-méme cette garantie; mais elle saura 
aussi, au besoin, la faire respecter. 

Ein ce moment déa, elle se considére appelée & veiller au maintien de ce 
principe sur le Danube. Ses troupes resteront dans les Principautés jusqu’a la 
conclusion la paix, et elle espére que cette eccupation empéchera toujours les 
complications actuelles de s’étendre davantage du cdté qui menace le plus 
l'Allemagne. 

Quant au quatriéme point, ’Autriche s’en rapporte aux sympathies de 
l'Europe pour les Chrétiens placés sous la domination de I’Empire Ottoman et a 
limportance que mettent toutes les Puissances 4 une réglement définitif et 
salutaire de cette question. L'intérét dont les Puissances maritimes ont donné 
tant de preuves i la Porte, la promesse de la Russie de vouloir respecter I’indé- 
pendance du Sultan, mettent hors de doute que lorsque le moment sera venu, 
toutes les Puissances prendront A coeur les intéréts des Chrétiens en Orient, en 
respectant toutefois les droits de souveraineté du Sultan. 

‘L’Empereur serait heureux si, dans un avenir prochain, lceuvre de la paix 
pouvait étre reprise. Ila fait déclarer dans les Conférences, qu'il ne souléverait 
aucune demande dans son intérét personnel; & Vavenir encore, il se bornera aux 
exigences concernant la sécurité et les intéréts généraux de la famille des Etats 
de 'Europe. Il se maintiendra dans l'attitude actuelle aussi longtemps qu’ll 
pourra conserver l’espérance d’atteindre le but de ses efforts par une vote 
paciique, 

Sa Majesté a, dans ces circonstances, eru pouvoir alléger les charges que 
l'état des choses a jusqu’ici imposées 4 la Monarchie Autrichienne. 

Néanmoins, en raison de Vincertitude qui régne dans la situation, les 
mesures qui seront prises seront telles qu’on pourra étre prét en tout état de 
cause avec des sacrifices moindres, Des changements auront lieu dans la 
position des troupes qui se trouvent sur le pied de guerre; ils seront combinés de 
maniére & couvrir non seulement la position des troupes Impériales dans les 
Principautés Danubiennes, mais aussi de manitre ’ permettre qu’en cas de 
besoin larmée soit retablie dans sa force préeédente dans le plus bref delai. 

Au milieu de circonstances difficiles dont elle s’est en vain efforcée de 
rompre l'enchainement funeste, la Cour Impérinle a été uniquement guidée dans 
ses résolutions par la poursuite énergique d'un but que la Confédération Ger- 
manique tout entiére a reconnu comme étant sulutaire et propre 4 ramener un 
état Iégal et pacifique durable en Europe. Tout en s’efforgant d’attemdre 
complétement ce but, elle l’a limité d'une manitre juste et équita ile. 

Les Gouvernements Allemands, dans Vesprit vraiment fédéral qui les 
anime, reconnaitront combien sa manitre dagir est conséquente, et ils ne 
lui refuseront pas approbation franche et préyenante qu'elle croit pouvoir 
réclamer, 

Mais la Cour {mpériale n’a pas eu la satisfaction de pouvoir annoncer a ses 
eonfédérés Allemands la solution de la grande question actuelle, La lutte 
malheureuse qui tient le monde en suspens, nest pas encore terminée; les 
intéréts les plus importants y sont encore en jeu, et la réalisation des buts en 
faveur desquels I’Allemagne s'est déclarée n'est pas encore assurée. 

Comme Puissance Fédérale Allemande, l'Autriche peut done se prononcer 
pour que la Confederation maintienne Pattitude qu'elle a prise par les résolutions 
du 9) denna brs: 1854, ct du 8 Février de cette année. Le desir que Sa Majesté

imposante et forte par son union le développement ultérieur des événements,

Inclosure in No, 113. 
Admiral Slade to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Arsenal of Constantinople, July 23, 1855. 

I WILL do myself the honour to inform you of the state of the Turkish 
navy. ; 
The ships are disarmed in general for the conveyance of troops or provi- 
sions, except the “ Muhaderné Khair,” 84, which is about to return from 
Eupatoria, where she has been lying three months to land her guns. 

The ™ Mahmoudée,” 120, and the ** Peth Safer,” 96, have been prepared 
since three weeks for the conveyance of troops, supposed to the eastward. They 
are lying in the harbour ready to sail. Exeepting a flotilla in the Archipelago, 
and some small vessels in the Bosphorus, all the sailing-ships are now employed 
in the Black Sea. ; | ie 

Four of the five steam-frigates in the Imperial navy are now at Constan- 
tinople. One only of these, the * Saiki Shade,” is in a state to go to sea. Che 
‘Paisi Bari’ which has been idly used as flag-ship in the Black Sea, returned 
here a fortnight since; she is under repairs, and another month I am informed 
will be required to complete her. It is to be regretted that she had not been sent 
back two months ago for that purpose. ‘The hull of the “ Medjédié ’" is rotten, 
and cannot be trusted at sea; on the other hand, the boilers of the “ Jait "are 
worn out ; it is therefore proposed to transfer the boilers of the © Meidjédié” to 
the “‘Jaif’? and then the latter may serve another year. But this is not 
certain : the transfer of the boilers will probably be a two months job. The 
‘“Mookberi Sourdur,” serew-frigate, now in the Black Sea, requires repairs, but 
of no great amount, | believe. Nothing in my opinion should be allowed to 
operate against repairing all the steamers before winter; in that season we shall 
have to trust mainly to them, ‘There are four steam-corvettes 1n fair condition ; 
two merehant steamers in port are being looked at with a view to purehase by 
the arsenal, and orders are to be sent to England to buy four large merchant 
steamers, reported to be nearly ready for launching. Thus we have some reason 
to hope to possess by winter cight large and six ordinary steamers. 

These, aided as weather permits by sailing-ships, will suthice, T hope, for 
provisioning and otherwise attending on the Turkish army in the Crimea and on 
the eastern coast of the Black Sea, provided the Cavalry be not numerous. 

Since my entry into the Turkish service, 1 have constantly urged-on the 
authorities the necessity of building screw-ships; about two years ago the 
principle was udopted. Since then a line-of-battle ship has been converted, but 
as her lines are coarse, engines may not be placed in her. ‘lwo other liners are 
building—one at the arsenal, and one ut Ismail; and a first-class frigate has 
recently been launched at Sinope, all for screw propulsion, ‘The engines for 
them—650 horse-power for each liner, and 500 horse-power for the fi rigate—have 
been contracted for with Messrs. Napier’s firm at Glasgow, Models and draw- 
ings of approved line-of-battle ships in the English navy have been applied for, 
and it isintended, on their arrival, to lay down two more ships. Jn a paper, on 


1) 


a steam-navy, I gave the Captain Pasha some months ago, I suggested the 
following force as sufficient for the wants of the Empire at present: 


Six line-of-battle ships, 100 guns, 600 to 650 horse-power. 


Four frigates, 60 , 800 ag 

Four corvettes, o4 ,, 260 - 

Four paddle-frigates and six avisos adopted for dispatch or 
gun-boats. 


My ruling idea has been, that while the Russian navy is in progress of 
destruction, a Turkish serew-fleet shall be in process of formation. This will at 
once give Turkey the preponderance in the Black Sea, and it will be her fault 
then if she does not retain it. 


(Signed) A. SLADE. 


No. 114. 
Lord Stratford de Redeliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—{ Received August 8.) 


(No. 555.) 
My Lord, Therapia, July 30, 1855. 

THE unfavourable judgment passed by Her Majesty’s Government on the 
plans which haye been lately under discussion, with a view to the relief of the 
Sultan's army at Kars, has naturally increased the Porte’s embarrassment. It 
was my duty to make it known to the Turkish Ministers, not only as an opinion, 
but with respect to General Vivian's Contingent, as a veto. A most serious 
dilemma is the immediate result. Her Majesty's Government not only with- 
hold the Contingent, but express a decided preference for the alternative of 
sending reinforeements to Erzeroom by the way of Trebizond, This opinion ts 
not adopted by the Porte, or indeed by any official or personal authority here. 
The Seraskicr, Omer Pasha, General Guyon, our own. officers, as far as I have 
means of knowing, agree with the Porte and the French Embassy in pre- 
ferring a diversion on the side of Redoute Kaleh, as offering better chances 
of success, supposing, of course, that the necessary means of transport supply, 
and other indispensable wants can be sufficiently provided. France is at the 
same time decidedly adverse to any diminution of force in the Crimea; and 
Omer Pasha, ready to place himself at the head of an Asiatic expedition, 
requires for that purpose a part of the troops now there. 

Such being the present state of the case, | am precluded from contributing 
to the Porte’s extrication from its difficulties, otherwise than by countenancing 
some new location of the Contingent, which, without exposing the corps to a 
premature trial, might enable aforee of the same amount to be detached for 
service elsewhere. 

No final decision has yet been taken by the Porte. My colleague, the 
French Ambassador, has written for General Pelissier’s opinion, and Omer 
Pasha is stillin attendance on his Government. 

A memorandum inclosed herewith, will present to your Lordship a com- 
parative view of the several ways in which effect might be given to the idea in 
question. 

Meanwhile the advices from Kars are not encouraging, and time of precious 
value is unavoidably wasted in doubt and uncertainty. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

No, 116. \ 
The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Stratford de Redclife. 


(No. 656.) 
My Lord, Foreign Office, August 13, 1855, 

I HAVE been in communication with the War Department on the subject 
of your Excellency’s despatch No. 555 of the 30th ultimo, and I have now to 
transmit to your Excellency, for your information and guidance, a copy of a 
letter from that office, stating the grounds upon which Lord Panmure is of 
opinion that the Turkish Contingent should be sent a a body to Eupatoria. 

am, &c 


(Signed) CLARENDON. 


Inclosure in No. 116. 
Colonel Mundy to Mr. Hammond. 


Sir, War Department, August 13, 1855. 


1 AM directed by Lord Panmure to acknowledge the receipt of your 
letter of the 10th instant, and to request that you will acquaint Lord Clarendon, 
that after careful consideration, his Lordship is of opinion that the Turkish 
Contingent should be sent in a body to Eupatoria, 

The grounds upon which Lord Panmure founds that opinion are— 

1. The Contingent requires organization before it can do credit either to 
itself or to us, or be efficient as a corps d’armée in the field. 

This force must not be looked upon in a false light; when completed it 
will be as well appointed in Cavalry, Artillery, and Engineers as our own 
army, and in some respects even better. 

The Infantry arm may well be increased; and should it be necessary to 
move in the field next spring, important results may, in his Lordship’s opinion, 
be anticipated from this Contingent. 

2. If sent to Eupatoria, the Contingent will be able to maintain that 

ition even in its present state; and the Cavalry under Major-General 
Shirley will almost immediately be able to act against the Russian convoys. 

3. There will be no greater difficulty in supplying and provisioning the 
Contingent at Eupatoria than there would be elsewhere. 

4. Lord Panmure would regret that the force should be detached in separate 
bodies until more fully organized, and General Vivian has acquired a full know- 
ledge of his officers, and the Turks have become acquainted with and accustomed 
to obey the British officers. 

The above are the views which, as at present advised, his Lordship 
entertains ; but he is well aware that there may arise circumstances which may 
alter them. 

lam, &e. 
(Signed) G, C, MUNDY.

No. 117. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Recetved August 8.) 


(No. 557.) 
My Lord, Therapia, July 30, 1855. 


INCLOSED herewith, for your Lordship’s perusal, is a continuation of my 
correspondence with Major-General Beatson and others of Her Majesty's official 
servants, respecting the late desertions and acts of insubordination committed by 
a part of the Albanians employed under British command at the Dardanelles. 

Your Lordship will observe that I lost no time in sending back, with neces- 
sary instructions, the officer who brought me General Beatson’s despatch. The 
circumstances of the case, and the relation in which I am placed towards the 
Corps in question are so unusual, that I rely with full confidence on your Lord- 
ship’s appreciation of my endeavours to redress the existing evils, and to prevent 
their recurrence. 

The assistance afforded to me, not only by the Turkish authorities, but also 
by Lieutenant-General Vivian, Lord William Paulet, Brigadier-General Mansfield, 
and Rear-Admiral Grey, deserve my warmest acknowledgments. 

I abstain from hazarding any premature remarks on General Beatson's 
resignation and correspondence. I shall probably be able to exercise a clearer 
judgment on both after the receipt of some communication from General Neill, 
who had reached the Dardanelles before Captain Bourke came away, but not till 
after he had received the despatches of which he was the bearer, 

It is almost superfluous to remark, that the precautionary idea of disarming 
the Albanians was suggested by a wish to narrow as much as possible the chances 
of an effusion of blood; and that although the preparations for that measure, 
supposing it to be found necessary, were intended to be as secret as prompt, 
they were by no means planned with a view to secrecy in the execution. 

| have, &e. 


(Signed) | STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.
