Inclosure 2 in No. 117. 
General Beatson to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 
My Lord, Dardanelles, July 24, 1855. 


YOUR Excellency’s despatch of the 21st instant has this moment reached 
me, and in reply I beg to say that, as far as | am personally concerned, I am 


‘quite indifferent if the corps were disbanded to-morrow. 


The direct opposition I have met in some quarters, with the lukewarm 
support in others, looks as if Her Majesty’s present Government are anxious 
that a measure which was originated by the late Government should fail. 

It is impossible otherwise to account for the delay that has occurred in 
furnishing me with means of organizing the corps, as repeatedly pointed out by 
me. The means of ee a: justice, when immediate example was neces- 

De & SeTious CVI. , 
Ne a Caay ane the late occurrences, Mr. Skene proceeded to Constanti- 
nople to lay before your Excellency the full state of the case, and my most 
particular request was that I might have a law-officer sent to me to enable me 
to enter on the trial of the deserters. ‘ 

Mr. Skene came back, and informed me that immediate measures would be 


taken to arm me with the powers of the law. Still steamer after steamer comes 


in without bringing me a law-officer, or power to carry out the sentence of any 
Court such as ought to deal with grave offences, = | 

I had thus no alternative but to leave everything in abeyance, and there 
could be no stronger proof that the spirit of insubordination was of a very 
partial nature, when this delay has led to no further outrages. That the general 
discipline of the force is by no means affected, 1 can offer as proofs, that a 
quarrel have taken place in the town, a few days ago, between a French soldier 
and an Albanian, in which both parties were wounded, I confined the Albanian 
in the prison, on hearing which the officers of his troop came and begged that 
I would make him over to them, as they did not think his wound would be 
properly attended to in the prison. I assembled a Medical Board to satisfy 
myself that the man was in no danger, and then peremptorily refused to release 
him, with the justice of which they were perfectly satished. 

yesterday assembled a court-martial—an illegal one, T must allow, as | 
have no law-ofticer, but the best I could get under the circumstances—for the 
trial of two Bulgarian soldiers, who, in a fit of drunkenness, had threatened their 
vukbashee. ‘The Court was composed chiefly of Albanian officers, and they 
sentenced the prisoners to be flogged. Ste Se aha 

Thus I feel confident [ can support discipline if power is given me to 
carry out the sentence of any Court immediately. ; 

"The inquiry which your Excellency wishes, I shall order immediately ; and 
as my desire as well as that of your Excellency, 15 ies it ahoule be as full and 
jal_as possible, | shall pul none of my own officers on it. 
bier ae putpass 1 have requested Mr, Skene, Mr. Calvert, Lieutenant- 
Colonel Blacklin, the Commandant at Abydos, to form the Court. 

Mr. Skene not having received the statement referred to in your letter, [am 
unable to say anything on that subject, nor do I know to whiat rumours your 
Excellency alludes. If they be those that appeared in the Constantinople paper 
ubout the British flag being torn down, they are totally false, Pw nee 

Had I followed my own personal feelings and convenience, the difficulties 
thrown in my way by the War Department would have made me resign long 


Se ti ee a ee 


1g 


ago; but 1 felt that [ had a higher duty to perform in bringing into order th 
body of men, which I still feel I can do if 1 am properly supported, and have 
the means placed at my disposal. 
1 have, &e. 
(Signed) W. F. BEATSON. 


The “ Journal de Constantinople ° of the 19th July contains a tissue of 
falsehoods. 

The men never presented themselves before me to demand the release of 
the prisoner. ‘They released him before | knew anything about it. 

They never refused to go to the Crimea, for the simple reason that I never 
asked them. The story about the flag is utterly false; but the following circum- 
stances may have given rise to the falsehood:—On the day that the disturbance 
took place, | was changing my head-quarters from one camp to another, and got 
a working party from one of the regiments to dig round the flag-staff and take it 
up, it having been very deeply imbedded in the ground, 

The flag still floats in front of my tent, and has never been treated with 
disrespect, 

(Signed ) W. F. BEATSON, 


Inclosure 3 in No. 117. 
Consul Calvert to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Dardanelles, July 25, 1855. 

] HAVE the honour to report to your Excellency that on the night of the 
2\st instant about fifty men of the Arab Irregulars deserted with their horses 
from Beatson’s camp. | 

The first intelligence received of them was from the Mudir of Tchan, who 
had found means to secure eight of them, and these he dispatched in charge of 
an escort, who had likewise about 35,000 or 40,000 piastres of Government 
taxes for delivering here, 

On their way hither, they fell in with twenty-eight of the fifty deserters, 
and who not only released their comrades, but plundered the Government 
money. 

Four of them have been since captured at Bairamitsh, and Lieutenant- 
Colonel Lankey, with some of his men, have gone in search of the others, but 
nothing further respecting them has as yet transpired. 

LT have, &e. 
(Signed) F. W. CALVERT.

Inclosure 5 in No, 117. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to General Beatson. 


Sir, Therapia, July 29, 1855. 

I HAVE received from Captain Brouke son despatch dated yesterday, 
and I lose no time in preparing an answer, wit which that officer will return 
at once to the Dardanelles. . 

Not having heard from General Neill, who, in place of General Michel, has 
gone down to the Dardanelles with my instruction, I confine myself for the 
present to those parts of your communication which require immediate notice. 

It will be for Her Majesty’s Government to appreciate the circumstances 
under which you have announced the intention of resigning your command. 
The propriety of the course which I have thought it my duty to pursue, can 
only be judged with reference to the whole of your correspondence since the first 
desertion of Albanians, taken in connection with the statements which reached 
me from other sources, and the results of the present inquiries. 

I am glad, meanwhile, to perceive in the conduct of your troops, as now 
described, such improvement as may fairly warrant a hope that the ends of 
justice can be secured, without resorting to extraordinary measures. The 
present strength of the garrison may answer for the submission of men, as it 
would have provided for the safe execution of the measures: 

Gonvinced that General Neill, after communication with you, will have 
exercised a sound judement in that respect, Lam content to wait for the reports 
of the Mixed Commission and Court of Inquiry. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE, 


Inclosure 6 in No, 117. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to Consul Skene. 


Sir, Therapia, July 29, 1855. 

| HAVE hastened the return of Captain Brouke to the Dardanelles with 
my answer to General Beatson’s despatch, declaring an intention to resign his 
command, A copy of it is forwarded by General Neill, who will apprize you 
of its contents. Copies of the despatch itself, as well as of my reply, shall be 
sent direct to you by the earliest opportunity. 

_ As there seems to be no actual necessity for calling in the arms of the 
Albanians, | am but too happy to look exclusively to the result of the pending 
inquiry, and simultaneous court of justice, This you will understand to be my 
present view of the matter, in virtue of the latest advice. 

I have, &e.

Brigadier-General Mansfield to Brigadier-General Neill. 


Sir, Embassy, Therapia, July 29, 1855, 

| HAVE the honour, by desire of the Ambassador, to forward confidentially 
a copys a despatch from his Excellency to Major-General Beatson for your 
consideration, and to call your attention to the last paragraph of that commu- 
nication. 


I have to inform you, that according to the latest reports received by his © 


Excellency, it would appear the necessity for extraordinary measures, such as 
disarming the Albanian corps en masse, as contemplated in the despatch of the 
Ambassador to Major-General Beatson, of the 24th instant, no longer existe, 
and that it is therefore more desirable to trust to the regular action of the Court

of Inquiry, of the Mixed Commission, and the usual manner of firmly enforcing 
military discipline. 

His Excellency is confident that this important matter will receive the 
maturest consideration on your part, both as regards the permanent establish-

avoidance of conflict, if, as it is now stated, the latter can with prudence be 
insured,

Inclosure 8 in No. 117. 
Instructions for Brigadier-General Neill. 


Therapia, July 26, 1859. 

BRIGADIER-GENERAL NEILL, on arriving at the Dardanelles, will 
out himself in communication with Major-General Beatson, Mr. Skene, and 

Ir. Calvert, Her Majesty's Consul. 

If Major-General Beatson determines, in consequence of the representations 
of the Ambassador, to leave the Dardanelles for Constantinople, Brigadier- 
General Neill is empowered to assume command of the Irregular Cavalry, and 
consequently, as he will then be the senior officer on the spot, of all the troops 
now at the Dardanelles, the Turkish Colonel having been already directed by 
the Seraskier to act under Major-General Beatson. 

The object of the concentration of regular troops at that place, is to 
repress the excesses und check the disorders of a portion of the Irregular 
Llorse. 

ff great disorders prevail there at this time, and the measures taken appear, 
‘n the opinion of Brigadier-General Neill, to be insufficient, he will, on the 
command being transferred to him, adopt the means suggested by military expe- 
rience, for the restoration of order, and securing all offenders. 

The despatches addressed to Major-General Beatson, on the arrangements 
ordered for bringing the offenders to justice, and restoring order, as enumerated 
in the margin,® may be obtained from that officer by Brigadier-General Neill, or 
from Mr, Skene. If they have not already been acted on, the Brigadier- 
General will proceed to carry out the instructions therein conveyed. If Major- 
General Beatson determines not to leave the Dardanelles, but to remain there 
‘1 the exercise of his command, contrary to the wishes of the Ambassador, 
Brigadier-General Neill will not refuse to give him the aid of his counsel. 

If that counsel be rejected, and disorder continue in consequence, it will be 
for Brigadier-General Neill to communicate with Mr. Skene and the Consul, 
and to act on the result of their consultations. 

It should be constantly borne in mind that disorder must be stopped, — 
discipline restored, and the offenders brought to trial. 

The regular troops at the Hisporl of the Commander are :—300 Infantry, 
925 Cavalry, 400 Artillerymen. me very light guns are available, which can 
be easily moved, 

The Ambassador understands that Major Hackett, Deputy-Assistant 
Quartermaster-General, has been empowered by Brigadier-General Lord William 
Paulett, to use the British Detachment at Gallipoli towards the general purpose 
of these instructions. It is not certain whether 200 Infantry in addition went 
yesterday to the Dardanelles, but if not, they will be dispatched forthwith, 

Brigadier-General Neill will send a report whenever there is an opportunity, 
daily if possible, to his Excellency the Ambassador, till order is omnpletaly 
restored, 

By order of Viscount Stratford de Redcliffe. 
(Signed) WwW. R. MANSFIELD, 
Brigadier General.

Inclosure 9 in No. 117, 
Consul Skene to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Dardanelles, July 28, 1855. 


I HAVE the honour to acknowledge the receipt of your Excellency’s 
despatch, dated 24th July, by which I am appointed to represent Her Majesty's 
Ebay in the judicial proceedings connected with General Beatson’s Irregular 
avalry. 

D beg leave to express the high sense of the honour done me, by this 
flattering appreciation of any small services which I may have been fortunate 
enough to be able to render. 

The trial of offenders will commence to-day, a considerable number of 
them being in confinement for that purpose; and I have much pleasure in 
adding, that I feel confident of the complete success awaiting the measures 
adopted and arrangements made hy you Excellency to meet the grave emer- 
gencies which have arisen in General Beatson’s corps. 

I have, &c. 


(Signed) T. H. SKENE, 


Tnclosure 10 in No. 117, 
Consul Skene to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Dardanelles, July 28, 1855. 
IN reply to your Excellency’s note of the 24th, I can only say that I fear 


more reliance is placed on my poor endeavours than cireumstances have as yetjusti-: 


fied ; but I shall strain every nerve to merit that confidence. Your Excellency’s 
good Spent sO expressed, is more gratifying to me than anything else could 
be, No zeal or perseverance shall be wanting on my part to warrant it, 

a am convinced that the Mixed Commission will prove perfectly suc- 
CeSSriL. 

General Beatson has read me the despatch which he sends to your Excel- 
lency by this post. His state of excitement left little room for any remarks of 
mine; but 1 did not fail to remind him that there were, and still are, facts to 
account most fully for the manner in which the state of his corps is viewed, 
without gratuitously supposing it to be founded on other reports, which are 
either exaggerated or altogether untrue. I alluded to the incidents contained in 
my note to your Excellency of yesterday's date, which will go to-day, and 
which, though still unknown to you, form a continuation of a series of disorders, 
as well known to him as to me, He then expatiated on the breach of faith 
shown by the wish to disarm the Albanians. replied that, in all probability, 
nothing of all this could have happened, and nothing more of the kind would 
bappet if the condition of the men were not suchas to tempt them in a manner 
to follow their violent impulses; and besides that, | knew of no pledge given 
them on enlisting them that they were at all times to retain their arms. The 
General would not listen to me, and returned to his own assertions, that 
if was a sequel to the massacre of the Janisesries, which I might 
under other circumstances have attempted to prove to him was the saving 
of Turkey, and an act of mercy to the nation oppressed by them, but discussion 
was impossible, With regard to his resignation, I said that in his position, I 
should never throw up my commission in a huff, and that in matters of business 
all “ brave words" had no weight, but that if he felt he could not carry out the 
views of the Government I thought he would do quite right to resign, and need 
not talk of insults, &. &c. He said he would not disarm the Albanians under 
any circumstances, and that if the Government insisted on that measure he 
must resign. ‘This was taking a better ground to stand upon, and if his letter 
had gone no further, I should have said it was worthy of your Excellency's most 
serious consideration, I believe that the disarming the Albanians may become 
necessary, but as long as there is a chance of avoiding the conflict it must pro- 
duce, | would regard it as a lust resouree to he adapted only. when all other 


123 


means are exhausted. Now, this Mixed Commission may change the whole 
aspect of affairs, and afew prompt and vigorous applications of the law may 
disarm them in a moral sense without provoking any material conflict. I conceive 
that justice has a power over every class of men, and that the Albanians will 
find themselves, not subdued, but gained by its exercise. The hope of leniency, 
too well founded, destroys the effect of justice on their minds, but when they 
find that culpability is proved and inexorably penisted, when they cannot deny 
guilt and must witness its expiation, lam much mistaken if we do not succeed 
with the Albanians. The experiment is now going to be made, and I would 
most respectfully urge that the question of disarming be left in abeyance for a 
little time ; the garrison is sufficient to enable us to make examples without risk, 
their being immediately disarmed would not prevent desertion; the Arabs had 
no arms. At all events | hope the subject may be maturely weighed and con- 
sidered by General Mitchell before acting on any instructions he may have to 
that effect, and I shall certainly deem it my duty to state to him my views, which 
he will no doubt be able to refute if they should be erroneous. 

I shall continue to inform your Excellency of anything occuring here to 
interest you or enable you to form a correct judgment. 

Believe me, &c. 


(Signed) T, H. SKENE. 


Inclosure 11 in No, 117. 


General Beatson to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe, 


My Lord, Dardanelles, July 28, 1855. 
I YESTERDAY received your Excellency’s two despatches of the 24th 
instant. 


As my reports that the insubordination was of a partial nature, that the 
discipline of the force was progressing to perfect reestablishment, that I was 
able and willing to punish the offender, by having given to me the power to 
carry into effect any sentence awarded, I have failed to counteract the effect 

roduced on your Excellency by the falsehoods put forth in the newspapers, and 

y the Pashas of this place. {[ should still have considered it my duty to have 
remained in command till the Court of Inquiry now sitting had shown that I was 
right ; but when to the above is added the proposal to place the command in 
other hands, and to proceed to Constantinople, | cannot, in justice to myself, do 
otherwise than place my resignation in the Aaa of Her Majesty's Government, 
and make over the command to any officer whom your Excellency may send to 
take it, though I shall at the same time remain at the Dardanelles throughout 
the difficulties which will be eaused or increased by the unnecessary and inju- 
dicious measures now in progress with regard to military arrangements. 

I have already shown that ] court impartial inquiry, by forming a Court on 
which I have not put a single officer of my own force. 

T am ready to afford every assistance to the Mixed Commission now ordered 
to try offenders. 

But | must decidedly protest against secret arrangements for the disarming 
of the Albanians, which. however worthy of the Government which murdered the 
Janissaries, are not such as a British officer can with honour adopt. 

Had the order for disarming the Albanians been immediately sent when a 
portion of the men were in a mutinous state, it would have been a very different 
thing; but now that I have told them that I have confidence in their own 
officers to support discipline, and they justify this confidence by sentencing and 
penishing every offender, it would be treacherous on my part to take secret 
measures to disarm them. 

All the officers sent down from Scutari were astonished at the respectful 
demeanour of the men in camp, after the infamous reports they had heard at 
Constantinople. ‘These reports are chiefly got up by the French Consul, who 
is the correspondent of the “ Journal de Constantinople.” 

Besides what | have stated above, it would be a breach of faith on our 

rt. Every person knows thut the Albanians would rather part with their 

ives than with their arms; and if we intended to disarm them, we ought to- 

heve told them so before we disarmed them. We wight possibiy murder them 
2K 


124 


as the Janissaries were murdered; ‘but there is an end to all confidence in 
British faith and British honour. Thus the measures proposed would have 
éxactly the effect which all the intrigues of fanaticism at Damascus and other 
places have failed to produce. 

The new desertions alluded to in your letter were not from the Albanians, 
but from the Arabs. Most of them have been brought back by Lieutenant 
Colonel Sankey, whom I sent after them with a party of volunteers from their 
own regiments, and they state that they were induced to desert by a moulayien, 
who told them that we were going to force them to dress and act in every respect 
like regular troops. 

I have imprisoned the moulayien, reduced him to the ranks, and ordered 
him to be tried by his own officers, whose sentence I shall carry out. 

In one of the Albanian regiments I tried, by their own officers, and carried 
out their sentence of flogging, two Albanians the day before yesterday, 

This will show how far discipline is reestablished ; but all this will be 
upset the moment it is known that there is an intention of disarming the 
Albanians, . . 

The kind of assurance | have got from the Pasha here will be understood 
by my saying that a few days ago he sent to tell me that the Arab deserters had 
plundered some cavasses bringing in Government money , but instead of giving 
me this immediately, he gave it to me twenty hours after he had received it, 
thus giving the plunderers every chance to escape. It is the same in every- 
thing he does. 

He, like the rest of his tribe, is anxious that Christians should fail in 
attaching any portion of the inhabitants of ‘Turkey to them, and they stick at 
nothing to gain their ends, which they will only now gain by the withdrawal of 
that support I have heretofore received from Laoag 

lave, ke. 


(Signed) W. F. BEATSON.

Inclosure 3 in No, 118. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to Lieutenant-General Vivian. 


Sir, Therapta, July 27, 1855. 

I HAVE the honour to acknowledge the receipt of your despatch of this 
day’s date, inclosing copy of your reply to Lieutenant-Genera Simpson’s 
communication of the 17th instant, 

] observe that your suggestions as to the future position of the Contingent

[nclosure 4 in No, 118. 
Lieutenant-General Vivian to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Head-Quarters, Buyukdérd, July 29, 1855, 


YOUR Excellency’s letter of the 27th instant, acknowledging the receipt 
of copy of my despatch to Lieutenant-General Simpson, reached me this morning. 

The opinion that I offered in my letter to General Simpson was necessarily 
restricted to the military question submitted for my views, viz., the proposal of 
the officer commanding Her Majesty's troops in the East, and of his Highness 
Omer Pasha, to assemble and winter the Contingent at Shumla, and my 
observations had reference to the arrangement which would become necessary 
if the intention of the Commander of the Forces were to be carried into effect. 
Undoubtedly other than military considerations may have claim to attention, 
and I am persuaded your Lordship must feel convinced that any point submitted 
by your Excellency for my consideration, would at all times receive immediate 
and the utmost attention; but naturally any opinions of mine connected with 
my corps would be of a military character, unless subjects were submitted for 
my opinion, demanding the wider range of consideration to which your Lordship 
adverts. 

I desire to bring prominently to your Lordship's notice that | am im a state 
of the greatest solicitude lest the delay in arriving at a decision respecting the 
destination of the Contingent, should be attended with results prejudicial to its 
efficiency and well-being, 

Wherever the corps has to winter, very considerable arrangements have to 
be made to meet its requisitions, Stores and provisions have to be collected 
and provided for the whole winter, Depéts to be formed in various directions. 
Store room must: be prepared; perhaps hutting may have to be built. 

If the corps is to occupy an inland position, much time will be required to 
complete all the requisite arrangements, but in whatever quarter the position 
may be, there is now barely time to do all that is required, 

The speedy determination, my Lord, of the position for the winter quarters 
of the Contingent is a question on its efficiency as a military body for ulterior 
purposes appears to me in a great measure to depend; holding, as I do, this 
opinion, it cannot, I think, surprise your Lordship that I as the officer 
commanding this corps d’armée, should strongly urge that the point be ‘deter-

mined with as little delay as possible: and if, as 1 infer from your Lordship’s 
letter under acknowledgment, the selection of the position where my corps: is 
to winter must be influenced by the decision on a qnesticn now pending with the 
Porte, | would solicit your Excellency to draw the attention of the Sultan’s 
Government to the important subject which I have thus felt it my duty to bring 
to your Lordship's notice. 
I have, &c. 
(Signed) J.J. R. VIVIAN.

No. 119. 
Consul Longworth to the Earl of Clarendon.—( Received August 13.) 


(No. 7.) 
My Lord, ‘* Cyclops,’ Coast of Circassia, July 20, 1855. 

IN my despatch to your Lordship No. 5, under date of the 20th instant, 
I alluded to the difficulties raised by the Turkish authorities, who were averse 
to my proceeding into the interior. Sefer Pasha endeavoured to dissuade me 
under various pretexts, alleging, among other things, that travelling was unsafe 
at present; and Mustapha Pasha, the Commander-in-chief, also strongly recom- 
mended me not to leave the coast. But their evident anxiety to prevent my 
penetrating into the country, only served to increase my desire of so doing as 
early as possible, since I felt more and more convinced that there was much in 
the state of affairs, to say nothing of their own double-dealing and intrigues 
which they were interested in concealing, and that it was only in becoming more 
intimately acquainted with such particulars, as well as with the state of parties 
and the conflicting interests of the various tribes, that I should find the means 
of independent action in Circassia. 

As a necessary precaution, I first secured the escort of an influential Chief, 
with whom I had formerly been associated, and who agreed to accompany me, 
at the request of Mustapha Pasha, though that request I could perceive was 
very reluctantly made on his part. So visible indeed was the Pasha’s disincli- 
nation to serve me in this respect, that the person in question on our arrival by 
steamer at Soudjak Kalé, from which place as the most convenient, I intended 
to strike into the mountains, positively refused to start, unless I could obtain 
the additional attendance of two other Chiefs, as Mehmandars on the part of 
Sefer Pasha. 

I wrote accordingly to that functionary, demanding, in virtue of the Sultan's 
firman and the Bouyourouldi of his superior in command, Mustapha Pasha, 
that he would forthwith appoint two such individuals to accompany me. It was’ 
natural to expect that an escort so appointed would be chiefly occupied in 
watching and circumventing me for the benefit of their employers. I contrived, 
however, by dint of perseverance and presents, not only to elude their control, 
but to make docile instraments of them for my own purpose, | 

As I felt persuaded that little or nothing could be done for the furtherance 
of the objects of my mission in the section of the country (forming the north. 


_ western angle of the country between the River Kuban and the Black Sea, and

inhabited by the tribes of Shapsook and Natquoitch), I resolved to proceed AS 
quickly as possible to Bzadoog, and the other provinces which extend from 
some ‘antes below Ekaterinodar to the sources of the Laba and the Kuban. 
In these the traditional authority of the feudal Chiefs (Pshees and Ouzdens) 
had been hitherto preserved, and seemed to present a medium of intercourse 
and negotiation with foreign Governments, which in Shapsook and Natquoiteh 
had long ceased to exist. The power of the nobles in the latter provinces had 
never been at any time of the same absolute nature ; and the few privileges they 

ossessed (the chief of which was a higher assessment in the price of blood) had 
bean gradually extinguished. Still, at the period of my residence in the country, 
about eighteen years ago, some of the Chiets, from personal and family considera- 
tion, enjoyed undoubted influence ; and to these, as the organs of public feeling, 
an Envoy might have addressed himself with some degree of confidence. Nearly 
all of these persons are dead, and their survivors or successors possess no 
weight or consideration whatever. | se 

The encroachments of the commoners (tocavs) on the privileges of the 
nobles had been the cause of division; and the latter thus alienated from their 
countrymen had more readily accepted bribes from the Russian Government, 
which was deceived, however, as to the amount of advantage it could obtain by 
these means. It found to its cost that it had to purchase, not individuals, but a 
whole population—a very expensive operation ; still, by its conciliatory policy 
and the concession of commercial facilities (including a connivance at the Slave 
Trade), there is no denying that it had mace great progress in the paciaon of 
the Kuban Provinces. The principal check it sustained was in the reactionary 
movement, headed by the famous Naib, or lieutenant, of Schamyl, who, tssu-ng 
from his stronghold in the central province of Abassak, had visited with fines, 
confiscation, and in some instances, death, the disaffected inhabitants of Shap- 
sook and Natquoitch. Numbers of these, and more particularly of the class of 
Ouzdens, took refuge and even service with the Russians. The great majority 
of them, however, have returned to Circassia since the evacuation of Anapa, and 
together with those of their countrymen, a very numerous hody, who had been 
prescribed by the Naib, form the strength of the party which gives its support 
to Sefer Pasha. He is the only man, I believe, who hag the slightest influence or 
control over them, and that rather of a negatiye than an active character. Hehas 
neither the means nor the energy requisite to raise a Circassian force himself; but 
he is in some measure able, as he is evidently disposed, to prevent any recruitment 
on our part, making no secret of his objections on that seore. As to his organimiug 
anything out of this anarchy for administrative purposes, I consider such a thing 
as quite hopeless and out af the question. ‘ 

With this view of the state of Shapsook and Natquoitch, 1 was desirous of 
reaching, as soon as possible, Bzadooh and the other Kuban provinces, where 
the nobles, I knew, were possessed of substantial authority, which would enable 
them, if they chose, to dispose of their warlike resources for the benefit of the 
common cause. All these districts bordering on the Laba and the Kuban, 
namely, Bzadooh, Khatukoi, Demigoi, Beslenoi, and Karatchai, together with 
those of Great and Little Kabarda, had, from their contiguity to the Russian 
frontier, as well as the fact of their being situated on the plains, long since main- 
tained peaceful relations with Russia, and had become either neutral or subject 
to her. ‘The nobles, from the power and influence they possessed, could offer 
satisfactory guarantees for the security of the frontier ; and many of them even 
had been enrolled in the service of the border police. Upwards of twenty 
thousand familes, Tatar and Circassian, who were most exposed to the inroada 
of the hostile tribes inhabiting the mountains of Abassak, had emigrated across 
the Kuban, where lands had been allotted to them. Living there among the 
Cossack population, they appeared, however, to the Russian Government, when 
the war broke out, to present an element of danger; and an ukase had lately 
arrived from St. Petersburgh, ordering the emigrants to be removed to the 
idistance of 100 versts into the interior of Russia, or to be permitted to return 
to Circassia. Many chiefs availed themselves of this permission, well aware that 
they would, in future, have to side with their countrymen against Russia. All 
these circumstances apparently combined to favour the success of our mission, 
but on approaching the stream of Aphips,—a confluent of the Kuban which 
geparates Shapsook from Bzadoog,—we first received intelligence of a social 
revolution which had just dec inselfin the latter province. ‘The commonera 


129 


(in the Tartar language, Tocavs) had risen against the nobles (Pshees and 
Onzdens’, with a determination to overthrow their authority and privileges. 
The avowed instigator of this movement was the Naib, Mohamed Emin Pasha, 
who, at the head of 500 horsemen from Abbassahk, had proclaimed that feudal 
distinctions were opposed to the precepts of the Koran, which held all Mussul- 
mans equal in the sight of God. 

These levelling doctrines were eagerly embraced by the Tocavs of Bzadoog, 
who, of themselves were, in poimt of numerical strength, much more than a 
match for the Ouzdens; and what rendered them in the mouth of the Natb 
still more authoritative, was that, in addition to his religions prestige as the 
Lieutenant of Sheik Schamyl, he now, with the title of Pasha, was thought to 
exercise the delegated supremacy of the Sultan. In the semi-barbarous feudal 
institutions of the Caucasus, there is undoubtedly much that is odious and 
reprehensible. Not only has everything in the shape of revenue to be raised in 
kind, but the Chief himself, accompanied by his retainers, is the collector, living 
at free quarters among his vassals, and shifting them as often as he may find it 
convenient, The abuses of such a system are, of course, deplorable enough, 
though Tam told that of late vears they had been extensively reformed. The 
difficulty, however, is to substitute some principle of authority for that of a 
feudal character, exercised by the nobles. The theocratic principle proclained by 
the Naib is, in my opinion, the most objectionable of all. In the meanwhile, 
if we be not prepared to see all the Kuban provinces involved in the same 
anarchy which prevails in Shapsook and Natquoitch, the prescriptive rights of 
the privileged class must be maintained; they offer the only chance for raising 
a force for offensive operations in time of war, and, through the influence which 
the nobles have over the Mussulman population beyond the Kuban, of producing 
an insurrectionary movement there; but they are chiefly valuable from the 
guarantees they present for the security of the border, when peace shall have 
been re-established with Russia, If, anong the conditions of sucha peace, the 
independence of Circassia is to be comprised, an undoubted advantage will result 
from the circumstance that three-fourths of the frontier is occupied by tribes 
whose habits and institutions are such as to favour the maintenance of peaceful 
relations with their neighbours. | 

By these considerations I was prompted to give my support and counte 
nance to the cause of the nobles—a line of proceeding which was moreover in 
strict conformity with the tenour of the firman of which I was the bearer to 
Mustapha Pasha, and which forbade all interference with the ancient institutions 
and privileges of the Circassians. As a direct consequence of this step, which 
placed me virtually at the head of the Ouzden party, more than 200 of whom 
rallied about me immediately, | was brought into collision with the Nath, who 
was the leader of the revolutionary faction. Availing himself of the oppor- 
tunity afforded by the levy of horsemen which had been commanded b 
Mustapha Pasha (and which his Excellency having thus caused to be assembled, 
had abandoned, without a word in the way of instructions or orders, to their 
own devices, suddenly leaving the Circassian coast for Batoom), he had marched 
into Bzadoog, and joimed there by the Tocavs, engaged in the work of revolution 
and plunder under his auspices, a league had been entered into by the Tocays, 
by which they bound themselves, under pain of a heavy fine, not to receive the 
Quzdens into their houses, and not to pay them any of their customary dues 
and requisitions in future, They had, in some inatances, gone still further; and 
in the commission of these excesses, | was tald they had been encouraged by the 
Naib. Hearing, therefore, that he was in the neighbourhood, I invited him to a 
conference; he accepted the invitation, but on proceeding the next day (the 6th 
of July) to the place of rendezvous, 1 found that, in defiance of the rules of 
courtesy, he did not come out to meet me. I stopped short, therefore, 
and insisted on his so doing; for I felt assured that, if | submitted to 
this intentional slight, | should at cence forfeit the consideration which 
is indispensably necessary to the success of any negotiation here, Much 
fruitless parleying took place on this point of etiquette, in the course of which 
the Naib, through his messengers, repeatedly stated that his reason for not 
receiving me with the customary honours was, that when he had, on previous 
occasions, sought. to cultivate friendly, relations with Agents of the British 
Government, he had fallen into disgrace at Constantinople; that, in consequence 
of hia having paid a visit of ceremony to Her Majesty's Ambasandor, he had,

incurred the serious displeasure of the Porte, which had, since that time, 
suspended the monthly pay he was entitled to as a Pasha of first rank; and 
that, subsequently, on his return to Circassia, his friendly reception of two 
English Agents, Captain Brock and Captain Hughes, had also given offence to 
Mustapha Pasha, who had, in consequence, withheld a sword of honour, and a 
declaration which had been sent to him by the Sultan. I received, the same 
evening, a message from him through a confidential emissary, called a Mustazik, 
confirming the statement which had been thus publicly made, and adding that 
the people of Abbassahk had been much irritated by this provoking conduct of 
Mustapha Pasha, who, as a further proof of his displeasure, had done everything 
in his power to obstruct their commerce on the coast. They had, in conse- 
quence, threatened to put him to death if he held any further correspondence 
with the Christian Governments. 

In all these accusations against the ‘Turkish officials in Circassia, | fear 
there is but too much foundation in fact. The jealousy with which they view 
the establishment of cirect relaticns with the Cireassians on our part, is but too 
apparent Still it was equally obvious to me that the artful Lieutenant of 
Schamyl was, in addition to the satisfaction he derived from the prospect of a 
iiuntesatandline between the Porte and the British Government, by no means 
eorry at the excuse afforded him for waiving all political discussion with me, 
which, with reference to the part he was taking in the movements of the Tocays 
of Bzadoog, might have been anything but agreeable to him. In regard to the 
question of etiquette, however, [ completely gained my point; his own followers 
compelled him the next day to pay me the compliment which his fanatic pride 
had objected to; but in the popular assembly of armed and mounted Circas- 
sians, in the midst of which our conference took place, he persisted in his refusal 
to treat with me on public affairs, again affirming that Mustapha Pasha had been 
offended at his communicating with agents of the British Governmant, and 
finally referred me for an answer to the Chiefs of Abassakh, by whom he was 
surrounded. They, on their part, proved no less intractable, declaring that 
without an order from Mustapha Pasha they were not warranted in listening to 
anything that 1 had to say to them. I vainly offered to produce a copy of the 
Imperial firman, in which Mustapha Pasha is commanded to afford me every 
assistance in communicating with the Circassian Chiefs; and I as vainly appealed 
to the persons whom he and Sefer Pasha had appointed to accompany me, but 
it was all to no purpose; they dreaded my intervention in the affairs of 
Bzadoog, and were determined, if possible, to prevent my having a hearing on 
the subject. 1 nevertheless told them my inind, as weil as the contents of the 
Sultan’s firman with reference to the maintenance of the ancient institutions of 
the Circassians, and I flatter myself that my representations were not wholly 
without effect, since, as 1 was afterwards informed, the Naib and his troop of 
incendiaries retired immediately upon my departure into Abassakh, leaving the 
Ouzdens and Tocavs of Bzadoog to settle their disputes among themselves. 

I hope this may prove to be true; itis, inmy humble opinion, most desirable 
that the authority and privileges of the nobles should be maintained. 1 believe, 
that in return for our support, if it could be reasonably extended to them, they 
would prove useful auxiliaries in the war; they might even furnish us with a 
Contingent of Irregular Cavalry, But their assistance would be chiefly valuable 
to us in the event of an expedition being directed against the communications 
of Russia with her trang-Caucasian provinces, An expedition for this purpose 
might be undertaken either on the line of the Kuban, and with the cooperation 
of the Mussulman tribes, or from the south-eastern coast of the Black Sea, 
through the country of the Abkhases and Mingrelia, where the population is 
Christian. In the former case many advantages would present themselves, 
which, in the general ignorance prevailing with respect to these countries are, T 
apprehend, but little appreciated. 

The plains of the Kuban, from Anapa to the sources of that river. abound 
in every resource which an army could require; they are well watered, and 
would yield a plentiful supply of cattle and provisions. ‘The inhabitants, as far 
as the Keatard are eager to make war against the Russians, and would rally 
enthusiastically around an expeditionary force in that quarter. 

While in the neighbourhood of Ekaterinodar, we fell in with a deputation 
consisting of twenty Chiefs from Kabarda, who had come to ascertain what 
prospect there was of their being supported if-they should rise en masse against 


131 


the Russians. Now, as an essential condition of our being able to profit by the 
assistance of these tribes, it is necessary that they should be at peace among 
themselves. Nothing, therefore, could be more inopportune than the civil 
dissensions which have been fomented by the Naib. 

Fortunately for us, at the same time, the position of our Ambassador at Con- 
stantinople is such as, with a view of remedying these evils, to insure the application 
of the most effectual means and influences. ‘The mischievous power of the Naib 
in this respect is derived from the authority he claims as an expounder of the 
Mahomedan law; but the Sultan’s authority on such matters is far superior to 
his, and acknowledged in all these provinces to be paramount. If a firman, 
therefore, could be obtained by his Excellency condemnatory of the levelling 
doctrines of the Naib, ] doubt not it would be attended with the very best results ; 
and the Porte, 1am inclined to think, would the more readily concur with us 
that the pretensions of this man must be peculiarly obnoxious to her. She has 
long viewed his influence with growing alarm and mistrust; und the time perhaps 
is come when she would willingly put an end to it, and, as the promptest way of 
dealing with him for that purpose, issue a firman for his recall or removal from 
Cireassia—a course she would be fully justified in adopting, inasmuch as the Naib 
by his acceptance of the rank of Pasha in her service, has decidedly placed him- 
self in the position of a dependant on her. 

The opinion 1 have here expressed of the Naib, may appear perhaps to 
your Lordship too harsh and unfavourable; as it is generally believed, and with 
justice too, I rather think that he, more than anybody, contributed at a former 
period to uphold the independence of the Circassian tribes. But a system which 
may have been attended with its advantages at a certain epoch, and in the 
isolated state of the counfry, inay with a change of circumstances which 
eall for a liberal and unreserved cooperation with the Christian Powers, prove 
equally prejudicial. The Naib first made his appearance in Circassia, at a time 
when active resistance to Russian was almost exclusively confined to the 
central Province of Abbassakh; and he there, as the disciple and lieutenant of 
Schamyl, began to propagate the illiberal and fanatic doctrines of Shafy, by 
which all intereourse with Christians is held to be a contamination and a curse. 
Such a principle, while applicable only to the Russians, was of course favourable 
to the prosecution of the war to the last extremity; and as the Naib grew. 
more popular and powerful, he became also the scourge and terror of the 
surrounding tribes, who had, wholly or partially, capitulated. He is accused, 
but with what degree of truth I cannot say, of having behaved with cruelty and 
rapacity. But whatever mav have been his power in Circassia before the 
commencement of this wor, it was no sooner declared, and access to the coast 
afforded to the Turks by the instrumentality of the allied fleets, than the old 
feeling of loyalty to the Sultan, as Caliph, and the Supreme Head of the 
Mahomedan faith, was revived. Even the people of Abbassahk, with whom his 
influence had been the strongest, partook aE the general enthusiasm, and he was 
himself compelled to go with the tide. At a former period he had been accus- 
tomed to denounce the Osmanlees and their Sovereign as a degenerate race of 
Mahomedans; and he had even substituted the name of Schamy! for that of the 
Sultan in the weekly prayers for the Caliph’s health and prosperity. 

There unquestionably existed a design of founding a new Empire in the 
Caucasus, based upon the principles of Mahomedan fanaticism and domination, 
which were to be developed in all their primitive energy. This scheme, | 
believe, has not been abandoned by the Naib, but only adjourned to a more 
propitious moment. In the meanwhile he found it convenient to temporize; 
and, as the only means of retaining any share of power and influence, to present 
himself as an humble suitor at Constantinople. He was disappointed, however, 
in his expectations there; and not meeting the encouragement he had looked 
for, next addressed himself to the Representatives of the Great European Powers. 
The leapresnion he made on Her Majesty's Ambassador was, if I am rightly 
informed, not very favourable. Why it should have been so, I, at first, did not 


perfectly understand, nor did I do full justice to the coolness with which his 

Excellency met this man's advances, til [ was subsequently enabled, by my 

own observations, fully to appreciate his artful and dangerous character, ~~ 

Most certainly, if we are to choose between the cooperation of the Porte 

and that of the Naib for the accomplishment of our views in Circassin, we can 

have no hesitation in giving our preference to the former, His Excellency, |

12 


presume, must have felt from experience that he could deal much more confi- 
dently with the Porte, where something like liberality and enlightenment may 
be found, than the compound of cunning, ignorance, and fanaticism he would 
have to encounter in the Naib. crete 

Of the soundness of this policy 1 now feel thoroughly convinced, and 
though for the moment the functionaries, civil and military, whom the Porte has 
appointed to conduct her affairs in this quarter, appear to have been most 
unfortunately selected, I doubt not when proper representations shall have 
been made on the subject, a remedy will be applied. All these persons have 
evidently landed in Circassia under the false idea that they could not only 
reoccupy all the old Turkish possessions which had been ceded by treaty to 
Russia on the coast, but also make themselves masters of the whole country as 
far as the Kuban. All this they foolishly imagined could be done surrep- 
titiously, and without the knowledge and acquiescence of the allied Powers, 
Hence their jealousy and anxiety to prevent French or English agents penetrating 
into the interior. 

There can be no doubt that the Mushir, Mustapha Pasha, who had been 
appointed by firman Governor-General of Circassia, was much disconcerted by 
the tanour of the other firman of which I was the bearer, and in which the inde- 
pendence of the Circassian tribes is fully acknowledged and proclaimed. Though 
lavish, therefore, in his professions of friendship, and of his desire to forward 
the views of the Enelish and French Governments, | feel persuaded, and indeed 
have acquired the certainty, that he has exerted himself to the utmost to dimi- 
nish our influence and thwart our views. Not only the Naib, but his own 
agents and subordinates, both Turkish and Circassian, have admitted this to be 
the fact, and have everywhere pleaded his secret orders and instructions for 
disobeying those contained in e Bouyourouldi with which he had furnished 
us, We have, moreover, satisfactory evidence that even the operations of our 
Commissariat for the purchase of cattle on the coast have been in some measure 
obstructed by their intrigues, 

Next to Mustapha Pasha, the Turkish functionary who has shown himself 
most hostile to our views, as well as the most troublesome and impracticable, 
ig Sefer Pasha, He is by birth a Cireassian, but suddenly invested, for political 
reasons, with the rank of Pasha, he has the most extravagant ideas of the 
Sultan’s omnipotence, and of his own irresponsible power as his deputy. With 
respect to Circassian affairs also, he indulges in the most childish rhodomontade, 
go that it is impossible to treat with him seriously on any subject of importance. 
His superior in command, Mustapha Pasha, conscious of his indiscretion and 
utter incapacity, has placed him under the official control of a certain Mehemet 
Bey (a renegade Hungarian Colonel), who seems to have the power of diakaing 
a htiron all occasions when he may think it necessary. The only individ 
I have seen here in the employment of the Porte, who by his knowledge of 
affairs is capable of occupying a position in 4 manner honourable to himself and 
consistent with the real interests and dignity of his Government, is Behshet 
Pasha; and he also, according to his own acknowledgment, has been sent out 
in some anomalous Civil capacity to Soukoom Kaleh. 

Taking all the above circumstances into consideration, and convinced that 
the presence of the Turkish functionaries, civil and military, now stationed on 
the coast and in the interior is fraught with much more prejudice than advantage 
to the common interests of the allied Powers, I shall venture to submit to his 
Excellency Viscount Stratford de Redcliffe the propriety of suggesting to the 
Porte that they should all of them be withdrawn, In their place, an accredited 
agent, such as those appointed by the English and French Governments, might 
be instructed to act in concert with them for the attainment of such objects as 
the Porte would not be ashamed to avow. I have, in a former despatch to your 
Lordship, already observed that the religious supremacy of the Sultan is an 
element of power in Circassia, which it would be impolitic to overlook, and 
which, under prudent direction, might be of great avail in the prosecution of 
the present war. The agent of the Porte, whoever he might he and whatever 
his rank and office, should be fully informed as to the nature of this influence, 
so a8 not to confound it with eivil and military authority ; and he might be made 
to understand, that it was not more than an equivalent forthe material advan- 
tages which the English and French Governments had to offer for the develop- 
ment of the military resources of Gircassia, So forcibly have I been impressed 


183 


with the necessity of a complete revision of the system which the Porte has 
hitherto adopted in respect to Circassia, and also with the impossibility of 
carrying out the views specified in your Lordship’s instructions, unless a totally 
new policy be adopted, if not other functionaries employed, that I have, in 
concert with my French colleague, M. Champoiseau, determined for the present 
to suspend our negotiations with the Circassians, which we believe would have 
no useful result till we know whether our appeal to Constantinople has been 
successful or not. 

With a view of furnishing his Excellency Lord Stratford de Redcliffe with all 
necessary detail and explanations relative to the conduct of the Turkish agente! 
complained of in this despatch, 1 have sent to Constantinople a gentleman 
attached to the service of this mission, Colonel Yordan, a Polish officer in the 
employment of the Turkish Government; having the most implicit confidence 
in his integrity and intelligence. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) J. H. LONGWORTH.

My Lord, Therapia, August 2, 1855. 

IT does not appear that Omer Pasha entertains the intention of returning 
to his army before the middle of next week. I met him at a splendid dinner 
oren ee to his Highness, the new French Ambassador, and in general to 
the Ministers and Diplomate Body, by the Ottoman Secretary of State for 
Foreign Affairs. I hadno opportunity of conversing with him in private, but 
he expressed a wish to see me at leisure, and the day after to-morrow is 
appointed for our interview. 

In the meantime, it is evident that the Turkish Government persist in their 
plan of operations in favour of the beleaguered army at Kars, by means of a 
diversion on the side of Redoubt Kaleh. Opinions concur in rejecting the 
plan of sending reinforcements by Trebizond. The Seraskier assures me that

been duly conimunicated in the words of the instructions, to Fuad Pasha, the 
Grand Vizier, and the Seraskier, but they have had no effect in changing the 
intended plan, except that the Sultan's Ministers abstain from making any | 
numerical diminution of their forces in the Crimea, and cease to reckon upon. 
the Contingent as part-of the Asiatic expedition. 

The French Ambassador assures me that General Pelissier objects formally 
to any alteration whatever of the Turkish forces before Sebastapol and at 
soe i es eth docs not aoe the right of the Porte to provide for the

to be there completed and substituted for 20,000 men, who would be wit drawn

No. 121. 
Lord Bloomfield to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received August 18.) 


(No, 326.) 
My Lord, . Berlin, August 11, 1855, 

I HAVE asked Baron Manteuffel the impression which the Prince of 
Prussia has received as to the feeling produced at St. Petersburgh by the con- 
tinuanee of hostilities. His Excellency replied, that His Royal Highness had 
observed a good deal of despondency as to the probable results of the present 
campaign in the Crimea; and that he considered the pressure of the war 
was seriously felt throughout the Empire; but that there was a determination 
to carry it on withthe greatest vigour; and that Count Nesselrode had said to 
His Royal Highuess that if Sebastopol, or even the Crimea, were taken by the 
allies, of which he doubted, negotiations would not be rendered more easy : 
adding further, that he thought it would be useless to think of nevotiations until 
some important event had taken place, 

The Emperor Alexander, Baron Manteuffel continued, appeared to feel 
deeply the position in which he was placed, and the increasing difficulties of 
carrying on the war. The number of his forces, he observed, was enormous, 
but his Imperial Majesty was by no means confident of success, and happen 
what may, it appeared to be the general opinion, that his disposition to concede 
under reverses would not improve. 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) BLOOMFIELD,

No, 124. 
The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Stratford de Redeliffe, 


(No, 657.) 
My Lord, Foreign Office, August 13, 1855. 

WITH reference to your Excellency’s despatch No. 557 of the 30th ultimo, 
[ have to state to you, that instead of disarming the Albanians enlisted by Major- 
General Beatson, which would probably not be effected without bloodshed, it 
might be advisable to send the whole body of Irregular Cavalry now in process 
of formation at the Dardanelles, immediately to Eupatoria to be attached to the 
Contingent under Lieutenant-General Vivian, and there armed, and formed, and 
drilled. Cavalry is above all things wanted at Eupatoria, and it would be unad- 
visable to lose the services of the Albanians just when they are wanted, after 
having incurred the trouble and expense of getting them together. 

Your Excellency will ascertain Lieutenant-General Vivian's opinion on this 
matter, and take such steps with regard to it as you may together think 
desirable. 

lam, &c. 


(Signed) CLARENDON,

Inclasure in No. 126. 
Extracts from Consul Longworth's Despatch No. 7 of July 20, 1855. 


THE Naib said to Mr. Longworth, through his messengers, ‘‘that his reason 
for not receiving him with the customary honours was, that when he har on 
revious occasions sought to cultivate friendly relations with agents of the 
British Government, he had fallen into disgrace at Constantinople. In consequence 
of his having paid a visit of ceremony to Her Majesty's Ambassador, he had 
incurred the serious displeasure of the Porte which had since that time suspended 
the monthly pay he was entitled to as a pasha of first rank; and that subse- 
uently on his return to Circassia, his friendly reception of two English agents, 
aptain Brock and Captain Hughes, had also given offence to Mustapha Pasha, 
who had in consequence withheld a sword of honour and decoration which had 
been sent to him by the Sultan.” i ee A 3 
Mr. Longworth says ‘‘ the functionaries, civil and military, whom the Porte 
has appointed to conduct her affairs in Circassia, appear to have been most 
unfortunately selected,” and moreover “that we have satisfactory evidence that 
even the operations of our Commissariat for the purchase of cattle on the coast, 
have been in some measure obstructed by their intrigues.” 
Mr, Longworth observes “ that the religious supremacy of the Sultan is an 
element of power in Circassia, which it would be impolitic to overlook.

No. 128. 


Consul Gurdner to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received August 20.) 
(No. 14. Diplomatic.) 


My Lord, Jassy, August 8, 1855. 

THE paper which I have the honour to inclose for your Lordship's considera- 
tion, relates to the Moldavian Monasteries, which depend on the Greek Church 
beyond the frontiers, and is an historical record of their origin and foundation. 
It had been submitted at Vienna to the Conference, when it was communicated 
to me by the Prince, and was simultaneously forwarded to Constantinople, and 
I believe to his Excellency Viscount Redcliffe. It becomes the more interesting 
as there is at this momenta delegation from the Moldavian Government at 
Constantinople, consisting of the Minister of Public Worship and Instruction, and 
of M. Négri, Minister of Public Works, who had been the Prince's delegate at 
Vienna, endeavouring to procure from the Porte an approval of the projected 
reform of the Church Establishments in general in Moldavia, and of a larger 
apportionment of their revenues to the wants or the State. oe 

During the protectorate of Russia, the large revenues of these monasteries 
gave her a great ascendancy and influence beyond the frontiers, as the Greek 
Church of the East to whom they appertained, must have felt that they were more 
or less at her discretion ; but now that the baneful influence has providentially 
been removed, though it hovers still in opinion over the Church of the country, 
it becomes a question of deep policy if its whole establishment should be 
remodelled and assimilated for one local purpose or advantage by the Government. | 
The two branches of the Greek Orthodox Church, internal and external, are at 
present placed in Moldavia in open dissidence by their positions, The Moldavian 
Church pays one fourth of its revenues to the State, and its clergy receive a 
moderate stipend from the remainder. The church “ De Saints Lieux” . pays 
about one-twentieth part of its revenues to the State, and the remainder is subjected 
to no control but its own. ‘There may be therefore between the parties and the 
State at times, little Christian feeling. But the constitution of the Church in 
this country is a very serious consideration, my Lord, for the character of the 
Government, is the character which varying circumstances give to it; continual 
changes succeed on the soil, and the protection of Austria is merely a change, 
from being more beneficent, from the protection of Russia. 1 Warts. 

I have the honour to inclose an extract from the ‘ Moldavian Gazette,’’ of 
6th instant, which will dispel the doubts entertained by many of the continuance 
of the Austrian Military occupation ; tenders will be received for the victualling 
of the troops for seven months, from lst of October ensuing, Ryedtaa 48} 

I have not addressed his Excellency Lord Redcliffe, on the subject of the 
present, much of which would be superfluous, Iam not over confident in the 
postal route to the Turkish capital, but the Prince offers his own opportunities 
totally of course in opinion with him; I have no doubt that he acts with the 
best intentions, but may doubt if they be for the best. 1 had the honour of | 
forwarding on September 22, 1851, to Viscount Palmerston, copy. of the publi.

__ LES propri¢tés sises en Moldavie des Monastéres relevant des Saints Lieux, 
dérivent de donations faites i |’Eglise, soit par des Hospodars sur le domaine de 
Etat, soit par des particuliers sur leurs propres biens. 

Ces propriétés sont aujourd'hui au nombre de 215 réparties entre differentes 
communauteés religieuses de la maniére suivante : 


101 relevant du St. Sépulere; ‘ 
87 du Mont Athos; 
12 du Mont Sinai; 
5 du Patriar¢at de Constantinople ; 
3 du Patriarcat d'Alexandrie ; 
2 du Patriarcat d’Antioche ; 
5 du Monasttre Drian en Epire.

En consacrant partie de leur fortune a l’église, les fondateurs des monastéres 
ne se sont pas proposé uniquement un but d’abnégation et de picté; ils ont eu 
principalement en vue de fonder des établissements de charité et de bienfuisance 
qui fassent bénir leur nom, et de les doter d’un revenu propre a suffire aux 
besoins de leur destination, ainsi qu’A l’entretien en bon état ie édifices religieux 
et aux exigences du service Divin, destinées & perpétuer leur commémoration. 

Ces conditions originelles ressortent non seulement de la teneur de ceux 
parmi les actes de fondation qui ont vu le jour—attendu que les donataires 
soni here dit plus loin, se sont refuses en toute oceasion & les exhilier=maia 
Sent Sh fosumente publics qui se rapportent au réglement des affaires

édifices en bon état et de veiller 4 la repression dea abus et des dilapidations qui 
se commettaient sur les fonds mémes des propri¢tés. s

En 1799 le Prince Alexandre Ipsilanti fait prélever sur les revenus, des

aumdnes. La chrysobulle émanée & cet effet est basee sur les considérations

relevant des saints lieux en consacrant des biens-fonds i ces asiles saerés, obligent

Quant aux requisitions extraordinaires pour cause d’utilite publique, elles 
ont d’ailleurs été trés fréquentes, et les monastéres s'y sont toujours pretés 
comme i une exigence qui résultait de leurs obligations. 

En 1821 les héguménes (supérieurs) Grees ayant pris une part plus ou 
moins active i la révolution de la Gréce, et assisté cette cause par les moyens 
mis 4 leur disposition, ils furent expulsés par ordre exprés de la Sublime Porte 
de leurs monastéres, dont la gestion fat confiée 4 un Comité composé de boyards 
indigenes, et présidé par le métropolitain. 

‘Au mois de Mars de l'année 1826 le haut clergé Moldave invite a faire un 
bon choix de supérieurs pour les monastéres relevant des saints lieux, adressa & 
cet effet au Prince Jean Stourdza un anaphora qui a été revétu de la confirma-

monastéres sont grevés, eta la réparation des édifices qui se trouvent dans le 
plus mauvais état abandon et de délabrement. 

En Février 1828, sur V'insistance de la Cour de Russie & 1a suite du Traité

des fondateurs fussent suivies ponctuellement; au surplus d’avoir en vue la 
nécessité de réparer les edifices sacrés qui sont en ruine, et d’amortir les dettes 
dont les supéricures Grees avaient grevé les monastéres. Dans anaphora qui 
sien suivit de l’Assemblée Générale, ont pu remarquer les passages suivants :

toujours avoir leur ici en Moldavie, d’aprés lancienne coutume, et les contrats 
rédigés par les héguménes, les curateurs, et les boyards inspecteurs. Les affer-

de surveillants retribués, appews & défendre les intéréts qui leur sont conti¢s,

Inclosure 2 in No. 128. 
Consul Gardner to Viscount Palmerston. 


My Lord, Jassy, September 22, 1855. 

I HAVE the honour to submit to your Lordship, a correct statement of 
the revenues of the Greek Patriarchates and Monasteries in Moldavia, amount- 
ing approximately to 200,000 ducats per annum. To the amount of revenue 
applied to the above purposes, may be added a sum of at least equal magnitude 
from Wallachia, and a further sum of 100,000 ducats perhaps from the Greek 
Church in Bessarabia and in Transylvania, ‘These revenues are placed ina 
high degree, I believe, under the control or direction of the Russian Govern- 
ment, which was permitted by the Treaty of Kiuzuk Kainardji in 1774, to take 
the whole of the Greek Church in the Turkish dominions under its protection. 
The influence which it exercises beyond the frontiers must be therefore com- 
mensurate with the means placed at its disposal, and the rich endowments of 
the Moldavian Church are distributed with an equal attention to the same local 
influence, 

One of the chief monasteries in Moldavia, of the congregation of Mount Athos, 
has, since my residence here, been placed under the charge of an Ionian Archi- 
mandrite, or Abbot. It is the Monastery of the Three Saints, or Hierarchs. 
During three suecessive changes, the nationality of the incumbent has been the 
same. ‘The present abbot is the Archimandrite Nilos, of Cephalonia. Nothing 
can impugn the respectability of these nominations, which have proceeded from 
the congregation of Mount Athos; hut they have undoubtedly been submitted 
to approval here, 

I have, &e, 
(Signed) SAML. GARDNER, 


146 


Observations on the foregoing. 


The Treaty of Koutchouk-Kainardji corresponds in date almost with the 
frst dismemberment of Poland in 1772. The cession of the Bukovine was of 
two years preceding it, 1770. 

The Monastery of Three Saints is the celebrated gem of Byzantine archi- 
tecture, which contains the relics of the Patroness of Moldavia, Saint Paraskewa. 
When Larrived at Jassy, the Archimandrite Nicodeme Assant was abbot, born 
in Cephalonia, succeeded by the Archimandrite Sophronio Makri, also of Cepha- 
lonia, on whose appointment to the Monastery of Teodereni, the Archimandrite 
Nilos was sent to replace him. He claimed British protection as an Ionian, but 
I find that he is born in Gallipoli in the Dardanelles, and may be what he says, 
of Ionian origin. He has beer removed to another monastery, and the Asch 
mandrite Macuarius, an Ionian from Salonica, intitled “ calogero,” or monk, on 
Mr. Blount’s passport, has lately arrived, and has been installed as successor. It 
is quite a speculation, the incumbent making his terms or bargain with the 
congregation of Mount Athos, and farming the domains to others, generally ata 
great profit, but at some personal hazard and risk. Thus, when the Russians 
entered the Principality, or had a Te Dewm chaunted for the Emperor Nicholas, 
or his armies, the monasteries always furnished the choirs, to protect their 
revenues, 

The present Metropolitan Primate of Moldavia, with his clergy, chaunted 
the Te Deum for the victory of the Russians at Smope, and the whole of the 
Council of State and functionaries assisted, with the Moldavian troops, and the 
salute was fired by the Sultan’s own Artillery, previously offered and accepted, as 
a testimony to their loyalty. The Russians had military possession of the 
country. 

Jassy, August 6, 1855. 

(Signed) SAML. GARDNER.

No. 129. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Ear! of Clarendon,—{Received August 20.) 


(No. 577.) . 
My Lord, Therapia, August 4, 1855. 

THE time is more than come when, with all the deference due to Her 
Majesty's Government, I must claim relief from the very inconvenient and 
compromising position in which I have been long placed with reference to such 
parts of my duty as bring me into contact with matters of a military bearing. 
Your Lordship is aware, that in the exercise of my public functions under the 

resent unusual circumstances, 1 have relations to maintain with several 
nranches of service established, for the time, in this neighbourhood, and also a 
correspondence to carry on with some of them. Iam the channel of commu- 
nication between the Turkish Goyernment and the Commanders-in-chief of Her 
Majesty’s Naval and Military Forces in the Black Sea; and L look to both the 
latter for early information of such occurrences In the prosecution of war as the 
public interests comtmitted to my charge require. Subordinate departments, 
—the military depot for instance, and the He station here, the Commissariat, 
the Land Transport Service, the superintendence of Russian prisoners,—come 
also into frequent contact with Her Majesty's Embassy, and atten accasionally 
no small share of my attention. With the Tureo-British Contingent and 
Beatson’s Irregular Cavalry my intercourse is more direct, and my interposition 
necessarily more frequent. A certain degree of undefined responsibility attaches, 
moreover, to my action over these corps, the individuals of which, being for the 
most part Turks, can never so merge their character of subject in that of 
soldier as to be wholly removed from my interference. The respective com- 
manders of both are in the habit of referring to me on various matters of 
arrangement; but while 1 endeavour to afford them all the assistance in my 
power, I do not feel myself entitled to prescribe any particular course which in 
my judgment it would be best for them to pursue, | conceive, with respect to 
the Contingent, that I am at liberty to adyise, suggest, and in cases of 
emergency to recommend, and even protest, as to matters touching its con- 
struction, general discipline, and location; but I am not aware of having any 
authority to insure compliance with my suggestions. The position with respect 
to * Beatson’s Horse” is somewhat different in virtue of a power to disband his 
corps on certain contingencies; but the measures which are now in progress 
under iny direction for the re-establishment and maintenance of order at the 
Dardanelles would never have been questioned, and in part counteracted by 
General Beatson, if their warrant, instead of being derived from necessity, had 
rested on the clear ground of an expressed authority. 

The whole of this subject Foul lave been recommended sooner to your 
Lordship's notice, had I not heen restramed by a natural unwillingness to raise 
embarrassing questions at a time of much general occupation and difficulty. 
The inconvenience and risk of haying to act with uncertain responsibility were 
not, however, unfelt from the beginning, and now that the evil has largely 
exceeded its original proportions, 1 offer no excuse for secking a remedy, where 
alone it is to be found in any effectual shape. Without prejudice to the public 
service, as well as injustice ta myself, | can no longer sustain the twofold 
responsibility of either allowing # iistaken course to be followed out in spite of 
its pernicious consequences, OF arresting it in its progress by the exercise of a 
questionable and sometimes insulted authority. 

If due provision had been made for the circumstances inwhich Iam placed, 
‘+ is not too much to say that | never could have been exposed to the official 
disparagement with which a recent decision of the War Office, accepted by your 
Lordship, has to all appearance, visited this Embassy. 

L huve no wish to depreciate the merits of General Vivian, or to question 
his professional claims on Lord Punmure's attention; but ber Majesty's Repre- 
sentative is also entitled to some consideration, espectully under circumstances 
involving an imperative obligation on the Porte to net, # necessary appeal on her 
part to our Byrnpathics, and an alternative between most perplexing difficulties. 
All my reports on the subject bear witness to the conditional terms on which | 
entertained und considered the Porte’s expedients with the advantage of General 

2 4) 


148 


Mansfield’s advice. According to the answers addressed or transmitted by 
your Lordship to me, it might be supposed that General Vivian alone had fore- 
seen the objections, and indicated the wants of an expeditionary force, whereas 
the Porte’s suggestions were made byme, not, indeed, with general discouragement, 
which at such a moment would have been no less cruel than unwise, but with 
every precautionary guard against the dangers of an ill-prepared attempt, and 
every exertion to bring the original plan into a practicable shape, dependent for 
ite execution on an ample supply of means, on the consent of the allied 
authorities in. the Crimea, and, above all, on the approval of Her Majesty's 
Government. 

With respect to my correspondence with Major-General Beatson, and the 
proceedings which I have authorised at the Dardanelles, | am content to await 
redress at the hands of my immediate superior, and to trust for my justification 
to the circumstances, as they are established not only by my statements, but also 
by the various accompanying documents and opinions derived from other 
sources. I look to your Lordship for vindication from unprovoked aspersions, 
and to Her Majesty's Government for support in the exercise of an indispensable 
interference. 

Looking to the whole question of my exceptional position, as now brought 
under notice, I wish to be understood as seeking no increase of power, no 
extension of sphere, but simply a clearer view of what is expected from me in 
the several relations, to which I have already alluded, and a better defined 
circumscription of my duties and powers in those respects, more limited or more 
ample as the interests of Her Majesty's service and their effectual promotion 
may be found, on experience and further examination, to require. 

At no antecedent period has there been so much need as now of having at 
Constantinople a centre of weight and authority capable of controlling the 
many divergent tendencies brought incidentally into play, of meeting the 
demands, and of carrying out the views of Her Majesty's Government, The 
task is by no means an easy one. 

‘Throughout this extensive empire, disjointed and often discordant elements 
obstruct, by their actual want of arrangement and connection, the currents of 
public business, though in reality, by their fitness for better combinations, they 
contain the seed, if not the immediate promise, of those administrative and 
social improvements without which the efforts and sacrifices of the war will be 
made to little purpose. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE,

No. 130. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Eurl of Clarendon.—(Recetved August 20.) 


(No. 578.) 
My Lord, Therapia, August 7, 1855, 

| CANNOT better convey to your Lordship a full and accurate knowledge 
of my later proceedings with respect to Beatson’s Horse, and the grounds of 
their justification, than by transmitting the correspondence which has tuken 
piace Peepocting them, since the date of my last report to your Lordship on that 
subject, 

1 have found it necessary, in the exercise of a questionable authority, to 
act in some respects less simply and directly than I could have wished to do. 
My course hus been limited by the necessities in which it originated, and 
modified as well by the imperfect nature of the means at my disposal, as by 
consideration for the public interests involved in the maintenance and progress 
of the corps. Being led by information from various sources to liclieve that the 
irregularities and criminal offences committed by the troopers were attributable, 
with few execptions, rather to mismanagement than to inherent mulice or rooted 
discontent, | have endeavoured, by means of inquiry and correction, not onl 
to ascertain the true causes, but to arrest the progress of the existing evil. 
| was anxious to respect General Beatson’s Scant so long as he conformed 


149 


to the course of justice, and showed a readiness to act in support of the Mixed 
Commission, . 

The language in which he has thought proper to indulge on more than 
one occasion, will naturally attract your Lordship’s attention, and I am entitled 
to expect that such notice will be taken of it by superior authority as may 
secure me from a repetition of insulting or disrespectful insinuations on his part. 
He cannot be ignorant that he enjoyed the full benefit of my support and 
concurrence, until | had reason to apprehend that the disorders subsequent to 
the first desertions from his corps were in part to be ascribed to causes for which 
he was tesponsible himself. How far his conduct may find a sullicient excuse 
in the ate lee of his staff, and the difficulty of his position, I have no means 
of deciding to my complete satisfaction without a report from the Court of 
Inquiry. It is not, however, to be forgotten, that the Major-General neglected 
to embrace the opportunity offered deliberately for conferring with the Seraskier 
and myself. 

[| am assured by Mr. Skene that the labours of the Court are drawing 
towards a conclusion, and | have directed its final report to be communicated 
to me without delay, Meanwhile there is ample evidence of a continued, 
though partial, state of disorder among the troops, controlled to a certain 
degree by the reinforcements which have been sent, at my request, from here, 
in aid of the regulars who garrison the castles of the Dardanelles. The 
complaints, apparently well-founded, of the French, as conveyed to me, partly 
by their Ambassador and partly by General Simpson, contribute not a little to 
the awkwardness and gravity of so anomalous a situation. 

I have, &e. 


(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.
