Inclosure 1 in No. 150, 
Major-General Beatson to Lord Stratford de Redelitfe. 


My Lord, Dardanelles, July 30, 1855, 

I MAKE no further comments on the measure which your Excellency has 
thought proper to adopt in regard to myself, than by acquainting you that | this 
morning carried out the punishment awarded by the court-martial, of whose 
proceedings an extract is herewith sent, without the aid of a single Nizam 
soldier, and without disarming the Albanians, who with the rest of the troops 
formed three sides of a square in which the punishment was inflicted to the full 
extent, two medical men standing by. 

Having brought to your Excvllency’s notice in-a former letter the conduct 
of the civil Pasha, in delaying to give me notice of the robbery of the cavasses, 
I have now agraver accusation to make against him, that of an impertinent 
attempt to obstruct the course of justice, by refusing to give me up the prisoners 
this morning for punishment till 1 had sent several officers to demand them. 
And one of his officers, the Colonel commanding the troops, himself a member 
of the Court, had the insolence to say that the reason of the Pasha not giving 
up the prisoners, was that the proceedings were not quite finished. 

Such a remark was an insult to General Neill the President and me, which 
I hope your Excellency will notice, by demanding the dismissal of both the 
Pasha and Colonel. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) W. F. BEATSON.

The Court, having taken into consideration the evidence for the prosecu-

(Signed) W. F, BEATSON, Major-General. 


Proceedings continued. 


Twenty-three deserters from the 4th Regiment of Beatson’s Horse, taken 
by the ‘Turkish authorities, were accused of attacking four cavasses, who were 
carrying treasure and papers belonging to the British Government, and robbing 
them of the same. 

Only two of these men were identified by the cavasses, Coslan, and Hassan 
Gharfi, on the first of whom the papers were found after his capture. Hassan 
Gharfi returned to camp, and was arrested upon the evidence of his comrades. 

The Court, having heard the evidence, find the prisoners guilty, and sentence 
them to receive 500 lashes each, and to be exiled, this being the punishment 
awarded by Turkish law for highway robbery, and recommend Major-General 
Beatson to have the sentence at into execution with as little delay as possible, 
and before the whale force, as a wholesome lesson to the rest. 

(Signed) T. G. NEILL, 
Brigadier-General. 


————— ee ee 


Approved and confirmed. 


The sentence to be carried into effect to-morrow morning at 6 o'clock in 
presence of the whole of the troops, drawn up in contiguous close columns, 
under the direetion of Lieutenant-Colonel Morgan, Ist Regiment, on the low 
“round in front of the 2nd Regiment's camp. 

A medical officer will attend, who will stop the punishment when consi- 
dering it would endanger the prisoner's life, us I will not allow humanity to be 
ounces inany force under my command by allowing a prisoner to be flogged 
to death. 

After the punishment the prisoners will be made over to the civil authori- 
lies, to be sent into exile, according to the sentence, 

(Signed) W. F. BEATSON,

Inclosure 3 in No. 140, 


Mr, Skene to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe, 

(Private } 
My Lord, Dardanelles, July 30, 1855, 

ON seeing your Excellency’s letter to Mr. Calvert on the subject of the 
landing of any more of the Irregular Cavaly that might remain here, I took upon 
myself the responsibility of the immediate disembarkation of a ship-load of 
Arabs, a had been four-and-twenty hours in the harbour, and were jump- 
ing overboard. Three of them had already swam on shore. Ifeared that the 
order not to land them might produce some serious consequences; and I, 
therefore, decided the question at once—acting at least for the best. Mr, Cal- 
vert, to whom your Lordship’s letter was addressed, was not inthe way; but I 
was convinced that the spirit of the instructions was met by my doing so. He 
afterwards thanked me for it, aod General Neill fully concurred in what I had 
done. | trust your Excellency will will give me your approval. 

I need not relate the circumstances of the punishment which took place 
this morning, and at which | was present, as General Neill's letter of to-day 
expresses all 1 would state on the subject. He and 1 are quite of the same 


151 


mind in everything that concerns the Irregular Cavalry; and he shows me all 
his letters, If he were in command of it, your Lordship would have much 
reason to be satished, Indeed, I begin to think that the best thing that can be 
done would be to attach it to the Contingent, 

I beg to be instructed whether the report of the Court of Inquiry should 
remark on the measures (and no measures) taken by General Beatson, or 
merely be confined to a narration of facts proved by evidence. 

; L remain, &c. 


(Signed) J. H. SKENE,

Inclosure 4 in No. 130. 
Consul Calvert to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Dardanelles, July 31, 1855. 

I HAVE the honour to acknowledge the receipt of your Excellency’s 
despatch of the 28th instant, delivered to me by Captain Bourke. 

The Pasha, Mr. Skene, and myself, being all agreed with General Beatson 
that no harm could result, either to the peace of the town or to their own 
discipline, from the landing of the Irregular Cavalry brought here from Alex- 
andretta by the transport “Thames,” (previously reported by error as the 
Medway”), I have sanctioned the disembarkation of this portion of the force, 
which was effected at once. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) W, F. CALVERT.

Inclosure 5 in No. 130. 
Major-General Beatson to Lord Stratford de Redeliffe. 


My Lord, Dardanelles, July 31, 1855. 


| HAVE received your Excellency’s despatch of the 29th instant from 
Captain the Honourable J, Bourke, in eich you are pleased to remark that it 
will be for Her Majesty's Government to appreciate the circumstances uoder 
which I have announced my intention to resign my command, 

These circumstances are: The withdrawal of that support which I freely 
confess | have had up to the period of the desertion of afew of my men, but 
which failed me at the first difficulty, and at the time when it was most necessary 
to my success that it should be given to me earnestly and unreservedly : 

‘And the want of courtesy shown to me as a general officer, whose reputa- 
tion is based on the services he has performed in all situations, in a period of 
thirty-four and a-half years, by your Excellency communicating with an officer 
junior to me, on matters relating to the force under my command, otherwise 
than through me, Nay, to make the slight even more marked, I find that copies 
of your despatches to me are sent to this officer, but no communication is made 
to me of your instructions to him. | 

Brigadier-General Neill appears to be an officer of diseretion, and of him 
I have not the slightest reason to complain ; but to your Excellency’s way of 
acting in this matter, I feel confident that every military man whose opinion is 
worth having, will agree with me in thinking | have great reason to object. 

Further, instructions are sent to naval officers and Consuls regarding the 
detaining my troops on board ship, and then for the disembarkation of these 
troops, and the first intimation I receive of these measures is from the naval 
officer or the Consul. 

Your Excellency, knowing the opinions I have expressed as to the value of 
the corps I have been employed in organizing, and my confident belief, after due 
experience, that the task, with your support, was practicable—and knowing 
also the success which I assert has hitherto attended my exertions—can jud 
how painful it is to me to place my resignation in the hands of Her Majesty's 
Government, but I feel it due to my position as a general officer, and the 
character J have acquired in my long service to withdraw from a task in which, 
with your aid and encouragement, I might have hoped to have been of great 


2K 


f i ta! 
lie ee 


Pate 


service in this war, but in which—with lukewarm support at best, and some- 
times with not doubtful opposition—I know it will be impossible to succeed, and 
1 appeal to Her Majesty’s Government to judge if I have been fairly treated. 
I have, &e. 
(Signed) W. F. BEATSON.

Inclosure 6 in No. 130. 


Major-General Beatson to Brigadier-General Neill. 
Sir, Head-Quarters, Dardanelles, August 1, 1856. 
| HAVE the honour to acknowledge the receipt of your letter of this day’s 
date, making suggestions to me as to the discipline of the force under my 
command, You state that no respectable woman can leave her house, and that 
the soldiers are in the habit of fixing the prices of the goods they wish to 
purchase in the bazaars. I haye to request you to state any instance in which a 
respectable woman has been insulted in the town, or any instance lately in 
which goods have been sold from fear of danger from the arms carried by my 
men. Before I can believe such a statement, it must be shown to me that arms 
have been used for this purpose: for the slightest knowledge of the customs of 
the country will show that high words constantly occur between vendor and 
purchaser, without any probability of violence being used; and mere unsupported 
statements do not warrant you in making such charges against the force under 
my command, 

I beg to remind you that your statements, that it is unmilitary for troops 
to carry their arms, ts incorrect, as it is customary in all Continental armies, ard 
in the native Irregular Cavalry in India; and I must also remind you that the 
inhabitants of this country are eenerally in the habit of carrying arms, and I 
decline to expose my men unarmed to insult and murder. 

_ In conclusion, 1 must request you to recollect that you were sent here to 
assist me as my junior officer; and it was only a suggestion of Lord Stratford's 
that you should be employed as President of a Court of Inquiry ; and I have to 
request that you attend solely to the duty upon which I have employed you ; 
and if you think any communication with me requisite, that you will personally 
attend to make it, and receive my orders on the subject. 

I have, &c. 


(Signed) W. F. BEATSON.

Major-General Beatson to Brigadier-General Neill. 
Sir, Head- Quarters, Dardanelles, August 2, 1855. 
I HAVE the honour to acknowledge the receipt of a letter from you in 
answer to one I addressed to you yesterday. 
You did not use the courtesy due to me as your superior officer by neglect 
ing to call and personally offer me the advice you are bound by your instructions 
to offer me, and the reasons you may have for giving that advice. 

Nothing has transpired to my knowledge to warrant me in taking the 
measures you propose, and [ will not, from mere unfounded rumours, take a 
step fraught with the utmost danger, and likely even, if successful, to alienate 
the affections of my troops from me. 

| Lhave, &e.

Mr. Skene to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Dardanelles, April 3, 1855. 


1 DEEM it unnecessary to trouble your Excellency with a a i 
of all the incidents occurring here, which I see fully reported by rigadier- 
General Neill. I shall, therefore, confine myself to a mere genera statement : 
ist, that the procecdings of the Court of Inquiry are fast drawing to a close, 
and comprise a mass of evidence of a grave nature; 2ndly, that the Mixed 
Commission progresses also in the dispensation of justice, but, | must regret to 
say, not without considerable difficulties, and occasional failure in meeting: its 
ends, arising from obstacles appropriately commented on in General Neill’s 
despatches to your Excellency ; and Brdly, that the disorderly state of the 
Irregular Cavalry has certainly been in some degree improved in the town, where 
the troopers are overawed by the presence of a Regular. force; but that depre- 
dations im the country have, if anything, rather increased, probably on account of 
the produce being now more generally ripe. 

Tam not aware of any steps being taken to repress these disorders. I do 
not cease to offer General Beatson every information I may possess, with the 
view of assisting him, according to your Lordship’s instructions, and of inducing 
him to adopt measures of obvious expediency. With what success these endea- 
yours may meet, remains to be seen; hitherto I cannot boast of their having 
produced any results, 

1 can now state, as a fact of which 1 feel certain, what I expressed pre- 
viously as a matter of opinion, that no ‘nsurmountable impediment exists to the 
perfect organisation of this force as an eflicient Light Cavatry. 

] have, &¢. 


(Signed) J. H. SKENE. 


Inclosure 10 in No. 130. 
Brigadier-General Neill to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Dardanelles, August 3, 1855. 
IN forwarding for your Excellency’s information the following correspon- 
dence connected with the Mixed Commission, of which Tam President, on the 
trial of trooper Ahmed Hassan, I have the honour to state that before the 
assembling of the Court 1 have asked General Beatson to detail more expe- 
rienced British officers of higher rank as members, and afford me the assistance 
usual on these cecasions, a staff officer to attend the Court, and stationery to 
enable it to make out its proceedings, General Beatson having notwithstandin 
on this trial given me two very inexperienced officers, Captains Platt aa 
Mathews, who have just landed from England, no staff officer, and no writing 
materials, 1 had some difficulty in getting the proceedings written; Captain 
Mathews although most willing, and did his best, was incompetent for this 
duty. I had many errrors to correct, and in forwarding them to General 


154 


Beatson I again urged him to grant me the detail I had applied for, to be 
present at the Court which T had directed to reassemble on the following day 
the Ist instant at 2 p.a, The following forenoon I received a letter from 
Gereral Beatson stating that his Adjutant-General would receive immediate 
directions to hear the prisoners’ evidences, &c., present at the hour requested, 
that he had already issued such orders as will enable me to recover any outlay I 
might be put to in providing stationery, which he was informed could be 
procured ut this place; but the steps thereto he must leave to me, General 
Beatson also reminded me that I was responsible for the writing and the pro- 
ceedings. That he could not attach a staff officer for that purpose, and that it 
was not usual ornecessary for such to attend the Court. He stated he had placed 
as members the twooflicers of his force next in seniority to those in command 
of regiments, with whose presence it would be hurtful to discipline to dispense. 
In a postcript to his letter (which Ido not forward as I have unfortunately 
written on in the rough draft of my reply), General Beatson concludeded by 
stating that if I considered it absolutely necessary for my assistance to have 
attached to the Court an officer to act as Judge Advocate,” and make official 
application for such, he would appoint one, and forward my letter as a voucher 
for the expense incurred, With this letter was inclosed, for my information, to 
be returned, the proceedings of the trial of Ahmed Hassan, with the Major- 
General’s remarks. 

I received the above after I had been one hour at the place of assembly of 
the Mixed Commission, and after | had sent Captain Mathews, the only officer 
preset with a message to General Beatson, informing him that the officers he 
had promised were not present; but receiving no satisfactory reply, and one 
hour and u-half having elapsed, I adjourned until 2 pm. the next day, I 
addressed General Simpson, vide copy to my letter dated the 1st instant, 
annexed, and the following morning returned the proceedings of the trial of 
Alimed Hassan (having taken a copy of it, which I enclose), with a letter from 
me dated the 2nd instant, copy of which is annexed; this produced General 
Beatson’s reply through his acting Deputy Adjutant-General, which was satis- 
factory (it algo accompanies this correspondence); and on the Commission 
meeting at 2p. although two junior officers, and one of therm Captain Platt, 
were furnished as members; the Staff-officer, with his writing material, was 
present, and this Court has been the first which has been properly conducted, 
and the proceedings regular 

| have now troubled your Excellency with a long correspondence, caused 
by what appeared to me # disinclination on General Beatson’s part to assist in 
bringing these prisoners to trial. I beg to direct attention to his remarks on 
the trial of the two men sentenced on the first day the Commission assembled 
to 500 lashes each, I am informed your Lordship has been furnished by the 
Major-General with a copy of these remarks. I conceive they were pertectly 
uncalled for, and could only tend to produce an injurious effect on the minds of 
the troops, and bring the Commission into diseredit with them; the punishment 
was by no means severe, and was not so severely inflicted as it ought. With 
regard to his disproval of, and remarks on the sentence of 200 lashes to the 
Arab trooper Ahmed Hassan, again the Court is severely reflected upon, and 
an offender deserving punishment eseapes. It gives to these troops the appear- 
ance of a desire to screen their crime, and save them from punishment. 

That the effect being produced on the minds of the men, is tending to evil, 
I have to report as follows: 

Yesterday it was the intention of a body of these Irregulars to ride into 
the town for the purpose of stating to General Beatson their presrence for 
his administration of justice to that of the Mixed Court, Information of their 
intention was conveyed to General Beatson by Mr. Skene, and they did not 
come into town, as by Mr. Skene’s advice he was induced to go out to camp to 
speak to them; this J understood he did. 1 am not in possession of further 
facts or particulars ; but this, in connection with the Major-General’s not afford- 
ing the Court proper assistance and support, and the remarks I have requested 
your Excellency’s attention to, speak for themselves. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) J. G. NEILOG,

Brigadier-General Neill to Major-General Beatson. 


Sir, Dardanelles, August 1, 1855. 


I REGRET much to report to you, that in consequence of the non- 
attendance to-day of all ‘Le acaba of the Mixed Court, of which tam 
President, [ was obliged, at half-past 3 1.M., to adjourn it The prisoner, and 
all the witneases against him, with Colonel Sankie, were present, also Captain 
Mathews, but neither staff officer, the other member, nor writing material, as | 
requested, aul | 

Your letter of this day’s date, objecting to comply with my request of 
yesterday reached me about 3 p.a., and before | received your reply to my 
message conveyed by Captain Mathews, that the Court was waiting for the 
other members. oar | abae 

I again beg to repeat my application for semor and more experience 
officers than Captains Mathews and Platt, who have just, as they inform me, 
arrived: the first is very willing, the other absented himself to-day ; neither are, 
I conceive qualified for this important duty, if others of greater experience are to 
he had. 

As regards the stationery, it is usual to furnish it at each garrison or camp 
where a trial takes place; Iam much engaged here, and it is impossible T can 
provide writing materials for the trial of the soldiers under your NON 
surely it can be supplied by some staff officer or department of your force. f 
you cannot provide me, | must apply to the ‘Turkish Government for the 
assistance | cannot obtain from you, this I trust [may not be compelled to do. 

A staff officer to attend a Court as Prosecutor is required at all courts- 
martial inferior to general, At a general court-martial a Judge Advocate: 
General performs the duty of Prosecutor, &e. ; at all Courts: the Prosecutor 
writes the proceedings. The President 1s responsible that the Court is properly 
conducted, and for the correctness of the writing. At the majority of the 
trials I have. presided at here, the proceedings have been irregularly and badly 
written, in consequence of my not having had the proper person present to 
perform that duty furnished to me; badly supplied as I have been with writing 
materials, and with inexperienced, although neglected to be so recorded. 

I strongly object to putting the State to the expense of a Judge Advocate, 
who is not required, but 1 agam urge wpon you the necessity of having the 
British officers, with the writing material I have requested, ready to-morrow, at 
2 p.u., at the place of assembly, in order that there may be no further delay in 
bringing to a close the trials of the prisoners now in confinement,

Sir, Dardanelles, August 2, 1855. 


I HAVE the honour to return the proceedings on the trial of Ahmed 
Hassan, of the 2nd Arab Regiment, and in reference to your disapproval and 
remarks, bez to observe as follows: | 

The offence with which the prisoner was charged was # breach of good 
order and military discipline, and wherever committed was of a purely military 
nature; in this ease it was committed in a garrison town, close to where your 
troops were encamped, : 

The frat witness Beken Mulazim, ought not to have been sworn as you 
remark, he, however, liad been duly and properly affirmed according to the Pu rkish 
law, and that a record of the same was not entered on the proceedings, was in 
consequence of the inexperience and inability of the officer who wrote the pro- 
ceedings, As you will observe on the proceedings; I had to correct them in 
many places, but overlooked this eeNnn badly written as they are, L had

some difficulty in getting them completed mn any way the officer was most willing 
and did his best. Had you returned the proceedings and inquired why the 
witness had not been affirmed, the mistake would have been rectified. 

As regards your remark, and 1 presume excuse for the prisoner, that the 

istols were loaded with powder only, there was no proof how they were loaded. 

I regret exceedingly that by the escape of this culprit, the ends of justice 
should have been so defeated, and the example of a punishment so well-deserved 
lost. ‘To guard against all possibility of the recurrence of what is so detrimental 
to discipline, the escape by any slight flaw or mistake, of a culprit so deserving 
of punishment, | have most urgently to beg of you to afford me your utmost 
support and assistance, in enabling me sufficiently to prosecute the trials of these 
prisoners. | have heen sent down for the performance of this and other most 
important duties, by order of his Exellency Lord Stratford de Redcliffe, and look to 

ou for every assistance in placing efficient means at my disposal for carrying out his 
Lordship's orders. If the duties of your commandants of corps are so important 
at this time, that they cannot be spared for employment on the trial of your 
men, may I beg that the services of some of the officers sent down here by 
General Lord William Paulet. with a view to their services being made use of, 
may be granted for this most important duty ? 

In conclusion, I beg to remind you that much valuable time has already 
been lost in consequence of the late or non-attendance of parties under your 
command composing the Mixed Court ; on Monday aud Tuesday little was done ; 
yesterday nothing. 

The effect, this mode of proceeding cannot fail to produce on the minds of 
the Turkish officers of rank acting with me, must be most prejudicial, and T can 
answer for the painful impression on myself, as displaying a want of respect to 
Her Majesty's Ambassador. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) J. G. NEILL,

Inclosure 14 in No, 130. 
Report of the Trial of Ahmed Hassam. 


THE Mixed Commission, of which Brigadier-General Neill is President, 
assembled at the Dardanelles, on the 31st July, 1855, 


Members : 


Captain T. J. Mathews. 

Captain F. W. Platt. 
Brigadier-General Suleyman Pasha. 
Colonel Muhiedin Bey. 

Major Mehmet Aga. 


Commissioners : 


Mr. Skene. 
Djemel Pasha. 


Sworn Interpreter, Isanc Russo, 


The President, Members, Commissioners, and Interpreters being all present, 
Trooper Ahmed Hassan, of the 4th Arab Regiment, is brought before the Court, 
aa the following charge is made against him by the head of the police of the 
place: 

Yesterday, at 4 o'clock p.m., the prisoner knocked ata door in the town ; 
it was opened by an Armenian woman. He asked her for water to drink. She 
was frightened, ond ran to the house; he fired his pistol after her. A 
sentry of the Turkish Regulars hearing the shot, came up and made him a 
prisoner. 

The prisoner pleads Not guilty. 

The Court proceeds to hear evidence in support of the charge against him, 


157 


Bekir Mulazim, of the Turkish Regulars, stated® that he was on euard 


yesterday, that he heard a shot, and on looking round he saw the prisoner with 
a pistol in his hand, and the sinoke still near him, He asked him who fired the 
shot. ‘The prisoner said he fired it, because the woman would not give him 
water to drink. 


Armik.an Armenian woman, stated, after being duly sworn, that she heard a 


shot, and saw a pistol in the hand of the prisoner, and then she shut her door. 
Being asked if the shot was fred at her, she says she does not know, and that 
there was no other woman near. 


The prisoner's two pistols are produced in Court, one loaded with powder 
alone, and the other discharged. 

There being no more witnesses to produce before the Court, the evidenee 
for the prosecution is closed, and the prisoner 18 called upon to state his 
defence. 


Defence. 


The prisoner states that he had bought the pistols yesterday, and loaded 
them with powder, then fired one of them to see if it would fire. 

The Court having considered the evidence adduced in support of the pro- 
secution, as well as what the prisoner has stated in his defence, is of opinion 
that the prisoner is guilty of having fired his pistol; but that it is not proved 
that he fired it against the woman or any one else. 

The Court, therefore, sentence the prisoner to receive 200 lashes, according 
to the custom of the ‘Turkish army, to be inflicted at such time and place as the 
officer confirming these proceedings shall be pleased to direct. 

The Court is adjourned until 2 r.at. to-morrow. 

(Signed) J. G. NEILL, President, 
Brigadier-General, Turlnsh Contingent. 
Dardanelles, July 31, 1859. 


Disapproved. 


‘The whole proceedings are irregular. The offence having occurred in the 
town was clearly of a civil nature : therefore, the President should have been a 
Turkish officer. 

Bekir Mulazim, the witness, was nut sworn, 80 his evidence goes to the 
ground. 

From these irregularities, added to the fact of the pistols having been 
loaded only with powder, I directed the release of the prisoner, who is to be 
admonished that he is not to amuse himself by firing even blank-cartridge in a 
town. 

August |, 1855. 

(Signed) W. F, BEATSON, 
Major-General, Commanding at the Dardanelles, 

‘The proceedings to be returned. 

(Signed) W. F. BEATSON.

Inclosure 16 in No. 130. 
Brigadier-General Mansfield to Major-General Beatson. 


Sir, Embassy, Therapia, August 5, 1855. 

I HAVE the honour, by command of Her Majesty’s Ambassador, to 
acknowledge your despatch of the 31st ultimo received yesterday, 

His JUixeellency further desires me to inform you, that in consequence of 
your having announced, in your previous despatch of the 28th ultimo, an inten- 
tion to resign, and transfer your command to any officer who might be sent 
down to receive it, he has been under the impression that it was your intention 
to cease immediately from the performance of military duty, while at the same 
time, as stated in that despatch, you remained at the Dardanelles to use your 
influence with the troops for the preservation of order. 

Although, therefore, a copy of the despatch of his Excellency to you in 
answer ta that communication, was transmitted to Brigadier-Gencral Neill for 
his guidance, it had become, in the belief of his Excellency, unnecessary to 
trouble you with further communications, | 

Despatches have been received from Brigadier-General Neill, reporting 
progress to the 2nd instant, since his arrival at the Dardanelles. It appears in 
the two latest, that the reports which have reached his Excellency from various 
and influential quarters are fully confirmed by the Brigadier-General, after the 
Courts over which he presides had been sitting for some days. 
_ The rigadier-General is able to state, from personal observation, as well as 
from what hus transpired in the course of his judicial inquiry, that although the 
demeanour of the troops under your command is good on parade, and that the 
appearances of discipline have been maintained in the public punishment of 
offenders, the town and country in the neighbourhood of your camp are not 


only ina state of alarm, but absolutely in danger, in consequence of the lawless 


conduct of the Irregular Cavalry, and the absence of measures to apply a proper 
degree of restramt, j 


159 


His Excellency has been favoured by the Brigadier-General with copies of 


the letters addressed by the latter to you on this subject, and of your replies, and 
he is compelled to give his testimony in favour of the soundness of the recom- 
mendations, which, in the exercise of your discretion, you have been pleased to 


reject. 3 
Ungracious as the task may be, his Excellency cannot shrink from the duty 


imposed on him. He cannot, in consequence of your despatches, refuse atten- 


tion to the reports on the state of your troops, with reference more particularly 
to the population amid which they are stationed, now that they have been 
guaranteed by the deliberate report of the General Officer, who has acted with 
so much discretion and calmness in the performance of the difficult duty with 
which he has been charged by his Excellency. aye 

I am to request, therefore, on the part of the Ambassador, as speaking m 
the name of Her Majesty's Government, that you will either proceed without 
delay to act, as regards the means of quelling disorder on the suggestions of 
Brigadier-General Neill, or that you will adhere to the determination already 
announced in your despatch of the 98th ultimo, and transfer the command of 
the troops at the Dardanelles to that officer till the pleasure of Her Majesty's 
Government shall be known. The words used by you in that despatch are as 
follows :— 

« J cannot, in justice to myself, do otherwise than place my resignation in 
the hands of Her Majesty’s Government, and make over the command to any 
officer whom your Excellency may send to take it over, though 1 shall at the 
same time remain at the Dardanelles throughout the difficulties which will be 
caused or increased by the unnecessary and injudicions measures NOW in progress 
with regard to military arrangements.” 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) W. R. MANSFIELD. 


P.S. A copy of this despatch has been sent to Brigadier-General Neill.

Inclosure 18 in No, 130. 
Brigadier-General Mansfield to Major-General Beatson. 


Sir, Embassy, Therapie, August 6, 1859. 


I HAVE the honour to inform you by desire of the Ambassador, that the 
proceedings of the Mixed Commission of which Brigadier-General Neill is 
President, in the trial of trooper Ahmed Hassan of the 4th Arab Regiment 
under your command, have been under the consideration of his Excellency, 


together with your disapproval and remarks. 


His Excellency regrets that he is unable to eoincide in your views, expressed 


in the Jatter. 


I have it in command to refer you to his Excelleney'’s despatch of the 
Pb 


24th ultimo, in whieh it is laid down that the Mixed Commission is to be a final 
tribunal for the trial of all offences, cases of murder, and other crimes requiring 
capital punishment, being alone referred to the civil courts. 

[t appears from your remarks and orders of release for the prisoners, that 
you have somewhat misconceived the abject and power of the Mixed Com- 
THISSION. 

Tt is an extraordinary tribunal formed under the concurrent authority of 
Her Majesty's Ambassador and of the Porte, to meet a very extraordinary 
combination of circumstances, into the detail of which it ig not necessary for me 
to enter. Its power transcends, if 1 may be allowed the expression, that of an 
ordinary court-martial. The Mixed Commission does not, in the opinion of his 
Excellency, require your approval, although to ensure appearances of unanimity, 
such approval may be desirable. But the disapproval you have been pleased to 
record, and the order for release of the prisoners condemned by the Commission, 
are, in the opinion of his Excellency, in the highest degree calculated to destroy 
the effect of a measure, which has been devised to restore the authority of justice 
in your neighbourhood, and to introduce a salutary dread of the consequence of 
lawless excesses among your sop His Excellency requests, therefore, that 
you will be pleased to consider the Mixed Commission as characterized in this 
despatch, and if you have cause for disapproval of its proceedings, that you will 
contine its manifestation to a recorded protest, for immediate communication to 
the Commission and to his Excellency, which is not to be made known to the 
prisoners or to the troops. 

Brigadier-General Neill has received instructions to transmit all proceedings 
against which you may direct such a protest, for the consideration of his Exeel- 
lency before execution of sentence. 

I am further to inform you that several despatches have been received from 
various authorities at the Dardanelles, bearing date to the 3rd August, in which 
it is stated, that although some improvement is apparent in the behaviour of 
the Irregular Cavalry in that town, owing to the presence of the regular troops, 
the conduct of the former, if there be a difference, has been more outrageous and 
dangerous in the neighbouring country than it was before. 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) W. R. MANSFIELD.

as senior officer succeeding him, will, in the natural course, address them direct 
to his Excellency.

Inclosure 24 in No. 180, 
Major-General Beatson fo Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, The Dardanelles, August 3, 1859. 
IN forwarding the accompanying copy of a letter from Major Hackett 
whom I had yesterday officially appointed to act as Judge Advocate to the 
Court on which Brigadier-General Neill presides, and in the feelings expressed 
in which 1 entirely concur, Ihave to state that | have at once relieved Major 
Hackett from the daty in question, and that 1 have only refrained from noticing 
Brigadier-General Neill's conduct, in not giving effect to my orders, by placing 
him. under arrest, {rom a desire not to impede the progress of the important 
inquiry upon which he is engaged. 1 leave it to my superiors to take such 
notice as they think fit of the conduct of the Brigadier sent down on this special 
service, and merely state what I have done to exonerate myself for not taking 
a step which could not but be painful to my feelings, but which military disci- 
pline would naturally have dictated.

according to the opinion of his Excellency, the power and functions of the Mixed 
Commission consist. 

On consideration, therefore, you will perceive that the presence of a Judge 
Advocate-General, whose duty it is to conduct the proceedings of, and to advise, 
4 court-martial under the AnRioney of the President of such Court, as well as to 
prosecute, 1s inadmissible in the extraordinary Court constituted under the 
coneurrent authority of Her Majesty's Ambassador and the Porte, which has 
been denominated a Mixed Commission. 

His Excellency cannot but believe that you are under some misapprehension 
with relation to any intentional disobedience of orders on the part of Brigadier, 


163 


General Neill, who, a5 President of the Mixed Commission, is probably of the 
same opinion as his Excellency, that he was not em owered to admit any 
authoritative functionary besides the. officers civil and military already convened 
as above stated. ne 6 : 

A report has not been as yet received from Brigadier-General Neill on this 
subject, but his Excellency is aware that he had requested the attendance of a 
staff officer to write the minutes and conduct the prosecution.

Inclosure 26 in No, 130. 
Brigadier-General Neill to Lord Stratford de Redeliffe. 


My Lord, Dardanelles, July 30, 1855. 


I HAVE the honour to acknowledge the receipt. of Brigadier-General 
Mansfield’s letter of the 29th instant, with copy of your Lordship’s of the same 
date to Major-General Beatson. 

The Court of Inquiry, of which I am President, commenced its proceedings 
early this morning; ¥t adjourned at noon, until to-morrow at 6 A.M. 

The Court-martial will reassemble to-day at 2 P.M., and as much as possible 
will be gone into of the two casts disposed of on the afternoon of the 28th: the 
frst, a Bashi, concerned in the affray in which a French soldier was wounded by 
another Arab, was fully acquitted. ‘The second case, of four other men, also 
‘Arabs, charged with plundering @ treasure escort, two men acquitted, and two 
found guilty, and centenced, according to the Turkish code, to 500 lashes each, 
and afterwards to be exiled, All this punishment was inflicted this morning 
before all the Irregular Cavalry, paraded for the purpose. Had it been a punish- 
ment parade before the best-diseiplined corps i Her Majesty's or any other 
service, the demeanour of the men could not have been better, ‘There was no 
talking nor excitement of any kind; the men sat steadily on their horses, 
observing closely what took place, but grave, silent, and respectful im their 
bearing. None of the regular troops were present, Major-General Beatson 
assuring me of his perfect confidence in his men. 

The punishment, had it been properly inflicted, ia said to bea very severe 
one, The man is laid down on his face, and flogged witha rod on his posteriors. 
‘The chaoushes, however, did not at frst strike as strongly as they ought, until 
pressed to do their duty. British medical officers were in attendance, watching 
the punishment, as is the order in our service ; and this, | believe, was the only 
deviation from the Turkish practice. After the punishment was fully inflicted 
on both prisoners, they were handed over to the civil powers to be exiled, 


a 


[shall proceed with the Court of Inquiry, and the trial of the prisoners, 


with all despateh, and shall to-morrow, if there is an opportunity, do myself the 


honour of making 4 further report to your Lordship. 
I have, &c. 
(Signed) J. G. NEILL,

Inclosure 27 in No. 150. 
Brigadier-General Neill to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Dardanelles, July 31, 1855. 

1 YESTERDAY did myself the honour of reporting progress up to noon. 
In the afternoon the Mixed Court assembled at a later hour than I intended, in 
consequence of some mistake; but we disposed of one case, that of a Kiatib, for 
violent conduct and language to the Commissariat Officer, of which he wns 
found guilty. In consideration, however, of the prisaners good character, that 
he had been carried away by over geal, and that he had been in close confine- 
ment for some days, the Court awarded him only ten days further imprisonment. 
There were other circumstances connected with this man, which although not 
hrought before it, weighed with the Court, He is the person whose wile was 
violated by Adam Kolassi, Hussein Mulazim, and others. 

2U 


js | 


__ This morning the Court of Inquiry commenced its proceedings, and went 

into as much as it could. 

From what has already come to light in the inquiry, there can be no doubt 
but that the mistaken and ill-judged eonfidence placed in Adam Kolassi, has 
caused much of the excess and disorders which have occurred; also that he had 
headed the party which released Hussein Mulazim, took him to General Beat- 
son's house and obtained his arms; and that the Kolassi and Mulazim both 
violated the writer's wife. Considering, therefore, that it 18 of the utmost import- 
ance that these two men should be secured, brought to justice, and if found 
guilty, made an immediate example of hetore the troops here, | beg to suggest 
to your Lordship that a reward of 40/. should be offered for the apprehension 
and delivery here of Adam, and 20/. for Hussein Mulazim. I would at once 
myself have advanced these sums, so confident do I feel that by such means 
only would the culprits be soon secured; but not seeing my way clearly as 
to who would eventually repay mt, may | beg your Lordship’s advice and 
instruction. i ) 

I may also state, that from what has fallen under my own observation, | am 
of opinion that the interior economy and discipline of the Irregular Cavalry here 
would be better promoted and its organization ensured, had General Beatson an 
efficient staff, such as Adjutant-General and Quartermaster-General attached 
to him. These should be intelligent, experienced, and steady staff’ officers 
acquainted with the duties of their respective departments, through whom all

: Inclosure 28 in No. 130. 


Brigadier-General Neill to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Dardanelles, August 1, 1855. 


| HAVE the honour, in continuation of my report to your Excellency of 
yesterday’s date, to state that the Mixed Commission, of which Tam President, 
assembled yesterday afternoon; but, in consequence of the witnesses against the 
prisoners not having been in attendance; no Staff officer nor materials for 
recording the proceedings having been provided; and the two British officers, 
Captains Mathews and Platt (two young men who arrived from England on the 
previous day), not being, in my Gpinion, of sufficient rank und experience for 
such an important duty, [ could on get through one trial, preferring to get on 
with that rather than incur further delay, as 1 felt assured that my reference 
to General Beatson to remedy the detect could not have been immediately 
attended to. 

The Court disposed of the case of an Arab trooper, charged with having 
fired his pistol at a respectable woman of this town, at whose door he had 
knocked and asked for water, which was refused to him. ‘The man could only 
be convicted of having fired his pistol, but against whom could not be decided. 
He was sentenced to 20%) lashes. I have not heard whether the sentence has 
been approved of by General Beatson, and if so, when and where it is to be 
earried out. | 

This morning, at 6 A.M., the Court of Inquiry, of which Lam President, 
assembled, and we made fair progress ‘ni our investigation. We have just 
adjourned until the same hour to-morrow. . 

From what transpired at the Court of Inquiry this morning, and at the 
trial yesterday, also from the number of armed troopers | see riding into the 
town, and lounging about, I addressed General Beatson as follows : 


‘From what has been stated in evidence before the Court of Inquiry, of 
which Lam President, it would appear that, up to this time, the troopers of the 
force under your command are allowed to ride into this town, and in the 
neighbourhood of your camps, with their arms, their pistols being loaded. 


Tn such a state of alarm are the inhabitants, that no respectable woman, it 
is said, leaves her house ; and there are frequent disputes at the shops in the 
bazaar in consequence of your troopers insisting upon fixing their own prices 
for articles they wish to purchase ; and, as has been too frequently the case, the 
temptation to use those arms may be indulged in. Under all these circum 
staiices, and considering that confidence will not be restored, nor order re-esta- 
blished, until your men are forced to conduct themselves in a more peaceable 
manner: and it being against every military rule that troops, except before an 
enemy, should be permitted to go about, when not on regular duty, armed, I 
have earnestly to press on your consideration the propriety of immediatel 
issuing such orders as will in future prevent your men leaving camp with 
their arms.” 

I trust that General Beatson will see the necessity of losing no time in 
complying with my request ; should he not do so, and disorders in consequence 
of these armed men going about continue, I shall consult with Mr. Skene and 
Mr. Calvert, as the instructions of the 26th ultimo direct, and request your 
Excellency’s representative Mr, Skene to put me ina position, empowering me 
to carry out these orders and putting a decided stop to the irregularities which 
still exist. 

I again report to your Lordship my decided opinion that there are no 
difficulties whatever in organizing these men, and 1 feel contident that under 
proper discipline, they will become most eflicient light cavalry. 

Since the above was written T have received Major-General Beatson’s reply 
of this date, which I beg to inclose. I have informed the Major-General that by 
your Excellency’s instractions of the 26th ultimo, I have addressed lim ane 
have quoted for his guidance the particular paragraph. | have also informed 
him that | derived my information from the Court of Inquiry, and from the 
trial of the Arab troopers yesterday. My counse! having been rejected, should 
it be nec sssary 1 shall not eases to obey your Lordship’s orders. 

L have, &e. 
(Signed) J, G. NEILL,

Sy much had been written when | returned from the place of assembly of

In conclusion | beg to impress upon your Excellency the necessity of forcing

to a good state of efficiency by firmness and a regular system of discipline. 
Let them know. that justice will be done them, and their comforts attended to, 
but crime and excess put down at onee, whenever it appears. and punished. 
Courting popularity by permitting or countenancing irregularities, or what 1s 
worse, screening crime with & view of keeping these men in good humour, 18 a 
evievous error; such a false system induces crime, and gives these men an idea 
that their officers fear them. They must be ruled firmly amd justly, or the 

manization will not succeed.

No. 131, 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon. —(Received August 20.) 


(No, 552.) \ 
My Lord, Therapia, August 8, 1855. 
OUR latest advices from Kars, received to-day, are of the 21st ultimo. 
As Brigadier-General Williams addresses your Lordship, I need not repeat the 
substance of his despatch to me. I observe, on the one side, with satisfaction, 
that the garrison had in store nearly three months’ provisions, and that nothing 
had yet occurred to impair its means of resistance. It appears, on the other, 
that General Mouravielf, operating by means of his Cavalry, had blocked up the 
Olta road to Erzeroum, and also that which passes through Ardahan, The 
defences of the town are described as having been strengthened at the point most 
likely to be attacked, while notice is given that reinforcements, unaccompanied 
by convoys of provisions, would be of no avail. It would seem that the com- 
munications by messenger, though probubly circuitous, were still open, und I 
should hope that the alarming rumours of the arrival of Russian troops at 
Tiflis from the evacuated forts in Circassia were, at least, premature. 
__It results, on the whole, from these circumstances, that the arrival of 
timely relief on the Asiatic frontier is still by no means an impossibility, ‘Phe 
natural difficulties, however, are greatly increased by differences of opinion and 
numerous conflicting demands on the scanty and scattered resources of the 
Porte. Whatever is to be done must be undertaken within certain limits, and 
in such manner as neither to trench on the means employed against Sebastopol 
We : 


= 6 > = 
al os pay ; 
ee 


nor to expose the Contingent to any trial of strength for which it is not fully 
prapared. Such is the opinion of Her Majesty’s Government, which leans, 
moreover, with all its weight on Trebizond, as the only true channel of relief. 
The commander of the French forces would see with regret the departure of 
Omer Pasha from before Sebastopol, especially if his Highness were attended by 
any portion of his troops now stationed there. The Pasha himself is ready to 
take the command of an expeditionary force directed on Kutats from Redout- 
Kalé, and he reckons for that purpose on 10,000 men from Batoom and the 
Circassian coast, on 17,000 from Balaklava, on 4,000 from Kertech, and on 
5.000 from Bulgaria, with the necessary addition of Artillery and a small force 
of Cavalry. He would like to replace the 20,000 men thus proposed to be 
withdrawn from the Crimea by the Contingent, completed to its full amount by 
a draft of 10,000 men from Bulgaria. He gives as the reasons of his preference 
for the troops at Balaklava, that he knows them better than the battalions at 
Eupatoria, and that they are habituated to his command. He complains, as to 
Kertch, that the numbers of French and Turkish troops left in station there, 
were by agreement, to have been equal, and he seems to be resolved on vindi- 
eating his right by reducing his own detachment from 8,000 to 5,000 men, the 
deficit occasioned by its reduction having to be supplied by a proportionate 
increase of the French. 

It is not unworthy of remark, that Omer Pasha, on whom would devolve 
all the risk, fatigue, and responsibility of the expedition, reckons with cheerful- 
ness on the means of carrying it into effect, and even anticipates with con- 
fidence a successful result. According to his convictions the army, and the 
two fortresses, which protect the Asiatic frontier of Turkey, are in imminent 
danger, and their fall would expose the empire on that side to the most perilous 
disasters, including a heavy loss of supplies to the allied armies in the Crimea, 
He does not conceal his opinion that an attempt to send forward reinforcements 
by ‘Trebizond would infallibly prove abortive ; and that the only fair prospect of 
frustrating the Russian attack, would be a diversion from Redout-Kalé. He 
asserts, that no serious obstruction is to be apprehended to the west of the 
defiles beyond Kutais, where the arrival of the expeditionary force would at 
once compel the Russians to retire from Kars, in order to cover their vast 
unprotected magazines at ‘Teflis. 

It can hardly be denied that the scheme is plausible. The military autho- 
rities here, in so far as 1 have access to them, are decidedly favourable to it; and 
the French Ambassador, in bringing to my knowledge the adverse opinions of 
his Government and of the Comm«ander-in-chief in the Crimea, made but a faint 
attempt to disguise his personal leaning towards the Turkish enterprise. 

The course which 1 have pursued amidst so many motives to a vigorous 
support of the only practicable scheme of relief, and so many perplexing con- 
siderations fatal alike to every hope of realizing it in time, partakes naturally of 
the difliculties which I haye had to encounter. 1 made no reserve in communi- 
eating the adverse opinions of Her Majesty's Government to the Porte; I have 
continued to urge the necessity of respecting the paramount interests engaged in 
the Crimea, and to abstain from experiments unprovided with ample means of 
execution, 1 have at the same time lent am attentive ear to all the suggestions 
of the Porte, and L have not repressed those hopes which sometimes ripen into 
views of just conception and successful issue. 

An expedition composed of European forees would not ory give better 
promise of success, but also a security against the political results of ‘Turkish 
conquest, But I have yet to learn that there is any prospect of succour in that 
form, or any chance of relief, but what is derivable from the Sultan's troops, 

It only remains for me to inform your Lordship, that several duvs ago I 
received from Fuad Pasha an official note, whieh, as it related in part to the 
Contingent, was addressed to me alone, but communicated, in copy ta the 
French Ambassador, 

| have since replied to it, though in some respects T should have preferred 
taking the same line as my French colleague, by waiting for your Lordship’s 
instructions ; but my position was different trom that of M. Thouvenel, who had 
received only a copy of the note, while I, to whom the note was addressed, 
could not, without danger of misconstruction, leave unnoticed some passages of 
so Unportant a document, 

Copies of both the notes ure forwarded to your Lordship herewith; and it 


169 


will be found, I imagine, on a perusal of mine, that in secking to correct what- 
ever was overdrawn or mistated in the Turkish note, [ have studied to attain my 
object less by any controversial process than by offering a statement of my own, 
more strictly in keeping with fact and real intent. 
[ have, &e. | 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

Inclosure 2 in No. 131. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to Fuad Pasha. 


Therapia, August 4, 1895, 

THE official note which the Undersigned has received from his Excellency 
Fuad Pasha, under date of the 3st ultimo, relates, t must be allowed, to 
matters requiring immediate and earnest attention, 

The advices which have reached the Undersigned from Kars, agreeing 1n 
substance with those received by the Porte, leave no doubt as to the hazardous 
position in which the Sultan’s army there has been placed by the advance of 
the Russian forces from Gumri. Whatever may be the sentiments with which 
he looks back upon the treatment experienced by that army, notwithstanding 
his frequent remonstrances and urgent recommendations, he is now only anxious 
that while there is yet time, efficient measures may be adopted, if possible, for 
‘ts relict, ‘The discussions and explanations, in which he has been for some time 
engaged, may be contidently a ypealed to in proof of his sincerity. When latterly 
called upon to declare the opinions of his Government, he performed that duty 
with a painful sense of the embarrassments which surrounded the Porte and its 
Ministers, ‘The interest with which he had received their communications, and 
entertained the consideration of their plans, sufficiently evinced his sympathy, 
which, however, if taken in the most favourable light, could possess no value 
beyond that of a comparative und conditional assent, under circumstances 
warranting a more than ordinary degree of haste and risk. 

The friendly counsel of an ally so cordial and disinterested as Great Britain, 
éould have but one purpose in view, namely, tint of inducing the Porte, instead 
of heaping one error upon another, to adopt and carry into etlect such measures, 
as appeared at a distance mast calculated to effect the desired result. 

twas to be hoped that sa essential an object might be obtained without 
prejudice to operations of equal or greater importance elsewhere, without dimi- 
nishing the allied forces united in the Crimea, or compromising by premature 
experiments, the organization and efficiency of the ‘Turco-British Contingent. 
There might not be the same object to withdrawing troops from Bulgaria, 
he Contingent completed by a draft from that quarter, might, perhaps, take its 
station in the Crimea, and occupy without impradence a position vacated by a 

tay F 


—————S 


oe en | 
ie? 
- =e 2 


— ——

172 


part of the Turkish army now there. Though Her Majesty’s Government have 
declared their decided preference for a more direct operation by Trebizond and 
Erzeroum, their objections to diversion on the side of Circassia would, in all 
likelihood be modified, if the force employed were of a compact and reliable 
character, and the dangers arising from local unhealthiness, avoided by an 
immediate advanee into the interior, supposing at the same time that a compe- 
tent knowledge of the localities and aclequate arrangements for transport and 
supply were to come in aid of those advantages. 3 

While thus replying in a spirit of personal good-will, to Fuad Pasha's note, 


the undersigned niust not be understood to answer for the final concurrence of 


his Government, Were the Porte to announce an intention of operating, as its 
only resource, from the coast of Asia for the purposes and under the limitations 
traced out above, the undersigned, however he might wish to obtain facilities for 
its success, could only engage to lose no time in referring as well to his Govern- 
ment as to the naval and military authorities in the Crimea, One thing is 
unquestionable: a danger in which the whole alliance is interested in averting, 
impends over the Porte, and whatever measures least open to objection may 
reasonably be viewed as efficient for that purpose ought, if employed at all, to 
be employed with vigour and despatch. 
The Undersigned, ke.

| HAVE received your Exeelleney’s despatch No. 582 of the 8th instant. 
My various messages by telegraph and my despateh No. 630 of the 4th instant, 
which you will have received since the date of your despatch, will have shown 

ou that Her Majesty’s Government, in conjunction with that of the Emperor 
of the French, were willing that Omer Pasha should proceed to Asia to effect a 
diversion for the relief of Kars, and Her Majesty’s Government in that case, no 
longer insisted on the view which they had entertained at first, that the relief 
should be effected by the way of ‘l'rebizond. 

Omer Pasha, as commander of the Sultan's troops, will be free to direct 
his movements in the manner most beneficial to the common cause; and the 
only limitation placed by the two Governments on is pre is the condi- 
tion that the movement on Asia shall not lead to the diminution of the Turkish 
force employed before Sebastopol and Yenikale; while the Turkish Contingent 
under General Vivian, may be made available for filling up the room of the 
Turkish troops whom Omer Pasha may take with him from Eupatoria.

No, 133. 
The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


(No. 673.) 
My Lord, Foreiqn Office, August 23, 1855. 
HER. Majesty’s Government have fully considered the observations offered 
by your Excellency in your despatch No. 577 of the 4th of August, with the 
view to obtain a distinct intimation as to the position occupied by Her Majesty's 
Ambassador at Constantinople in regard to military matters arising out of the 
rosecution of the war; and I have to state to your Excellency in reply that 
Jer Majesty’s Government are of opinion that the line of demarkation of the 
Ambassador's duty in regard to the matters treated of in your despatch is not 
difficult to be drawn. 
All the military officers employed by the British Government in the 
Crimea and at Constantinople, and elsewhere in the Bast, must of course be 


lid 


deemed to be under the orders of the Home Government, and must receive 
instructions and decisions on doubtful questions from the Secretary of State for 
the War Department, or in the ease of Brigadier-General Williams from the 
Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs. 

Bui all communications which those officers have to make, and all applica- 
tions which they have to address to the Turkish Government would best be 
made through the Ambassador, if of sufficient importance to require his 
intervention. 

If the Ambassador were to give orders and directions to the military officer, 
there would be danger of conflict between such orders and those which 
might be coming out from England, and much inconvenience might thus arise. 
In any case of such difference of views something would be done in the Levant 
which the Home Government might wish to have done differently, or if the 
‘nstructions from home came in time to prevent the execution of those iven by 
the Ambassador, the position of the Ambassador would not be iinproved. 


Tam, &e. 
(Signed) CLARENDON.

No, 135, 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Karl of Clurendon.—(Received August 24.) 


(No. 608.) 
My Lord, Therapia, August 13, 1855, 
AT my interview yesterday with the French Ambassador and Fuad Pasha, 
the latter repeated the assurances already conveyed to me in such decided terms 
through M. Pisani, of the absence of every pretension on the part of the 
Turkish Government to interfere with the internal administration of Circassia or 
in any way to impede the full enjoyment of national independence by the tribes 
inhabiting that country. His Excellency observed in particular, that even if the 
Porte had entertained the wish of founding a claim upon any jurisdiction which 
had ever been exercised in her name within the precincts of Circassia, the cession 
which she had made of the whole territory to Russia by the Treaty of Adrianople, 
would preclude her from attempting to gratify it. He denied that any orders had 
been given to the Porte’s functionaries on the coast in contradiction to those 
which had been issued at my request. M. de Thouvenel agreed with me in 
proposing that an Ottoman Agent should be joined with those of England and 


174 


France for the purpose of communicating, when necessary, with the natives, and 
obtaining from them such assistance as they might be disposed to lend towards 
carrying out the common object of the war, and the adoption of such measures 
as might be found indispensable for enabling them to act in concert, and maintain 
in time of peace the obligations of good neighbourhood. In order to act through- 
out in consistency with such a course of proceeding, it would be necessary, we 
said, to confine the Turkish authorities now in Circassian to duties of a strictly 
military character, and it was doubtless desirable, I added, that some kind of 
change should take place among those who had evidently given a wrong direc- 
tion, or at least a false colouring, to the policy of their Governments, and who 
might be justly suspected, like Mustapha and Safer Pashas, of being actuated by 
personal motives and local interests. oe 

Foad Pasha made no objection to the political part of these suggestions, but 
he did not appear to enter with much conviction mto my views respecting the 
removal from office of either of the above-mentioned Pashas, and as the French 
Ambassador seemed rather to lean towards his way of thinking, I did not feel 
myself at liberty to press the matter to a decision at that moment. 

On the whole, I entertain a sanguine hope of being able to obtain the 
Porte's concurrence in that line of policy respecting Cireassia which is most 
congenial with the wise and liberal sey oe the two Western Governments. 

I have, «ce. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

yresent at its deliberations. ‘The decision, whatever it may be, at its close, will

mode of action ealculated to impede or discourage the general operations of the 
alliance appeared to meet with the full admission of Fuad Pasha.

No. 138. 
Lord A. Loftus to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received August 29.) 


(No. 10. Confidential.) 
My Lord, Berlin, August 21, 1855. 

I HAVE obtained from a private and most secret source communication of 
a despatch lately addressed by Prince Gortchakoff, at Vienna, 9 his Govern- 
ment. It cominences by stating that the greatest skill and caution must be 
observed in the relations of Russia towards Austria; that one false move at the 
present moment would entirely destroy the fruits of a whole year's labour ; that 
it required “une main de velour ;” and that, more especially, the greatest care 
should be taken to consult the feelings of the young Emperor, and not to wound 
his susceptibility, 

It refers, in terms of great satisfaction, to the success already obtained, in 
having brought Austria to “lower the flag of the enemy ;" and at having thereby 
annulled an army of 300,000 men at so small a sacrifice to Russia, treating the 
evacuation of the Provinces and the occupation by Austria as being rather of 
advantage than a loss, and referring to the concessions made with respect to the 
Danube, as not being more than what previous Treaties had provided for. It 
further states that thus much had been gained by skilful management at a time 
when Austria, although in a state of bankruptey, could not have been restrained 
(ne se serait point laissée arrétée”) [rom cooperating with the Western Powers, 
It expresses great satisfaction that, through the instrumentality of Prussia, the 
German Diet had not bent to the wishes of Austria, in expressing an approba- 
tion of her policy which would have amounted to a“ concurrence’ with it 
(qui aurait valu son concours”’), and that in this respect it was highly advisable 
that Germany should make no further advance, or take fresh engagements. It 
was this policy which he sought to impress on the German Representatives at 
Vienna; and he considered that Frankfort was now the great and important 

ont. 
; He then remarked that on no account should any outward expression of 
triumph be given vent to, and that too much care and precaution could not be 
taken to prevent any falling back of Austria towards the Western Powers, He 
added that the success which had been so far obtained was not due to his 
exertions, but to the protection whieh God vouchsafed to holy Russia. 

The foregoing is but a very imperfect report of the text of this despateh, 
which was of considerable length, and drawn up with consummate skill and 
dexterity. It was read to me in so hurried a manner that {could merely retain its 
general substance and the impressions which it conveyed, 

En résumé, | may state to your Lordship that the general tone of this 
despatch seemed to exhibit great satisfaction at the result so fur obtained, in 
detaching Austria from an active alliance with the Western Powers, and, in 
inculeating the great necessity of acting with skill and precaution, some latent 
fears ure apparently entertained that Austria may still be forced to make common 
cause with the enemies of Russia. 

I have, &

(Signed) AUGUSTUS LOFTUS.

No. 140. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Receined Augus! 29.) 


(No. 601.) 
My Lord, Therapia, August 15, 150. 

HE course pursued by Major-General Beatson is a cause of increasing 
embarrassment and anxiety to me, as well as to those who are acting under my 
instructions at the Dardanelles; instead of employing the means which | have 
placed at his disposal for maintaining his troops ina proper state of discipline, 
and protecting the inhabitants of the neighbourhood from their irregularities, he 
asserts, in contradiction to the statements and evidence derived from other 
responsible sources, that the preventive measures recommended for his adeption 
are both unnecessary and injurious, nor does he seruple to act in such a manner 
as to impede the course of justice, and to make his corps an object of alarm 
and disgust, to those who have most reason to desire its success. 

I forward some additional correspondence, which will enable your Lordship 
to test the correctness of these remarks. 

Among the documents which are inclosed in copy herewith, is a despatch 
addressed by me on the Isth instant, to the Commander-in-chiel of Her 
Majesty's army in the Crimea, for the purpose of requesting that an officer of 
sufficient rank may be immediately sent to supersede Major-General Reatson, in 
the command of the Irregular Cavalry. 

Having no authority to set him aside myself, and having exhausted all avail- 
able means of bringing his conduct into harmony with the public interests, at 
the same time that 1 am anxious to'spare Her Majesty’s Government the morti- 
fication of disbanding that corps, | have no alternative but to apply to 
Lieutenant-General Simpson for support and relief, until the pleasure of Her 
Majesty’s Government could be made known by telegraph. 

Meanwhile, 1 have sanctioned the enforcement by the civil authorities, of 
those preventive measures, whieh I had vainly recommended to the adoption of 
Major-General Beatson, and 1 am waiting anxiously for the report of the Com- 
mission of Inquiry, and the decision of the Commatder-in-chief,

Inclosure 1 in No. 140. 


Lord A. Loftus to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Berlin, August 18, 1855. 

IN conversation yesterday with Baron Manteuffel, [ referred to a reported 

communication which had lately taken place between the Austrian and Prussian 
Cabinets on the Oriental question. His Excellency informed me, that it was 
true a note had been lately addressed by the Austrian Cabinet to that of Berlin, 
but that he did not consider it of any importance, and that in reality it was mere 
phraseology, and meant nothing. 
He then told me that in this communication Count Buol informed him 
that the Austrian Cabinet had received a declaration from France, which had 
been concerted between London and Paris, stating that the Western Powers 
considered those Articles of the Treaty of December as still valid which had 
been in apparent abeyance ; and secondly, that the Western Powers still main- 
tained the four points as the minimum of their terms, reserving to themselves 
full liberty to make any additional demands which they might judge necessary, 
according to the eventualities of the war. 

Count Buol, in apprising the Cabinet of Berlin that the maintenance of the 
four points was thus recognized by the Western Powers, secks to induce 
Prussia to accept the four points in their totality, and to appropriate them in the 
same manner, and to place herself with respect to them on the same basis as 
Austria had done. 

| am given to understand that a civil but vague and deelinatory answer has 
been returned by Baron Manteuftel, and Tam confidently assured that there is 
no intention on the part of the Government to enter into any fresh engagements 
with Austria, with respect to the Oriental question, 

Baron Manteuffel evidently considers that this invitation was not made 
with any seriousness on the part of Austria, He seems rather to think that 
Count Buol is anxious to allow the matter to remain for the present in 
suspense. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) AUGUSTUS LOFTUS.

Lord Stratford de Redelijfe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Reeeived August 29.) 


(No, 607.) 
My Lord, Therapic, August 16, 1855. 
THE Ottoman Council was assembled yesterday for deliberation as to the 
most effectual means of succouring the beleaguered army at Kars. M, Pisani 
having been suddenly taken ill, and Fuad Pasha not having attended at the 
Porte, 1 am still in ignorance of the decision, if any, which may have been taken 
hy the Council. The Porte’s intention, beyond a doubt, is to operate a diversion 
by Kutaig, and the question is now with what troops, and in what manner that 
intention may best be earried out, The Government and Omer Pasha alike 
acquiesce in the condition that the number of the Turkish troops in the Crimean 


shall not be diminished, and that the 'Turko-British Contingent shall make no 


part of the expeditionary force ; but the Pasha objects most positively to the 
plan, transmitted from London by telegraph, of stationing the Contingent at 
Eupatoria, and he is not prepared to assume the responsibility of commanding 
the expedition unless the ‘Turkish troops before Sebastopol be allowed to form 
part of it, , n 

{ am indebted to Lieutenant-Colonel Simmons fora memorandum containing 
the whole of his Highness’s suggestions, together with the reasons on which 
they were bottomed. It 1s ‘nclosed herewith for your Lordship’s more complete 
information. . ; 

T have also reeeived from Brigadier-General Mansfield a series of remarks 
corresponding i a greal measure with the views of the Ottoman Commander- 
inechicf, and L feel myself bound in duty to recommend the contents of both 
these papers to the serious and dispassionate consideration of Her Majesty's 
Government. | cannot presume to offer a personal eho on matters of this 
kind, but neither can | coneeal my impression that a ¢ isregard of Omer Pashas 
advice would expose the success of a yery important and unavoidable enterprise 
to the most perilous chances, 

[ have, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLITFE.

Ineclosure 1 in No. 142. 


Memorandum by Liewtenant-Colonel Simmons. 


August 15, 1855. 


Substance of a Draftof Note addressed by Omer Pasha to the Seraskier, tu be 
submitted to the Porte, relative to the measures to be taken to relieve the 
army at Kars. 


ALL the misfortunes which have happened to the army in Asia are to be 
attributed to faults in its composition, and the fact that its Chief was not 
invested with full powers. | : 

very army ina similar condition must necessarily become demoralized, 

‘To renew the “morale” of any army in such a condition, the only remedy 
is to be found in energetic measures undertaken by an adequate force, 

For this reason the Pasha considers it necessary to make the following 
observations relative to the proposal arrived by telegraph from the Cabinet of 
London. re soem 

|. The troops now at Eupatoria, are composed of different materials, Purks 
and Egyptians, and are deficient ‘n the means of land transport. In case the 


179 


means of transport should be taken from the Ottoman troops before Sebastopol, 
these troops will be reduced to a similar condition. It 1s evident, therefore, 
that the troops from Eupatoria are not capable of taking the field and of 
manceuvering. 

The troops at Eupatoria have in stores in that place, provisions for four 
months, both for men and horses, 60,000 chekis of firewood, and a large 
quantity of reserve ammunition and baggage. ‘The Turkish fleet will not suffice 
for the transport of these stores; and even if the allied fleets lend their aid, 
there will be great loss of time as well as of provisions in moving them. 

Moreover, at the camp before Sebastopol, the Turks have no port or 
wharfs, no store-houses for provisions, or magazines for ammunition; they have 
no hospitals; and in case the Egpytians should be taken to the camp before 
Sebastopol, in order to allow of the Turks being taken thence to Asia, being 
without huts, the Egyptians without being utilized for the war, would, before the 
spring, perish of cold. 

If the Egyptians were to go to Asia, as it will be necessary to keep the 
field during the commencement of the winter, coming as they do from a hot 
climate, and being without the means of transport; they could not perform the 
necessary manceuvres ; and the army being composed of different materials, there 
would be but little chance of success in the great and important operation to be 
undertaken. 

By the execution of this project, the unity of the Ottoman as well as 
of the English army will be destroyed, and it is to be observed that much of the 
energy. it not the existence of an army in warfare depends upon its unity. 

‘rom the above observations it is evident that any General who would 
undertake the contemplated operation under the proposed conditions, would 
not only lose his reputation, but would place the alliance in great difficulties, 

As the Pasha has the sacred desire of being of service to the alliance, as 
also of maintaining his military honour, and as, if he accepted these conditions, 
by temporarily relieving the three Cabinets of their responsibility, and taking it 
upon himself, he would bring a greater responsibility upon the same Cabinets at 
a later period, and cause them greater embarassments, he feels it neces- 
sary to make the following proposal, the execution of which will not endanger 
any operations already undertaken. 

He proposes that the troops at Eupatoria should remain where they are, 
thus avoiding the difficulties above indicated, and that the Anglo-Ottoman 
Contingent should occupy Balaklava and Kertch. 

In both places the troops of the Contingent will be within fortified lines, and 
will be separated from other influences, and bemg from the same army, they 
will no doubt render as good service in those positions as the troops which now 
plas them, 

e proposes that the Contingent shall be completed by the troops now at 
Kertch, and that any deficiency in numbers after these troops haye jomed then 
shall be made up from the troops now before Sebastopol. 

He proposes that the troops for the expeditionary force for Asia shall be 
taken from Bulgaria and from before Sebastopol. 

By these operations all difficulties will be removed, and the unity of both 
the British and Ottoman armies will be maintained, 

The Pasha observes that as every General in warfare ought to coniders 
beforehand the most difficult circumstances in which he may be placed by the 
events of war, and to provide as far as possible against misfortune, he supposes 
the ease, that the army of Kars is destroyed before his arrival in Asia, and that 
the Russians have advanced beyond that place, and states that in such a case, 
being with an army composed of different materials, in whieh he could not 
place entire confidence, he would find himself with his army in similar difficulties 
to those in which the army of Asia is now placed, and would thus not only 
cause great danger to the Turks, but also to the whole alliance 

The Pasha further observes that every General to whom an operation is 
confided ought to consent to the operation and its mode of execution, in order 
that he may be held responsible for its conduct, and adds that if the conditions 
he proposes are accepted, and full powers given to him for their execution, then 
trusting to the goodness of God, lie will take the entire responsibility of the 
operation upon himself,
