Inclosure 2 in No, 142. 


Points of Consideration relative to the Orders of Her Majesty's Government 
conveyed in the Telegraphic Despatch dated August 9, 1855, 


| 

OMER PASHA and the Seraskier strongly oppose the breaking up of the 
army accustomed to the command of the former. | 

Permanent arrangements have been made by the Seraskier, for the supply 
of the garrison at Eupatoria, 

That garrison is composed of mixed troops, that is, Egyptians, Arabs, and 
Turks, 

The Contingent if panpuled with its full complement from the detachments 
about to be drafted from | ulgaria and from Kertch, will be almost equal in 
numbers to the divisions belonging to Omer Pasha, excepting Eupatoria. 

As far as the numbers of the allied army before Sebastopol, therefore, are 
concerned, there need be no diminution, if the views of Omer Pasha are acceded 
to; advantages to ourselves are :— st 

That General Simpson will be strengthened in council if he appears as the 
immediate commander of 50,000 men instead of 30,000. 

There will be more unity of command, and the Turkish soldiers under 
British officers are sure of protection from oppression on the part of either 
British or French, a thing otherwise by no means certain in the absence of Omer 
Pasha, of which we have already had experience, | ee 

If the Contingent go to Balaklava, the affairs of its commissariat become 
simplified at once. At present, no arrangements having been made, it is clear 
from the conversation and official letters of the General, that unavoidable delay 
must ensue if absolutely new establishments have to be organized. 

As a question of military organization, it is not very desirable that both the 
officers and men of the Turkish Contingent should have the benefit of example 
and contact with our British regiments and system generally, _ 

If Her Majesty's army have to winter in its present position before Sebas- 
topol, will it not bea great advantage to it to have 20,000 Turks to share its 
duty ? 

” Unlike the French and Russian armies, ours has never yet had any relief 
from siege duty. The Contingent after being for some time at Balaklava, could be 
so used according to the pleasure of the Commander of the Forces. 

In other quarters mischief has already arisen from want of centralized 
authority, according to the rule of military command; although such deplorable 
cireumstanees cannot be anticipated under such an officer as General Vivian ; 
still there cannot be a doubt, that as regards supply, ammunition, military chest, 
discipline, the adaptation of the various principles of rank, newly brought into 
play in the mixed service of these armies, and the right assimilation of the Turks 
in our pay to our purposes, much will be gained by its taking its regular place 
immediately under the eye of the Commander of the Forces. Pa a 

The Turks, at first, will not be exposed at Balaklava, indeed it is believed, 
not 60 much as at Eupatoria, until General Simpson should feel inclined to bring 
them up. ‘ 

(Signed) | W. R. MANSFIELD, 
Brigadier-General,

WITH reference to my despatch No. 607 of this date, 1 have to remark 
thut other considerations beside those put forward by Omer Pasha appear to 
recommend Balaklava as preferable to Eupatoria for the location of General 
Vivian's Contingent. 
Paid and officered by Her Majesty’s Government that corps, ifstationed at

in that point of view, would contribute to the influence and moral weight in 
council of the British Commander-in-chief. 

The Contingent might also be expected to derive advantage from its vicinity 
to the British army, also from the habitual intercourse which it would naturally 
keep up with the officers, and, to a certain degree also, with the privates, of

No, 144. 
Sir A, Malet to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received August 30.) 


(No. 100.) 


My Lord, Frankfort, August 27, 1855. 

THE recess of the Diet leaves me very little of interest to communicate to 
your Lordship from hence. 

The efforts which are being made by the Austrian Government to obtain 
adhesion to the four points, are not, as far as [ can learn, successful, 

The rejection which the proposal has encountered at Berlin, is no doubt 
known to your Lordship, and although if the question should be brought before 
the Diet, Austria would no doubt have several votes, it seems as yet doubtful 
whether she could securely reckon on a majority. 

It is not very easy to calculate, what direction the future policy of Austria 
would take, if she succeeded in obtaining the support of the Dict to her view of 
the terms of accommodation which it is desirable to propose to Russia, but | 
apprehend we have no good reason to build upon its being one that would coin- 
cide with the way of judging the questions in dispute entertained by Her 
Majesty’s Government and by that of the Emperor of the French. 

Austria would, as | imagine, be more likely to use the strength which such 
support would give her, in endeavouring to impose terms of peace which should 
be more favourable to Russia than those we are striving to obtain; and in 
this view of things the dissidence still existing between the two great German 
States, which, as long as it lasts, will prevent unison in the Diet, is not to be 
considered as so prejudicial to the Powers allied against Turkey, as it would be 
if Austria could be reckoned upon with certainty, as sharing their views as to 
the mode of taking security against Russian aggression ss the future. 

wave, &e, 


(Signed) A, MALET,

Inclosure in No, 145. 
Lord Panmure to Major-General Beatson. 


Sir, War Department, August 25, 1855. 

I HAVE the honour to acknowledge the receipt of your despatch of the 
Ath instant, and in reply to acquaint you that I have seen with much vexation 
and regret the disturbances which have occurred in the force under your 
command. 

It appears to me, that Lord Stratford de Redcliffe, acting on such informa- 
tion as he could procure at a distance from the scene of action, and under 
unusual and difficult circumstances, showed becoming zeal in sending regular 
forces and contingent instructions to you, with officers to aid you with their 
eounsel and assistance, and a conditional recommendation to repair to the capital 
to confer personally with his Lordship and the Seraskier. 

Although willing to make considerable allowances for imperfect discipline 
in « corps such as you are endeavouring to bring into subjection, it is Impossible 
forme not to condemn in the strongest terms the acts of violence and the 
contempt of all control which have been exhibited by some portion of the men 
under your very eyes, 

Lam willing to admit, in part, the excuse that you have not been provided 
with a sufficiency of officers; but this has arisen from your own irregular 
proceedings in not sending proper returns of your force, by which I could be 
guided in my views as to the number of officers which should be attached to 
you. 

Your own fitness to command must consist much more in your power over 
your men in the ficld, and capacity to lead them against an enemy, than in the 
judgment which your correspondence with Her Majesty's Ambassador at Con. 
stantinople exhibits, or the temperance of the general orders to your force. 

In the conviction that these defects spring rather from momentary irritation 
than from any determination to set authority at defiance, and considering the 
zeal which you have displayed in getting this foree together, I have resolved not 
to necept the resignation of your command tendered by you to Lord Stratford 
de Redcliffe—a course which at first appeared to Her Majesty's Government as 
best calculated to give a chance of securing efficiency to the force. 

In order to avoid any conflict between civil and military authority in future, 
| have resolved to place you and your corps under the superior military com- 
wand of General Vivian, and shall therefore attach you to the ‘Turkish Con- 
tingent. You will immediately and in future report yourself to that officer, and 
send through him your military communications. 

You will also hold your force in immediate readiness to move so soon as 
you may receive orders to that effect from General Vivian. 

Tam, &c. 
(Signed) PANMURE.

No. 147. 


Lord Cowley to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received September 1.) 
(No. 1,148.) 
My Lord, Paris, August 31, 1855. 
I HAVE furnished the Emperor with a copy of Colonel Simmons’ report 
No. 42 of the 20th instant to your Lordship, and have had some conversation 
with His Majesty on the matter of it. His Majesty deplores the state of affairs 
in Asia Minor, and is quite sensible of their gravity, but he will not admit that 
the relief of Kars is to be put in the same scale with the capture of Sebastopol ; 
and he insists that if any of the Turkish troops before that place are relieved, they 
shall be replaced, not only by others of equal numerical strength, but of as good a 
quality, His Majesty will not admit that General Vivian’s Contingent answer this 
requirement, and he grounds his opinion on a conversation which M. Thouvenel 
reports he has had with that officer, who, he says, declared that the troops under 
his orders were not fit to take the field, . 
The Emperor, however, still leaves the decision of this question to the 
allied Generals. 
I have, &e. 
(Signed) COWLEY.

Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received September 3.) 


(No. 637.) 
My Lord, Therapia, August 23, 1555. 

| HAVE the honour to state that I propose to embark for the Crimea this 
afternoon, in execution of Her Majesty’s commands conveyed to me by your 
Lordship. ‘The steamer appointed by Sir Edmund Lyons to receive me and the 
gentlemen by whom I am to be accompanied, is under weigh, and | hope to be 
on board in less than an hour. 

Omer Pasha has been detained beyond his apparent intention, and the 
badness of the weather has prevented his coming up the Bosphorus to sce me. 
1 am led to hope that he will embark to-morrow, and that he will reach the 
Crimea while Lam there, His plans will not be definitively settled before he 
has held an official consultation with the other Commanders-in-chiet. 

M. de Thouvenel, with whom I have communicated on the subject, does not 
feel himself at liberty to concur with me in stating to the Porte that provided no 
diminution of the Turkish forces takes place before Sebastopol, or at Kertch, the 
allied Governments have no objection to the proposed expedition into Asia. 
The preparations continue meanwhile, and 1 presume that Lieutenant-General 
Vivian is also preparing to follow out Lord Panmure's instructions, so fur as the 
prevailing uncertainty allows. 

The Russians having retreated from before Erzeroom, and having been 
foiled, according to Turkish accounts inclosed herewith, in a partial attempt on 
the defences of Kars, there is still room for hope that a diversion may not 
impossibly be effected in time. 

I have, &e. 


(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

Inclosure in No. 150. 


Vassif Pasha to Vely Pasha, 


(Translation.) August 4, 1855. 

AT 7 o'clock this morning the Russian army moved from its position and 
attacked Ramli Battery with all its force. By the help of God it was resisted, 
and after a cannonade on both sides which lasted two hours, the enemy, unable 
to resist our fire, was beat back, and retired in confusion. Besides the dead 
horses and the men which the Russians were able to carry off, a hundred bodies 
remained on the field, according to the last account. 

A General, indeed, was killed, and a gun destroyed, Upon our side, God 
be praised ! not one man was hurt in repulsing the enemy, and thanks are given 
to the Almighty for this proof of the eventual success of the Imperial army. 

I sce by the communications from Erzeroom that you had been instructed, 
after the late battle before Kupri-Ruy, to retire to the fortifications of Déné 
Boyni. ‘This proceeding on your part is fully consistent with good tactics, The 
troops sent by the Russians in that direction are thirteen battalions of foot, 
withdrawn by them from this place. ‘The Cavalry and Artillery are small in 
proportion to this foree, and they have no siege-guns with them. We are aware, 


that inspired with your well-known valour, you are making your best endea- 
yours in this matter. 

You will, with the help of God, beat back the enemy and save Erzeroom. 
You will act according to your instructions, and keep me informed of events,

No. 151. 
Lord A. Lofius to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received September 3.) 


(No. 31. Confidential.) 
My Lord, Berlin, September 1, 1855. 

1 ASKED Baron Manteuffel to-day, ina casual way, if he knew whether 
the Russian Government would have accepted either of the Austrian proposals 
for the settlement of the third point discussed at the Conferences ut Vienna. 
His Excellency replied that he was quite ignorant of what would have been the 
Russian decision on this subject. 

L inquired whether the Prince of Prussia had obtained any information on 
this subject during his stay at St. Petersburgh. He replied by stating that the 
Prince might have done more than he did, but that he was no diplomatist, and 
had spoken out freely his own views and opinions in lew of secking to discover 
those of the Russian Government. 

I have been, however, recently informed that his Royal Highness did put 
the question to Count Nesselrode, who merely replied, “that if these proposals 
had been accepted by the Western Powers, Russia would have been placed in a 
most embarrassing position.” 

It appears likely to me that Russia may have wished to conciliate Austria 
by declaring, as reported in Mr. Elliot's despatch No, 75 of 22nd August, that 
she would have accepted her second proposal, whilst she may wish to withhold 
this avowal from Prussia and Germany, in the hopes that at a later period she 
may represent her future aceeptation in the light of a sacrifice and ‘* concession” 
in behalf of peace. 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) AUGUSTUS LOFTUS. 


Lord A, Loftus to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Receiwed September 3.) 


(No. 33.) 

My Lord, Berlin, September 1, 1855. 
A STATEMENT having appeared in the German papers, that a fresh 

circular has been addressed by the Russian Government to their Legations at 

Foreign Courts, | took occasion to inquire from Baron Manteutfel to-day 

whether he had received communication of any such paper. 

His Excellency replied, that he had received no such communication very 
lately; but that about a fortnight ago Baron Budbere had read to him a 
despatch from his Government, of which no copy had been left in his hands. 

His Excellency stated, that it contained a repetition of the pacific disposi- 
tion of Russia, but that she could only accept an honourable peace. It cited the 
efforts she had made in this sense at the Conferences at Vienna, and that their 
result had proved that the Western Powers were intent in carrying on the war at 
all price, and that on them rested this heavy responsibility, 

There were evidently other passages in this circular, in addition to the very 
hackneyed phraseology reported tome by Baron Manteuffel, which led me to 
ask whether it contained any proposals to the Prussian Government. His 
Excellency replied in the negative, and he did not seem to treat the paper as 
being of any interest or importance, although T am informed that his Excellency 
stated to another person that there were certain points of this circular directed 
against Austria, which was probably the reason why no copy of this document 
was given to him, 

T have, &c. 
(Signed) AUGUSTUS LOFTUS.

No, 153. 
Mr. Elliot to the Earl of Clarendon,—(Received September 3.) 
_ (No. 83.) 
My Lord, Fienna, August 29, 1855. 


BARON DE BOURQUENEY has recived a despatch from Count 
Walewski in which the hope is expressed that the engagement which binds the 
Governments of England, France, and Austria not to enter separately into 
negotiation with Russia, may be strictly carried out. 

Count Walewski, after making all due allowances for the position of 
Austria towards a Power with which she still maintains diplomatic relations, 

oints out the inconvenience of the course which Count Buol had conceived 
himself at liberty to follow by the communication made to the Russian Govern- 
ment after the rupture of the late negotiations. 

M. de Bourqueney informs me that Count Buol received this communica- 
tion in a very good spirit, and assured him he might rely on the Austrian 
Government acting literally up to their engagement of not entering into any 
negotiation with Russia. ‘That ifthe Russian Minister came to him with a pro- 
position which did not appear suflicient, he should not consider himself at hberty 
to discuss it or mould it into a shape to render it acceptable; but in the case 
—which he was far from foreseeing —of a proposal being brought which fully 
comprehended all the demands of the allies, he conceived that the course for 
him to adopt would be to transmit it to the Governments of England and 
France, without expressing to the Russian Miuister any opinion on its merits, or 
in any way committing himself in its favour. 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) HENRY ELLIOT,

My Lord, Vienna, Auguat 29, 1855.

desired by him: for to a Power like Austria, so habitually addicted to allying 
itself to the strongest side, all representations of the strength of the allies serve 
asa warning to avoid the danger there would be to her im separating from 
them,

No. 158. 
Lord Cowley to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received September 17.) 


(No. 1,223. Confidential.) 

My Lord, Paris, September 16, 1855, 
COUNT WALEWSKI has received confidential information that Baron 

Budberg, the Russian Minister at Berlin, has asked M. de Manteuffel whether 

the Prussian Government would undertake to mediate between Russia and the 

Western Powers for the eurabbahmany of pene. ' Baron Budberg proposed, as gq 


basis, that the first two of the four points should be considered as settled by the 
Conferences at Vienna ; that the third should be taken as a matter of fact (résolu 
de fait); and that the fourth should be left for discussion. ; 

AM. de Manteuffel, before giving a decided answer, appears to have asked 
for farther information from St. Petersburgh, particularly as to the sense to be 
attached to the words ‘‘ résolu de fait.” Did they apply to the future, as well 
as to the present moment? In the meantime he informed the Austrian Govern- 
ment of the overture made to him, with a view of ascertaining whether the 
Cabinet of Vienna would accept the mediation of Prussia. He was informed 
that Austria would not separate herself from the Western Powers, and that any 
overtures for peace must come directly from Russia herself. 

I conceive, however, that there must be some error in the latter part of this 
information, because Count Buol was not at Vienna when the communication 
from Prussia must have been made, and it is not likely that an answer would 
have been returned in his absence. Be this as it may, Count Walewski has 
profited by Count Hatzfeldt’s asking him whether Prussia would be admitted to 
any future Conferences for peace, to go into both questions with him, 

In the first place he has told Count Hatzfeldt that neither the French nor 
English Governments will hear of any further Conferences. Russia must be 
prepared to come forward with clear and direct’ propositions on which prelimina- 
ries of peace can be signed. The part which Prussia may take in any discussions 
following the signature of such preliminaries must depend partly upon her own 
conduct. ; . 

Secondly. With regard to the mediation of Prussia, France and England 
would accept no mediation or employment of good offices on the part of any 
other Power. ‘They would treat directly with Russia herself, In saying this 
‘much, however, Count Walewski added that he did not wish to convey the 
idea that if Prussia was commissioned by the Russian Government to inform 
the Western Powers that Russia desired to treat for peace, the message would 
not be received and replied to. | 

In giving me this information, Count Walewski stated that he had been 
careful to let Count Hatzfeldt understand that he was only offering his own 
sninion. He had, however, had an opportunity sinee then of speaking to the 

mperor, Who had approved the language which he had held. 


I have, &c. 
(Signed) COW LEY.

No, 159. 
The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Stratford de Redeliffe. 
(No. 726.) 
My Lord, Foreign Office, September 17, 1855. 


LIEUTENANT-GENERAL VIVIAN has forwarded to Lord Panmure 
copies of his despatches to your Excellency of the 8th, 19th; and 22nd of 
August, respecting the importance of measures being taken to induce the Porte 


to maintain the Turkish Contingent under his orders at the full amount stated 
in the Convention, not only by completing the foree at once up to the specified 
numbers, but also by making provision for filling up any vacancies which may 
occur by casualties or desertions. 

It is perfectly true that there 15 no express stipulation in the Convention 
for su pplying vacancies so occurring; but in its absence the gencral engagement 
to assign over to the British Government the full number of 20,000 men must 
be taken to imply an engagement to keep the force up to that amount. 

Accordingly I have to instruct your Excellency to request that measures 
may forthwith be adopted by the Government of the Sultan for placing at the 
disposal of Her Majesty's Government not only the number of troops stipulated 
for in rhe Convention, which the Porte has hitherto omitted ta do, but also for 
replacing all the casualties which may occur in the force, ‘and your Excellency 
will urge this matter with the greater earnestness 00 the Porte on the ground

It is strange that the Pasha should have remained here so long. During

Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Recetved September 20.) 


(No. 670.) 
My Lord, Therapia, September 6, 1850. 

HAVING seen Aali Pasha the day before yeaterday I took occasion to 
make inquiry, according to your Lordship’s wishes, relative to the part which 
he had taken at Vienna on the occasion lately referred to by Lord John Russell 
in his place in the House of Commons, and brought under my notice by your 
Lordship's instruction No. 663 of the 16th ultimo. 

The Grand Vizier replied that he had been present at Conference with 
M. Drouyn de Lhuys and Lord John Russell; that the former had opened the 
question on the Austrian ultimatum, recommending it on the ground of its 
tending to detach the Court of Vienna from Russia; that Lord John Russell 
had thereon expressed his persuasion that there was “ étoffe pour la paix ” in 
that idea, and that he, coming next, had admitted the advantage of securing the 
Austrian connection, but referred to his Government for an opinion on the 
whole subject ; and later, that he had been directed by the Porte, without entering 
into the merits of the question, to follow in the track of France and England, 
the ie of separating from her allies being the Porte’s motive for taking 
that line. 

it would seem that the Ottoman Plenipotentiary did not refer to his 
Government by means of an official despatch, and consequently that the anawer 
was returned in a private form. 

I have, &c. . 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

No. 164. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received September 20.) 
(No. 672.) 
My Lord, Therapia, September 8, 1895. 


L HAVE the satisfaction to inform your Lordship that, in company with 
the Seraskier Pasha, 1 attended to-day a review of the Turkish regiments 
commanded by General Vivian. 

It was a pleasing and very interesting sight to me, though I cannot 
presume to offer an opinion of the troops in a military point of view, or of their 
fitness for active service. Their general appearance was good ; the word of com- 
mand was obeyed without hesitation or embarrassment. ‘They went through 
several evolutions with promptitude and good effect. New clothes and accoutre- 
ments would not be misplaced ; the horses of the Cavalry looked small, and 
some of the companies of Infantry were deficient in number. 

T understood that about 7,000 men of all arms were on the ground. Only 
two out of four batteries of artillery, attached to the corps, were in the field. 

The movements of the soldiers were characterized by cheerfulness and 
alacrity. ‘The Seraskier expressed his satisfaction, and repeated his previous 
assurance that the Contingent, on reaching Balaklava, would be completed to the 
Conventional number of 20,000, 

J have, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE,

Lord A. Loftus to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received September 24.) 


(No. 73.) 
My Lord, Berlin, September 21, 1855. 
AT an interview I had this day with Baron Manteuffel, and in reply to 
certain inquiries which | addressed to him, his Excellency informed me that he 
had lately made a communication to the Cabinets of London and Paris 
expressing the ardent hope of His Majesty that as the first act of the drama was 
over, the moment would be judged a favourable one for renewing the endeavours to 
restore peace. He said that this had been the constant aim of His Majesty, and 
that in submitting this question quan to the serious reflection of the two 
Cabinets, His Majesty was desirous of renewing his offer and of expressing his 
readiness to contribute his efforts in any way which might be conducive to the 
general good. | 
Baron Manteuffel stated that, as their position towards Austria was a different 
one, he had simply notified to the capone’ of Vienna the steps which he had

His Excellency said that this appeal in favour of peace to the Western

direct from Russia in @ positive and precise form, and that no mediation or good 
offices would be of avail or likely to meet with success, 

His Excellency replied that some third party would be necessary as an 
intermediary. It was very evident from his Excellency’s language that the King 
of Prussia is in ardent expectation of playing the part of “ mediator.”

No, 167. 
Lord A. Loftus to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received September 24.) 


(No. 74.) 
My Lord, Berlin, September 21, 1855. 

1 INQUIRED to-day of Baron Manteuffel whether he had any knowledge of 
the cause which had led to the sudden change in the Emperor's intended plan of 
visiting Warsaw. His Excellency said that he had learnt from St. Petersbungh 
that it was on account of the cholera, which was raging in that capital, All he 
knew for certain was, that the Emperor was to go to Moscow, and he did not 
scem to think that his visit to Nicolaieff was positively decided upon. 

I have, however, good reason to believe that His Imperial Majesty and the 
Grand Dukes will repair from Moseow to Odessa and Nicolaieff. On the other 
hand, it is very much doubted whether His Majesty will continue his journey 
to the Crimea, as announced by the newspapers. 

Various rumours are afloat as to the reasons which have induced this change 
in the Emperor's plan, but they can only be conjectures : amongst them | may 
cite to your Lordship as the most probable, the absence of the King of Prussia 
at this moment from Berlin, whose presence at Warsaw might have been 
expected and sought for. 

Baron Manteuffel further told me that he had received no late intelligence 
from St. Petersburgh, and that Baron Werther had only written him a few lines 
on receiving intelligence of the fall of Sebastopol, but i was not then able to 
report the impression which that event had produced.

(No, 90. Confidential.) 

My Lord, Berlin, September 29, 1855. 
M. DE WILDENBRUCH, the Prussian Representative at Constantinople, 

left Berlin yesterday, to return to his post. . 
During his stay here he has been constantly with the King; and I am 


privately informed that, in his reports to His Majesty on Eastern affairs, he has 
spoken with much interest and impartiality in support of ‘Turkey, and has 
strenuously represented the importanee of maintaining the independence and 
integrity of the Ottoman Empire. | 

I am also privately informed that His Majesty has charged him with a 
letter for the Sultan, in which His Majesty is represented to have strongly urged 
the neeessity of peace. His Majesty is further stated to have counselled the 
Sultan to exercise his own independence in the selection of his Ministers, 
without undue bias from his allies; and to have expressed an opinion, if it did 
not even assume the form of advice, that any prolonged occupation of his 
territory by the allies would be a matter of grave inconvenience. 

It is impossible for me to vouch for the truth or accuracy of this informa- 
tion, but it is my duty to report it to your Lordship in the shape in which 
it reached me.

My Lord, Rerlin, September 29, 1855. 
IN the absence of Baron Manteuffel I waited yesterday on M. Balan, the 
Acting Under-Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs, and expressed to him my 
astonishment that no communication had been made to your Lordship of the 
nature which Baron Manteuflel had represented to me in my last interview with 
his Excellency, as reported in my despatch No. 73 of the 21st instant. 
M. Balan replied, that very pee Count Brandenburg had forwarded his 
reports to His Majesty direct to ‘oblentz, as the latest despatch received from 


him was of the 20th instant, in which he acknowledged the receipt of these 


instructions, and stated that he should take the carliest opportunity of comimu- 


nicating them to your Lordsbip. These instructions were dated, I believe, the 
13th, but they were not finally dis atehed from here till the 17th instant. 

It is somewhat remarkable that the “ Kreuz Zeitung,” and other Berlin 
newspapers, have strenuously denied the statement (which first ap d in the 
“ Independance Belge”) that any advances of a pacific nature had been made 
by the Prussian Government to the Cabinets of London and Paris; and I have 
reason to think that Baron Manteuffel somewhat regrets having acted with such 
precipitation in this matter. 

1 have the honour to forward to your Lordship an article from the 
‘National Zeitung” of this day, which is evidently from an official source. It 
seeks to reject, for the Prussian’ Goveroment, the responsibility of their own act, 
and to give to this initiative on their part the character of a simple desire for an 
exchange of opinions between the respective Cabinets. | 

L further inquired from M. Balan whether any reply had been received to 
the communication made to the Austrian Cabinet. He informed me that Baron 
Werner had said that no official reply could be given until the return to Vienna 
of the Emperor Francis Joseph and of Count Buol; but he gave Count Arnim to 
understand that he did not think the present moment favourable for any 
proposals of mediation. M, Balan further stated that Count Georges 
Hsterhazy, the Austrian Minister at this Court, had expressed himself in a 
sitnilar sense,

Inclosure 1 in No. 170. 
Extract from the “ National Zeitung” of September 29, 1855. 


THE statement of an application having been made by Prussia to Paris 
after the fall of Sebastopol is denied indeed; but it appears only to require 
correcting in the form of expression. As to the fact itself, semi-official 
correspondents of many journals spoke lately as follows; 

“It would be going too far—it would be false, to say that no exchange of 
opinions had taken place with reference to the events of the 8th of September 
between the Prussian Ministers at London and Paris and the respective 
Cabinets. It is far more probable that confidential inquiries were made by 
Counts Hatzfeldt and Bernstorff, as indeed could hardly be avoided after such 
an event as the full of Sebastopol, as to the ulterior objects of the Western 
Powers, and the eventnal wishes for peace of the Governments concerned, 
Conversations of this nature between diplomatists are not inspired by the 
Government, in each special ease, and no official character is to be attributed 
to them.”

No. 171. 
Mr. Elliot to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received October |.) 


(No..121,) 
My Lord, Vienna, September 27, 1855, 

1 HAVE inquired from Baron Werner whether it was true that a communi- 
cation had lately been made to him by the Prussian Government,’ with the view 
of coming to some understanding as to the policy which could at present be 
most advantageously followed by the two Governments, and by which their 
united influence might be exerted in the interest of peace, 

Baron Werner replied that he had very unexpectedly been spoken to on the 
subject by Count Arnim, but that the communication was of so very vague & 
nature that it was nearly impossible for him to convey a proper impression 
of it. 

Count Arnim had not been instructed to make any formal proposition, or 
even to communicate a despatch of his Government, but had been directed 


195 


generally to express their wish that an understanding might be come to which 
should advance the interests of peace. 

His efforts had, in fact, apparently been limited to preparing the ground for 
some more formal communication, which, I should infer from Baron Werner's 
language, he is not disposed to think improbable. He assured me that nothing 
like a mediation on the part of Austria and Prussia had been suggested by 
Count Arnim, who, he added, must be aware that such a proposal would be so 
contrary to the engagements which already bind Austria to the Western Powers 
that neither the good faith nor the prudence of the Imperial Government would 
for a moment allow them to entertain it; but the position of Prussia was, he 
said, so different from that of Austria,, that he did not see that she would be 
open to reproach for endeavouring to bring about 4 more united policy. 

I replied that, before acquiescing in the justice of his observation, it would 
be necessary exactly to know the nature of the proposal which might be made; 
but that any suggestion by Prussia of a course involving the breach of the 
engagements of Austria towards the allies would, I apprehend, be manifestly 
insulting to the Imperial Government, as implying a doubt as to how far they 
intended to adhere to those engagements. 

I have, &e. 


(Signed) HENRY ELLIOT.

No. 172. 
Mr. Elliot to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received October 8.) 
(No. 133.) 
My Lord, Vienna, October 3, 1855. 


BARON WERNER informs me that he has already forwarded to Count 
Karolyi, for communication to your Lordship, a copy of the answer he has 
returned to the late communication of the Prussian Government, It is 
unnecessary therefore for me to allude to this document further than to state 
that Baron Werner said the unwillingness shown to meet the views of the 
Prussian Government in a manner which might be distasteful to the Governments 
of England and France, would, he hoped, be taken as a proof of the importance 
attached by the Imperial Government to the maintenance with them of the 
harmony and good understanding which they so much desire. 

I told Baron Werner that Her Majesty’s Government were without any 
very precise knowledge of the nature of the proposal to which he had just 
replied, as they had received no communication from the Prussian Government 
on the subject, but that I understood the Cabinet of Berlin professed more 
anxiety to maintain their independent action than to enter into concert with 
Austria for the combined action of the two Powers, and | asked whether this 
was the construction he would put upon the despatch read to him by Count 
Arnim. 

Baron Werner expressed much surprise at learning that no communication 
had been made to Her Majesty's Government, which was certainly stated by 
Baron Manteuffel to have been done both in London and Paris. 

With respect also to any independence of action which the Prussian Govern- 
ment might affect to wish to retain, he could only observe that if the Imperial 
Government had adopted their views, it would have become necessary to instruct 
the Austrian Representatives in London and Paris to lend the weight of their 
support to the proceedings to be adopted by the Prussian Minister; that, 
moreover, it was suggested that an attempt should be made to induce the other 
States of the Confederation to ator a similar policy, and as this could only be 
done by the concerted influence © the two Great German Powers, he considered 
that the cooperation of Austria had certainly been invited by Prussia, however 
obscure might be the language in which she had veiled her proposal, or however 
vague might be that proposal itself, , 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) HENRY ELLIOT.

No. 173. 
Mr. Elliot to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Recetwved October 8.) 


(No. 139.) 
My Lord, Vienna, October 3, 1855. 

COUNT BUOL returned to Vienna on Saturday last, after an absence of 
about three weeks. 

Upon my waiting on his Excellency at the Foreign Office, he said he must 
take that first opportunity of expressing to me his gratification at the great 
events which had taken place in the Crimea during his absence from Vienna, 
the intelligence of which had caused him the greatest pleasure; and he begged me 
to convey these sentiments to your Lordship, with the assurance of their entire 
sincerity. 

te Was not, however, only his own feelings which he wished to express to 
your Lordship, but he was anxious Her Majesty s Government should be made 
aware how entirely they were in conformity with those of His Majesty the 
Emperor Francis Joseph. 

His Majesty had received the intelligence of the fall of Sebastopol with the 
most lively satisfaction, and the sleasure he had derived from hearing of the 
brilliant exploit of the allied armits was increased by the hope that a step had 
thus been made towards the attainment of that solid peace which he longed to 
ace established. 

In His Majesty's name, therefore, as well as in his own, he requested me 
to convey these feelings to the knowledge of Her Majesty’s Government. 

I have, Xe. ) 
(Signed) HENRY ELLIOT.

No. 174. 
Mr. Elliot to the Earl of Clarendon.—( Received October 8.) 


(No, 142.) 
My Lord, Vienna, October 3, 1895, 


COUNT BUOL remarked to me yesterday that the success of the allied 
armies in the Crimea had already been so brilliant and so great, that he hoped 
the two Governments would think it advisable to come to an understanding on 
the terms on which they considered peace might be made, He entirely 
disclaimed, on the part of the Imperial Government, the slightest desire to 
interfere or mediate between the belligerent parties, as might be seen from the 
answer they had lately returned to the Prassian Government, but he merely 
wished to suggest that the allies should be prepared with the terms of peace 
they might intend to offer. 

| said this was a matter on which | had not received any instructions from 
Her Majesty's Government, but I had no hesitation in stating it as my own 
individual opinion that they had no intention whatever of offering any terms of 
peace; that when Russia was really desirous of peace, she would make known 
the conditions upon which she was willing to conclude it, and that these condi- 
tions would then receive from Her Majesty's Government, in concert with that 
of His Majesty the Emperor of the French, all the consideration which might 
appear to be due to them. 

Count Buol remarked that he did not think it would be practicable to 
adhere to this course; if, for instance, the Russian Government were to say, 
“'The ullies have had a great success, and we are anxious tor peace; let us 
know the conditions on which we can have it,’—how, he persisted, would it 
then be possible to refuse to state them ? 

| replied that in this case I apprehended the answer would be, that as the 
objects for which England and France had entered upon the war had been 
openly declared, if the Russian Government were desirous of peace, they should 


197 


state oe means by which they proposed that those objects should be carried 
into effect. . 
I repeated, however, that in plea | this opinion I was in no wise 

authorized to give it as that of Her Majesty’s Government. 

Count Buol also stated that i suggesting what he considered might be 
the course of the Russian Government, he must not. be understood as having 
any knowledge of their intentions, as he was entirely without information on this 
oint 5 and, indeed, he had not even seen Prince Gortchakoff since his return to 

ienna, 

T have, &c. 
(Signed) HENRY ELLIOT. 


No, 175. 
Lord A. Loftus to the Earl of Clarendon.—( Received October 8.) 


(No. 104.) 
My Lord, Berlin, October 5, 1855. 


ON waiting upon Baron Manteuffel this morning, his Excellency informed 

me that he had just received a visit from Baron Brunnow, late Russian Envoy 
at Her Majesty’s Court. 
In answer to my inquiry as to what impression he had received from Baron 
Brunnow with regard to the present feelings and views of the Imperial Cabinet, 
his Excellency replied that at this moment, in consequence of the absence from 
St. Petersburgh of the Emperor and the Court, there had been a stagnation in 
political affairs. 1 could gather, however, from his general observations, although 
conveyed in very vague and mysterious language, that there was, on the one 
hand, a great desire to terminate the war, and a feeling of anxiety to escape 
from the arduous difficulties and dangers which threatened the safety of the 
Empire; while, on the other hand, there evidently interposed a feeling of 
determined obstinacy to make no concessions or terms which should be 
humiliating to the honour and national pride of Russia, 

Baron Brunnow remarked to Baron Manteuffel that it must not be supposed 
that affairs were carried on now at St. Petersburgh in the same way as under 
the late Emperor, whose iron will suffered no dictation. It appears that Baron 
Brunnow represented the Emperor Alexander as bein mild, moderate, and 
anxiously disposed for peace, but at the same time his nimitted the great 
obstacles which often interposed, and which rendered his own will subservient 
to that of others. 

Baron Manteuffel told me that the Grand Duke Constantine had left 
Nicolaieff to join the army in the Crimea, and that the movements of the 

Emperor were undecided, but that it was supposed that he would return to 
St. Petersburgh. 

L could elicit no information from his Excellency as to any determinations 
with respect to the Crimea, or other military matters.

No. 180. 
Mr. Elliot to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Recetved October 15.) 


(No. 148.) 
My Lord, Vienna, October 10, 1855. 

IN reply toa question I put to Count Buol, respecting the nature of the 
reports he received as to the Bey disposition of the public at St. Petersburgh, 
he informed me that, at the date of his last accounts, there was no appearance of 
any pacific feeling prevailing there. 

The loss of Sebastopol had been felt as a severe blow, but the general 
impression was in favour of a continuance of a vigorous and energetic resistance. 

T have, &c. 
(Signed) HENRY ELLIOT.

Lord A. Loftus to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received October 15.) 


(No 122. Secret and Confidential.) 
My Lord, Berlin, October 12, 1855. 

1 AM informed, from a very secret and confidential private source, that, 
Austria is using every endeavour to gain over Prussia, and that proposals have 
heen made to this Government to conclude an Offensive and Defensive Treaty, 
combining with it a guarantee by Prussia of the whole Austrian territory. Iam 
told that these proposals have been declined, and that on the communication of 
this reply by Count Arnim, Count Buol showed great irritation and violence of 
temper, 

Pot having received this intelligence from an official quarter, I cannot of 
course vouch for its authenticity, but | have great faith im the private and 
confidential source from whence I derived it, and which I have hitherto found 
to be correct, and 1 therefore feel it to be my duty to report it to your Lordship, 
with the request that it may be considered as seeret and confidential. 

I have, &e.

Inclosure in No, 182. 
Baron Manteuffel to Count Arnim. 


(Translation.) Berlin, September 14, 1855. 


THE telegraph has brought us from time to time the news of the capture 
of the Malakhof Tower; of the retreat to the north side of Sebastopol; and of 
the destruction of the Russian fleet. We do not yet know the particulars of 
these events; we do not yet know what impression they have made either in 
St. Petersburgh, in London, or in Paris: but we believe not to be mistaken in 
supposing it will cause a great influence on the general situation of things. We 
hope that it may possibly bring about an understanding between the belligerent 
Powers, This hope is based on the reflection of those cireumstances on which 
the aloes of the Vienna Conferences followed. What was it that caused the 
rupture of negotiations? Without regard to the principal point—merely the 
refusal of Russsia to limit her fleet to a fixed extent, and on the side of the 
Western Powers the conviction that the solution proposed by Russia and 
Austria was not of a nature to cause a suitable balance of power in the Black 
Sea between the contending Powers—the Western Powers saw a perpetual 
danger to the independence of Turkey in the disproportion between the Russian 
and Turkish fleets. Now, indeed, this disproportion exists no longer. The 
fate of the Russian fleet was sealed the moment the south side of Sebastopol 
fell into the hands of Russia's opponents. With this act the former propositions 
of the allies appeared in a new light. No one will deny that even before this 
the maritime power of Russia was shut out of the Black Sea, and that therefore 
no important change will take place; for the Russian flag was prevented, even 
before the fall of Sebastopol, from showing itself in that sea, hut it demanded a 
continual expenditure of force on the part of the allics to produce this result. 
To-day the position is different. The English and French fleets can leave the 
Black Sea without abandoning the Porte to an overpowering opponent. If it 
was formerly the object to limit the preponderance of Russia, it only rests 
now to prevent the fresh continuance of such a superiority. If it was judged 
necessary to limit the Russian ships to a certain number in the first instance, 
now it can only be necessary for Russia to promise not to increase her fleet 
above a certain extent. We do not know whether Russia will consent to 
enter into such an agreement, for, as above remarked, we have no news asto the 
impressions which the late events on the field of battle have caused in St, Peters- 
burgh, still less has the Russian Court informed us upon what conditions it is 
ready to enter upon fresh Treaties for peace. Whatever it may be, we are 
disposed to admit that an engagement of this kind would at present be useless, 
‘1 order to obtain that object which the Western Powers have inscribed upon 
their colours. It is true that if Russia docs not agree to an engagement of 
this kind she will be able to build a new fleet, stron ger and more dangerous than 
the one destroyed. . In the meantime will they, in the event of such a possibility, 
continue a strife which has already cost nb sacrifices and brought such deep 
wounds to the prosperity of the people? We do not believe it, for though it is 
possible it is not probable. Without doubt the present war has exhausted 
Russia's financial powers to the last degree; It is therefore not to be supposed 

that she will soon be in a position to retrieve her losses. 

Turkey, on the other hand, inasmuch as she reserves to herself the right of 
opening the Straits to her allies when she considers her independence to be 
threatened, finds herself in a position at any moment to put a stop to a menacing 
attitude of the Russian fleet, And Russia, even if she oui all the materials 
for the speedy reconstruction of her ships, would guar herself from defying the 
unanimous voice of Europe, and, by aspiring for the command of the Black Sea, 
from counting among her enemies those Powers who to-day believe in the 
sincerity of her peaceful intentions. We will never dispute the sanctity of 
Treaties, We will not recall to our memory the words of a Russian despatch 
which quotes the possibility of the force of circumstances compelling Russia to 
agree to the conditions of her opponents. But we doubt that stipulated agree- 

ments will weizh heavier than actual deeds, and we believe that force of circum- 
stances, as long as they appear lasting, 18 more binding than an Article of Treaty, 
Therefore we think that the present moment is auspicious for the renewal of 


ee pee, a eo ee

No. 188, 
Lord Cowley to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received October 18.) 


(No. 1,863.) 
My Lord, Paris, October 17, 1855. 

I SUBMITTED some days ago to Marshal Vaillant the resons which 
induced Her Majesty’s Government. to think that the docks at Sebastopol 
should be immediately destroyed. The Marshal promised to take the Emperor's 
orders on the subject. 

This morning J had an opportunity of speaking to His Imperial Majesty 
myself. His Majesty said that there were good arguments both for destroying 
and preserving the docks: and if it was certain that they could be destroyed at 
the last moment, he should prefer letting them continue in their present state, 
since their destruction would be looked upon by the Russian Government as 
showing an intention on the part of the allies to restore the Crimea eventually to 
Russia. On the other hand, His Majesty felt that to destroy them after negotia- 
tions for peace shall have been renewed, would not, perhaps, be a dignified 
proceeding on the part of the allies. On the whole, therefore, he agreed with 
Her Majesty's Government that it was desirable to undertake the work of 
demolition at once. 

; I have, &c. 
(Signed) COWLEY.,

No. 189. 
4 
The Karl of Clarendon to Mr, Magenis. 
(No. 41.) 
Sir, Foreign Office, October 18, 1855. 


AS considerable delay has unavoidably taken place in resuming the question 
ofa Treaty with Sweden, it will be convenient to recapitulate what has passed 
on the subject, 

I stated to you in my (lespatch No. 21 of the 28th of June, that, ‘with 
reference to the uttempts which haye of late years been made by Russia to 
obtain a footing in Norway, which attempts Russia, so far from abandoning, is 
evidently intent upon pushing forward at the first convenient opportunity, as is 
sufficiently proved byt ie coercive measures she has put in force against Norway, 
in consequence of t | 
Russia for the concession of more extended fishing-rights on the coast of Norway ; 
with reference also to the fortifications, commenced with so much secrecy, and 
upon a scale so vast, at Bomarsund, which leave no doubt that the ambitious 
designs of Russia are as menacing to the sufety and independence of Europe in 
the north as they have been proved to be in the south, and that ina few years 
her power, if unchecked, would become most dangerous ; Her Majesty's Govern. 
ment, taking these facts into consideration, and being earnestly desirous to 
prove the friendly feelings by which Her Majesty is animated towards her ally 
the King of Sweden and Norway, and to render him any assistance in resisting 
the demands which, at the conclusion of peace, will, ne doubt, again be addressed 
to him by Russia, will be prepared to enter into an agreement by which the 
King of Sweden and Norway shall declare his intention to make uo concession 
to Russia, whether of fishery-rights, or pasturage-rights, or of territory; and the 
Queen of Great Britain shall engage, should any attempt be made by Russia to 
obtain any such concessions by force, to come to the assistance of the King of 
Sweden and Norway, with such an amount of naval force as may be necessary 
for the purpose of repelling any ageressive act on the part of Russia. 

It appeared from your reports of your communications with the Swedish 
Government on these matters, that it had not eseaped their notice that much 
danger would result to Great Britain if Russia should succeed in obtaining a port 
in Finmark, especially in the Warenger Fiord, where the sea never freezes, and 


ic refusal of the latter to accede to the repeated demands of 


205 


where there exist every capability for making it the Sebastopol of the north, 
which it would speedily become in the hands of Russia. 

It further appeared that notwithstanding the urgeney of the Swedish 
Government to induce the Cabinet of St. Petersburgh to settle at once a question 
touching rights of fishing, that Cabinet had evaded doing so on the ground that 
when Russia was engaged in so great a war it was impossible to attend to so 
trivial a subject—a proceeding which the Swedish Government considered as 
justifying suspicion of the ulterior intentions of Russia; and it also appeared 
that the Swedish Government looked upon the overture which you were 
instructed to make to it, as the most important event which had occurred for the 
policy of the north of Europe since the commencement of the present war, 
being of opinion that if the ambitious designs of Russia were checked in the 
south, she would endeavour by increased activity to obtain in the north a 
compensation for that check ; and this consideration appeared to weigh with the 
Swedish Government as an inducement to come into such an arrangement as 
that which you were instructed to lay before it. 

The result of the communication which your French colleague had with 
the Swedish Government, served to show that that Government was of opinion 
that the object which England and France had in making such an overture as 
that which you had laid before it, and which had also been submitted to it by 
the French Minister, could only be obtained by a defensive Treaty extending to 
all the Swedish and Norwegian possessions, and the Swedish Government was 
therefore desirous that the proposed defensive Treaty should extend to both of 
those kingdoms; the object being to strengthen and support Sweden in esta- 
blishing a barrier against Russian agerandisement in the north; because if 
Russia were to obtain possession of Gothland, she would at once command the 
Baltic; and if of Landskrona, she would command the Sound. 

Her Majesty’s Government cannot but agree with you that such would be 
the result of Russia obtaining possession of these positions ; and as regards 
Gothland more particularly, the Port of Stilo in that island being rarely frozen, 
Russia by acquiring possession of it would not only reap all the benefits of a 
commanding position in the Baltic, but would also achieve one of the great 
objects of her ambition, by oceupying a port from which her fleets could, with 
very rare exceptions, put to sea at any period of the year. 

Her Majesty's Government concur m your opinion that the danger to England 
would indeed in that case be less imminent than if Russia obtained a port in 
Finmark, but that it would still be eonsiderable : and every successful ageression 
of Russia would increase her power, ani weaken the power of resistance of 
Sweden. 

Her Majesty’s Government also concur in your opinion that a general 
guarantee of the Swedish possessions, though a parently involving a greater 
responsibility, would practically avert all future dangers since the fact of a 
guarantee on the part of two Powers like England and France would, when 
known, put a stop to any projects of aggrandizement which Russia might cherish 
against Sweden, whilst such a guarantee could hardly fail to bind Sweden more 
closely to the interests and cause of the Western Powers. 

fer Majesty’s Government having taken the whole subject into their 
mature consitleration, have determined, in concurrence with the Government of 
the Emperor of the French, to aceede to the wish of the Swedish Government 
that under certain contingencies the whole of the Swedish and Norwegian terri- 
tories should be guaranteed by England and France; and you are accordingly 
instructed to propose to the acceptance of the Swedish Government the Treaty 
of which n draft is inclosed. 

Lam, &e. 
(Signed) CLARENDON,

No. 190. 
The Karl of Clarendon to Mr. Magenis. 
(No. 42.) | 
Sir, Foreign Office, October 18, 1855, 


HER Majesty's Government having determined, in conjunction with that of 
the Emperor of the French, to accede to the wishes of the Swedish Government, 
and to extend to the whole of Gweden and Norway the guarantee which they 
originally limited to Finmark alone, 1 consider it my duty to place on record the 
motives which have led to this determination, and which have induced the two 
Governments not to require of Sweden, in return for this guarantee, to enter into 
any engagement, present or prospective, to take part in the war against Russia. 

Such an engagement would be a totally different thing, and have a totally 
different bearing, from the Treaty whieh you have been authorized to propose to 
the Swedish Government, It would be to ask Sweden to take a step tending 
sooner or later to engage her asa party in the war we are now waging, and, of course, 
tending to engage England and France to cooperate with Sweden 5 whereas the 
Treaty which they propose is entirely a measure of prospective precaution, and 
leaves untouched the condition of the present. It may be followed or not, as shall 
hereafter appear expedient, by an accession of Sweden to the alliance; but it will 
not constitute a state of ‘hostility on the part of Sweden against Russia, and it 
will not, therefore, compel us to allot military forees to aid io her defence agaist 
her neighbour. Aah ; 

The Treaty will be the attainment by anticipation of part of the objects af 
the war, the main and real object of which 15 to curb the aggressive ambition of 
Russia, England and France went to war to save Turkey from falling into the 
hands of Russia; but as they have astrong interest In preventing the encroach- 
ments of that Power upon Sweden and Norway, they are justified in taking 
measures for that object ; and England and France will gain greatly if they are 


207 


able by diplomacy to check the ag cession of Russia in the Baltic as they are 
now doing by force of arms in the flack Sea. 

It would be incorrect to say that Sweden may eafely be left to take care of 
her own territory, and to eall upon England and France for aid when her terri- 
tory is in danger. First of all the knowledge of such a Treaty will be a powerful 
check upon Russia, and will prevent her from pressing Sweden as she has been 
in the habit of pressing Turkey, and carrying her point by a coup de main before 
application for support and assistance could be made, considered, agreed to, and 
complied with, In the next place Sweden, “f left free to act as she liked, might 
have inducements held out to her which might lead her to consent to Russian 
demands. Russia, for instance, might propose an exchange of Aland and 
Bomarsund for the Warenger Fiord in Finmark, and the exchange might in 
some respects be a benefit to Sweden, but it would be one full of danger to 
England and France, and yet if there were lo Treaty they would have no right to 
object. Her Majesty’s Government are therefore disposed to regard the Treaty in 
question as part of along line of cireumvallation to confine the future extension of 
Russia, even if by the events of war her outposts should not be driven in at any 
part of her present circumference ; and we must never forget that whatever the 
conditions of peace may be, the Russian Government will labour, the moment peace 
‘g concluded, to strengthen all that has been found weak ; to prepare for aggres- 
sion everything that has hitherto been ineffective; to redeem by encroachments 
on the weak the position and power which she will have lost by the assaults of 
the strong ; and that by endeavouring effectually to confine her on all sides we 
shall take the best means of avoiding future collisions. 


am, &c. 
(Signed) CLARENDON,

No. 191. 
Lord A. Loftus to the Earl of Clarendon. —( Received October 22.) 


(No. 137.) 
My Lord, Berlin, October 20, 1855. 


1 AM informed that since the Council of War, held on the Emperor of 
Russie’s first arrival at Nicolaieff, some change has apparently taken place in the 
decisions then taken. General Baron Lieven, Chief of the Etat-Major of the 
Russian army, has been (as I am informed) sent by the Emperor to the Crimean 
army, and is charged with full powers to decide whether further military opera- 
tions shall be carried on in defence of the Crimea, or whether its immediate 
evacuation shall take place. ‘The position of Prince Gortchakoff’s army is daily 
becoming one of greater danger an risk. He is unable to act on the offensive, 
and has been vainly awaiting an attack and an advance of the allied armies; in 
the meantime his rear is threatened; and every day now lost is of the utmost 
consequence, should he find himself compelled to retreat. 

Various rumours, though none of & very substantial nature, are current 
here of the intended evacuation of the Crimea by the Russians; but they seem 
to rest more on the general opinion entertamed in the military circles, that this 
step is not only the most advisable for Prince Gortchakoff to take, but that it is 
indispensably necessary for the safety of the Russian army. 

The Emperor’s return to St. Petersburgh is indefinitely postponed ; and I 
am informed that His Imperial Majesty, shortly after his arrival at Nicolaieff, 
proceeded as far as Perekop to review the Grenadiers and some militia regiments, 
numbering about 30,000 men, now stationed there, ‘The prolonged stay of the 
Emperor in the south is attributed to his desire to await the decision to be 
taken by Baron Lieven and Prince Gortchakoff, and partly, also, the arrival 
there of the Russian army, if they determine er the Crimea.

Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received October 22.) 


(No. 807.) 
My Lord, Therapia, October 9, 1855. 

THE French Ambassador informed me yesterday that he had been received 
by the Sultan the day before at an audience, of which the ostensible object was 
the delivery of an Imperial letter concerning Veli Pasha’s recall from the Embassy 
at Paris. His Excellency told me that he had gratified the Sultan by informing 
him that Osman Pasha, who commanded and was taken prisoner at Sinope, had 
been exchanged for a Russian General taken by the French; and that His 
Majesty had given him assurances of his intention to enforce reform and to 
bring all classes of his aubjects, if I understood M. Thouvenel right, in harmo- 
nious relations with each other. 

It is to be hoped that so fair a promise will he realized without unnecessary 
delays. I have received so many pledges of similar purport with tardy or 
abortive results, that I need proof to secure my confidence. I repeated to 
M. Thouvenel that, in my opinion, the only real ground of hope for working out 
the regeneration of the Empire, was the joint concurrent action of the two 
Embassies, properly directed and strenuously exerted. It requires but little 


discernment to perceive that, without the vigorous application of such a lever, 


national prejudice, religious antagonism, Eastern apathy, and official corraption 
will frustrate every good intention and every friendly admonition. 


I have, &e. : 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

No. 194. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Recewed October 22.) 
(No, 813.) 
My Lord, Therapia, October 11, 1895. 


SINCE I last had the honour of addressing your Lordship, I have prepared 
the draft of a note for presentation to Fuad Pasha, with the view of carrying into 
effect, your Lordship's instructions on the subject of religious persecutions in 
Turkey, and particularly of punishment inflicted, in pursuance of law, on apostates 
from Islamism. . 

Yesterday I called upon the French Ambassador by appointment, and 
resumed, amongst other matters, the short conversation which I had previously 
had with his Excellency on that subject. On the former occasion he told me 
that he had not yet received any special instructions, but that he thought him- 
self at liberty to enter upon the question under his general instructions. At our 
interview yesterday, he repeated that he was still without instructions authorizing 
him to act with me for the special object preseribed by your Lordship; an 
when I showed hin Lord Cowley’s despatch reporting Count Walewskt's assent 
to your Lordship’s proposition, and his promise that corresponding instructions 


209 


should be sent to the Ambassador forthwith, he persisted in declining to take 
any part in the affair, and expressed, on the contrary, opinions directly and 
strongly adverse to the demand which Iam enjoined to press upon the Porte’s 
acceptance. Having failed to produce any change of purpose on his part by 
reading such passages from your Lordship's despatch to me as best displayed the 
strength of argument and force of resolution which characterize that important 
instruction, | dropped the discussion without declaring any intention as to the 
course 1 should pursue, or communicating the draft, which I had in my pocket, 
of the official note Brepaced for Fuad Pasha. : 

Another mail will perhaps bring the corrective of this defect in my col- 
league’s position ; but judging from the decided manner in which M. 'Thouvenel 
expressed his personal objections, I feel how important it is that the instructions 
from Paris should leave no room for any essential deviation on his part from the 
course of proceeding, or at least from the line of argument and requisition, which 
1 am commanded to pursue.

As M. de Thouvenel sesured Lord Stratford that he had received no

taken place on this subject between his Excellency and myself, and asked him 
how it happened that the promised ‘nstructions had not heen sent. His Excel-

abolition of the punishment of death in cases of apostacy ; but he now found 
that it had assumed a much wider range, and that the Porte was to be asked to 
make a formal declaration that apostates should be visited with no punishment 
whatever. He did not think that, supposing it possible to obtain such a declara- 
tion, it would be prudent to risk, at the present moment, the outery which 
would be raised against the Sultan for giving it, by the more fanatic portion of 
the Turkish population. It would, perhaps, be better to urge it when the 
question of religious toleration came to be discussed in the consideration of the 
fourth pomt. |

which your Lordship desived should be addressed to the French Ambassador at 
Constantinople, it is his own fault; for he had read your Lordship’s despatch 
No. 1,033 of the 17th ultimo," stating the views of He Majesty’s Government 
on this subject. 

Upon the general question, I observed to him that Her Majesty’s Govern- 
ment considered that there could not be a more fitting moment to bring the 
matter belore the Porte than the present ; that I presumed he was of the same 
opinion as Her Majesty’s Government, that no punishment should be inflicted 
on Mahomedans who embraced Christianity; and that 1 could not conceive why 
M. de ‘Lhouvenel should not be instructed to support any representations which 
Lord Stratford might make upon the subject. 

Count Walewski rejoined that he assented of course to the general principle, 
but that he must leave to M. de Thouvenel to decide the mode and moment in 
which it should be pressed upon the Porte. He again adverted to the discussion 
on the fourth point as furnishing, in his opinion, the best opportunity.

No. 198. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received October 29.) 
(No, 832.) 
My Lord, Therapia, October 16, 1895. 


THE French Ambassador continuing to declare that no instructions similar 
to mine on the subject of punishment for apostacy had hitherto reached him, 
notwithstanding the assurances given in that respect to Lord Cowley by Count 
Walewski, and communicated to me by your Lordship, I have not felt myself 
at liberty to bring the question forward in as formal and decided a shape as | 
might otherwise have done, with a view to producing the necessary effect ; but 
neither could [ reconcile it with my sense of duty to keep your Lordship’s 
representations ina state of abeyance until the result of a reference to London 
could be known at this distance. Instead, therefore, of presenting an official 
note, or demanding a conference, I called, without notice, on Fuad Pasha, and 
subsequently on the Grand Vizier, for the purpose of making inquiry a5 to the 
present state of the Turkish law concerning apostacy, and of making known to 
each of those Ministers the sentiments an expectations of Her Majesty"s 
Government, 

Having these objects in view, and guided by your Lordship’s instructions, 
[entered into conversation with the Ottoman Seeretary of State, who received 
my remarks and sugeestions with much courtesy of manner and liberality of 
expression, Ie assured me that he had not heard of the executions at Aleppo 
and Adrianople; that the intentions of his Government were opposed to such 
acts of mistaken severity; and that, far from ‘elding to a spirit of religious 
persecution, or even 0 religious proselylism, \imself and those who thought 
with him, did all in their power to dissuade Mahometan converts from carrying 
out their purpose, and to save apostates—whether originally Mussulmans or 
not—from punishment. He made no distinction between the two cases, He 
would not admit that Protestants were viewed with less favour than other 
religious classes in Turkey, but asserted, on the contrary, that the principle 
of Protestantism was one which the Porte found it her interest to encourage. 
If the Protestants really had reason to complain, their vexations were not, 
attributable to the Turks so much as to their fellow-Chriatians of the Church 
they left. The Porte’s system was easentially tolerant ; the abolition of the 
haratch, and the admission of Christian evidence, had removed the principal 
grievances ; those which remained were the effects of an imperfect administration, 
operating quite as much on the Mussulman as on the Cristian. The Govern: 
ment was anxious to improve its administrative system, but so great a work 
could only be brought about gradually, by the introduction of successive reforms, 
leading to a total change, The only punishment to which offenders were liable 
on the score of religion, was that int ieted on persons guilty of blasphemy, by 
which was meant the offence of yublicly disparaging the Prophet of Mecca, or 
either of his inspired precursors, Moses and Our Lord. 

Such was, in substance, the language of Fuad Pasha, who, in the course of 
discussion, fully acknowledged the claims of Great Britain and its Christian 


213 


allies to the gratitude of the Porte, and also the necessity in which the latter 
was place of putting itself in harmony with the Powers of Christendom. He 
ended by remarking, in proof of his Government's sincerity, that while the 
Porte was urged only to treat its Christian subjects with equity, measures were 
in progress for investing them with a share of administrative power. 

The Grand Vizier, whom I visited at a later hour, held much the same 
language as his colleague, Some shades of difference were, however, observable, 
arising perhaps from difference of position, or, it may be, from difference of 
character and opinion in the individuals. There was the same declaration 
as to religious tolerance, administrative reform, and the necessity of European 
connection; the same acknowledgment of benefits derived from the alliance; 
and the same assertion that Bes ohanig alone of religious offences, was now, in 
practice, liable to capital punishment. But there was by no means the same 
clear admission of the extent which I felt myself at liberty to give to the Sultan's 
promise and to the Porte’s engagements of March 1844. The Vizier professed 
ignorance of the religious executions alleged to have taken place at Aleppo and 
Adrianople; he said that the penal laws were still unaltered, but that, in practice, 
the Government took care to prevent their application in cases of apostacy on 
the part of Christians. He was not disposed to favour conversions to Islamism, 
but, on the contrary, he had ever done his best, as occasions offered, to discourage 
individuals who professed a change of creed, He intimated, nevertheless, that 
the law was stubborn, if net unalterable; that the Christians among themselves 
were more intolerant and acrimonious than were the Turks towards them, 
that if liberty of conscience were to be generally established, there were countries 
in Christendom—such, for instance, as Tuscany—where improvement was more 
to be required than in Turkey ; and that, considering the ignorance and fana- 
ticism which still prevailed in parts of this Empire, it was more prudent, and 
perhaps in the end it would prove more effectual, to let the law become obsolete, 
ag sometimes occurred even in England, than to shock the national prejudices 
by repealing it at once. 

In reply to both Ministers, I employed the arguments which obviously 
occurred; and I read such passages of your Lordship’s instruction as were best 
calculated to place our demand in its proper light, and to show with what 
conviction and serious, though friendly, determination Her Majesty's Government 
are prepared to urge its acceptance by the Porte. The would not admit that 
in making a loan they had departed from the law of the Koran, which, according 
to them, forbids only to take, but not to give, interest. ‘The distinction, whether 
correct or otherwise, is of little consequence, 4s the Porte, in abolishing harateh, 
tore out a leaf, as Fuad expressed it, from the sacred volume. They know well, 
as | have often told them, that the regeneration of this Empire is, humanly 
speaking, impossible, while the principles and forms of legislation applied to 
acne ERIE and judicial matters are invested with the inflexibility of Divine 
truths. 

It has been found necessary to ascribe a suspending or dispensing power to 
the Head of the State ; and in the exercise of that prerogative lies a principle of 
vitality capable of imparting fresh vigour under new forms to the most seth 
institutions. Soliman the Magnificent departed as much from the established 
maxims of the Koran, when he made his first durable ‘Treaty with France, as 
Sultan Abdul Medjid would do in setting his seal on the perpetual abolition of 
penal enactments avainst apostacy. That great and glorious Monarch perceived 
the necessities of his Empire in their very germ; and, with the foresight of 
genius, laid the first stone of that broad causeway over which the Western 
nations were in due season to advance. to the suecour of his descendants, not 
only with the pomp and powers of war, but yet more effectually with the arts of 
peace and the miracles of science. 

When Aali Pasha made the invidious remark that mueh remained to be 
done in Christian countries for the emancipation of the human conscience, I 
reminded him of the total want of analogy which existed between the relations 
of those countries towards each other, and their common relation towards the 
Ottoman Empire, especially under the present circumstances of undeniable 
weakness on the one side, and of sacrifices not to be exaggerated on the other. 
I claimed, moreover, for England, however it might be with other States, the 
right of pleading her own example in support of the representations which | was 
instructed to make, not only as to freedom of conscience in spiritual matters, 


but as to the removal of civil disabilities on grounds of religious difference, and 
the benefits derived from a comprehensive system of government, I undertook 
to obtain more positive irJormation with respect to the executions before alluded 
to, and I drew attention to the harsh treatment, independent of penal infliction, 
experienced in many instances by those who were charged with religious offences 
in some provincial town oF district, particularly by Protestants, who were not 
sufficiently protected by the T urkish authorities. 

Redress will, | trust, be obtained in the latter respect. The former complaint 
was met with an excuse, which it 1s dangerous to accept. The confinement of 
accused individuals, their removal to a distance for trial, and their temporary 
exile, were treated us means employed of necessity by the Government to evade 
the violence or to deceive the bigotry of an exasperated populace. 

Upon the whole, my proposals were received with fewer appearances of 
irritation and repugnance than 1 had ventured to expect. I was careful not to 
excite those feelings by any premature insistance or attempt to carry my points 
by surprise. | recommended the demands of Her Majesty's Government to the 
serious and immediate consideration of the Porte; and I agreed, at the Pasha’s 
request, to send in a note, pro memorid, explanatory of their extent and the 
grounds on which they rested. Objections and difficulties are to be expected in 
a later stage ; but 1 am willing to believe that firmness and lee will 
ultimately prevail, if not to obtain the formal repeal of established laws, to 
remove all uncertainty from the intentions of the Porte, and to obtain the prac- 
tical cessation of punishment and minor kinds of persecution in the case of every 
aeceder from a religious creed. It is most desirable that Christian and other 
spiritual dignitaries exercising authority over the Rayahs, should be comprised 
in the intended renunciation. 

I presume that the interru tion of public worship, or any grave and 
scandalous offence against the Deity, involving a breach of the public peace, 
would, in the opinion of Her Majesty's Government, be justly abandoned, on 
due conviction, to the rigour of the law, when brought within reasonable and 
well-defined limits. 

| have, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.
