No. 200. 


The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Cowley. 
(No. 1,254,) 
My Lord, Foreign Office, October 29, 1855. 

1 HAVE received your Excellency’s despatch No. 1,898 of the 24th instant, 
reporting a conversation which you had had with Count Walewski, on learning 
that instructions to M. de Thouvenel to press the Porte to prevent the infliction 
of the punishment of death on future apostates from Islamism had been omitted 
to be sent to him, and giving Count Walewski's reasons for this omission. 
And 1 have to state to your Excellency that I have learnt the decision of 
Count Walewski with much regret, as there can be no doubt that the present 
moment, when England and Franee are rendering such great services to the 
Sultan, and imposing upon themselves such great sacrifices for his cause, is the 
fitting one for demanding the abolition of a law which is an outrage to humanity 
and to the Christian religion. 

Her Majesty’s Government are now placed ina position of some embarrass- 
ment, for, relying upon the assurance that Count Walewski would send to M. de 
Thouvenel instructions similar to those which were addressed to Lord Stratford 
de Redcliffe, Her Majesty’s Ambassador will have already acted upon his 
instructions, and have adyised the Porte to abolish the existing law and practice, 
and he must either withdraw his application, and thereby give the sanction of 
Her Majesty's Government to the continuance of a practice which is revolting 
to the feelings of the people of England, or he must find himself, by no fault 
of his own, in direct antagonism with the French Ambassador, whose advice 
being in consonance with the fanaticism of the Porte, will naturally be preferred 
by the Turkish Minister to that of his colleague.

(No. 1,415.)

No. 206. 
Lord A. Loftus to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received November 5.) 


(No. 160, Confidential.) 


My Lord, Berlin, November 3, 1855. 

IN conversing with Baron Manteuffel on a late occasion, I profited of an 
opportunity which occurred, to inquire from his Excellency whether he had 
received any intelligence from St. Petersburgh which might give hopes that 
wiser and more conciliatory counsels were operating on the Im rial Cabinet, 
and that the Emperor would be induced to offer terms of peace ; his Excelleney 
replied that both the Emperor and Count Nesselrode were undoubtedly desirous 
for a pacific arrangement, but that he could perceive that the difficulties to 
obtain this end were very great. He read to me some extracts from a 
private letter from Baron Werther, in which these opinions were clearly 
expressed: Baron Werther stated that the subject of Baron Manteuffel's 
communication had not been ‘responded to on the part of Count Nessel- 
rode,” who had observed that, however desirous they were for peace, still 
they were at a loss to discover the form, as well as the nature, of the 
proposals to be cubmitted, and that they therefore felt that there was nothing 
left but to continue the war with perseverance, for they would never consent to 
a dishonourable peace. I inquired from Baron Manteuffel of what nature was 
the Prussian communication to which Baron Werther adverted. His Excellency 
replied that he had lately addresserl a despatch to Baron Werther for communi- 
cation to the Imperial Cabinet, informing them of the result of the last pacific 
attempt made by His Prussian Majesty in London, Paris, and Vienna; and that 
he had on that occasion urged on the Imperial Cabinet the necessity of their 
making direct and suitable proposals for peace to the Western Powers; that it 
was this invitation to which Count Nesselrode’s reply referred, 

Count Nesselrode likewise observed that he was unable to take the initiative 
during the Emperor’s absence, but he thought that on the Emperor's return 
something might be effected in accordance with the advice proffered by the 
Prussian Cabinet. 

Baron Manteuffel stated, from ‘nformation which had reached him, that 
Russia was in great difficulties, and he seemed evidently to fear some internal 
movement. He expressed much compassion for the Emperor and Count 
Nesselrode, both of whom, he said, were most desirous to bring this unfortunate 
war toa conclusion. 

Acting in the spirit of the opinions expressed by your Lordship, I stated 
to Baron Manteuffel that if the King of Prussia were now to step forward, and 
to hold strong language to the Emperor Alexander, urging him to make the 
concession necessary for the attainment of peace, it would probably be the most 
valuable service he could render to the Emperor ; and it would give His 
Imperial Majesty an additional excuse for peace in the eyes of his people, and 
would avert further disasters from his country. 

Baron Manteutfel replied that he had nade a proposal of this nature to the 
King, but that His Majesty had observed that it would be too harsh were he to 
cause fresh annoyance by menacing language to the Emperor ata moment when 
he was suffering under the weight of disaster and defeat. 

Baron Manteuffel remarked that a luimiliation of Russia was certainly not 
desirable for Europe; and he aevmed to claim for the Emperor Alexander, in his 
prose difficulty and dangerous position, some compassion on the part of the 
Vestern Powers. | 

I replied to his Excellency that the object of the war had not been to humi- 
liate Russia, and that if this result was, as he seems to conceive, likely to ensue, 
it would be solely owing to the obstinacy of Russia, and her perseverance in 
resisting the moderate and just demands of the Western Powers. 

I hiave, &c. 
(Signed) AUGUSTUS LOFTUS.

SINCE the Emperor Nicholas divided the Empire into the Eastern and 
Western Districts, increasing the levies to 13 in 1,000, there has been general 
recruiting ; before that time the conseriptions for completing the army scarcely 
reached 7 or 8 in 1,000. oe 

How great the loss must have been in the last year's campaign 1s seen by 
the fact that the whole Empire, with the exception of the seven Governments 
which have suffered most severely, is called on to raige 10 in 1,000 souls at the 
next enrolment.

Inclosure 2 in No. 207. 


Address. 
(Translation.) 


THE “Moscow Gazette’ contains the address of the Minister of the Interior 
to the Marshals of the Nobility of the various Governments, dated S'5ery— 


“J have been named by His Imperial Majesty's Decree of the 20th August, 
Minister of the Interior, In taking upon me, with most submissive reverence, 
this most gracious appointment of our beloved Emperor's, I can but be proud 
that to the post of Minister of the Interior is joined the high obligation of repre- 
senting before the throne the high-minded Russian nobility which, from all ages, 
has been known by its devotion to the Imperial House and its ardent love for our 
fitherland, and which has now, in times of difficulty, shown itself to be animated 
by the same sentiments. 

“Our Most Gracious Sovereign has commanded me to maintain intact the 
pavieee of the nobility granted by his ancestors. 

7 esteem myself happy in making known this act of Imperial favour, at 
the same time I assure you, with ratifying consciousness, that I have always 
been firmly convinced that the nobility are most Important cooperators, and the 
firm support of the fatherland. With this conviction | expect from you the 
vealous fulfilment of all the duties of Government, and cordial cooperation in 
carrying out the orders of the authorities, and | hope that 1 shall find no 
as rivalry than that which consists in aiming at the same object, the common 
welfare. 

« You well know how much depends on the choice of fit nobles for service, 
what obligations are imposed on them, and how closely the fulfilment of these 
duties ave connected with the repose and well-doing of private individuals ; and 
one may, therefore, with propriety hope that appointments to elective offices 
may fall on men who not only bear the title of nobility, but who are noble by 
their intellects and hearts. 

(Signed) « LANSKOL"”

No, 208. 
Mr. Magenis to the Earl of Clarendon, —(Recetved November 5.) 


(No. 82.) 
My Lord, Stockholm, October 29, 1855. 


I HAVE the honour to acknowledge the receipt of your Lordship’s 
despatches from No, 41 to No. 45 inclusive, together with their several inclosures, 
by the messenger Ball, yesterday. 


aL 


220 


I lost no time in conferring with my French colleague as to the course we 
should pursue. I learned from M. Lobstein that he had not received his 
instructions, nor had any ‘timation reached him, excepting Lord Cowley's 
telegraphic despatch to your Lordship of the 18th instant, inelosed in your 
Lordship’s No. 44, which I communicated to him, that they were about to be 
forwarded to him. He thinks it probable that they may come by the next Stettin 
steamboat, which is due on the Ist of next month. We agreed, however, that 
it was advisable to lose no time, and Laccordingly apprized Baron Stierneld 
that my messenger had arrived, and that | had a communication to make to 
him ; his Excellency proposed calling on me this morning, and has just left me. 

I commenced by communicating to him Lord Cowley’s telegraphic despatch, 
stating that the French Government approved of the instructions your Lordship 
had prepared for my guidance, and would send off to M. Lobstein in a day or 
two similar instructions. I then read to him and gave him a copy of the raft 
of ‘Treaty which I was. instructed to propose to the Swedish Government, 
observing to him, that although short, it was highly important to this country, 
as it contained a general guarantee by Great Britain and France of His Majesty’s 
Swedish and Norwegian possessions against any aggression on the side of 
Russia, in return for which we required alone that His Majesty should neither 
cede any portion of those territories, nor any right of pasturage or fishery, to 
that Power. 1 read slowly over to his Excellency the project of the ‘Treaty in 
French, and he thus assured himself that the copy which I had put into his 
hands was correct. ‘The only observation which he made was, that he was 
desirous that the naval and military forces which the Contracting Parties engaged 
to place at the disposal of the King of Sweden, in case of necessity, should be 
specified in the Treaty. 

L answered that the qualification of * sufficient” was far more advantageous 
to the Swedish Government, than ifwe specified any particular number of troops 
and ships; that two Governments, such as those of Great Britain and France, 
would not compromise the safety of any expeditionary corps they might send to the 
aid of Sweden, or endanger their national honour by sending an inadequate 
force, and by not duly supporting and reinforcing it, till the object they sought 
was attained. Baron Stierneld added, it was more to satisly pea opinion and 
the Chambers than fromany doubt of the force of my reply that he had stated 
this objection. 

His Excellency then promier me to forward immediately the project of 
the Treaty to the King, who is still at Drottningholm, an hour from this capital, 
and Lurged him to move His Majesty to give him his full powers to treat, 
adding that I had received, by the messenger, Her Majesty's, and that I had no 
doubt M, Lobstein would very shortly receive his. His Excellency promised 
me that no time should be lost, and as soon as M. Lobstein’s messenger arrives 
we can enter on negotiations. 

send this despatch to Berlin by a servant who is leaving my service, and 
for the present I shall detain the messenger, 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) ARTHUR CG. MAGENIS.

Mr. Magenis to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Recewed November 12.) 


(No. 85. Seoret and Confidential ) 


My Lord, Stockholm, November 1, 1855, 
ae HAD years) the honour to reccive an invitation to dine with the 
King at Dro unene m, and afler dinner I had a long and very satisfactory 

conversation with is Majesty on the subject of the Defensive Treaty. 

His Majesty having taken me into a room where we were alone, invited 
me to sit down with him on a sofa, and informed me that he had carefull 
examined the draft of Treaty which I presented to Baron Stierneld on the 
29th ultimo. He expressed generally his satisfaction with that project, and 
added that he had few and unimportant alterations to propose. : 

_ His Majesty then said, with great frankness, that he had direeted Baron 
Stierneld, who is to be his Plenipotentiary, to endeavour to come to an under, 
standing with my French colleague and myself, with respect to the change in 
the dratt which he desired, but not to make its adoption a condilio sine gud non. 
His Majesty observed, that it was always his wish to be courteous (courtois), and 


* No, 210. 


222 


to avoid giving unnecessary offence, when it did not weaken his own cause. 
He desired, then, that ‘‘la Russie’ should be substituted for “l’Empereur de 
Russie” in the draft of Treaty. His Majesty disclaimed any wish to avoid this 
difficulty by substituting “ a une Puissance queleonque,” thus omitting even the 

ted the Treaty was and could be directed 


mention of Russia. He frankly sta 
ainst Russia alone, and that it would be absurd to suppose that Great Britain 


and France should be called on hereafter to assist Sweden, if at war with 
Denmark or Holland; and he desired alone from the motives I have mentioned, 
to avoid naming “!Empereur de Russie,’ and to substitute ~ la Russie."’ 
Such a substitution would, His Majesty observed, avoid personal offence, and 
that he was fully justified in concluding a Treaty such as we had proposed to 
him ; it was purely defensive; if Russia made no aggressions on his territories 
‘t was still-born (né-mort) ; if she did, his precaution was fully justified, and the 
past history of Sweden was an open book, in which the world might read that 
every act of precaution against Russia was warranted on the part of a Sovereign 
of Sweden. 

J observed, that 1 supposed the terms “ PEmpereur de Russie” had been 
adopted because that Monarch was the incarnation of government and power 
in that country, and that if any Sovereign could truly use the words of Louis XIV, 
«L'Btat e’est moi,” it was His Imperial Majesty. I confessed I thought “la 
Russie” would fully express the object we had in view, and I promised to repo 
to your Lordship this proposed alteration, and to ask your instructions, which 
I have done by telegraph this day, in a cyphered despatch. 

It was, however, impossible in a despatch by telegraph, to do more than 
hint at the reasons which make me think it advisable that we should accept 
His Majesty's suggestion, and | will now more fully advert to them. 

It appears to me, in the first place, that the substitution of “la Russie’ 
for “ ’Empereur de Russie,” in no way alters the sense or spirit of the Treaty ; 
on the contrary, it makes if more comprehensive; it would apply with equal 

force, whether the Ruler of that country was an Emperor or an Kmpress, and 
your Lordship will recollect, that since the death of Peter the Great, in 1725, 
than four ruling Empresses of Russia, The alteration 


there have been no less 

would also be equally applicable whatever the form of Government of that 
country might be; whereas if His Swedish Majesty only stipulated to make no 
concession or exchange of territory with the Emperor of Russia, a quibble might 
possibly be raised in the two alternatives which | have put forward. 

1 may further add, that His Majesty is somewhat justified in asking for 
this alteration, because in all his conversations with my French colleague and 
myself, he has invariably spoken of Russia, and not of the Emperor of Russia: 
and when proposing to M, Lobstein a general, instead of the partial, guarantee 
whieh we had offered, His Majesty’s words were; “Pour ma part je prendrai 
engagement & ne céder a la Russie, ke. ;" and finally, that it was the late, and 
not the present Emperor, who made the attempts to gain a footing in Finmark, 


which have led to the present ‘Treaty. 

Such are the reasons which lead me to think it will be advisable to adopt 
the alteration in the draft of Treaty which His Majesty desires. 1 have reason 
to know that his Ministers would have preferred omitting all mention of Russia, 
and turning the difficulty by the general terms of “ une Puissance queleonque ;” 
a proposal which I should have strenuously opposed, and we have been saved 
that difficulty by His Majesty's candour and firmness. I think, too, it would be 
a satisfaction to His Majesty if we adopt his suggestion, and 1 candidly confess 
that his straightforward conduct on this question uppears to me to deserve any 
concession which we can make, when unattended with a sacrifice of principle, 

At the end of our conversation His Majesty said that there was another 
part of the draft of Treaty which required fuller explanation, namely, that at 
the end of the IInd Article, which states >” la nature, l'importance, et la desti- 
nation des forces dont il s’agit, seront, le cas échéant, arrétées d'un commun 
accord entre les trois Puissances,” should be added, ‘fil est bien entendu que 
entretien de ces forces reste aux frais des Puissances qui les auront envoyées,” 
His Majesty added, 1 must reeallect, that he was concluding a ‘Treaty for the 
future as well as the present ; and he was unwilling that it should be said 

hereatter he had been unmindful of the future interests of his country. 

I answered, I could not believe it was ever intended that any auxiliary 
force, which might, in virtue of this ‘I'reaty, be sent to protect Sweden, should

No, 214. 


The Eorl of Clarendon to Lord Cowley. 
(No. 1,315.) 
My Lord, Foreign Office, November 15, 1855. 


HER Majesty's Government learn with great satisfaction, from the contents 
of your Excellency’s despatch No. 1,442 of the sth instant, that the Government 
of the Em concur with them in opinion as to the necessity of obtaining 
from the Porte, before the recommencement of negotiations for peace, the 
settlement of all doubtful and difficult questions, relating both to the treatment 
of the non-Mussulman subjects of the Porte, and to religious matters in general. 

Your Excellency will again bring under the consideration of Count 
Walewski the instruction which, if the French Government had concurred in 
it, would in May last have been addressed to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe, and 
you will state that, in the opinion of Her Majesty's Government, it comprises 
the principal points upon which dificulties are likely to arise, and the settlement 
of which, without the intervention of Russia, appears to be of urgent importance. 

Your Excellency will consult Count Valewski as to any amendments or 
additions which he might desire to introduce, and you will suggest that no time 
should be lost in instructing the two Embassies formally to bring the whole 
subject under the consideration of the Porte. 

Her Majesty's Government would have wished to decline the cooperation 
of Austria in these matters: first, because that cooperation was probably offered 
with reference rather to the settlement of the first point than of the fourth; 
and, secondly, because it does not seem politic to allow Austria to enjoy the 
advantages of being a belligerent, without bearing any of the burthens of that 
condition, She has not placed herself in the same line with England and France, 
and does not take part in the war, and as she does not share in the sacrifices 
and exertions, she is not entitled to share in the fruits which those sacrifices and 
exertions may produce. 

Her Majesty's Government will not press their own opinion upon this 
subject, as Count Colloredo has read to me a despatch from Count Buol, by 
which it appears that Count Walewski has expressed to Baron Hubner the 
ceadiness of the French Government to accept the Austrian offer of cooperation 
upon the fourth poe but the intervention of Austria with respect to the 
settlement of the first point, is open to erie objections. 

We have already had ample evidence that the arrangements desired by 
Austria are inconsistent with the welfare and eood government of the Prinel- 
palities ; that in fact she aims at transferring to herself the power hitherto 
exercised by Russia over the Principalities, or, at least, of sharing that power 
with Russia; and England and France could not, with any regard to justice or 
humanity, be parties to an arrangement whieh would deprive the Principalities 
of all hope, by consigning them to a yoke more grinding and oppressive than 
that which they have hitherto undergone. 

It would seem expedient, therefore, that in the first instance the two 
Embassies should communicate confidentially with the Turkish Minister upon 
this important subject, and invite the Porte to draw up a plan for the future 
administration of the Principalities, which scheme should be submitted for the 
consideration of the English and French Governments. 

Your Excellency will further state to Count Walewski that Her Majesty’s 
Government are convinced that no improvement in Wallachia is possible so long 
as Prince Stirbey continues to govern that country, and their desire is, that the 
Porte should be enjoined not to reappoint the Hospodar when his turn of office 
shall expire next spring, and moreover not to nominate his son in his place. 


Iam, &c. 
(Signed) CLARENDON.

No. 216, 


Mr. Magenis to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received November 27.) 


(No. 97.) 


My Lord, Stockholm, November 19, 1855, 

_ GENERAL CANROBERT, having concluded his special embassy to 
this Court, left Stockholm, after a residence of a fortnight, this morning, for 
Copenhagen, 

His Excellency, during his stay here, has been treated with the most 
marked attention by the King and the Court, and he received, from His 
Majestv’s hands, on the 17th instant, the Grand Cross of the Seraphim, Iam 
told there was a question of giving him the First Class of the Order of the Sword, 
of which there is at present but one, Count L. Walmoden; but it was thought 
that such an honour, conferred on a F'rench General for his success against 
Russia, would be too open a declaration for the present moment. 

The reception which General Canrobert has met with from the public has 
been e hay! warm and flattering; and if it is to be interpreted as a proof of 
sympathy for the cause of the Western Powers—which it undoubtedly in part 
was—nothing could be more gratifying. Crowds of people thronged his passage 
wherever he went ; and the only ae he appeared in the theatre he was received 
with the warmest cheering, and the French national air was played. He had 
arrived previously to the Royal Family; and upon the King’s entry, His Majesty 
wae Breeton with still greater warmth, and the General was invited into the | 

yal box, 

General Canrobert, with great judgment, has avoided as much as possible 
these public ovations, and declined a banquet, which the merchants of this place 
wished to invite him to. It is now proposed to forward to France, for the 
benefit. of soldiers wounded in the war, what the dinner would have cost. | 

The General travels by land to Helsingborg, in Royal carnages, accom- 
panied by an Aide-de-camp of the King; and he is to see, at each of the places 
where he stops, a portion of the Indelta army quartered in the neighbourhood. 
He has made himself intimately acquainted with the military resources of this 
country, and has had every facility offered to him in the attainment of that 
object; and he cannot ful to have learned His Majesty’s views and intentions, 
should the present war continue next year. 

T have reason to believe that General Canrobert’s mission was rather to 
make inquiries than proposals, and rather to treat the military than the diplo- 
matic question; and his Excellency expressed himself to me in the highest terms 
of satisfaction with its results. 

I have, &c. 


&c 
(Signed) ARTHUR C. MAGENIS.

General Nordin says he has good grounds for believing that the Russian 
army under the orders of Prince Gortchakolf, occupying the position from the 
north side of Sebastopol to Baktchi-Serai, amounted to 115,000 men, of which 
foree from $5,000 to 90,000 were Infantry.

No. 218. 
Mr. Magenis to the Earl of Clarendon.—( Received November 27.) 


(No. 99.) 
My Lord, Stockholm, November 20, 1855. 

I HAD to receive, on the evening of the 18th instant, your Lordship’s 
telegraphic despatch of the preceding day, in reply to my telegraphic despatch, 
which I may add was dated the 12th, though possibly the date was not 
transmitted by the telegraph. As my French colleague did not receive the 
instructions of his Government till the evening of the 19th, I did not mention to 
Baron Stierneld, at the usual conference on that day, that I had received your 
instructions, but merely observed that the delay appeared to me to offer grounds 
for supposing that objections were felt by the British and French Governments 
to the alteration proposed by the King, and that [thought His Majesty would 


do well to consider, Buppoaine my apprehensions proved correct, whether he: 


could not accept the wording of the end of the IInd Article which he had already 
proposed. 

M. Lobstein and myself having informed Baron Stierneld that we hail 
received the answers of our respective Governments, his Excellency appoimted 
us to meet him this day at the Foreign Office. We had previously agreed upon 
the common language which we should use, and though M. Lobstein’s instruc. 
tions, of which | inclose a copy, gave him a greater latitude than I conceived I 
had from your Lordship’s, and though he had in consequence at first intended to 
say, that if His Majesty made the maintenance of the words, ‘‘ to place at the 
disposal of His Majesty,” &c., a conditio sine qué non, he would inform his 
Government, and if he received no contrary orders, would be prepared to sign 
the Treaty with those words on the 24th instant, yet at my suggestion he at 
once consented not to make this statement, 

We agreed to say, and as M, Lobstein is my senior here, | consented that 
he should be the spokesman, that it was true the draft of Treaty originally 
sroposed contained the expression “to place at the disposal of His Majesty,” 
Ke, but His Majesty had thought the * rédaction” required further explanation 
and had proposed an addition to it. ‘That our respective Governments, when 
considering His Majesty’s proposal, bad come to the conviction that the com- 
mencement of the clause, whieh was susceptible of the interpretation that the Kin 
of Sweden should have the supreme command of any auxiliary forces whic 
might be sent in virtue of the Treaty, was in contradiction with the end of the 
Article, which stated that “the description, number, and destination of such 
forces was to be determined by the common consent of the three Powers,” and 
they had in consequence proposed the alteration already in his hands; that no 
doubt every deference would be paid to the opinions of a King of Sweden at the 
head of his army, defending his own territories, but that we could not consent to 


227 


his haying absolute command over our forces; and we pointed out the possible 
misuse which such a power might entail. We added, that if His Majesty made 
the maintenance of the words ‘‘to place at the disposal of His Majesty,” &c., a 
conditio sine qud non, we requested he would be pleased to state his reasons, as 
well as the sense which he attached to those words, and whether he conceived 
they implied that the King of Sweden was to exercise the supreme command 
over the auxiliary forces. 

Baron Stierneld admitted the apparent contradiction which we had pointed 
out, and then proceeded to enlarge on the dangers and difficulties of a divided 
command. He asked whether the contradiction might not be avoided by the 
omission of the end of the Article, which we at once declared to be impossible, 
ae he candidly allowed that such a stipulation was customary in all defensive 

ities. 

I then stated, that I was instructed to adhere to the form already proposed, 
and that if His Majesty persisted in maintaining the words which we wished to 
omit, I would refer to my Government, and report His Majesty’s reasons. I 
could not promise that they would be accepted, but at all events delay would 
ensue, and it was the evident interest of the three Contracting Parties to conclude 
the Treaty with as little loss of time as possible, I said I had looked over the 
numerous Treaties concluded by Great Britain in 1813; that I had found in one 
alone the expression “place at the disposal,” and that in that one with Prussia 
i be not troops, but subsidies, that we placed at His Prussian Majesty's 

aaah: . 
L added, that when we were willing to incur the heavy responsibility of 
guaranteeing the possessions of His Majesty, it was clear we felt it to be our 
interest to do so; we wished to draw a line of circumvallation to Russian 
ambition : if, then, the case arose, both the interests of their country, and the 
Conon danger, would produce concert between the auxiliary and Swedish 
‘orees. 

Baron Stierneld’s tone, during the whole of our conference, was more 
favourable than I had expected, and he promised us to lay our observations and 
requests at once before the King, and to acquaint us as soon as possible with His 
Majesty's decision. 

I received this evening a letter from Baron Stierneld, of which J inclose copy, 
informing me that His Majesty had endeavoured to find a modification of the 
terms ‘‘to place at the disposal,” &c., which would be acceptable, and that he 
thought the term ‘“‘fournir” offered a solution, His Excellency added, that “ the 
King had ordered him not to conceal from us that this was the last concession 
which he could make,” &c. 

Llost no time in communicating Baron Stierneld’s letter to my French 
colleague, and we were both of opinion, that under all the circumstances of the 
case we ought to agree to the proposed modification, and we have in consequence 
informed Baron Stierneld that we accepted it, and it is not impossible the Treaty 
may in consequence be signed to-morrow, 

I venture to hope that my acceptance of the proposed modification may 
meet with your Lordship’s approval. 1 think it avoids the objections justly felt 
to the former “ rédaction,” and, in conjunction with Baron Stierneld’s explana- 
tions, removes those difficulties, while it expresses nothing more than we had 
proposed. 

have, &c. 
(Signed) ARTHUR C. MAGENIS,

Mon cher Mr. Magenis, Mardi 20. 

LE Roi s'est occupe a trouver une modification au terme “mettre & la 
disposition,” &c., acceptable aux deux Parties Contractantes, et Sa Majesté s'est 
arrété au terme ‘fournir,” qui n'implique en aucune maniere ce que vous

No. 219. 
Mr. Magenis to the Earl of Clarendon —(Reeeived November 27.) 


(No, 100.) 
My Lord, Stockholm, November 21, 1855 


1 HAVE the satisfaction to inform your Lordship that the Defensive 
Treaty between Great Britain and France on the one part, and Sweden on the 
other, was signed this afternoon by the respective Plenipotentiaries of those 
countries at the private residence of the Swedish Minister for Foreign Affairs 5 
and | have the honour to transmit herewith my counterpart of that Act, 
together with certified copies of the full powers of the French and Swedish 
Plenipotentiaries ; the full powers of the latter are in copy and translation, 

he King of Sweden 1s desirous that the ratifications should be exchanged 
as quickly as possible, and, until that is done, that the ‘Treaty should be kept 
secret. ‘The secret has hitherto been wonderfully kept; and though suspicions 
may have been awakened, and conclusions drawn, from the late embassy of 
General Canrobert, I have no reason to helieve that any one beyond the few 
initiated, is aware of what has been doing, 

As M. Lobstein forwards his counterpart of the Treaty by an Attaché to his 
Mission, direct to Paris by way of Lubeck, Lam unwilling that mine should 
travel by the circuitous way of Copenhagen. I have in consequene® decided to 
dispatch the messenger Ball by the Lubeck steamboat to-morrow morning, and 
1 have apprised Mr. Buchanan, by telegraph, to forward any despatches he may 
have for your Lordship to the Consulate at Hamburgh, where the messenger 15 
directed to call. 

T cannot avoid offering my sincere congratulations to your Lordship on the 
conclusion of this important Treaty, which has been under discussion nearly five 
months. 1t affords 9 stron proof of the foresight of Her Majesty's Government, 
and while it guards us, 9s believe it will, against a great and possible future 
danger, it can hardly fail at once to Increase considerably the sympathies of the 
United Kingdoms of Sweden and Norway for the cause of the allied Western 
Powers. 

In conclusion, | have great pleasure In bearing testimony to the cordial, 


229 


loyal, and zealous assistance which I have met with from my French colleague, 

. Lobstein, not only in the negotiations which have led to the conclusion of 
the present Treaty, but on every occasion since my arrival in Sweden, when the 
interests of our respective countries have been concerned. 


I have, &c. 
(Signed) ARTHUR C, MAGENIS,

I HAVE the honour to acknowledge the receipt of the note which your 
Excellency addressed to me, dated this day, in which you inquire whether I am 
authored: to declare that the maintenance of the naval and military forces which 
Her Majesty the Queen of Great Britain and Treland engages, by Article IT of

Sweden and Norway, the case arising, should be at the expense of the British 
Government; and I have no hesitation in replying to your Excellency that it
